# Majmu' al-Fatawa - Tome 9

## Page 4270

PART NINE
THE BOOK OF LOGIC

In the name of Allah, the Most Merciful, the Bestower of Mercy.

All praise is due to Allah alone, and blessings and peace be upon him after whom there is no prophet.

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—was asked:

“What do you say about ‘logic’? Is someone who says that it is a communal obligation correct or mistaken?”

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah.

As for logic, if someone says that it is a communal obligation and that anyone unfamiliar with it can have no confidence in any of his knowledge, then this assertion is utterly unsound on several grounds

---

## Page 4271

—many in number—and involves unsound notions and numerous false claims that cannot be exhaustively examined here.

Indeed, experience, past and present, shows that anyone who binds himself to using logic in his inquiries and debates is unsound in both inquiry and debate, and frequently unable to substantiate and explain his knowledge.

The most charitable interpretation of such a claim is to suppose that its proponent and others like him had been utterly ignorant and astray, having lost all means of guidance, and found nothing to turn them away from those errors except some of the sound elements contained in logic. Some of these elements have indeed led many such people to abandon some of their false beliefs, even if they gained no beneficial truth and fell into other falsehoods. Even so, the obligation to study logic cannot in any way be ascribed to Islamic law. Anyone in this condition has only himself to blame: he abandoned the truth Allah commanded until he came to need falsehood.

It is well known that declaring logic obligatory is the position of its extremists and ignorant adherents. Even its accomplished practitioners do not adhere to its rules in all their fields of knowledge. Rather, they disregard them, whether because of their length, their lack of benefit, their unsoundness, or their lack of clear distinctions and the vagueness and ambiguity they contain. For many passages in it are meat from a scrawny camel atop a rugged mountain: neither is the mountain easy enough to climb, nor the meat fat enough to be worth carrying away.

---

## Page 4272

For this reason, Muslim scholars and the leading authorities in religion have continually condemned logic and its practitioners and warned against both. I have even seen a fatwa from later scholars bearing the signatures of several eminent figures of their time, including leading Shāfiʿī and Hanafī scholars and others, containing forceful statements prohibiting it and calling for the punishment of its practitioners. Among the well-known accounts that have reached us is the following:

Shaykh Abū ʿAmr ibn al-Salāh ordered that a well-known school be taken away from Abū al-Hasan al-Āmidī, saying, “Taking it from him is better than capturing Acre.” Yet no one in al-Āmidī’s time was more deeply versed than he in speculative theology and philosophy, and he was among the best of their practitioners in adherence to Islam and the soundest in creed.

It is well known that subtle matters—whether true or false, whether matters of faith or unbelief—can only be understood through intelligence and discernment. Likewise, the practitioners of logic may deem someone ignorant because he does not share their learning, even if his faith is better than theirs, when he falls short in intelligence and eloquence. They are as Allah Most High described:

“Indeed, those who committed crimes used to laugh at those who believed;
and when they passed by them, they would exchange derisive glances;
and when they returned to their own people, they would return jesting;
and when they saw the believers, they would say, ‘These people are truly astray.’
Yet they had not been sent as guardians over them.
So today those who believed are laughing at the disbelievers,
reclining on adorned couches, looking on.
Have the disbelievers been repaid for what they used to do?”

Thus, when they blindly accept from their false masters that whatever is not obtained through this syllogistic method is not knowledge, even though many of them may obtain nothing through this syllogistic method by which to acquire

---

## Page 4273

the faith required of them, a person may thus become an unbeliever, a heretic, a hypocrite—ignorant, astray and leading others astray, profoundly unjust and ungrateful—and become one of the greatest enemies of the messengers, concerning whom Allah said:

“Thus We have appointed for every prophet an enemy from among the criminals. But your Lord is sufficient as a guide and helper.”

“Those who disbelieve say, ‘Why was the Quran not sent down to him all at once?’ Thus it is, so that We may strengthen your heart thereby, and We have recited it with measured deliberation.”

“They never bring you an argument without Our bringing you the truth and the best explanation.”

Some of them may acquire faith, whether through this method or otherwise, while also acquiring hypocrisy through it. A person may therefore possess both faith and hypocrisy, being a believer in one state and a hypocrite in another. He may apostatize, either from the foundation of the religion or from some of its prescribed laws, with an apostasy of hypocrisy or an apostasy of disbelief. This is widespread and prevalent, especially in times and lands where ignorance of divine guidance, disbelief, and hypocrisy prevail. These people exhibit such astonishing ignorance, injustice, falsehood, disbelief, hypocrisy, and misguidance that there is no room here to recount it all.

Hence, when many of them recognized the ignorance and misguidance inherent in this denial, they began to say: “Sanctified souls—such as the souls of the prophets and the allies of Allah—receive knowledge through emanation without the syllogistic method.” They all agree that some souls have no need to weigh their knowledge

---

## Page 4274

by the formal standards of logic. They may, however, say that such a person is a philosopher by natural aptitude: syllogisms form in his mind without his having to learn this discipline, just as an Arab speaks Arabic without studying grammar, and a poet composes poetry without knowing prosody. Yet the fact that some people can dispense with these standards does not mean that others can. None of them disputes that many souls have no need of this discipline.

The question here is whether, in attaining knowledge, souls can dispense entirely with the aforementioned syllogism itself and its particular premises. Dispensing with this kind of syllogism is one thing; dispensing with the rule-governed discipline by which syllogisms are assessed is another. For they claim that it is “a rule-governed instrument whose observance prevents the mind from erring in its reasoning.” The falsity of this claim has been explained at length elsewhere.

Having established that it is of no benefit—even though it may contain knowledge obtainable without it—we have further established that, even if we assumed it could impart some knowledge to some people, it would still be impermissible to say: “Neither that person nor any other human being has any way of attaining that knowledge except through something like the logical syllogism.” Such a claim is made without knowledge and is an outright falsehood. For this reason, Muslim speculative theologians—even though there is a measure of religious innovation among them—have long refuted it and its adherents, explained how one can dispense with it, and shown how it engenders harm, ignorance, and disbelief, in ways that

---

## Page 4275

are beyond the scope of this discussion—to say nothing of other Muslim groups, their scholars, and their leading authorities—as Judge Abū Bakr ibn al-Bāqillānī mentioned in his book al-Daqā’iq (The Subtleties).

As for the poetic syllogism, it serves merely to stimulate the imagination and stir the soul. This becomes clear from their division of syllogisms into five types: demonstrative, rhetorical, dialectical, poetic, and fallacious or sophistical. The sophistical resembles truth but is false: it is counterfeit wisdom. This is not our concern here; rather, our concern is with those three.

They say: “The dialectical syllogism is one whose premises the interlocutor concedes; the rhetorical is one whose premises are widely accepted among people; and the demonstrative is one whose premises are known to be true.”

Many premises, despite being rhetorical or dialectical—or even poetic—are also certain and demonstrative. They are called demonstrative insofar as they are known with certainty; rhetorical insofar as they are widely recognized and accepted by people generally; and dialectical insofar as a particular person concedes them.

This is the account given by those innovators among the Sabians who neither discussed prophethood nor addressed it by denying or affirming it. Failing to believe in the messengers and follow them is disbelief and misguidance, even if one does not believe them to be lying. Disbelief and misguidance are broader than declaring them liars.

---

## Page 4276

As for the statement of some later scholars concerning widely accepted premises—“These are statements accepted because their proponent is supported by something that requires his word to be accepted,” and similar assertions—this is one of the additions that argument compelled them to make, and which they recognized they were obliged to accept according to the method of their predecessors. Hence most later Sabians, namely the philosophers, combined their beliefs with pure monotheism, just as most adherents of pure monotheism who entered into philosophy mixed pure monotheism with Sabianism and confounded truth with falsehood. I mean the innovated Sabianism that contains no faith—more precisely, no belief in prophethood—such as the Sabianism of the originator of logic and his followers.

As for ancient Sabianism, among its adherents are believers in Allah and the Last Day—those who believed and did righteous deeds; they shall have no fear, nor shall they grieve. Likewise, some forms of Judaism and Christianity had adherents who were misguided innovators even before Muhammad, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent, whereas others had adherents who followed the truth. These are those who believed in Allah and the Last Day and did righteous deeds; they shall have no fear, nor shall they grieve.

Those scholars who wrote on logic and said, “The rhetorical syllogism is that which yields probable belief, just as the demonstrative syllogism is that which yields certain knowledge,” neither understood what those thinkers meant nor spoke correctly. Both rhetorical and dialectical syllogisms may yield probable belief, just as demonstrative syllogisms may have premises that are widely accepted and conceded. Thus, syllogistic premises are classified according to the grounds on which they are accepted. Sometimes one accepts

---

## Page 4277

a statement because it is known to be true, for knowledge requires acceptance. If, however, it does not yield knowledge, accepting it requires some other reason. If it is accepted because it is widely held, then the syllogism is rhetorical, even if it yields neither knowledge nor probable belief. It is likewise rhetorical when its proposition is widely held, even if it does yield knowledge or probable belief. The same applies to the dialectical syllogism.

They sometimes illustrate widely held, accepted propositions that do not constitute knowledge with statements such as:

“Knowledge is good and ignorance is bad; justice is good and injustice is bad.”

These and similar rational judgments concerning conduct are affirmed by those who maintain that reason can discern good and evil. They claim that if we consult reason alone, we find no such judgment in it. They may also give as examples the propositions that an existent must either be separate from another existent or be co-located with it; that an existent must be situated in some direction; or that it must be capable of being seen. They claim that these are judgments of the estimative faculty, not of innate reason. They say: “For reason accepts premises through which the falsity of the original judgment becomes known.” All this is evident confusion to anyone who considers it carefully.

As for those propositions they call widely held but not known to be true, they are self-evident rational truths about which minds are more certain than they are about many truths of arithmetic and natural science. They are just as most Muslim theologians—indeed, most theologians throughout the world, of every persuasion—have said: self-evident rational propositions; but

---

## Page 4278

they may not explain this well. For the goodness and badness in question are the goodness and badness of actions. An action is good insofar as it brings about the ends that a living being seeks and desires for their own sake; its badness is the opposite. This is indeed the case: knowledge, truthfulness, and justice bring about ends sought and desired for their own sake. Thus, an action’s goodness or badness consists in its bringing about the end desired for its own sake or being contrary to it.

For this reason, the term “true” is sometimes used with reference to affirmation and negation: “This is true,” meaning established, and “This is false,” meaning negated. When applied to actions, it refers to attaining the intended end. Thus, one says, “This action is right,” meaning beneficial or productive of the intended end; or one calls it “vain,” meaning that it has no benefit, and so forth.

As for their claim that self-evident intuition and innate reason may make a judgment whose falsity subsequently becomes apparent to them through a syllogism, this is mistaken. A syllogism necessarily relies on self-evident, innate premises. If such premises could be erroneous, with their error becoming apparent only through a syllogism, then the innate premises themselves would conflict with the conclusion entailed by a syllogism whose premises are innate. Rejecting these innate premises on account of those is no more justified than the reverse. Rather, error is more likely in what has fewer premises. Thus, what is known through a syllogism involving innate premises is more liable to error than what is known through innate reason alone.

---

## Page 4279

They invoke this when denying Allah’s exaltedness above the Throne and advancing similar falsehoods.

The point here is that their early representatives did not mention premises received from the prophets, whereas their later representatives structured it on that basis. They did so either through the approach of the Sabians who mingled pure monotheism with Sabianism, such as Ibn Sīnā and others like him, or through the approach of the speculative theologians who thought well of what the logicians had presented and argued that it could establish knowledge of what the prophetic revelations entail.

As for the former, he classified the prophets’ knowledge as intuitive knowledge, owing to the exceptional clarity and purity of those holy souls, and held that souls’ intuitive powers have no fixed limit.

The world must have an order established by someone wise. Thus, the souls granted support are endowed with the power to know through intuition what others do not know. Speech and angels appear to them inwardly, so that they hear and see within themselves what others do not hear. They also possess a practical power, through which the prime matter of the world obeys them, that others do not possess. These extraordinary capacities in the faculties of knowledge, hearing and sight, and in practical power and ability, constitute prophethood in their view.

Yet it is well known that intuition rests on analogical reasoning, as explained previously. As for what a person hears and sees within himself, it is of the same kind as dreams. Experiences of this kind occur to many ordinary people and unbelievers, let alone Allah’s allies and prophets. How, then, can this be regarded as the utmost extent of prophethood? Even if what they attribute to the prophets is more perfect and nobler, it is like one king being more powerful than another.

For this reason, they came to say that prophethood can be acquired, and they did not affirm the descent of

---

## Page 4280

angels from Allah to those servants whom He chooses and selects. Nor did they affirm that He intended to speak to any particular one of His messengers. Thus, one of their early representatives is reported to have said to Moses son of ʿImrān: “I believe you in everything except that the Cause of causes spoke to you. I cannot believe you in this.” Consequently, those led astray by such discourse have come to claim equality with the prophets and messengers, or even superiority over them. This is widespread among people who consider themselves the most perfect of humankind, yet are among the most ignorant, unjust, unbelieving and hypocritical.

As for the speculative theologians who follow logic, they say: “Through this reasoning, one can establish the Creator’s existence and power, the possibility of sending messengers, and Allah’s supporting them with what requires that they be believed in what they say.” This approach is closer to that of the believing scholars, although it may contain various kinds of falsehood: sometimes arising from what they have uncritically adopted from the logicians, and sometimes from their own innovations, which are not our subject here.

The Jewish and Christian logicians follow a similar course. Yet guidance, knowledge and clarity are greater among Muslim philosophers and speculative theologians than among the people of the two Scriptures, owing to the corruption within those two religions. The purpose here, however, is to establish prophethood in general. The adherents of the revealed religions agree on this, but the Jews and Christians believed in some messengers and disbelieved in others, while the Sabian philosophers and their like believed in some attributes of messengership but not others. Thus, if a philosophizer happens to be one of the People of

---

## Page 4281

the Scripture, he combines both forms of disbelief: disbelief in the Seal of the Messengers, and disbelief in the reality of the attributes of messengership in all the messengers. Such, then, is their position.

They may therefore be asked—given their knowledge that human beings differ in their powers of perception—what prevents someone’s hearing and sight from being extraordinarily enhanced, so that he hears and sees things existing outside the mind that others do not perceive? As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“I see what you do not see and hear what you do not hear. Heaven creaks, and well may it creak: there is not a space four fingers wide in it without an angel standing, sitting, bowing, or prostrating.”

This is perception, through the outward or inward senses, of something existing outside the mind. Likewise, you know that self-evident universal knowledge has no fixed limit among human beings. How, then, do you know that certain souls possess self-evident knowledge peculiar to themselves alone, or shared with others like them, which is not self-evident to you?

If this is possible—and people across the earth generally affirm that it occurs among those who are not prophets, let alone among prophets—then your logic offers no way of attaining such knowledge. It belongs neither to widely accepted premises nor to dialectical premises, nor do you regard its underlying premises as certain. Yet you have no knowledge that would establish its nonexistence, while the majority of people across the earth, in earlier and later generations, affirm its existence.

If you reject it, then—in addition to disbelief, rejection of the truth, and loss in this world and the Hereafter—you also abandon your own logic and depart from the obligation you have imposed upon yourselves: to assert only what a syllogism entails. For you have no syllogism to support this denial

---

## Page 4282

nor any argument worth mentioning. That is why you have offered no argument for it; this denial has simply found its way into your discourse without any supporting argument.

If you say, “Rather, it is true,” you acknowledge that some truth cannot be weighed on the scales of your logic.

If you say, “We do not know whether it is true or false,” you acknowledge that the greatest and noblest objects of inquiry cannot be weighed on the scales of logic. If you affirm it, logic does not support you. If you deny it, logic does not support you. If you remain in doubt, logic does not help you.

It is well known that balances used for weighing wealth are not intended to weigh firewood and lead while excluding gold and silver. Prophethood and what the messengers brought hold a higher place among the branches of knowledge than gold does among forms of wealth. If your logic contains no balance for weighing this, then its balance—though it is a balance—is biased and unjust, as well as incapable. It is thus an ignorant, unjust balance: either it rejects and repels the truth, thereby being unjust; or it neither weighs it nor clarifies its nature, thereby being ignorant; or it combines both failings, rejecting and repelling the truth—the very truth for which souls have no substitute, with which they cannot dispense, in which alone their happiness lies, and only through abandoning which do they perish.

How, then, can you reasonably say that it, together with the paltry wares you have weighed with it—being unjust and biased in weighing them, neither weighing with an upright balance nor reasoning from clear signs—is the criterion of true knowledge and certain wisdom

---

## Page 4283

which brings happiness to those who know it and misery and failure to those ignorant of it—and is the capital of the eminent? Yet the most that a fair-minded scholar among you can do is acknowledge that your balance is incapable of weighing it. As for the general run of your scholars, they deny and reject it. Even if your logic refutes them, your distortion of its nature leaves you no better off than the Jews and Christians in their distortion of Allah’s Book, which, in its original form, is undoubtedly true and a source of guidance. Such, then, is the case. There is no power or strength except through Allah.

Moreover, they agree that it yields only universals posited in the mind. It provides no knowledge of anything that actually exists outside the mind except through the mediation of something else. Mental universals are neither external realities nor knowledge of those realities. Every existing thing has a reality that distinguishes it from everything else and makes it what it is; that reality is not universal. Knowledge of what things have in common is therefore not knowledge of that reality. Thus, the logical syllogism provides no knowledge of the concrete reality of anything, although that is precisely what is sought.

They also criticize analogical reasoning on the grounds that it yields only probable conclusions. They sometimes discuss particular analogical arguments concerning subsidiary questions or fundamental principles whose premises are weak or merely probable. An example is found in the remarks of al-Suhrawardī, who was executed for heresy, the author of al-Talwīhāt (The Intimations), al-Alwāh (The Tablets), and Hikmat al-Ishrāq (The Wisdom of Illumination). In his philosophy, he drew upon the Sabian Romans and the Persians

---

## Page 4284

who were Magians.

These two sources are also those of the esoteric Qarmatians and those who have joined or continue to join their ranks, including the Ismāʿīlīs, the Nusayrīs, and others like them. They are among those encompassed by the Prophet’s words, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, in the authentic hadith:

“You will surely follow the ways of the nations before you, handspan by handspan and cubit by cubit, so that even if they entered a lizard’s hole, you would enter it.” They asked, “The Persians and the Romans?” He replied, “Who else?”

The point is that these remarks of al-Suhrawardī concern an analogy he proposed: the heaven came into existence, by analogy with a house, because both share the feature of being composed of parts. He must therefore establish that composition is the cause of the building’s coming into existence and that it is also present in the case being compared with it.

The truth is that “analogical reasoning” is more effective in yielding knowledge and certainty than the “logical syllogism.” Although the knowledge yielded by the logical syllogism is more extensive, that yielded by analogy is greater. Analogical reasoning stands within rational inference as sight does within sensory knowledge, whereas the logical syllogism stands as hearing does within sensory knowledge.

There is no doubt that sight is greater and more complete, while hearing is wider and more comprehensive. Analogical reasoning is thus like sight, as in the saying: “One who compares what he has not seen with what he has seen” (1). The logical syllogism resembles hearing in its general scope. Furthermore, whether either form of reasoning yields certainty or probability depends on its premises.

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) Thus in the original.

---

## Page 4285

Thus, in analogical reasoning, whether concerning sensory matters or anything else, if we know that one thing is like another, we know that what holds true of one holds true of the other, even if we do not know the cause of that judgment. If we do know its cause, we infer the judgment’s applicability from the presence of that cause. A judgment can therefore be known through either analogical reasoning or causal inference.

Causal inference is, in reality, a form of logical syllogism. Its distinguishing feature, however, is that its middle term—which serves as the evidence—is the cause of the judgment. It is called a “causal syllogism” and a “causal demonstration.” That is called an “evidential syllogism” and an “evidential demonstration.” If, however, we do not know the similarity and the cause with certainty, but merely consider them probable, then the judgment is likewise probable.

The same applies to the logical syllogism: if both premises are known with certainty, the conclusion is known with certainty; otherwise, the conclusion follows the weakest premise.

As for their claim that analogical reasoning does not yield certain knowledge, this is an outright error, demonstrably so. Indeed, most of humanity’s purely rational knowledge derives from analogical reasoning.

Moreover, as for the sciences for which they primarily intended this discipline to serve as a criterion, this logical discipline is of very little use in them.

For it is well known that those who have engaged in the mathematical sciences—arithmetic and the calculation of magnitude, whether conceived in the mind or existing externally—both ancient and modern, have been able to pursue them independently, without reference to this

---

## Page 4286

logical discipline and the terminology of its practitioners.

Likewise, in the sound portions of the general natural sciences and medicine, one finds that their accomplished practitioners have not drawn upon any part of the discipline of logic. Indeed, Hippocrates, the foremost authority in medicine, made statements that physicians have accepted and found confirmed by experience. He also formulated universal propositions that rank among the most significant matters in the estimation of reasonable people. Yet he drew upon none of this logical discipline; indeed, he lived before its founder.

Although they regard natural science as a discipline of greater learning and higher standing than medicine, it is undoubtedly connected with it. Knowledge of the natures of particular, perceptible bodies enables one to know the natures of other bodies and the principle of motion and rest within a body. The whole is inferred from the part. Thus, practitioners of these two disciplines frequently debate and disagree over particular questions, just as jurists and speculative theologians debate many questions common to their respective disciplines. The latter claim a broader scope of inquiry. Yet error and mistaken judgment are more prevalent among speculative theologians and philosophers than among jurists and physicians. The discourse and knowledge of jurists and physicians are more beneficial, whereas the others are further astray and less beneficial. This is because they sought through syllogistic reasoning what cannot be known through it, and challenged the innate disposition and prophethood in a way that led them into such opposition to both that they became devils among humankind and jinn, inspiring one another with embellished speech to deceive. Pure medicine, by contrast, is a beneficial science, as is pure jurisprudence.

As for metaphysics—although they exalt it and say that it is first philosophy, the universal science that studies being and its concomitants, and their later thinkers call it the science of

---

## Page 4287

the divine, and their First Teacher claimed that it was the ultimate aim of their philosophy and the culmination of their wisdom—only a scant few of its propositions are true. Most of it concerns knowledge of mental judgments, not external realities. Nor is most of it supported by logical syllogism. This includes abstract existence, necessity, possibility, abstract cause and effect; their division into what constitutes the essence—matter and form—and the two causes of its existence—the agent and the end; and the discussion of the division of being into substances and the nine accidents: quantity, quality, relation, place, time, position, possession, acting, and being acted upon. As one of them expressed it in verse:

Zayd, tall and black, the son of Mālik,
Was reclining in his house yesterday;
In his hand a sword: he drew it, and it was drawn—
These are the ten categories, all alike.

Neither these matters nor their subdivisions are supported by logical syllogism. Rather, most of this is mere induction, much of which has been challenged by those disputing its proponent.

If, then, their discipline ranges between sciences in which logical syllogism is unnecessary and matters in which they cannot employ it, it is of no benefit to their sciences. Indeed, its distraction of the heart from beneficial knowledge and deeds has harmed many people

---

## Page 4288

just as it has barred many of them from the path to knowledge and cast them into valleys of error and ignorance. What, then, of sciences beyond their own, whose scope is boundless for earlier and later generations?

Moreover, you will not find anyone on earth who has mastered a science and become a leading authority in it by relying on the discipline of logic, whether in the religious sciences or any other field. Physicians, arithmeticians, scribes, and others achieve mastery in their sciences and crafts without the discipline of logic. Within Islam, works have been written on grammar, lexicography, prosody, jurisprudence and its principles, speculative theology, and other subjects. None of the leading authorities in these fields paid any heed to logic; indeed, most of them lived before this Greek logic was translated into Arabic.

As for the sciences inherited purely from the prophets—although jurisprudence and its principles are connected with them—they are far too exalted and sublime for one to suppose that their scholars paid any heed to logic. For among the first three generations of this community—which is the best community brought forth for humanity, and whose finest generations are those first three—none paid heed to logic or concerned themselves with it. Yet their mastery and perfection of the sciences reached a height no one else can attain. They possessed the deepest knowledge, were the least given to affectation, and had the most righteous hearts.

Whenever others discuss the subjects they discussed, you will find a disparity between the two greater than the distance between the foot and the crown of the head. Indeed, what we have established through induction is this known fact: among those who engage in the sciences, those who practise this

---

## Page 4289

discipline are the most beset by doubt and confusion, the least possessed of knowledge and precision, and the furthest from attaining sound, well-balanced knowledge—even though some among them may establish certain points of knowledge. That, however, is due to the soundness of the subject matter and evidence they examine, and to the soundness of their intellect and perception, not to logic. Indeed, introducing the discipline of logic into sound sciences lengthens their exposition, obscures their meaning, makes accessible knowledge remote, and turns what is easy into something difficult. Thus, you find that those who have introduced it into juristic disputation, speculative theology, the principles of jurisprudence, and other fields have gained nothing but an abundance of words and elaborate discourse, with little knowledge or precision. It is therefore clear that it is among the greatest sources of verbiage and among the things furthest removed from the way of sound-minded people.

Admittedly, it cannot be denied that certain elements of logic may benefit someone immersed in unbelief and error, blindly following the ignorant people among whom they grew up—such as ordinary Christians, Jews, the Rāfidah, and others like them—and that logic may lead them to abandon the beliefs held by those people. Most of these individuals, however, end up pandering to their common followers and leading them astray from Allah’s path, or become hypocritical heretics who affirm neither truth nor falsehood, but abandon truth just as they abandoned falsehood. Thus, the clever members of misguided sects are either pandering deceivers or heretical hypocrites; scarcely any of them falls outside these two categories.

---

## Page 4290

But as for logic having brought them to a truth by which they find guidance, that is not something logic accomplishes. In short, whatever sharpening of the mind, abandonment of falsehood, or expression of truth some people achieve through it is due only to their having previously been in a worse condition, not to any perfection in the discipline of logic.

It is well known that when a polytheist becomes a Zoroastrian, or a Zoroastrian embraces Judaism, his condition improves relative to what it was before. Yet this cannot properly be made a foundation for those who follow the manifest truth. Nor is this benefit peculiar to logic. Rather, it applies to anyone who studies subjects involving subtle distinctions and a measure of comprehensiveness, as we find in grammar. It is well known that its practitioners possess a degree of rigorous analysis, precision, classification, and definition that practitioners of logic do not, and that they discuss the forms in which rational concepts are expressed according to the most complete rules.

Meanings are innately accessible to reason and require no special convention, unlike their verbal molds, which vary. Thus, when people learn the most complete forms and molds for meanings while possessing a sound natural disposition, this is more complete, more beneficial, and more conducive to rigorous mastery of the sciences than a conventional discipline dealing with matters innately accessible to reason, which require no special convention. This, by my life, addresses its usefulness in the other sciences.

---

## Page 4291

As for its usefulness specifically in Islamic learning, this is too evident to require explanation. Thus, you find that those who received the sciences of the ancients and recast them in Arabic, bringing Muslim minds to bear on them, achieved a degree of completeness, precision, comprehensiveness, and concision absent from the writings of the ancients. Although some of these later thinkers harbored hypocrisy and misguidance, they nevertheless benefited, on the whole, from the blessing of the concise yet comprehensive speech with which the Messenger of Allah—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—was sent, and from the knowledge and eloquence bestowed upon his community, in which no other community has shared.

Furthermore, the discipline of logic was devised by their First Teacher, Aristotle, the author of the teachings followed by the innovators among the Sabians. He devised it as a measure by which to assess what he and his like discussed of their wisdom and philosophy, which represented the height of their attainment.

This philosophy has two branches: theoretical and practical. The soundest theoretical subjects—the gateway to truth—are those of arithmetic and mathematics. As for practical philosophy, it concerns the cultivation of good character and the sound management of the household and the city.

Undoubtedly, this includes knowledge and practices that distinguish them from those ignorant members of humanity who have neither a revealed scripture nor a messenger sent to them, and that entitle them to precedence over such people. It also offers benefits for the sound ordering and flourishing of worldly life that are encompassed by what the messengers brought. Likewise, it includes speaking and following the truth, enjoining justice, and forbidding corruption—all of which are encompassed by what the messengers brought.

---

## Page 4292

Thus, compared with the ignorant among other peoples, such as the nomadic Turks and their like, they are better, provided they are free of their misguidance. With their misguidance, however, those ignorant members of humanity who retain their natural disposition may be better than they are. As for even the most misguided adherents of religious communities—such as ignorant Christians and the Samaritans among the Jews—they are more knowledgeable, better guided, wiser, and more faithful to the truth than these philosophers. I have discussed this at considerable length elsewhere.

The purpose here is simply to show that this discipline offers little benefit and contains much superfluous material. This is because the discipline of logic is seldom useful in practical and ethical matters. Although universal affirmative propositions do occur in practical matters, those who govern themselves, their households, and their realms arrive at such general judgments through means that do not require logic. Once such a judgment has been reached, its benefit lies in putting it into practice.

Moreover, practical matters do not depend on a general judgment. Whenever someone knows that an action benefits him, he performs it; whatever action harms him, he abandons it. He may know this through outward or inward perception; it does not depend on a general judgment. It is therefore clear that logic cannot properly be applied to most practical matters. This is why

---

## Page 4293

those who discipline themselves and their households and govern their realms do not weigh their judgments by the art of logic, except to a very limited extent. Those who follow it are generally left hesitant and inactive.

If those who hold these judgments depended on weighing them by this art in order to understand and apply them, the harm they suffered would far outweigh any benefit they derived. Moreover, all the knowledge, moral qualities, and actions they prescribe are insufficient to save anyone from Allah’s punishment, let alone secure the bliss of the Hereafter. Allah Most High said:

“Until, when they have all gathered there, the last of them will say of the first, ‘Our Lord, these led us astray, so give them double punishment in the Fire.’ He will say, ‘Each will have double, but you do not know.’”

He also said:

“Have they not travelled through the earth and seen how those before them met their end? They were more numerous than them, greater in strength, and left more impressive traces on the earth, yet what they earned availed them nothing.”

—up to His words—“the disbelievers.”

Here He related what He also related in Surah al-A‘rāf: when those who turned away from what the messengers brought saw Allah’s punishment, they affirmed Allah’s oneness and renounced associating partners with Him, but that did not benefit them. Likewise, He related that Pharaoh—who denied both Allah’s oneness and the message—said, when drowning overtook him:

“I believe that there is no deity except the One in whom the Children of Israel believe, and I am one of those who submit to Him.”

Allah said: “Now? When you had disobeyed before and were among the

---

## Page 4294

corrupt.” Allah Most High also said:

“And when your Lord took from the children of Adam—from their loins—their descendants and made them testify concerning themselves, ‘Am I not your Lord?’ They said, ‘Yes, we testify.’ This was lest you should say on the Day of Resurrection, ‘We were unaware of this,’ or say, ‘It was only our forefathers who associated partners with Allah, and we were their descendants after them. Will You then destroy us for what those who followed falsehood did?’”

Allah Most High also said:

“Has the news not reached you of those before you—the people of Noah, ‘Ād, Thamūd, and those after them whom none knows but Allah? Their messengers came to them with clear proofs, but they put their hands to their mouths and said, ‘We disbelieve in what you have been sent with, and we are in grave doubt about that to which you call us.’

Their messengers said, ‘Can there be any doubt about Allah, the Originator of the heavens and the earth? He calls you so that He may forgive you some of your sins and grant you respite until an appointed time.’ They said, ‘You are only human beings like us. You wish to turn us away from what our forefathers worshipped. So bring us clear proof.’”

This also appears elsewhere in the Quran, where He explains that all the messengers were commanded to affirm Allah’s oneness by worshipping Him alone, without any partner, and were forbidden to worship any created being besides Him or take it as a deity. He declares that those who attain bliss are those who affirm His oneness, while those who associate partners with Him are the wretched. He relates this concerning the messengers in general and explains that those who did not believe in the messengers are people who associate partners with Allah. Thus, affirming Allah’s oneness and believing in the messengers necessarily entail one another. Likewise, belief in the Last Day and belief in the messengers necessarily entail one another. All three, therefore, are inseparable.

For this reason, He brings them together in statements such as:

“Do not follow the desires of those who deny Our signs and those who do not believe in the Hereafter

---

## Page 4295

and who set up equals to their Lord.”

For this reason, He declares that those who do not believe in the Hereafter associate partners with Him. He Most High says:

“When Allah alone is mentioned, the hearts of those who do not believe in the Hereafter recoil.”

He also states that the messengers warned all those destined for misery about the Last Day. He Most High says:

“Whenever a group is cast into it, its keepers will ask them, ‘Did no warner come to you?’ They will say, ‘Yes, a warner did come to us, but we denied him and said, “Allah has revealed nothing; you are only in great error.”’”

Thus He states that the messengers warned them and that they denied the message. He Most High also says:

“Those who disbelieved will be driven to Hell in groups until, when they reach it, its gates will be opened”—to the end of the verse.

Thus He states that the message reached the people of the Fire and that they were warned about the Last Day. He Most High also says:

“On the Day He gathers them all together, He will say, ‘O company of jinn, you have led many humans astray.’ Their allies among humans will say, ‘Our Lord, we benefited from one another, and we have reached the term You appointed for us.’ He will say, ‘The Fire is your dwelling, where you will remain forever, except as Allah wills. Indeed, your Lord is All-Wise, All-Knowing.’

“Thus We make some wrongdoers allies of others because of what they used to earn. O company of jinn and humans”—until His words—“and they will testify against themselves that they were disbelievers”—to the end of the verse.

Thus He states concerning all jinn and humans that the messengers conveyed to them Allah’s message, which consists of His signs

---

## Page 4296

and that they warned them of the Last Day. He likewise says:

“Say, ‘Shall We tell you who are the greatest losers in their deeds? Those whose efforts in this worldly life have gone astray’”—up to His words—“Those are the ones who disbelieve in their Lord’s signs and in meeting Him.”

Thus He states that they disbelieved in His signs, which are His message, and in meeting Him, which is the Last Day.

He also states in several places that the message reached all the children of Adam and that the messengers came bearing glad tidings and warnings. He Most High says:

“Indeed, We have sent you with the truth as a bearer of glad tidings and a warner. There is no nation without a warner having come among them.”

He Most High also says:

“Indeed, We have revealed to you as We revealed to Noah and the prophets after him”—up to His words—“And Allah is Almighty, All-Wise.”

He Most High also says:

“We send the messengers only as bearers of glad tidings and warners. Whoever believes and acts righteously will have nothing to fear, nor will they grieve. But those who deny Our signs will be afflicted with punishment for their defiance.”

Thus He states that those among the earlier and later generations who believe in the messengers and act righteously will have nothing to fear, nor will they grieve. He Most High also says:

“We said, ‘Descend from it, all of you. Then, when guidance comes to you from Me, whoever follows My guidance will have nothing to fear, nor will they grieve.’”

Similar to this are His words:

“Indeed, those who believe and those who are Jews”—up to His words—“will have their reward with their Lord”—to the end of the verse.

He thus states that those among these groups who believe in Allah and the Last Day are the ones who attain salvation and felicity. In the former verse He mentioned belief in the messengers, and in the latter belief in the Last Day, because the two

---

## Page 4297

are inseparable. Likewise, belief in all the messengers is indivisible. Whoever believes in one of them has believed in them all, and whoever disbelieves in one of them has disbelieved in them all. As He Most High says:

“Indeed, those who disbelieve in Allah and His messengers”—up to His words—“Those are truly the disbelievers”—the verse and the one that follows.

He thus states that those who believe in all the messengers attain felicity, while those who distinguish between them by believing in some but not others are truly disbelievers.

He Most High also says:

“We have bound every person’s deeds to his neck, and on the Day of Resurrection We will bring forth for him a record which he will find spread open. Read your record! Today you are sufficient to reckon your own account. Whoever is guided is guided only for his own benefit, and whoever goes astray does so only to his own detriment. No bearer of burdens will bear the burden of another. And We never punish until We have sent a messenger.”

These three foundations—affirming Allah’s oneness, believing in His messengers, and believing in the Last Day—are inseparable.

In short, affirming Allah’s oneness and believing in His messengers and the Last Day are inseparably linked with righteous deeds. Those who possess this faith and do righteous deeds are the ones who attain felicity among the earlier and later generations. Those outside this faith associate partners with Allah and are doomed to misery. Whoever rejects the messengers can only be one who associates partners with Allah, and everyone who associates partners with Allah rejects the messengers. Everyone who associates partners with Allah and disbelieves in the messengers also disbelieves in the Last Day. Whoever disbelieves in the Last Day disbelieves in the messengers and associates partners with Allah.

For this reason, He, Glorified and Most High, says:

“And thus We have appointed for every prophet

---

## Page 4298

an enemy—devils from among mankind and jinn, inspiring one another with embellished speech to deceive. Had your Lord willed, they would not have done so. So leave them and what they fabricate.”

“And so that the hearts of those who do not believe in the Hereafter may incline toward it, be pleased with it, and commit whatever they commit.”

He thus informs us that all the prophets have enemies: devils from among mankind and jinn, inspiring one another with embellished speech—that is, speech adorned and beautified—which they use to deceive. Deception consists in misrepresentation and concealment. Such is the nature of every statement and every deed contrary to what the messengers brought, whether among the philosophers, the speculative theologians, or others of earlier and later generations. Then He says:

“And so that the hearts of those who do not believe in the Hereafter may incline toward it and be pleased with it.”

He thus informs us that the hearts of those who do not believe in the Hereafter incline toward the speech of the messengers’ enemies. We therefore know that opposing the messengers and failing to believe in the Hereafter are inseparably linked. Whoever does not believe in the Hereafter listens to the embellished speech of their enemies and thus opposes the messengers, as is found among the various kinds of disbelievers and hypocrites in this community.

He Most High also says:

“We have certainly brought them a Book which We have explained in detail with knowledge, as guidance and mercy for people who believe.”

“Are they waiting for anything but its fulfillment? On the Day its fulfillment comes, those who had previously neglected it will say, ‘The messengers of our Lord certainly came with the truth. Have we any intercessors to intercede for us?’”—to the end of the verse.

He thus informs us that those who abandoned following the Book—which is the message—will say, when its fulfillment comes—that is, what it foretold—“The messengers of our Lord came with the truth.”

This is like His saying:

“But whoever turns away from My remembrance will certainly have a life of hardship, and We will raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection.”

“He will say, ‘My Lord, why have You raised me blind when I used to see?’”

“He will say, ‘Thus

---

## Page 4299

Our signs came to you, but you forgot them; likewise, today you will be forgotten.’”

He informs us that those who abandoned following His signs will suffer what we have mentioned. It has thus become clear that the foundation of happiness and deliverance from punishment is to affirm Allah’s oneness by worshipping Him alone, without partner, to believe in His messengers and the Last Day, and to do righteous deeds.

These things are absent from their innovated wisdom and philosophy. It contains no command to worship Allah alone or prohibition against worshipping created beings. Rather, all polytheism in the world arose through the ideas of people of their kind. They based it on the powers and natural properties of spirits and bodies, and on the notion that making talismans and idols and worshipping them brings benefits and wards off harm. They are thus the ones who command polytheism and practise it. Those among them who do not command it do not forbid it either; instead, they accept both groups. Even if monotheists are given some preference, another of them may favour the polytheists, or may disregard both alternatives altogether. Reflect on this, for it is most beneficial.

For this reason, their leading figures, both earlier and later, used to command polytheism. The earlier ones called the celestial bodies the lesser gods and worshipped them through various forms of worship. Likewise, those within Islam did not forbid polytheism and require monotheism; rather, they permitted polytheism, commanded it, or did not require monotheism.

---

## Page 4300

I have seen writings of theirs on the worship of celestial bodies, angels, and disembodied souls—the souls of prophets and others—that are at the very root of polytheism. When they claim to affirm Allah’s oneness, they do so only in words, not in worship and practice. Yet the monotheism brought by the messengers necessarily entails devoting all religion sincerely to Allah and worshipping Him alone, without partner. This is something they do not understand.

The monotheism they claim is nothing but a denial of the realities of Allah’s names and attributes. It contains disbelief and misguidance that are among the greatest causes of polytheism. Had they affirmed His oneness in their statements and discourse—by describing Allah as His messengers described Him—they would have possessed monotheism in words, though not in practice. That, however, is insufficient for happiness and salvation. Rather, Allah alone must be worshipped and taken as God, to the exclusion of all else.

This is the meaning of the declaration:

“There is no god but Allah.”

How, then, can their claim stand when, even in their statements and discourse, they negate and deny, rather than affirm His oneness and devote themselves sincerely to Him?

As for belief in the messengers, the First Teacher and his like have no known discourse on this subject. Those among them who joined religious communities believed in some of the messengers’ attributes and disbelieved in others.

As for the Last Day, even the best of them acknowledges only the resurrection of souls, not bodies.

---

## Page 4301

Some of them deny both kinds of resurrection. Others affirm the resurrection of knowledgeable souls but not ignorant ones. All three positions were held by their Second Teacher, Abū Nasr al-Fārābī. Their views on this are so inconsistent that it is clear they have not found the truth concerning it.

Through their specious arguments, they have led astray countless people affiliated with religious communities—so many that only Allah knows their number.

Since they entirely lack that through which happiness and deliverance from misery are attained, their prescriptions concerning morality, conduct, and governance are as Allah Most High says:

“They know only the outward aspects of this worldly life, while they are heedless of the Hereafter.”

As for the theoretical sciences they discuss, whatever is sound in them is beneficial in this world. As for “metaphysics,” they possess nothing of it through which salvation and happiness can be attained. Indeed, most of what they possess in this field is not knowledge established with certainty. The leading philosophers have explicitly stated that certainty is unattainable in metaphysics, and that discussion in this field proceeds only in terms of what is more probable and plausible. Thus, they have nothing but conjecture concerning it:

“And conjecture is no substitute for the truth.”

Their opposition to the messengers is therefore immense and glaring. Indeed, one of the eminent scholars versed in speculative theology, philosophy, hadith, and other disciplines was once asked, “What separates the prophets from the philosophers?”

He replied, “The blood-red sword.”

He meant that whoever follows their approach, seeking to reconcile their statements with what the messengers brought, becomes entangled, through sophistry and Qarmatian esotericism, in all manner of absurdities that no rational person would accept. Such was the approach of the authors of the Epistles of the Brethren of Purity and others like them. It is from this that

---

## Page 4302

the Qarmatians, the esotericists, and those who shared in some of this went astray. This is a subject that would take a long time to explain, and discussing it is not our purpose here. The point is simply that their teacher devised their logic to weigh their statements concerning these subjects into which they delve, and which are of little benefit.

Most of their benefit lies in worldly affairs, and even in worldly affairs they may be dispensed with.

As for using this discipline to weigh matters outside their fields of knowledge and beyond their capacity, or to weigh that which brings happiness, bliss, and deliverance from painful punishment—this lies outside its scope. Allah has set a measure for everything. Although these people possess intelligence and discernment, and some among them possess asceticism and moral virtues, these qualities alone do not bring happiness and deliverance from punishment without the foundations already mentioned: faith in Allah, affirming His oneness and worshipping Him alone with sincerity; faith in His messengers and the Last Day; and righteous deeds.

Strength of intellect is merely like bodily strength and strength of will. Someone endowed with virtues of intellect and will without these foundations is like someone endowed with physical strength without these foundations. Those possessed of judgment and knowledge are like those possessed of kingship and authority, and each of these

---

## Page 4303

and those will gain no benefit from this unless they worship Allah alone, without any partner, and believe in His messengers and the Last Day. These matters are inseparable. Whoever worships Allah alone must also believe in His messengers and the Last Day, thereby deserving reward. Otherwise, he is among those subject to the threat of punishment, remaining in torment forever. This applies when the proof has been established against him through the messengers.

Since those endowed with authority and those endowed with knowledge may either oppose the messengers or follow them, Allah has mentioned this in numerous places in His Book. He mentioned Pharaoh; the man who disputed with Abraham concerning his Lord because Allah had granted him kingship; and the chiefs among the peoples of Noah, ‘Ād, and others who arrogantly rejected the messengers. He also mentioned what their scholars said, as in His words:

“When their messengers came to them with clear proofs, they rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed, but they were overwhelmed by what they used to ridicule.”

“When they saw Our punishment, they said, ‘We believe in Allah alone and renounce those we used to associate with Him.’”

“But their faith did not benefit them once they had seen Our punishment. Such is Allah’s established way with His servants. And there the disbelievers lost everything.”

Allah Most High also said:

“None dispute concerning Allah’s signs except those who disbelieve, so do not let their going to and fro throughout the lands deceive you.”

“Before them, the people of Noah denied, as did the factions after them. Every nation plotted to seize its messenger, and they argued with falsehood to refute the truth thereby. So I seized them—and how severe was My punishment!”

—up to His words—

“Those who dispute concerning Allah’s signs without any authority having come to them—greatly hateful is this to Allah and to those who believe. Thus does Allah seal the heart of every arrogant tyrant.”

Authority means the revelation sent down from Allah, as is mentioned in more than one

---

## Page 4304

place, as in His words:

“Or have We sent down to them an authority that speaks in support of their associating partners with Him?”

And His words:

“for which Allah has sent down no authority.”

Ibn ‘Abbās said, “Every occurrence of ‘authority’ in the Quran means proof.” Al-Bukhārī cited this in his Sahīh.

In this surah, “Hā Mīm Ghāfir,” Allah has related instructive accounts of kings and scholars who opposed the messengers, whose statements, disputation, and arrogance resemble those of the philosophers and their scholars. For example, He says:

“Those who dispute concerning Allah’s signs without any authority having come to them have nothing in their hearts but a longing for greatness they will never attain.”

And He says:

“Have you not seen those who dispute concerning Allah’s signs? How are they turned away?”

“Those who deny the Book and what We sent Our messengers with will soon know,”

“when shackles and chains are around their necks, and they are dragged”

“through boiling water, then burned in the Fire.”

—up to His words—

“That is because you rejoiced on earth without right and because you were insolent.”

He concluded the surah with His words, Most High:

“When their messengers came to them with clear proofs, they rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed.”

The same is true of Surahs al-An‘ām and al-A‘rāf, most of the Meccan surahs, and a number of the Medinan surahs. They address these people, present them with parables and analogies, and recount their stories and the stories of the prophets and their followers in their dealings with them.

Thus He, glorified be He, said:

“We had certainly given them power such as We have not given you, and We gave them hearing, sight, and hearts. Yet they gained no benefit from

---

## Page 4305

their hearing, sight, or hearts, since they denied Allah’s signs, and they were overwhelmed by the very thing they used to ridicule.”

He thus described the various faculties of perception and action with which He had endowed them. He also stated that these availed them nothing, since they denied Allah’s signs—the message with which He sent His messengers. In this connection, the son of Shaykh al-Hasīrī related to me from his father, Shaykh al-Hasīrī (*)—the leading Hanafī scholar of his time—who said: “The jurists of Bukhara used to say of Ibn Sīnā: ‘He was an intelligent disbeliever.’”

Allah Most High said:

“Have they not travelled through the land and seen how those before them met their end? They were greater than them in strength and in the marks they left upon the earth”—to the end of the verse.

Strength encompasses both the theoretical capacity for perception and the practical capacity for action.

He said in the other verse:

“They were more numerous than them, greater in strength and in the marks they left upon the earth.”

He thus described their superiority in quantity and quality: they were mightier both in themselves and in the marks they left upon the earth.

He, Most High, said:

“Yet all that they used to earn availed them nothing.”

“When their messengers came to them with clear proofs, they rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed, and they were overwhelmed by the very thing they used to ridicule.”

He, Most High, said:

“This is Allah’s promise. Allah never breaks His promise, but most people do not know.”

“They know only the outward aspects of this worldly life, while they are heedless of the Hereafter.”

—up to His words—

“Allah originates creation, then brings it back, then to Him you will be returned.”

He, Most High, said:

“They denied the truth when it came to them, but news of what they used to ridicule will soon reach them.”

—up to His words—

“And We raised up another generation after them.”
