## Page 4306

And He, glorified be He, said concerning the followers of these leaders—rulers and scholars who opposed the messengers:

“On the Day their faces are turned about in the Fire, they will say, ‘If only we had obeyed Allah and obeyed the Messenger!’”

“They will say, ‘Our Lord, we obeyed our masters and our great men, and they led us astray from the path.’”

“‘Our Lord, give them twice the punishment and lay upon them a mighty curse.’”

He, Most High, said:

“When they argue with one another in the Fire”

—up to His words—

“Allah has already judged between His servants.”

There are many such passages in the Quran describing the words and deeds of the messengers’ enemies and the faculties of perception and action they had been granted, which availed them nothing when they opposed the messengers.

Allah, glorified be He, also mentioned the hypocrisy and misguidance found among scholars, worshippers, and rulers who profess to follow the messengers, as in His words:

“O you who believe, many rabbis and monks consume people’s wealth unjustly and turn people away from Allah’s path. As for those who hoard gold and silver and do not spend it in Allah’s cause, give them tidings of a painful punishment.”

The verb in “and turn people away from Allah’s path” is also used intransitively: one says sadda sudūdan, meaning “to turn away,” as in His words:

“When they are told, ‘Come to what Allah has revealed and to the Messenger,’ you see the hypocrites turn away from you in aversion.”

One also says sadda ghayrahu, yasudduhu (“he turned someone else away; he turns him away”). Both descriptions apply to them. Another example is His saying:

“Have you not seen those who were given a portion of the Scripture

---

## Page 4307

who believe in superstition and false gods, and say of the disbelievers, ‘These are better guided than the believers’?”

In the two Sahīh collections, Abū Mūsā reports that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“The believer who recites the Quran is like a citron: its taste is pleasant and its fragrance is pleasant. The believer who does not recite the Quran is like a date: its taste is pleasant, but it has no fragrance. The hypocrite who recites the Quran is like basil: its fragrance is pleasant, but its taste is bitter. The hypocrite who does not recite the Quran is like a colocynth: its taste is bitter, and it has no fragrance.”

He thus made clear that those who recite the Quran include both believers and hypocrites.

Section

Here I am not discussing disputed points, lest it be said that I am using one disputed matter to establish another. Rather, I am describing the general character of their discourse. I say:

There is no doubt that all their discourse is confined to definitions that yield conceptions—whether essential, descriptive, or verbal—and inferences that yield judgments—whether through generality and inclusion, similarity and analogy, or induction and systematic examination.

Most of their discourse is not free of contrivance, whether in matters of knowledge or of expression. This involves either

---

## Page 4308

presuming knowledge of what they do not know, and thus speaking without knowledge, or, when something is known to them, laboring to explain it with needless additions, padding, effort, and roundabout exposition. This is objectionable and blameworthy according to both revelation and reason. Allah Most High says:

“Say, ‘I ask you no reward for this, nor am I one of the pretentious.’”

In the Sahīh collection, it is reported that ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd said:

“O people, whoever knows something should speak on the basis of that knowledge, and whoever does not know should say, ‘I do not know.’ For it is part of knowledge that a person should say, concerning what he does not know, ‘I do not know.’”

Allah has condemned speaking without knowledge in His Book, as in His saying:

“Do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge.”

This applies especially to speaking about Allah, as in His saying:

“Say, ‘My Lord has only forbidden shameful deeds, whether committed openly or secretly, sin, unjust aggression, associating with Allah that for which He has sent down no authority, and saying about Allah what you do not know.’”

Likewise, He has condemned excessive speech that serves no purpose and commanded us to speak soundly and effectively. Most of these people’s discussion of definitions consists of such excessive, unprofitable speech. Their discussions of syllogisms and arguments may also be lengthy: much of this is likewise unprofitable, and much of it is false—speech without knowledge and contrary to the truth.

As for the first point: they claim that the definitions they present convey conceptions of the realities of things, and that this can only be accomplished by mentioning the essential attributes, both shared and distinguishing, so that the definition is composed of the shared genus and the distinguishing differentia.

They may also say that conceptions

---

## Page 4309

can be acquired only through definitions. They also say: “Composite definitions apply only to species composed of genus and differentia, not to simple species.”

Elsewhere I have summarized logic and its content, and indicated some of the error and misguidance into which it has led many people. This is not the place to elaborate on that, but we shall mention several arguments here.

The first argument:

Their statement, “A conception that is not self-evident can be attained only through a definition,” is false, because a definition is a statement made by the person defining. Here, a definition means a statement indicating the essence of the thing defined. Knowledge acquired through a definition can therefore arise only after that definition.

If the person who formulated the definition came to know the thing defined without a definition, their claim that “It can be known only through a definition” is invalid. If he came to know it through another definition, the same reasoning applies to that definition as to the first. If this definer came to know it after the first definition, circularity necessarily follows; if it was deferred, an infinite regress necessarily follows.

---

## Page 4310

The second argument:

To this day, they have not established an undisputed definition of anything: whatever some of them claim, others contest. If the fundamentals can be conceived only through definitions, it follows that no one to this day has known anything, nor would anyone remain awaiting its validation. For the person presenting it requires knowledge acquired without a definition, and such knowledge is manifold. Thus mankind would possess no knowledge whatsoever. This is sophistry and fallacious reasoning.

The third argument:

Those who discourse on definitions constitute a small minority of mankind, particularly those engaged in the discipline of logic. Its founder was Aristotle, and a group of people followed him in it. Yet it is well known that mankind—ordinary people and specialists alike—possesses knowledge without this. Their claim that “Knowledge depends on it” is therefore invalid.

As for the prophets, there is no doubt that they have no need of it. The same applies to their followers, both scholars and ordinary people. Indeed, the first three generations of this community—who surpassed all mankind in learning and knowledge—did not engage in laboriously devising

---

## Page 4311

such definitions. They had not devised them, nor had the foreign Greek books been translated into Arabic for them. These definitions arose only after their time, among innovators from the dialectical theologians and philosophers. Since their emergence, disagreement and ignorance have spread among them to an extent known only to Allah.

Likewise, in “medicine,” “arithmetic,” and other disciplines, you will not find the leading authorities laboriously devising these definitions composed of genus and differentia, except for logicians who have incorporated this into those disciplines.

The same applies to “grammarians,” such as Sībawayh, whose book has no equal in the world and contains the wisdom of the Arabic language. In it, he did not laboriously devise definitions of the noun, the subject of a verb, and similar terms, as others did. When grammarians undertook to define the noun, they proposed numerous definitions, all of which they themselves found open to criticism. Likewise, the efforts of later grammarians to define the subject of a verb, the subject and predicate of a nominal sentence, and similar terms were not undertaken by anyone whom they regarded as a leading authority or an accomplished practitioner of the discipline.

The same applies to the definitions that some jurists laboriously devise for ritual purification, impurity, and other terms in common use among them. Likewise, the definitions laboriously devised by those studying the principles of jurisprudence—for such things as reports, analogical reasoning, knowledge, and so forth—were undertaken only by those who were not leading authorities in the field. To this day, not one of their definitions has escaped objection. The same applies to the definitions of the dialectical theologians.

---

## Page 4312

Since the most accomplished people in every field of knowledge have mastered it without these laboriously devised definitions, the claim that knowledge depends on them is invalid. As for the knowledge possessed by people who do not write books, only Allah can encompass its extent. They possess insights, unveilings, precise understanding, and knowledge that the proponents of these laboriously devised definitions do not possess. How, then, could knowledge of things depend on such definitions?

The fourth argument:

Allah has endowed human beings with outward and inward senses through which they perceive and know things. Through hearing, sight, smell, taste, and outward touch, a person acquires the knowledge these senses afford. Through what he witnesses and perceives within his own soul and heart, he also comes to know things greater still. These are the means by which things are known. Words alone, however, cannot conceivably convey knowledge of individual things except through analogical comparison or combinations of words. Neither of these provides a conception of a thing’s real nature.

The point is that if someone has conceived of a thing’s real nature through his inward or outward faculties, he has no need of a verbal definition. If he has not conceived of it through these faculties, he cannot conceive of its real nature through a verbal definition. This is something perceptible that a person finds within himself. For anyone who is familiar with things perceived through taste

---

## Page 4313

—such as honey, for example—a definition does not furnish him with a conception of them. Someone who has not tasted a thing—such as a person told about sugar without having tasted it—cannot conceive of its real nature through words and definitions. Rather, comparisons are offered to give him an approximate idea, and he is told: “It tastes like this,” or “It tastes like this and that.” Such comparison and analogy are not the kind of definition they claim to provide.

The same applies to inwardly perceived things, such as anger, joy, sorrow, distress, knowledge, and the like. Whoever has experienced them has conceived of them; whoever has not experienced them cannot conceive of them through a definition. Thus, someone born blind cannot conceive of colours through a definition, nor can an impotent man conceive of sexual intercourse through a definition.

Therefore, whoever claims that definitions provide conceptions of the real natures of things is asserting a falsehood whose falsity is known through both inward and outward perception.

The fifth argument:

Definitions are merely universal statements, such as “a rational animal,” “an utterance that signifies a meaning,” and the like. Conceiving their meaning does not preclude its applying to more than one thing, even if such shared application is impossible for some other reason. They therefore do not indicate any particular reality as such, but only a universal meaning. Universal meanings exist in the mind, not in external reality. What exists externally, then, cannot be individually identified or known through a definition alone, while what exists in the mind is not the real nature of things. Thus, a definition does not provide a conception of any real nature at all.

---

## Page 4314

The sixth argument:

A definition consists of words. Words do not convey their meaning to a listener unless he has already conceived of the meanings of their individual terms by means other than words. For a single term does not convey its meaning to a listener unless he knows that it has been assigned to that meaning, and he cannot know this until he knows the meaning itself. Conceiving of simple meanings must therefore precede understanding what is intended by words. If those conceptions were themselves acquired through words, circularity would necessarily follow.

This is a matter of direct experience. Unless someone using a single term makes its meaning clear to the listener so that he grasps it through his senses or reflection, it is inconceivable that the listener should grasp it through a statement composed of a genus and a differentia.

The seventh argument:

A definition distinguishes and differentiates the thing defined from everything else. It thus accomplishes what names accomplish by distinguishing and differentiating the thing named from everything else. There is no doubt that this serves to distinguish. It does not, however, furnish a conception of a thing’s real nature. Definitions may nevertheless spell out in detail what a name indicates in summary, but this has nothing to do with apprehending the thing’s real nature.

The condition for this is that the attributes be essential. Rather, it is akin to division: defining the whole is like dividing it into its particulars. This will become clear.

---

## Page 4315

The eighth argument:

The inner and outer senses furnish a conception of a thing’s real nature as such. Its universality or particularity, however, is a matter of intellectual judgment. The heart apprehends a meaning in one particular thing and a corresponding meaning in another, so that a general, shared meaning arises in the heart. This is its intellectual apprehension—that is, its apprehension of universal meanings.

When it apprehends the meaning of animality found in this animal and that animal, and the meaning of rationality found in this human being and that human being, which is distinctive to humans, it apprehends that the human species contains one meaning whose counterpart is found in other animals, and another meaning that has no counterpart in other animals. The first is called the genus, and the second the differentia; both are present in the species.

This is true. Yet this wording has taught it nothing that it did not already know through its intellect: that one meaning is common to humans and other animals—in the sense that what exists in one has a counterpart in the other, since universality does not exist in external particulars—while the other meaning is distinctive to humans.

Thus, there is no difference between saying, “The human being is a rational animal,” and saying, “The human being is the rational animal,” except in the comprehensiveness and exclusivity of the latter, not in forming a conception of

---

## Page 4316

its real nature through words. Comprehensiveness and exclusivity consist in the distinction achieved by the name alone, as when you say “human being” or “person.” For once the referent of such a name is understood, it provides the same distinction as “rational animal” does insofar as it is free from objections.

As for conceiving that it contains both a general and a specific meaning, this is not peculiar to definition, as explained earlier. What is distinctive to definition amounts to no more than the distinction achieved by names. This is clear to anyone who reflects upon it. Admittedly, the name alone may not draw attention to the fact that some of its attributes are shared with other things while others are distinctive to it. Yet this can be brought to one’s attention both by a definition and by other means. Thus, a definition contains nothing beyond what is found in names or in the attributes mentioned of the thing named.

These are two familiar kinds:

First: the meanings of individual names.

Second: knowledge of compound sentences, both nominal and verbal, through which statements are made about things and things are described.

Neither kind requires an artificially constructed definition. It is therefore established that definition offers no benefit that is not already available in names and ordinary discourse without contrivance. The claim that definition has a distinctive benefit thus falls away.

---

## Page 4317

The Ninth Argument:

It is true that we know there are shared and distinctive attributes. However, distinguishing between these attributes by treating some as essential constituents of the thing defined and others as necessary concomitants of its essence is an invalid distinction. Rather, all attributes inseparable from the thing defined—in both directions of implication—belong to a single category. There is therefore no difference between differentia and property, nor between genus and common accident.

For the essence composed of these attributes must mean either an essence existing outside the mind, an essence conceived in the mind, or something else. If an essence existing outside the mind is intended, then rationality and laughter in human beings are two inseparable realities peculiar to them. If the essence conceived in the mind is intended, then the mind understands that these two attributes belong uniquely to human beings and not to anything else.

If it is said: Rather, conceiving the essence depends on one of these two attributes: one cannot conceive of a human being in the mind without understanding rationality, whereas understanding laughter follows upon understanding what a human being is. This is what they mean by saying:

“An essential attribute is one without understanding which the essence cannot conceivably be understood, or one upon which the essence depends, both in the mind and in external reality.”

---

## Page 4318

It may be replied:

Mental apprehension is relative and relational. For the mind’s inability to understand one thing before another concerns the mind’s own apprehension; it is not something inherent in the thing described. If one attribute is a constituent of the essence while the other is not, then the distinction between the essential and the accidental must rest on a feature established in reality itself, whether or not it is apprehended. Otherwise, no such distinction can be made.

The Tenth Argument:

It may be said: If the claim that the mind cannot understand one thing before another refers to particular minds—those which have conceived of matters in this way—then this constitutes no proof, because they themselves have arranged matters thus.

The claim would then amount to this: Whatever we place before the essence in our minds is essential, and whatever we place after it is accidental. The matter thus comes down to our arbitrarily treating some attributes as essential and others as accidental, whether inseparable or separable. If that is so, this distinction is nothing but an arbitrary assertion without any warrant.

It is hardly surprising that these people should conflate distinct things and distinguish between things that are alike. How often this occurs in their logical arguments, through which they have gone astray and led others astray! They were the first to corrupt the religion of the Muslims and introduce the innovations by which the Sabians altered the doctrines of rightly guided believers.

If they say:

“Rather, no human mind, nor any sound mind, apprehends a human being

---

## Page 4319

without first conceiving of its rationality, whereas conceiving of its capacity for laughter is not required.”

They may be answered:

This is not correct. Such an ordering is scarcely found except among those who accept these definitions on your authority, following you in the matters you have made the standard for judging intelligible things. Otherwise, neither attribute may occur to a person, or one may occur without the other, and vice versa.

Even if both attributes occur to someone, and he knows that a human being is a rational animal capable of laughter, merely knowing these attributes does not mean that he has grasped the essence of a human being at all. All this is evident to sense and reason. Let no rational person deceive himself about it out of deference to these people, who are among the most misguided of all creatures despite their claims to rigorous inquiry. Among the ancients, they are like the speculative theologians of Islam among the later generations. Since Muslims are better than the followers of the two Scriptures and the Sabians, their theologians are better, more knowledgeable and wiser than those philosophers. Reflect on this, for it is immensely beneficial.

Hence they say:

“Essential definitions are difficult, and discerning essential attributes is hard. Most definitions people possess are descriptive definitions.”

All this is because they have drawn a distinction between two things solely through an arbitrary stipulation of their own making.

It is well known that whatever has no reality either in the external world or in thought, but is merely a fabricated innovation devised to distinguish between two like things precisely in the respect in which they are alike—cannot be grasped by minds

---

## Page 4320

that are sound—for this belongs to the study of unsound doctrines that have no governing principle.

Most of what you find these groups extolling as their knowledge, and claiming to be the exclusive preserve of their eminent members, is falsehood with no reality, as we have pointed out previously.

The Eleventh Argument

Their assertion: “An essence is composed of genus and differentia: the genus is the shared component, and the differentia is the distinguishing component.”

They may be answered:

This composition must exist either in the external world or in the mind. If it exists in the external world, there is no universal species there to which this definition applies; there are only perceptible individuals. Each individual possesses an attribute whose counterpart is found in other animals, such as sensation and voluntary movement, and an attribute whose like is not found in other animals, namely rationality. These two attributes coexist in each individual, just as other attributes and substances subsisting in things composed of the attributes placed within them coexist.

If by animality and rationality you mean substance, a human being does not contain two substances, one living and the other rational. Rather, he is a single substance possessing two attributes. If, then,

---

## Page 4321

the substance is composed of two accidents, that is untenable. If it is composed of a general substance and a specific one, no such components exist within it. Thus, the claim that the externally existing essence is composite is invalid.

If they treat it sometimes as a substance and sometimes as an attribute, this is comparable to what the Christians say about the hypostases, which scholars agree is among the most self-contradictory positions.

If they say, “What is composite is the intelligible essence in the mind,” the first answer is that this is not what definitions seek to describe unless it corresponds to external reality. If there is no composition there, it cannot properly be attributed to the mental essence either. What is in the mind is merely the conception of a rational living being: a single substance possessing two attributes, as we explained earlier. There is therefore no composition in it whatsoever.

Know that there is no dispute that some attributes of species and genera are shared with others, as in the case of genus and common accident. Some attributes are inseparable from the essence, while others belong to it only contingently—that is, they are present at one time but not another, whether they disappear slowly or quickly. The issue is precisely the distinction between an essential attribute and an inseparable accident. This distinction rests upon the arbitrary judgment of the person formulating the definition.

Nor is there any dispute that some attributes may be more evident and nobler than others. The capacity for rational speech, for example, is nobler than the capacity for laughter. Hence Allah uses speech as a comparison when He says:

“It is indeed true, just as surely as you speak.”

The issue, however, is treating this attribute, rather than the other, as essential and as that through which the essence is conceived.

---

## Page 4322

The Twelfth Argument:

These essential attributes may be known without thereby yielding a conception of the innermost nature of the thing defined, as in this example and others. It is thus clear that knowing them does not guarantee an understanding of the essence.

The Thirteenth Argument:

If a definition has two components, both must be conceived, as with “living being” and “rational.” If each component requires a definition, this entails an infinite regress or circularity. If, however, the components are conceived in themselves without a definition—whether the conception is of a living being, a sentient being, something that moves voluntarily, something that grows, or a body—then it is well known that these are more general.

If they are more general because the senses perceive a greater number of their instances, then, if sensory perception of their instances suffices to form a conception, the senses have also perceived instances of the species. If it does not suffice, however, the components are not known. Thus, the thing to be defined requires something to define it, and the components of the definition require a definition.

---

## Page 4323

The Fourteenth Argument:

Definitions must distinguish what they define. The fewer the instances, the easier this distinction is; the more numerous they are, the harder it becomes. For the intellect, accurately grasping a universal with fewer instances, while also grasping its universality, is easier than grasping one with more instances. If a universal with many instances is easier for it to apprehend, that is only when it apprehends it in an unrestricted sense, since the unrestricted concept is attained through any one of its instances.

That being so, the components of the thing defined must, at the very least, be distinguished in their universal scope, so that one may know whether or not they are attributes of, or predicable of, the thing defined. If grasping them as universals is harder and more taxing than grasping the instances of the thing defined, this amounts to defining what is easier to know through individual components that are harder to know. This is the reverse of what is required.

The Fifteenth Argument:

Allah, glorified is He, taught Adam all the names. He distinguished each named thing by a name indicating what sets it apart from the genus it shares with others and what belongs specifically to it rather than to anything else, thereby making clear what delineates its meaning

---

## Page 4324

in the mind. Knowing the limits of what names denote is obligatory, for human welfare depends on this in the use of speech, which Allah has made a mercy for them. This applies especially to the scope of terms Allah has revealed in His Books, such as intoxicating drink and usury. These limits distinguish what falls within the named category and is covered by that name and the attributes it signifies from what does not. Hence Allah condemned those who called things by names for which He had sent down no authority, for such a person attributes a false quality to something, as when divinity is attributed to idols.

Spoken names are learned through hearing. Conceiving the meanings themselves, however, is innate: it arises through inward and outward sensation, through sensory perception and direct observation by a person’s inward and outward sight. Through hearing, a person learns their names, and with the heart grasps their shared and distinctive attributes. Allah brought us forth from our mothers’ wombs knowing nothing, and gave us hearing, sight, and hearts. As for contrived definitions, they offer no benefit to reason, sensory perception, or hearing, except insofar as they serve as names, only at greater length, or distinguish things, as other attributes do.

Thus, when they recognized this, they divided definitions into two kinds:

One is based on the name: it explains what falls within its scope. The other is based on the attribute, the reality, or the thing named; and they claimed to reveal

---

## Page 4325

the reality and provide a conception of it. If the reality in question is expressed in words, it falls within the first category; if it is not expressed in words, it cannot be apprehended through words or defined verbally except as already explained. These are brief observations drawing attention to the main points intended; this is not the place to elaborate on them.

The sixteenth argument:

Concerning shared and distinctive essential attributes—such as animality and rationality—if by “shared” they mean that the very same attribute existing outside the mind is shared, this is false. Particular entities cannot be shared: their very conception precludes this.

If, however, by “shared” they mean that another species possesses an attribute like that one, they may be told:

Undoubtedly, human animality and equine animality have something in common. Likewise, their voices and their powers of discernment have something in common. A human being has discernment, and a horse has discernment. The former has a voice, namely speech, and the latter has a voice, namely neighing; each voice has thus been given its own distinctive name. If, then, the reality of one differs from that of the other and is peculiar to its own species, on what grounds have you made the animality of one equivalent to the animality of the other in definition and reality?

Why not say instead that their animality has both a common aspect and a distinguishing aspect, just as their voices

---

## Page 4326

do?

For sensation and voluntary movement may belong either to the body or to the soul. The body senses and moves voluntarily, and the soul likewise senses and moves voluntarily, although the two attributes differ just as the two realities differ. Similarly, rationality belongs to the soul through discernment, knowledge, and inner speech, and also to the body through the heart’s discernment and knowledge and the tongue’s speech. Thus, both a person’s body and soul possess these two attributes.

The movement, will, knowledge, and rationality of the human soul are not like those of a horse, although they have something in common. Likewise, the sensation and voluntary movement belonging to the human body are not like those of a horse, although they have something in common. For the things suited to his body—food, drink, clothing, mates, scents, sights, and sounds—which he perceives and toward which he moves voluntarily, are not the same as those suited to a horse.

Sensation and voluntary movement, in their general sense, belong to all animals; in their specifically human sense, however, they belong to human beings alone. Exactly the same applies to discernment. Hence the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The names most beloved to Allah are ‘Abd Allāh and ‘Abd al-Rahmān. The names that most truthfully describe their bearers are Hārith and Hammām, and the ugliest are Harb and Murrah.”

Narrated by Muslim.

A hārith is one who works, earns, and moves. A hammām is one who is continually intent upon something, intent being the precursor to volition. Every human being is therefore a worker who acts by his own will, and this, too, is preceded by sensory perception.

---

## Page 4327

Thus, both a human being’s animal nature and his rationality include something shared with other animals and something peculiar to him alone. The same applies to the formation of his bodily constitution. Although his growth and nourishment have something in common with those of plants, they are not identical to them. For he feeds on what gives him pleasure and delights his soul, and he grows as his sensation, movement, intent, and work develop. Plants are not like this. The same applies to the various classes and individuals within a species. Thus, the rationality of the Arabs, manifested in the discernment of their hearts and the eloquence of their tongues, is more perfect than that of others. Indeed, some human beings fall below beasts in rationality and discernment, while others reach heights whose limits cannot be fathomed.

All this shows that when individuals within a class, classes within a species, species within a genus, and lower genera share the designation of a higher genus, this does not entail that the common meaning is present in them equally. Likewise, there is no single entity shared by externally existing realities. Rather, the mind grasps a meaning found in one thing, whose counterpart is found in another. It has already become clear that this is a counterpart by way of resemblance, not sameness, and that the reality of this common meaning in one differs from its reality in the other. This is where those who employ comparative reasoning go wrong: they attend to the common meaning that unites things, but not to the difference that distinguishes them.

And the Arabs, among the various peoples, and the Muslims, among the adherents of religions, are the foremost of people

---

## Page 4328

in perceiving differences and distinguishing shared features.

This is evident in their intellects, languages, sciences, and judgments. Thus, when the Arab theologians of Islam debated these Sabian theologians—the non-Arab Romans—and explained the superiority of their own reasoning and discourse over those of their opponents, the superiority of the Muslim theologians’ discourse became apparent. This is what Judge Abū Bakr ibn al-Bāqillānī did in his Book of Subtleties, in which he refuted many of the philosophers’ corrupt doctrines concerning celestial spheres, stars, intellects, souls, the Necessary Existent, and other matters. He discussed their logic and their classification of existing things, such as their division of being into substance and accident, followed by their division of accidents into the nine categories. He also presented the Muslim theologians’ classification, which distinguishes, groups, and differentiates things in ways absent from those philosophers’ discourse.

This is because Allah taught man clear expression, as He, Exalted is He, says:

“The Most Merciful,
Taught the Quran,
Created man,
Taught him eloquence.”

And He, Exalted is He, says:

“And He taught Adam all the names.”

And He says:

“Taught man what he did not know.”

Clarity belongs to both the heart and the tongue, just as blindness and muteness may affect both the heart and the tongue. As He, Exalted is He, says:

“Deaf, mute, and blind, so they do not return.”

And He says:

“Deaf, mute, and blind, so they do not understand.”

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Why did they not ask when they did not know? The only cure for ignorance is to ask.”

A transmitted report states:

“Incapacity lies in the heart, not in the tongue.”

Or he said:

“The worst incapacity is that of the heart.”

Ibn Masʿūd used to say:

“You live in a time whose scholars are many and whose orators are few. A time will come upon you whose scholars are few and whose orators are many.”

---

## Page 4329

The heart’s clear perception of things is the opposite of its confusion about them. As the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“The lawful is clear, and the unlawful is clear, and between them are doubtful matters”—the hadith continues.

The words of Allah, Exalted is He, “And so that the way of the criminals may become clear,” have been recited with “way” in both the nominative and the accusative—that is, “so that you yourself may discern their way.” Thus, a person discerns things.

The Arabs say: bāna al-shay’u (“the thing became clear”) and bayyantuhu (“I clarified it”); tabayyana al-shay’u (“the thing became clear”) and tabayyantuhu (“I ascertained it”); istabāna al-shay’u (“the thing became clear”) and istabantuhu (“I discerned it”). All these are used intransitively and transitively. An example is His saying, Exalted is He:

“If an evildoer brings you news, verify it.”

[and] (1) here the verb is transitive. Another example is His saying, “with manifest indecency”: mubayyina here means mutabayyina, “manifest in itself,” so here it is intransitive.

Bayān, like kalām (“speech”), may be a verbal noun, as in bāna al-shay’u bayānan (“the thing became clear”). It may also be a noun denoting the action of bayyana (“to clarify”), just as kalām (“speech”) and salām (“greeting”) denote the actions of kallama (“to speak”) and sallama (“to greet”). Thus, bayān can mean that something becomes clear, or that one makes it clear—that is, explains it. The latter is its predominant meaning. An example is the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Indeed, some eloquence is magic.”

The purpose of clarity in speech is to bring clarity to the listener’s heart, so that the matter becomes clear and evident to him. As Allah, Exalted is He, says:

“This is a clear explanation for mankind”—the verse continues.

Nevertheless, there are those to whom it does not become clear, as Allah, Exalted is He, says:

“Say: It is guidance and healing for those who believe. But those who do not believe have deafness in their ears, and it is blindness to them.”

And He says:

“And We have sent down to you the Reminder so that you may explain to people what has been sent down to them, and so that they may reflect.”

And He says:

“We have not sent any messenger except in the language of his people, so that he might explain clearly to them.”

And He says:

“The Messenger’s duty is only to convey the message clearly.”

And He says:

“Allah would not lead a people astray after guiding them until He had made clear to them what they should avoid.”

And He says:

“Allah makes clear to you, lest you

> **Footnotes:**

> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — coordinator of the book for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila (The Comprehensive Encyclopedia).

---

## Page 4330

go astray.” And He says:

“Say: I stand upon clear evidence from my Lord”—the verse continues.

And He says:

“Is one who stands upon clear evidence from his Lord”

And He says:

“And We have certainly sent down to you clarifying verses.”

And He says:

“Allah makes the verses clear to you so that you may understand.”

As for elaborating on things already known, when this adds nothing but verbosity, bombast, affected eloquence and arrogance, and explicitly mentioning things whose mention is indecent—such conduct is prohibited. As the hadith states:

“Indeed, Allah hates the eloquent man who rolls his tongue as a cow rolls hers.”

Another hadith states:

“Modesty and inarticulacy are two branches of faith, while obscenity and eloquence are two branches of hypocrisy.”

For this reason, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“A man’s lengthy prayer and brief sermon are a sign of his understanding.”

In the report of Sa'd:

“When he heard his son—or when he found his son—supplicating, saying, ‘O Allah, I ask You for Paradise, its bliss, its joy, and such-and-such; and I seek refuge in You from the Fire, its chains, its shackles, and such-and-such,’ he said: ‘My son, I heard the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, say: “There will be people who overstep the bounds in supplication.” Beware of being one of them. If you are granted Paradise, you will be granted it and all the good it contains; and if you are given refuge from the Fire, you will be given refuge from it and all the evil it contains.’”

Most logical definitions fall into this category: a mass of verbiage with which they explain things that were clearer before their explanations than afterward. Despite the considerable time they waste and the strain they place upon the mind and tongue, they produce nothing but blindness and error and open the door to

---

## Page 4331

wrangling and disputation, as each raises objections that invalidate the other’s definition while claiming that his own is free of them. Yet on close examination, you find them evenly matched, or nearly so, with neither having any clear advantage over the other. Thus, either all their definitions should be accepted, all rejected, or accepted in one respect and rejected in another.

This applies to definitions that alike distinguish the thing defined and set it apart from everything else. But when one person includes in a definition what another excludes, or vice versa, discussing this yields knowledge of the term’s definition and the breadth or narrowness of its scope. Examples include discussing the definition of khamr (wine): is it fermented grape juice, or every intoxicant? The same applies to the definition of backbiting and similar matters. This is what scholars discuss, as when the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked:

“What is backbiting?”

He replied, “Your mentioning your brother in a way he dislikes”—the hadith continues.

Likewise, he said:

“Every intoxicant is khamr.”

And ʿUmar said from the pulpit:

“Khamr is whatever clouds the mind.”

Similarly, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No one who has an atom’s weight of arrogance in his heart will enter Paradise.”

A man asked him, “O Messenger of Allah, a man likes his shoes and clothing to look good. Is that arrogance?”

He replied, “No. Allah is beautiful and loves beauty. Arrogance is rejecting the truth and looking down on people.”

This also includes interpreting speech, explaining it and clarifying it. Anyone who explains another person’s words, interprets them and clarifies their meaning must therefore know the definitions of the terms they contain.

---

## Page 4332

Thus, any definition expressed in words is merely a definition of a term, akin to translation and explanation. Sometimes it is purely verbal, when the person addressed already knows what is being defined. At other times, its meaning needs to be explained and clarified, when the person addressed does not know what the term denotes. This is done by giving an example or combining descriptions. Yet this does not convey a conception of the thing’s essence to someone who has not conceived it through something other than words. Let this be understood. As for what they call defining a thing, defining it according to its essence, or defining essences, it distinguishes the thing only by mentioning some of its attributes, as explained above. It also contains confusion, some of which we have already pointed out.

As for “the question of syllogistic reasoning,” it is to be discussed in two respects. The first concerns syllogistic reasoning in general, which they have made the standard for the sciences and have precisely formulated in logic. The second concerns the kinds of syllogisms they employ in the sciences.

Regarding the first, we say: there is no dispute that when two premises are known to be true and are properly combined, this yields knowledge of the conclusion.

A narration in Sahīh Muslim, traced back to the Prophet, states:

“Every intoxicant is khamr, and every khamr is forbidden.”

However, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, did not say this to argue against an opponent disputing with him. Rather, the combination of statements here is like what he also said

---

## Page 4333

in the Sahīh:

“Every intoxicant is khamr, and every khamr is forbidden.”

He intended to explain to them that all intoxicants fall within the meaning of khamr, which Allah has forbidden. This explains the meaning of khamr; they already knew that Allah had forbidden khamr. They used to ask him about drinks made from grape juice, as related in the two Sahīhs from Abū Mūsā:

“He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was asked about a drink made from sorghum called mizr and a drink made from honey called bitʿ. He had been endowed with concise, comprehensive speech, so he said: ‘Every intoxicant is forbidden.’”

Through this comprehensive statement—a universal proposition—he intended to explain to them that every intoxicant is khamr. He then added what they already knew, that “every khamr is forbidden,” to establish firmly in their hearts that intoxicants are forbidden, as he explicitly stated in his words: “Every intoxicant is forbidden.” Had he confined himself to saying, “Every intoxicant is forbidden,” someone might have interpreted this as referring to the final cup, as some indeed did.

For this reason, Ahmad said: “His statement, ‘Every intoxicant is khamr,’ is more emphatic.” For they do not call the final cup khamr. Had he merely said, “Every intoxicant is khamr,” some might have interpreted this to mean that it resembles khamr in being forbidden. But when he added, “And every khamr is forbidden,” it became clear that he intended to include it within the designation khamr, which Allah has forbidden.

The point here is that this form of syllogism is innate and requires no instruction. Rather, it is as familiar to people as arithmetic. Yet these men make their formulations lengthy and obscure.

---

## Page 4334

The same applies to dividing a premise—called a “proposition,” meaning a declarative sentence—into particular and universal, negative and affirmative, and the like; to the statements that a true proposition has a true converse and contrapositive, that its negation is false, and that its formulation varies; and to similar matters. Likewise, they divide syllogisms into simple categorical syllogisms, conditional syllogisms based on implication or mutual exclusion, and other types. Even if most of this is sound, it contains some falsehood.

Even the truth it contains involves needless length and multiplication of words, poor expression and inarticulate explanation, and abandoning the straight, short path for a distant, circuitous route. This is not the place to explain all that. Its useful truths are innate and do not require this discipline; whatever does require it offers no benefit beyond learning their terminology and method, or their errors.

Such is the case with everyone who espouses a false doctrine: acquaintance with his discourse is indispensable to understanding his terminology and misguidance. It is therefore needed to expose his misguidance, through which those endowed with certainty recognize his true condition. It also makes clear to them what Allah has explained concerning His judgment, both in recompense and in His commands, and that these people fall under the censure directed at those who strain to produce speech that conveys nothing and multiply words to no benefit.

The purpose here is to set out several points:

---

## Page 4335

The first point:

The syllogism in question yields knowledge only through a universal affirmative proposition. Every syllogism must therefore contain an established, comprehensive universal proposition. This, too, is acknowledged and agreed upon.

Hence their statement: “No syllogism can be formed from two negative premises or from two particular premises.” Accordingly, universal truths—comprehensive statements—must constitute the starting points of syllogisms and proofs, and the foundations on which they are built and upon which they depend.

They further state that the starting points of demonstrative syllogisms consist of certain knowledge: internal and external sensory perceptions, rational truths, self-evident truths, mass-transmitted reports, and findings established through experience. Some also add intuitive truths.

Yet neither internal nor external sensory perceptions contain universal propositions, since internal and external sensation apprehend only particular things, which are established only when the reporter has perceived through the senses what he reports; they are therefore dependent on sensory perceptions. Likewise, experience concerns only particular, perceptible things. The intellect judges similar cases by comparing them, which is reasoning by analogy. Intuitive truths—according to those among them who recognize them—are of the same kind as findings established through experience.

The difference, however, is that experience involves the experimenter’s own actions, as with foods, drinks, and medicines

---

## Page 4336

whereas intuition concerns what does not involve one’s own action, such as the changing shapes of the moon as its position relative to the sun changes. In reality, it is a purely cognitive experience involving no action. What it yields, too, consists of particular, individual matters, which become general only through reasoning by analogy.

As for self-evident truths—the primary knowledge that Allah places directly in souls, without any intermediary, such as arithmetic, including the knowledge that one is half of two—they do not yield knowledge of any particular thing existing outside the mind. Examples include judgments concerning number and magnitude in the abstract, and the knowledge that things equal to the same thing are equal to one another. Whenever you make a judgment about something existing outside the mind, this can only be through sensory perception, just as with intellectual apprehension. For intellectual apprehension consists in understanding what you have learned through internal or external sensation, by apprehending general or particular meanings. As for apprehending universals whose individual instances exist outside the mind without any sensory perception, this is inconceivable.

When a person examines himself, he finds that he does not apprehend this independently of internal and external sensation with regard to universals conceived in his mind, such as one and two, the straight and the curved, the triangle and the square, the necessary, the possible, and the impossible, and other such things that he himself supposes and conceives. But knowing that what is thus conceived corresponds to something existing outside the mind, and knowing external realities, necessarily requires internal or external sensory perception.

When sensory perception and intellect work together—as when sight and intellect work together—it becomes possible to apprehend the existing realities in question and understand the universal judgment that applies to them

---

## Page 4337

under which similar things, but not their opposites, fall, and to recognize similarities and differences. This is the intellect’s comparative and analogical reasoning. When internal or external sensation operates alone, it apprehends the existence of a particular thing. When abstract thought operates alone, it knows the universals conceived within it, which may or may not exist outside the mind. Whether their individual instances exist or not can be known only through internal or external sensation.

For when you say, “It is a fact that one hundred is one-tenth of one thousand,” you are not making a judgment about anything outside the mind. Indeed, even if nothing in the world could be counted in hundreds or thousands, you would still know that the hundred conceived in your mind is one-tenth of the thousand. But when you perceive men, animals, gold, and silver, and ascertain—through your own senses or the report of someone who has perceived them—that there are one hundred men or dirhams in one place and one thousand in another, or the like, you judge that one of the two quantities counted is one-tenth of the other.

The things counted can be apprehended only through sensory perception, whereas abstract number is apprehended by the heart. Through the heart’s understanding and sensory perception together, both number and the things counted are known. Geometrical magnitudes likewise belong to this category. Thus, primary, self-evident, purely intellectual knowledge concerns only things conceived in the mind, such as number and magnitude, not things actually existing outside it.

---

## Page 4338

Since most of the sources supplying the premises of demonstrative syllogisms—internal and external sensation, mass transmission, experience, and intuitive apprehension—yield knowledge only of particulars, not universals, while the faculty that apprehends primary, self-evident universals apprehends only things conceived in the mind, none of the aforementioned principles and premises of demonstration provides knowledge of a universal proposition applicable to things actually existing outside the mind. Yet a syllogism yields knowledge only through a universal proposition. It is therefore impossible to obtain certain knowledge through what they describe as the form and material of the syllogism.

This is clear to anyone who reflects upon it. Precisely formulating it and conceiving it clearly opens up vast fields of knowledge and understanding. We shall explain, Allah willing, how their confusion arose. Reflect carefully on this, for it is one of the secrets of the greatest branches of knowledge. Through it, you will perceive an indescribably great difference between the way grounded in innate disposition, reason, transmitted revelation, the revealed Law, and faith, and the way of syllogisms, logic, and speculative theology.

It has become clear to you, both from their unanimous agreement and through reason, that the logical syllogism yields knowledge only through a proposition. It has also become clear that the propositions they regard as the material and foundations of demonstration contain no universal proposition applicable to existing things. Nor do they contain any means of knowing a universal proposition other than abstract reason, which apprehends things conceived in the mind. If, then, the foundations of their demonstration contain no knowledge of a general proposition applicable to existing things, this furnishes no knowledge.

---

## Page 4339

Nothing in what we have mentioned is open to dispute except the self-evident propositions, for these possess generality. One might think that this generality provides knowledge of things outside the mind, and thus suppose that these propositions yield universal knowledge. But the remaining principles contain no universal knowledge. Only purely rational, self-evident propositions should therefore serve as premises of demonstration, since these alone are universal. The remaining propositions are particular, so how can they properly serve as premises of demonstration?

Unless it is argued:

Through these propositions, particulars are known, while through reason, universals are known; together, they complete the demonstration. For example, sensory perception establishes that one man has a thousand dirhams and another two thousand, while reason establishes that two is greater than one. It is thus known that the latter has more wealth.

The reply is:

This is correct, but it yields only a specific, particular proposition: that one man’s wealth exceeds the other’s. Knowledge of specific particulars does not require a syllogism. They may be known without a syllogism, through analogical inference, or through inference from two particular propositions. For you know through sensory perception that this thing is like that thing, and you know that this thing has such-and-such attributes; you therefore know that the other is like it, and that what holds true of a thing holds true of its like. Likewise, it may be known that Zayd is older than Amr and that Amr is older than Khālid, along with similar specific matters that can be known without the subsumptive syllogism for which they have laid down their stipulated conditions.

---

## Page 4340

It has thus become clear that this rational, logical syllogism which they have devised and defined cannot, by itself, yield any universal knowledge concerning external reality. Their claim is therefore invalid:

“It is the standard by which universal demonstrative knowledge is measured.”

It does, however, yield knowledge of specific, individual particulars, but these can be known better through other means.

This brings us to the second point. We say: Things that actually exist are known through inward and outward perception, through analogical inference, and through inference containing no universal proposition, subsumption, or generalization. Rather, its three terms—the minor, middle, and major—are individual particulars, and its two premises and conclusion are particular propositions. Knowledge of these specific things through such methods is sounder, clearer, and more complete.

Someone who sees Zayd with his own eyes in one place and Amr in another has no need to infer this from the principle that a single body cannot occupy two places. Likewise, someone who weighs quantities of dirhams, each amounting to a thousand dirhams, has no need to draw an inference about a thousand of those dirhams on the grounds that they equal the standard weight, that this is one and the same object, and that things equal to the same thing are equal to one another. There are many similar examples.

This is why these people are called “men of talk”: they have supplied no knowledge that was not already known. They have merely added further talk that may serve no purpose: the syllogisms they have formulated to clarify what is already known through sensory perception. Although this

---

## Page 4341

syllogism and others like it can be useful in another context and when dealing with those who deny sensory perception, as we shall explain, Allah willing.

Likewise, someone may know that this dinar is like that one, this dirham is like that one, and these quantities of wheat and barley are like their counterparts. If he then knows some of the attributes of one member of a pair and what holds true of it—whether by nature, such as its nourishing and beneficial effects; by convention, such as its value and price; or under revealed law, such as its permissibility or prohibition—he knows that the same holds true of the other. Analogical inferences thus undoubtedly yield greater certainty than subsumptive syllogisms. Once the likeness is known, there is no need to construct a subsumptive syllogism for the two cases; doing so merely adds something superfluous. In this way, particular propositions are known through analogical inference.

Whoever says that this occurs through a subsumptive syllogism formed in the mind—namely, that if this were coincidental, it would not occur in most cases—has spoken falsely. This does not occur to people who know things through their own experience. Rather, merely knowing that the cases are alike, they immediately judge them alike, because knowledge of their likeness itself entails this as a self-evident truth of reason. Just as it is known through self-evident reason that one is half of two, so it is known through it that what holds true of a thing

---

## Page 4342

holds true of its like, and that one is like one, just as it is known that things equal to the same thing are equal to one another. Likeness and difference in quality or quantity may be known through internal and external sense perception, while the knowledge that two like things are equal, and that what is more numerous and larger is greater and weightier, is self-evident to reason.

The same applies to an inference composed of propositions about particulars: for example, knowing that Zayd is Amr’s brother and Amr is Bakr’s brother, and therefore Zayd is Bakr’s brother. Likewise, knowing that Abū Bakr is superior to Umar, and Umar is superior to Uthmān and Alī, and therefore Abū Bakr is superior to Uthmān and Alī. Or that Medina is superior to Jerusalem, and pilgrimage to Medina is not obligatory, so no pilgrimage is made to Jerusalem. Or that the Messenger’s grave—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—is the finest of graves, yet touching or kissing it is not prescribed; therefore, touching or kissing the graves of so-and-so, so-and-so, and so-and-so is not prescribed.

The world abounds in such inferences. This is more effective in establishing what holds true of a particular than mentioning the general. A specific name identifies a particular more effectively than a general name does, even though the general name encompasses other things that the specific name does not.

It is thus clear that far more particulars are known through sensory perception, analogical inference, and inferences involving particulars than are known through subsumptive syllogisms. Thus, if the subsumptive syllogism—as they have formulated it—does not yield knowledge of universals, as explained above, and is not needed for knowledge of particulars

---

## Page 4343

—as has been shown—it has no use whatsoever and is needed for knowledge of neither universals nor particulars. Their discussion of the syllogism they have formulated thus becomes like their discussion of definitions. The two amount to the same thing. Reflect on this, for it is of immense importance.

The third argument:

If a syllogism must contain a universal proposition, while the senses do not apprehend universals, which are apprehended only by reason, and that proposition cannot be known through another syllogism because this would entail circularity or an infinite regress, then there must be universal propositions apprehended without syllogism, such as those they classify as self-evident.

We therefore say: Since it must be acknowledged that some rational knowledge of universals arises directly in the mind and presents itself as self-evident without syllogism, it must be firmly accepted that rational knowledge of universals can dispense with syllogism.

This is something they themselves, along with all humanity, acknowledge: some conceptions and judgments are self-evident and need not be acquired through definition or syllogism. Otherwise, circularity or an infinite regress would necessarily follow.

Since this is so, we say: If this is possible for one universal truth, it is possible for another, since between the self-evident truths that can be known directly and what cannot be known there is no

---

## Page 4344

consistent distinction. Rather, this varies according to the strength and clarity of the intellect and the extent of its apprehension of the particulars through which universals are known. Every universal truth can be known without logical syllogism. It is therefore unjustifiable to judge any universal knowledge to depend on it.

This becomes clear through the fourth argument:

We say: Granted that the form and matter of logical syllogism yield universal knowledge, on what grounds is it known that universal knowledge is otherwise unattainable? How, then, can these pretentious men, who follow that of which they have no knowledge—along with their imitators among the adherents of religious communities and their scholars—declare: “Conceptions and judgments that are not self-evident can be known only through definition and syllogism”?

Lack of knowledge is not knowledge of absence. Whoever makes such a claim has not examined his own mental experience. Had he done so, he would have found that he possesses universal knowledge without logical syllogism and many conceptions without definition. Even if he knows this to be true of himself or his fellows, how can he know that all the children of Adam—with their differing natural dispositions, knowledge, and gifts bestowed upon them by the True One—are just like him, and that Allah grants no one knowledge except through a logical syllogism formed in his mind?

These men even claim that the prophets were likewise. Indeed, they have extended their claim to the Lord of all worlds, asserting that His knowledge of the affairs of His creation comes only through logical syllogism. Yet for this negation, concerning a matter they have not encompassed in knowledge, they have no proof other than a lack of

---

## Page 4345

knowledge. Thus they claim knowledge, yet they have asserted this universal negative proposition—which encompasses more cases than anyone but Allah can count—without any knowledge of it whatsoever.

This is further clarified by the fifth argument:

The aforementioned principles that they regard as yielding certainty—namely, internal and external sensory perceptions, self-evident truths, findings established through experience, and intuitive apprehensions—undoubtedly yield sensory certainty. But on what grounds do they claim that certainty cannot be attained through anything else?

They must provide evidence for this negation before their statement, “Certainty cannot be attained without them,” can be valid. This is correct, but it is not what their leading authorities say. Undoubtedly, anyone possessed of reason and faith must oppose them in their denial of truths attained outside this method. It was through this that those among them who succumbed to hypocrisy became hypocrites and heretics.

Thus, sensible people, whether adherents of religious communities or others, came to regard logic as liable to foster denial of the truth, obstinacy, heresy, and hypocrisy. Someone even related to us that a non-Arab man came to study logic under one of their teachers. After studying a portion of it, he asked, “Master, which chapter deals with abandoning prayer?” They laughed at him.

---

## Page 4346

Observation bears this out: whoever thinks highly of logic and its practitioners, unless he has a foundation in religion and reason through which to acquire beneficial truth, will suffer corruption in both his reason and his religion. Thus, disbelief, hypocrisy, ignorance, misguidance, and corrupt words and deeds are found among them to an extent apparent to any observer. This is why the first person to mix logic with the principles of jurisprudence and similar Islamic sciences was beset by considerable confusion.

Many distinguished Muslims and scholars used to say:

“Logic is like arithmetic and similar disciplines: it cannot demonstrate the truth or falsehood of Islam, nor establish or refute it.”

Such words come from someone who has seen only its outward aspect: its discussion of simple elements, both expressions and meanings; then their combination into propositions, together with their contradictories, simple converses, and contrapositives; then their arrangement in definitions and syllogisms, and the content of syllogisms. In reality, however, logic contains many unsound notions and false claims that cannot be exhaustively discussed here. And Allah knows best.

All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds. May Allah bestow blessings and peace upon His servant and Messenger Muhammad, who calls to guidance and right conduct, and upon his family and those who follow his guidance.

---

## Page 4347

Shaykh al-Islām Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah sanctify his soul—also said:

To proceed:

I had always known that an intelligent person has no need of Greek logic and that a dull-witted person gains no benefit from it. Yet I used to think its propositions were true, since we had found many of them to be true. Later, however, it became clear to me that some of its propositions were erroneous, and I wrote something on the subject.

While I was in Alexandria, someone met with me whom I found extolling the philosophers through exaggeration and blind imitation. I explained to him some of the grounds on which they deserved to be judged ignorant and misguided. This prompted me to write down some remarks on logic in a single sitting between the noon and afternoon prayers, recording them there and then. This was not, however, my main concern; my attention was devoted to what I had written against them on “metaphysics.” It became clear to me that much of what they had stated about logic lay at the root of their erroneous views on metaphysics,

---

## Page 4348

such as their account of essences as composed of attributes they called essential, and their restriction of the paths to knowledge to the definitions and demonstrative syllogisms they described—indeed, their account of the definitions through which conceptions are acquired, and even their account of the forms of syllogism and its certain premises.

Someone then wished to copy the remarks I had written at that time criticizing their views on logic. I permitted this because it opens the way to knowing the truth, although the scope for refuting them thus opened could accommodate many times as much as I had written.

Know, then, that they based “logic” on the discussion of definition and the like, and demonstrative syllogism and the like. They said: “This is because knowledge is either conception or assent. The means by which conception is attained is definition, and the means by which assent is attained is syllogism.”

We say: The discussion concerns “four points”: two negative and two affirmative. The first of the two negative points concerns their claim that the conception sought can be attained only through definition; the second, that the assent sought can be attained only through syllogism. The other two concern their claims that definition yields knowledge of conceptions, and that syllogism—or demonstration as they describe it—yields knowledge of assents.

---

## Page 4349

The first point concerns their statement:

“Conception can be attained only through definition.”

This may be examined from several angles.

First: There is no doubt that one who denies a claim must provide evidence, just as one who affirms it must. A proposition, whether negative or affirmative, requires evidence unless it is self-evident. Negation without knowledge is simply speaking without knowledge. Their statement that conceptions can be attained only through definition is a negative proposition, and it is not self-evident. What, then, is their evidence for it? If this is a statement made without knowledge, yet it is the very first foundation they laid, how can speaking without knowledge serve as the foundation for the criterion of knowledge and for what they claim is a rule-governed instrument whose observance safeguards the mind against error in thought?

Second: It may be said that “definition” can mean the thing defined itself, but this is not what they intend here. It can also mean a statement indicating the essence of the thing defined, which is what they intend here. It sets out in detail what the name indicates in general. We therefore say: If a definition is a statement made by the definer, then the definer must have identified the thing defined either through a definition or without one.

If the former, the same question applies to the second definition as to the first, necessarily resulting in circularity or an infinite regress. If the latter, their negative claim is invalidated—namely, their statement that it can be known only through definition.

Third: All nations—those engaged in the sciences and doctrines, and those engaged in practical pursuits

---

## Page 4350

and crafts—know the things they need to know and master the sciences and practical activities in which they engage without articulating any definition. We find none of the leading authorities in the sciences employing these definitions: neither the authorities on jurisprudence, grammar, medicine or arithmetic, nor practitioners of the crafts. Yet they conceive the individual concepts of their disciplines. It is therefore clear that conception has no need of these definitions.

(Fourth: To this day, no definition is known that is sound according to their principles. Indeed, the most obvious of things is the human being, yet its definition as a “rational animal” is subject to the well-known objections. The same applies to the definition of the sun and similar things. Even the grammarians, when their later scholars ventured into definitions, offered twenty-odd definitions of the noun, all of which are open to objections according to their principles. Scholars of legal theory likewise offered twenty-odd definitions of analogical reasoning, all of which are also open to objections. Most definitions mentioned in the books of philosophers, physicians, grammarians, scholars of legal theory and speculative theologians are open to objections; only a few have escaped them. If conceiving things depended on definitions, people would not, to this day, have conceived any of these things. Assent depends on conception, so where no conception is attained, no assent is attained. Humanity would therefore possess no knowledge in most of its disciplines. This is sophistry of the highest order.

(Fifth: According to them, conception of an essence is attained only through a real definition composed of shared and distinguishing essential attributes—that is, one composed of genus and differentia. Such a definition is either impossible or difficult to formulate, as they themselves have acknowledged. In that case, no one would have conceived

---

## Page 4351

any essence, either at all or in most cases. Yet essences have been conceived, so it is clear that conception has no need of definition.

(Sixth: According to them, definitions apply only to composite essences: species possessing a genus and a differentia. As for what is not composite—that which does not fall under a genus together with anything else, exemplified by some of them by the intellect—it has no definition. Yet they have defined it, and its conception is among those they seek to acquire. It is therefore clear that conception has no need of definition. Indeed, if this can be known without a definition, those species can all the more readily be known, since they are closer to the genus and their individual instances are familiar.

They say that assent does not depend on the complete conception attained through a real definition. Rather, even a minimal conception suffices, if only through a distinctive property. The conception of the intellect belongs to this category. This is an admission on their part that conception as such does not depend on a real definition.

Seventh: Unless someone hearing a definition already knows its individual terms and how they signify their respective meanings, he cannot understand the statement. Knowing that a term signifies a meaning and has been assigned to it presupposes a conception of that meaning. If he already conceives what the term designates and means before hearing it, it cannot be said that he conceives it only by hearing it.

(Eighth: If a definition is a statement made by the person defining, then it is well known that conceiving meanings

---

## Page 4352

does not require words. A speaker may conceive the meaning of what he says without words, and a listener can do so without anyone addressing him at all. How, then, can it be said that simple concepts can be conceived only through definitions?

(Ninth: A person conceives existing things either through his external senses, as with taste, colour, smell, and the bodies possessing these attributes, or through his internal senses, as with hunger, love, hatred, joy, sorrow, pleasure, pain, volition, aversion, and the like. None of these requires a definition.

(Tenth: They say that an objector may challenge a definition by presenting counterexamples to its applicability or exclusiveness, or by opposing it with another definition. If someone hearing a definition can invalidate it, sometimes through counterexamples and sometimes through a competing definition—and it is well known that neither is possible without first conceiving what is being defined—then it follows that what is being defined can be conceived without the definition. This is precisely what we seek to establish.

(Eleventh: They acknowledge that some conceptions are self-evident and require no definition. It may then be said that whether knowledge is self-evident or inferential is a relative matter. What requires inference for one person may be self-evident to another because he has acquired it through such means as direct observation, mass transmission, or corroborating indications. People’s powers of apprehension vary beyond any fixed measure. Thus, what is self-evident to one person but not another may likewise become self-evident to the latter through means like those available to the former, without any need for a definition.

---

## Page 4353

The Second Point: “A Definition Yields a Conception of Things”

We say: Discerning scholars of rational inquiry hold that the function of a definition, like that of a name, is to distinguish what is defined from everything else—not to convey a conception of it or make its reality known. This latter claim is made only by the Greek logicians, the followers of Aristotle, and those among Muslim thinkers and others who follow their path in imitation of them. The great majority of those engaged in rational inquiry and theology, whether Muslims or others, hold the contrary view.

This view was introduced only by those who discussed the foundations of religion and jurisprudence after Abū Hāmid, towards the end of the fifth century AH. They were the ones who treated definitions according to the method of Greek logic. Other scholars of rational inquiry, from all the various schools—the Ash'arīs, Mu'tazilīs, Karrāmīs, Shi'a, and others—hold that a definition serves only to distinguish what is defined from everything else. This is well known in the works of Abū al-Hasan al-Ash'arī, Judge Abū Bakr, Abū Ishāq, Ibn Fūrak, Judge Abū Ya'lā, Ibn 'Aqīl, Imām al-Haramayn, al-Nasafī, Abū 'Alī, Abū Hāshim, 'Abd al-Jabbār, al-Tūsī, Muhammad ibn al-Haysam, and others.

Furthermore, there is no doubt that the art of definition described by the logicians is a convention of their own devising. Nations before them knew the realities of things without this convention, and the great majority of nations

---

## Page 4354

after them know the realities of things without their convention. If they themselves reflect, they will find that they know the realities of things without this conventional art. Yet they claimed that this art makes the realities of things known, and that these realities cannot be known except through it. Both claims are mistaken.

When they sought to achieve this, they had to distinguish between certain attributes and others. Since they made conception depend on what they deemed essential, they necessarily had to distinguish between what they regarded as essential and what they did not. This led them to distinguish between things that are alike, treating one attribute as essential but not another, although the two were equal or closely similar. Finding a distinction between things that are alike is impossible, and between closely similar things is difficult. What they seek is therefore either impossible or difficult to attain. If it is impossible, their undertaking fails altogether. If it is difficult, then even once attained, it adds nothing to what was already known before attaining it.

Thus, either they cannot meet the condition they have stipulated, or they meet it without achieving their intended aim.
