In either case, the definition they have devised is not a means of producing a conception of realities in the mind of someone who cannot conceive of them without a definition, although it may serve to distinguish what is defined in the same way that names do.

Fakhr al-Rāzī recognized the position held by the leading scholars of theology and established in his al-Muhassal (Compendium) and elsewhere that conceptions cannot be acquired. This is precisely what we mean when we say that a definition does not yield a conception of what is defined. This is “a point of great importance” that ought to be understood, for neglect of it has allowed corruption to enter

---

## Page 4355

the minds or religious beliefs of many people, as they mixed what the logicians said about definitions with the prophetic knowledge brought by the Messengers and preserved among Muslims, Jews, and Christians, and with the other sciences, such as medicine, grammar, and so forth. They began to magnify the importance of definitions, claiming that they alone had mastered the subject, and that the definitions offered by others were merely verbal and did not convey an understanding of essence and reality, unlike their own definitions.

They follow long, difficult paths and employ contrived, formidable expressions. Yet this serves only to waste time, exhaust minds, multiply senseless talk, claim rigorous understanding through lies and falsehoods, and occupy souls with what does not benefit them—and may even turn them away from what is indispensable. It entrenches in hearts the ignorance that is the root of hypocrisy, although they claim it to be the foundation of knowledge and rigorous understanding.

This is among the consequences of the speculative theology that the early generations used to prohibit, although what they prohibited was better and sounder than this, since it concerned proofs and judgments. The earlier speculative theologians were unwilling to delve into definitions in the manner of the logicians, as their successors so earnestly did, imagining this to be rigorous inquiry. It is nothing but a deviation from the straight path.

Thus, because these definitions and the like give a person no knowledge that he did not already possess, yielding only an abundance of talk, they were called “the people of kalām (discourse).” All this, by my life, concerns definitions that contain no falsehood. As for the logicians’ definitions, which they claim

---

## Page 4356

enable them to convey conceptions of realities, they are false: through them they conflate things that differ and distinguish between things that are alike.

There are several arguments showing that definitions do not convey conceptions of realities.

First:

A definition is merely the statement and claim of the person framing it. His statement, for example, “The definition of man is a rational animal,” is a declarative proposition and a mere claim unsupported by any proof. Either the listener knows it to be true independently of this statement, or he does not. In the first case, it is established that he did not acquire this knowledge through the definition. In the second case, merely stating something without supporting evidence does not impart knowledge to him. How could it, when he knows that the speaker is not infallible in what he says? Thus, on either assumption, a definition does not impart knowledge of definitions.

If it is said:

“It gives him only a conception of what the term designates, without judging whether this is, for example, the very thing being asked about or something else.”

We reply:

“In that case, it is merely like a single term signifying its meaning—that is, a name signifying what it names.”

This confirms what we have said: a definition signifies in the same way that a name does. A mere name does not give someone a conception of what it names unless he has already conceived it independently of that name. This is undisputed, and the same applies to a definition.

Second:

They say: “A definition cannot be met with a denial, nor is proof established for it; it is only possible to

---

## Page 4357

invalidate it through refutation and counterargument.”

The reply is:

If the person framing the definition has provided no proof of its soundness, the listener cannot come to know what is defined through it, since the person framing it may be mistaken. For if the listener does not know from the speaker’s statement that the definition is sound, and that statement may be true or false, he cannot come to know what is defined through that statement.

It is astonishing that these people claim that these are rational methods yielding certainty. They make knowledge of the simple the foundation of knowledge of the composite, yet place their reliance here on a definition that is merely the unsupported statement of the person framing it. It is a report from a single individual concerning a rational matter, not a sensory one, and admits of correctness and error, truth and falsehood. Then they criticize those who, in matters known through transmission, rely on a single transmitter’s report accompanied by contextual evidence that gives certain knowledge to a listener acquainted with it. They claim that a single transmitter’s report does not impart knowledge. Yet even if such a report does not impart knowledge, this is precisely what their own account of definition amounts to: it is a report from a single individual, with no proof of its truth. Indeed, according to them, no proof of its truth can even be established.

Thus, the definition does not convey a conception of what is defined. If, however, the listener has conceived what is defined before hearing the definition, or conceives it while hearing it or afterwards, independently of the definition, and knows that this is its definition, he knows that the person framing it has defined it truthfully. In that case, the definition has not conveyed the conception. This is clear.

In summary, conceiving what is defined through a definition is impossible without knowing that the statement of the person framing it is true. The truth of his statement cannot be known merely from his assertion. Hence, what is defined cannot be known through the definition.

Third:

It may be said: If a definition conveyed a conception of what is defined, one could not attain

---

## Page 4358

that conception except after knowing that the definition is sound, for the definition is the evidence for that conception, the means of attaining it, and what reveals it. It is therefore impossible to know the thing defined before knowing that its definition is sound. Yet knowledge of the definition’s soundness can be attained only after knowing what is defined. For a definition is a report about a subject [of the report] (1), namely, what is defined. It is impossible to know that a report is sound and true before conceiving its subject, without uncritically accepting the report and taking the reporter at his word concerning something of which both the reporter and the recipient have knowledge. This is not a case of reporting matters beyond one’s observation.

Fourth:

They define a thing through attributes that they call essential and accidental. They call these the parts of the definition, the parts of the essence, its constitutive elements, those included within it, and similar expressions. If the listener does not know that the thing defined possesses those attributes, he cannot conceive it. If he knows that it possesses them, he has already conceived it without the definition. Thus, in either case, he has not conceived it through the definition. This is clear.

For when it is said, “A human being is a rational animal,” someone who does not know that this is a human being needs to know this relationship. If he has not conceived what “rational animal” denotes, he needs two things: to conceive it and to know the relationship just mentioned. If he knows this, he has already conceived a human being without the definition.

Admittedly, a definition may bring the conception of what is defined to mind, just as a name does. The mind may be inattentive to something; upon hearing its name alone, a person turns his mind towards the thing indicated by the name or definition and conceives it. Thus, the benefit of a definition is of the same kind as that of a name, and definitions of species through their attributes are like boundaries

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) The text in square brackets is absent from the printed edition, and I have not found it in Siyānat Majmū‘ al-Fatāwā min al-Saqt wa-l-Tashīf (Safeguarding the Collected Fatwas against Omissions and Textual Corruptions).
> Usāma ibn al-Zahrā’ — book coordinator for al-Mawsū‘a al-Shāmila (the Comprehensive Encyclopedia).

---

## Page 4359

of individual things by reference to their directions.

For example, when it is said, “The boundary of the land on the side facing the qiblah is such-and-such, and on the eastern side such-and-such,” the land is distinguished by its name and its boundaries. The boundaries of the land are needed when there is a risk of including more or less than the name denotes. They serve to include everything within those boundaries and exclude whatever does not belong, just as the name does. The same applies to the definition of a species. This is achieved sometimes through verbal definitions and sometimes through definitions established by designation. In both cases, a definition merely explains what the name denotes and distinguishes what is defined from other things; it does not produce a conception of what is defined.

Since the purpose of a definition is to explain what a name denotes, and naming is a matter of linguistic convention, reference must be made to the intention and language of the person assigning the name. Hence the jurists say that some names have their limits determined by revealed law, while others have their limits determined by customary usage.

“Interpreting and explaining speech” also belongs here. If this means clarifying the speaker’s intention, it rests on knowing the limits of meaning in his words. If it means demonstrating and establishing the correctness of his words, it requires knowledge of evidence for their correctness.

The former involves clarifying the conception conveyed by his words, or conveying a conception of his words so as to convey, through translation, a conception of what the names denote. Sometimes this is for someone who has already conceived what is named but does not know that this is its name; at other times, it is for someone who has not yet conceived what is named. In that case, what is named is indicated as far as possible, either by pointing to the thing itself or to something analogous to it.

Hence it is said that a definition is sometimes of the name and sometimes of what is named. The leading authors who write on the art of definition according to the method of the logicians acknowledge, upon

---

## Page 4360

closer examination, that definitions serve the same kind of purpose as names and amount to rendering one expression by another. Al-Ghazālī states this in al-Mi‘yār (The Criterion), which he composed on logic; it is likewise found in the writings of Ibn Sīnā, al-Rāzī, al-Suhrawardī, and others.

Explaining unfamiliar vocabulary in the Quran, hadith, and other texts belongs to this category. Indeed, interpreting the Quran and other forms of discourse belongs, at its most basic level, to this category, since the purpose is to explain what the speaker intends by those terms and that discourse. They agree that this kind of definition is a verbal definition. Yet this is precisely what is needed when teaching the sciences set out in written works—indeed, when reading any book, and in every kind of communication. Anyone reading books on grammar, medicine, or other subjects must know what their authors intend by those terms and by the statements composed of them. The same applies to anyone reading books on jurisprudence, speculative theology, philosophy, or other subjects.

Knowing these definitions is a matter of religion with respect to every expression found in the Book of Allah Most High and the Sunnah of His Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Such knowledge may be an individual obligation or a collective obligation. Hence Allah Most High censures those who do not know these limits of meaning, saying:

“The Bedouins are stronger in disbelief and hypocrisy and more likely not to know the limits of what Allah has revealed to His Messenger.”

What He revealed to His Messenger includes terms that may be “unfamiliar” to the listener, such as dīzā (unfair), qaswarah (a lion or hunters), ‘as‘asa (to fall or recede, of night), and similar expressions. A term may also be “familiar,” yet its precise limits remain unknown, while its meaning is understood only in general terms—as with the terms prayer, zakat, fasting, and Hajj.

---

## Page 4361

Thus it is clear that a thing can be made known only through acquaintance with the thing itself or something resembling it. Whoever knows the thing itself has no need of a definition to know it. Whoever does not know it can come to know it through a definition only if he knows something resembling it, even if only in certain respects. Similar attributes shared by it and other things are then combined for him in a way that uniquely identifies the thing being defined. Whoever examines this closely will recognize its truth and understand how people know what they have been told about the unseen: the angels, the Last Day, and the various kinds of bliss and torment in Paradise and Hell. Their account of definition is thereby invalidated.

(Fifth: Simple conceptions cannot be sought and therefore cannot be acquired through definition. For if the mind is already aware of them, seeking them is impossible, since acquiring what has already been acquired is impossible. If it is unaware of them, the soul cannot seek that of which it is unaware, for seeking and intending presuppose awareness.

If it is said: “Yet a person seeks to conceive of angels, jinn, the spirit, and many other things of which he is unaware.”

The reply is: He has heard these names and seeks to conceive of what they denote, just as someone who hears expressions whose meanings he does not understand seeks to conceive of their meanings. When he conceives of what these names denote, he must also know that it is designated by the corresponding name. For if he conceived of a reality without that name being included in the conception, he would not have conceived of what he sought. Here, then, what is conceived is an entity together with the fact that it is designated by such-and-such a name. This is not a conception of the meaning alone, but of both the meaning and its name. There is no doubt that this can be sought. It does not follow, however, that the meaning taken on its own can be sought.

---

## Page 4362

Moreover, what is sought here cannot be attained through definition alone. Rather, the thing being defined must be made known by pointing to it or by some other means for which words alone do not suffice. Once it is established that simple conceptions cannot be sought, either a person already possesses them—in which case they are not acquired through definition, and definition therefore supplies no conception—or he does not possess them, in which case definition alone does not enable him to conceive of the things named when he does not know them. Whenever he has some awareness of them, he needs a definition for that awareness only in the same way that he needs a name. The point is to establish that a definition and a name serve the same purpose.

(Sixth): It may be said: According to them, what enables one to conceive of a reality is the general definition composed of essential, rather than accidental, attributes. This account rests on the distinction between the essential and the accidental. They say that the essential is what falls within the essence, while the accidental is what lies outside it. They divide the latter into what necessarily accompanies the essence and what necessarily accompanies its existence.

This account rests on two unsound principles: the distinction between an essence and its existence, and then the distinction between what is essential to it and what necessarily accompanies it.

(The first principle is their claim that an essence has an established reality outside the mind distinct from its existence. This resembles the claim that what does not exist is a thing, and is among the most untenable notions. The root of their misguidance is that they observed that a thing can be known and willed before it exists, and that a distinction can be drawn between what is within one’s power and what is beyond it, and so forth. They therefore said: “If it were not established, this would not be possible.” Likewise, we speak of the realities of

---

## Page 4363

things—that is, their essences—without regard to their existence outside the mind. The mistaken thinker therefore imagined that these realities and essences had an established reality outside the mind.

The truth is that all of this is established in the mind. What can be conceived in the mind is broader than what actually exists outside it. It exists and is established in the mind; it is not, in fact, devoid of both existence and established reality. Thus, distinguishing between existence and established reality, or between existence and essence, while claiming that both are outside the mind, is a grave error.

These people supposed that the realities of species, such as the reality of the human being, the horse, and the like, were established outside the mind, distinct from the individual entities existing there, and that they were without beginning and incapable of change. These are what are called “Platonic Forms.” Nor did they stop there: they also asserted the same concerning matter, essence, and place. They posited matter stripped of forms, established outside the mind: this is the prime matter on which they based their doctrine of the eternity of the world. The majority of rational thinkers judged them mistaken on this point.

The views of those who distinguish between existence and essence are discussed at length elsewhere. The purpose here is to draw attention to the fact that the distinction they make in logic between an essence and its existence outside the mind rests on this unsound principle. The proper distinction is that essence is the impression of a thing formed in the mind, whereas existence is what there is of that thing outside the mind,

---

## Page 4364

and this is a sound distinction. The distinction between what is in the mind and what is outside it is established, known, and beyond doubt. To posit a reality that is established neither in knowledge nor in existence, however, is invalid.

The “second principle”—the distinction between what necessarily accompanies an essence and what is essential to it—has no basis in reality. For if an essence outside the mind is taken to be stripped of its inseparable attributes, then existence outside the mind can likewise be taken to be stripped of these inseparable attributes. If, on the other hand, this is taken to be the essence itself together with its inseparable attributes, that is equivalent to saying: this is “existence together with its inseparable attributes.” Both are invalid. Evenness and oddness in a number, for example, are like being an animal and being rational in a human being. In either case, when the subject is brought to mind together with its attribute, the subject cannot be conceived without that attribute.

Their claim that what they classify as essential takes precedence in its mental representation is invalid on two grounds:

(The first is that this merely describes their own convention: they place one thing first in their minds and another later. This is nothing more than a judgment on their part. Anyone who gives this precedence over that is merely following them in doing so. It is well known that external realities, which are independent of us, do not depend on our conceptions. Our supposing one thing to come first and another later does not make them so outside the mind. Other people who follow them on this point do not have this precedence in mind

---

## Page 4365

or the corresponding posteriority. If this were innate, our innate disposition would apprehend it without imitation, just as it apprehends other innate matters. What our innate disposition recognizes is that all these inseparable attributes belong inseparably to the subject; they may or may not occur to the mind. But to claim that one lies outside the essence while another is included within it is sheer arbitrariness, supported neither by external reality nor by our innate disposition.

The second ground:

Whether an attribute is essential to its subject is determined by the reality that makes that subject what it is, whether our minds conceive it or not. Thus, if one of two attributes is essential while the other is not, the difference between them must arise from their external reality, which exists independently of the mind. For there to be something among external realities whose reality consists solely in precedence or posteriority in the mind would be possible only if reality and essence were what is posited in the mind, rather than what exists outside it. That, however, depends on what the individual mind supposes. The substance of this argument would then amount to things posited in minds that have no external reality: groundless imaginings and suppositions. This is common in their fundamental principles.

Seventh:

It may be asked: For a definition to be complete and to yield a conception of a thing’s reality, do they require that all its essential attributes shared with other things be conceived, or not? If they do, they must include all the attributes. If they do not, and deem the proximate genus alone sufficient, this is sheer arbitrariness. If they are challenged by someone who requires the mention of all

---

## Page 4366

genera, or omits all genera, they have no answer except that this is their own stipulation and convention. Yet genuine knowledge clearly does not vary with differing conventions. It has thus become evident that what they have described belongs to the realm of stipulation and convention, although they have presented it as essential realities and knowledge. This is the very essence of going astray and leading others astray. It is like someone taking two identical individuals and declaring one a believer and the other a disbeliever, one knowledgeable and the other ignorant, one blessed and the other wretched—not because of any difference between the two themselves, but merely by his own stipulation and convention. Thus, despite their claim to use rational inference, they distinguish between like things and equate unlike things.

(Eighth:

Their requirement to state the distinguishing differentiae, while maintaining their distinction between the essential and the accidental, is impossible to fulfil. For any distinguishing attribute that is among the properties of the thing defined and is coextensive with it can be treated by someone else as an accidental attribute inseparable from its essence.

(Ninth:

Their account involves circularity and is therefore invalid.

They say:

“The thing defined cannot be conceived unless its essential attributes are stated.”

Then they say:

“An essential attribute is one that must be conceived in order for the essence to be conceived.”

Thus, if the learner cannot conceive the thing defined until he conceives its essential attributes, and cannot know that an attribute is essential until he conceives the subject possessing it—which is the thing defined—and cannot conceive that subject until he conceives its essential attributes and distinguishes them from other attributes, then knowledge of the essence depends on knowledge of

---

## Page 4367

its essential attributes, while knowledge of the essential attributes depends on knowledge of the essence. Thus, neither the essence nor its essential attributes can be known. This is a compelling argument that demolishes the very foundation of their position. It shows that their stipulations are arbitrary, not founded on any basis of knowledge corresponding to realities.

Rather, they have declared, “This is essential, and this is non-essential,” purely by arbitrary stipulation. They have relied on nothing by which the essential can be distinguished from the non-essential. If the thing defined can be known only through its definition, yet that definition is impossible, then the thing itself cannot be known. This is false.

Tenth:

Disputes arise among them in this field that cannot be resolved on this basis. Whatever entails an equal balance of opposing proofs is false.

Section

Their statement, “No propositional judgment can be known except through the syllogism”—the one whose form and matter they have described—is a negative proposition that is not self-evidently known. They have offered no proof whatsoever for it. They have thus asserted what they have not established and spoken without knowledge. Indeed, knowledge of this negation is unattainable on their own principles. How, then, do they know that no human being can know any propositional judgment that they do not regard as self-evident except through the logical syllogism based on subsumption whose matter and form they have described?

---

## Page 4368

Moreover, they acknowledge what must necessarily be admitted: some propositional judgments are self-evident, while others are inferential. They cannot all be inferential, since inferential knowledge depends on what is self-evident. The point previously made concerning conceptions therefore applies here: the distinction between the two is relative, depending on the person. What requires inference for one person may be self-evident to another.

A self-evident propositional judgment is one in which conceiving its two terms—its subject and predicate—is sufficient to assent to it. It does not depend on anything mediating between them, namely, the proof constituted by the middle term. This holds whether or not the conceptions of the two terms are themselves self-evident. It is well known that people differ in mental capacity far more than they differ in physical strength. Some attain such speed and precision in forming conceptions that they far surpass others. Such a person may conceive both terms so fully that this complete conception makes implications apparent which remain unclear to someone who has not conceived them in this way.

It is evident that, for certain propositions, some people may need the mediating term that constitutes the proof, while others do not. Many people know a proposition through sensory perception, experience, demonstration, or mass transmission, whereas someone else comes to know it only through reasoning and inference. Thus, many people need proof that the predicate holds of the subject not for themselves, but for others. They explain this to others using proofs they themselves do not need, even providing illustrative examples.

---

## Page 4369

The logicians have stated that propositions known through mass transmission, experience, and the senses are confined to those who know them and do not constitute proof against anyone else, unlike other propositions, which are shared and may be used as proof against an opponent. This distinction is false and is one of the foundations of irreligion and disbelief. For, concerning miracles and other matters transmitted from the prophets through mass transmission, one of these people says, on the basis of this distinction: “This has not reached me through mass transmission, so it constitutes no binding proof against me.” Yet that is not a prerequisite.

This also includes the denial, by many adherents of religious innovation, speculative theology, and philosophy, of prophetic reports known to hadith scholars. They say, “These are not known to us,” just as some disbelievers say that the prophets’ miracles are not known to them. This is because they have not become acquainted with the means that necessarily yield knowledge of these matters. Nevertheless, the proof is binding upon them, whether or not these reports have reached them through mass transmission.

The philosophers who practise logic have embraced ignorant notions, such as their claim that the angels are the ten intellects, that they are eternal and without beginning, and that the intellect is the lord of everything other than itself. No Jew, Christian, or Arab polytheist has ever said anything like this. None has ever said that an angel is the lord of the entire world. They also say that the active intellect is the originator of everything beneath the lunar sphere. This, too, is disbelief that none of the disbelievers among the People of the Book or the Arab polytheists has ever reached.

They also say that the Lord does not act by His will and power, does not know particulars, and cannot change the world; rather, the world is an emanation that has flowed from Him without His will, power, or knowledge

---

## Page 4370

and that when someone seeking intercession turns to one of the higher substances he venerates—such as the intellects, souls, stars, the sun, or the moon—he establishes a connection with that venerated being whose intercession he seeks. Whatever emanates from the Lord upon that being then flows upon the petitioner through his intercessor. They liken this to the sun shining upon a mirror: the light falling upon the mirror is reflected onto another place, illuminating it. That place receives the light by facing the mirror, while the mirror receives it by facing the sun.

They also say that the angels are the ten intellects, or the good faculties within the soul, and that the devils are its evil faculties, along with other claims whose falsity is established by rational proofs—indeed, is necessarily known from the Messenger’s religion. If their polytheism and disbelief are greater than those of the Arab polytheists, what perfection can the soul attain through such ignorant notions? This and similar matters require extensive elaboration. The purpose here is to discuss their claims concerning logical demonstration.

Furthermore, if they say that knowledge can be acquired only through demonstration, which they regard as a universal syllogism that must contain a universal affirmative proposition (hence their assertion that no conclusion follows from two negative premises or two particular premises in any type of syllogism, whether classified by form—categorical, conjunctive conditional, or disjunctive conditional—or by content—demonstrative, rhetorical, dialectical, or even poetic),

---

## Page 4371

then the response is:

If everything they call a demonstration must contain a universal proposition, that universal proposition must be known—that is, its universality must be known. Otherwise, whenever it is admitted that it might be particular rather than universal, knowledge of what follows from it cannot be attained. An unquantified or unrestricted proposition whose wording permits either a universal or a particular interpretation has the force of a particular proposition.

If the knowledge obtained through the syllogism to which they reserve the name “demonstration” necessarily requires knowledge of a universal affirmative proposition, the response is this: if knowledge of that proposition is self-evident, then knowledge of each particular instance falling under it can, with even greater reason, be self-evident. If it is inferential, it requires self-evident knowledge, leading to intractable circularity or [an infinite regress in mass-transmitted reports] (*), both of which are invalid.

The same applies to all the other universal propositions they make the starting points of demonstration and call “those that must be accepted.” These may be propositions derived from external or internal sensation—the latter being what a person perceives within himself—or propositions based on experience, mass-transmitted reports, or intuition, according to those who count some intuitive propositions among the soul’s apprehensions that must be accepted. An example is knowing that the moon derives its light from the sun by observing how its phases change as its alignment with the sun changes: its appearance when it is close to the sun after conjunction, as on the night of the new crescent, differs from its appearance on the night of opposition, when it is full.

They disagree, however, over whether intuition can yield certainty.

The same applies to purely rational propositions, such as our statements that one is half of two and that the whole is greater than the part

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 78):
> “Al-tasalsul fī al-mutawattirāt” is a textual corruption. The correct reading is “al-tasalsul fī al-mu’aththirāt” (infinite regress of causes), as is well known and as found in the original text, al-Radd ‘alā al-mantiqiyyīn (Refutation of the Logicians), 1:120.

---

## Page 4372

and that things equal to the same thing are equal to one another, that two contraries cannot coexist, and that two contradictories can neither both be false nor both be true. There is no universal proposition of this kind used as a premise in a demonstration whose conclusion cannot be known without that demonstration as an intermediary. Indeed, this frequently occurs.

Thus, if it is known that every unit is half of any two units, and that half of any two units is one unit, then it is known that this particular unit is half of these particular two units, without inferring this from the universal proposition. The same applies to all the other propositions. Likewise, with any part, one knows that this particular whole is greater than its part without recourse to the universal proposition. Similarly, whoever conceives of two particular contradictories as contradictories knows that they cannot coexist.

Everyone knows that this particular thing cannot both exist and not exist, just as he knows this of another particular thing. This does not require an inference from the proposition that nothing can both exist and not exist. The same applies to contraries: a person knows that this thing cannot be both black and white, or both moving and at rest, just as he knows the same of another thing. To know this, he does not need a universal proposition stating that nothing can be both black and white, or both moving and at rest.

The same applies to every other pair known to be contraries: if one knows that two particular things are contraries, one knows that they cannot coexist. Knowledge of the universal proposition yields knowledge of the major premise, which contains the major term. Yet this does not suffice without knowledge of the minor premise, which contains the minor term. Knowledge of the conclusion—that these two notions are contraries and therefore cannot coexist—is possible without knowledge of

---

## Page 4373

the major premise—namely, that no two contraries can coexist. Knowledge of this therefore does not require the syllogism to which they have reserved the name “demonstration.” Yet in the speech of Allah and His Messenger, and in the discourse of scholars of all kinds, demonstration is not restricted to what they call demonstration. Rather, they themselves have restricted the term to what is contained in a syllogism whose form and matter they have limited in the manner they describe.

For example, suppose one wishes to refute those who affirm states and say that they are neither existent nor nonexistent. One might argue: “These are two contradictories, and two contradictories can neither both be true nor both be false. This makes one and the same thing neither existent nor nonexistent, and the state of a single thing cannot be regarded as neither existent nor nonexistent.” It is nevertheless possible to know that this particular thing cannot be both existent and nonexistent without knowing this universal proposition. Knowledge of the conclusion therefore does not require demonstration.

Likewise, one might say: “This is contingent, and every contingent thing must have a factor determining its existence rather than its nonexistence, according to the sounder of the two views—or determining one of its two alternatives, according to some people.”

Or one might say: “This has come into existence, and whatever has come into existence must have an originator.” That universal proposition—namely, our statement that everything that has come into existence must have an originator, and that every contingent thing must have a determining factor—has particular instances which they seek to establish through demonstrative syllogism, although these can be known without knowledge of

---

## Page 4374

the universal proposition without which, according to them, demonstration cannot be complete. Thus, one can know that this occurrence must have an originator and that this contingent thing must have a determining factor. If his reason doubts this and allows that he himself could come into being without an originator who brought him into being, or could exist—despite being contingent, admitting both existence and nonexistence—without a factor determining his existence, then the same possibility would apply all the more readily to other originated and contingent things. But if he is certain of this in his own case, his knowledge of the particular conclusion—namely, “This has come into existence, so it has an originator,” or “This is contingent, so it has a determining factor”—does not require a demonstrative syllogism.

This is further clarified by the following: you will find no human being seeking to establish a conclusion through reasoning and to prove it by a demonstrative syllogism whose validity he knows who could not know that conclusion without that logical demonstrative syllogism. Hence, among all other kinds of rational people, you will find no one who constructs his argument from two premises as these people do. Rather, they state the evidence that entails the conclusion. That evidence may consist of one premise, two premises, or three premises, according to the needs of the person reasoning and drawing the inference, for people’s needs differ. We have discussed this at length in our discussion of al-Muhassal.

We have also explained how the majority of rational people judge mistaken those who maintain that every instance of knowledge acquired through reasoning necessarily requires two premises, neither of which can be dispensed with, and that no more than two are needed. You should examine this using rational subject matter that is not established by the texts of the prophets, for this exposes the invalidity of their logic. If, however, you use subject matter known through the texts of the prophets, the need for the universal proposition becomes apparent, as when we wish to establish the prohibition of

---

## Page 4375

the nabīdh (a steeped drink) whose ruling is disputed, saying: “Nabīdh is intoxicating, and every intoxicant is forbidden,” or: “It is wine, and all wine is forbidden.”

Our statement, “Intoxicating nabīdh is wine,” is known from the revealed text, namely the Prophet’s statement, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Every intoxicant is wine.”

Our statement, “All wine is forbidden,” is known from the revealed texts and scholarly consensus. There is no dispute about this; the dispute concerns only the minor premise. It is established in Sahīh Muslim that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Every intoxicant is wine, and all wine is forbidden.”

Another wording reads:

“Every intoxicant is wine, and every intoxicant is forbidden.”

Some people may suppose that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, stated this in the form prescribed by logic, to demonstrate the conclusion through two premises, as logicians do. Such a supposition betrays great ignorance. His standing, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is far above employing such a method to impart knowledge. Indeed, even those inferior in reason and knowledge to individual scholars of his community would not accept following the method of these logicians. Rather, they regard them as ignorant people proficient only in practical disciplines such as arithmetic, medicine, and the like. As for the universal demonstrative sciences that yield certainty, and the sciences concerning God, they were not qualified practitioners of them. Muslim scholars of rational inquiry have explained this in their books and discussed these people at length.

The point is that Muslims already know that all wine is forbidden, so they have no need to learn this through a syllogism. Some of them were uncertain only about particular kinds of intoxicating drinks, such as nabīdh made from honey, grains, and other ingredients, as reported in the two Sahīh collections from Abū Mūsā al-Ash'arī

---

## Page 4376

that he said to the Messenger of Allah, may Allah bless him and grant him peace: “We have a drink made from honey called al-bit', and a drink made from sorghum called al-mizr.” He related that the Prophet—who had been endowed with concise, comprehensive speech—replied:

“Every intoxicant is forbidden.”

Thus, he answered them, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, with a universal proposition explaining that everything that intoxicates is forbidden. He also explained that everything that intoxicates is wine. These are two true, concordant universal propositions. Knowing either one entails knowing that every intoxicant is forbidden; knowing that every intoxicant is forbidden does not depend on knowing both propositions together.

For anyone who believes in the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and knows that he said, “Every intoxicant is forbidden,” knows that intoxicating nabīdh is forbidden. Doubt may nevertheless arise over whether he meant an intoxicating quantity or intoxicants as a category. This is uncertainty about the meaning of his statement. Once the person understands what he intended, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, he knows the conclusion sought. The same applies if he knows that nabīdh is wine. Knowing this establishes its prohibition even more firmly, for those who permit the disputed nabīdh do not call it wine. Once a person knows from the revealed text that “Every intoxicant is wine,” this alone constitutes proof that every intoxicant is forbidden for believers who know that wine is forbidden.

As for someone who does not know that wine is forbidden because he does not believe in the Messenger, the Prophet’s words cannot serve as proof for him. If, however, he knows that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah but does not know that he prohibited wine, the statement “Every intoxicant is wine” does not benefit him. Rather, what benefits him is the statement “Every intoxicant is forbidden.” Through this, he then knows that wine is forbidden, because wine and intoxicant are two names for the same thing according to

---

## Page 4377

the Lawgiver. According to the majority of scholars, who prohibit every intoxicant, the two terms are coextensive in his usage, in both their general and particular applications. The purpose here is not to establish “the legal question,” but to draw attention to the example, for Muslim scholars who have written on logic frequently use it.

Logicians give examples using a form stripped of its subject matter, signifying nothing specific, so that knowledge of the example is not derived from a concrete instance. Thus they say:

Every A is B, and every B is C.
Therefore, every A is C.

Yet the aim is to obtain the knowledge sought concerning specific subject matter. When that subject matter is removed, someone may suppose that this procedure is necessary in particular cases. This is not so. Rather, when they are asked to establish knowledge of the two universal premises in all their rational inquiries not derived from those protected from error, you find them appealing to evidence that also makes it possible to know the particulars under investigation without knowing the universal proposition. Knowledge of those particulars therefore does not depend on demonstration.

Propositions conveyed by the prophets do not require the rational syllogism that they call demonstration. Nor does knowledge acquired through reason require their demonstrative syllogism. It is therefore needed neither in matters known through revelation nor in those known through reason. Hence, it cannot be maintained that knowledge is acquired only through the demonstrative syllogism they describe.

A further clarification is that propositions based on sense perception can only be particular. Through our senses, we have perceived only the burning caused by this fire and that fire; we have not perceived that every fire burns. If we turn this into a universal proposition and say, “Every fire burns,” we would have no way

---

## Page 4378

of knowing with certainty that this universal proposition is true without that same knowledge being all the more accessible in particular instances.

If it is said:

“Knowledge of particular things is not what is intended, for demonstration yields only knowledge of a universal proposition. Conclusions known through demonstration can therefore only be universal, as they themselves maintain. Universals, however, are universal only in the mind, not in external reality.”

The reply is:

On this assumption, demonstration yields no knowledge of anything that exists, but only of things conceived in the mind whose realization in external reality is unknown. If demonstration provides no knowledge of anything that exists, it is of very little benefit—indeed, of no benefit at all. Yet they do not maintain this; rather, they use it to acquire knowledge of external and metaphysical realities. The truth of the matter, however, as we have explained elsewhere, is that inquiries in natural philosophy which concern not necessary universals but generalizations holding for the most part do not achieve the intended purpose of demonstration.

As for “metaphysics,” their universal propositions in this field are even more unsound than those of natural philosophy. Most of their discourse concerning it consists of false conjectures, far from being true propositions from which a demonstration could be constructed. Thus, we have been told through an unbroken chain of transmission that al-Khūnajī, the distinguished logician of his age and author of Kashf Asrār al-Mantiq (Unveiling the Secrets of Logic), al-Mūjaz (The Epitome), and other works, said as he lay dying: “I am dying, yet I have not come to know

---

## Page 4379

anything except that what is contingent requires a cause.”

Then he said:

“Dependence is a negative attribute, so I am dying without having come to know anything.”

They also related a similar account to us about another of their distinguished scholars. Anyone familiar with them knows this, and knows that they are the most ignorant people on earth concerning the methods by which rational and transmitted knowledge are acquired—except those among them who have acquired knowledge through methods other than those of logic. Their knowledge comes from those other sources, not from their logical methods, despite all their toil over the demonstration by which they claim to assess knowledge. Whatever knowledge any of them has acquired did not come through what they have elaborated in logic.

Another point showing that certain knowledge, whether universal or particular, does not depend on their demonstration based on a universal proposition is that knowledge of that universal proposition must itself have a source. If they know it by inferring the unobserved from the observed, on the principle that the same judgment applies to a thing and to its like—as when we know that this fire burns, and therefore that an unobserved fire burns because it is like this one, and the same judgment applies to a thing and to its like—then the reply is: this is reasoning by analogy, which they claim yields not certainty but only conjecture. If, therefore, they have come to know the universal proposition only through analogy, they have based their certainty on what they themselves say yields only conjecture.

If they say:

“Rather, when particulars are perceived through the senses, universal knowledge arises in the soul from the Giver of Intellect—or, upon perceiving particulars through the senses, the soul becomes disposed to receive the universal emanating upon it from the Giver of Intellect.”

Or if they say that it comes from what they call the “Active Intellect,” or something similar, they are told: the question here is how it is known that the universal judgment in the soul constitutes knowledge, rather than conjecture or ignorance.

---

## Page 4380

If they say, “This knowledge is self-evident or necessary,” this amounts to saying that these universal propositions are known self-evidently and necessarily, and that the soul cannot help knowing them. If this is true, then knowledge of particular entities and the various kinds of universals also arises in the soul self-evidently and necessarily—as indeed happens. For rational people are more certain about individual objects of sense perception than about universals, and more certain about universal propositions concerning species than those concerning genera. Knowledge of particulars reaches the natural disposition first, so its certainty about them is stronger.

As the intellect grows stronger, the scope of its universals broadens. Accordingly, one cannot say that knowledge of individuals depends on knowledge of species and genera, or that knowledge of species depends on knowledge of genera. Rather, a person may know that he himself is capable of sensation and voluntary movement before knowing that every human being is so; and he may know that human beings are so before knowing that every animal is so. Thus, his knowledge that other animals are capable of sensation and voluntary movement does not depend on demonstration.

When he knows that this judgment applies to all other people and animals, the soul arrives at that judgment through knowing that the unobserved is like the observed, or that it shares with it the cause that necessarily entails its capacity for sensation and voluntary movement, and through similar forms of analogical and causal reasoning used by jurists to establish rulings of Islamic law. Yet these people claim that such reasoning yields only conjecture, whereas their own syllogism yields certainty. We have explained elsewhere that this claim of theirs is among the most untenable of claims, and that

---

## Page 4381

analogical reasoning and syllogistic reasoning are equivalent, differing only in the particular material employed. If that material yields certainty in one, it yields certainty in the other; if it yields conjecture in one, it yields conjecture in the other. Syllogistic reasoning consists of three terms: the minor, the middle, and the major. Its middle term is what, in analogical reasoning, is called the effective cause, the basis of the ruling, and the common attribute.

Thus, concerning nabīdh, someone may say: “Every nabīdh is intoxicating, and every intoxicant is forbidden.” He must establish the major premise; only then is the demonstration complete. He can then say: “Nabīdh is intoxicating, so it is forbidden by analogy with grape wine, because both share the property of causing intoxication.” Intoxication is the basis of the prohibition in the original case, and it is also present in the case under consideration. The same evidence that establishes that every intoxicant is forbidden establishes, with even greater force, that intoxication is the basis of the prohibition. Indeed, making distinctions in analogical reasoning is easier for him, since the original case provides evidence for prohibition, and the ruling is therefore already known to hold in some particular instances.

In analogical reasoning, it is not enough to establish the ruling in one of the two cases on the grounds that it holds in the other merely because they share an attribute for which no evidence has established that it entails the ruling—as some mistakenly suppose. Rather, it must be known that their shared attribute entails the ruling; and this shared attribute is the middle term. Jurists and scholars of the principles of jurisprudence call this “the demand to establish the attribute’s effect on the ruling.” This is the most significant objection raised against an analogy, and answering it is generally what is needed

---

## Page 4382

in assessing the validity of the analogy. An objector may deny that the attribute exists in the original case, deny the ruling in the original case, deny that the attribute exists in the case under consideration, or deny that the attribute is the effective cause of the ruling. He may say:

“We do not grant that the shared attribute you have mentioned is the effective cause or an indication of that cause.”

There must therefore be evidence establishing this: a text, consensus, enumeration and elimination, suitability, or concomitance—where such reasoning is accepted as evidence. Whatever establishes that the shared attribute entails the ruling, whether as its effective cause or as an indication of that cause, also establishes that the middle term entails the major term, and thus establishes the validity of the major premise. If it establishes the effective cause, it is a causal demonstration; if it establishes an indication of that cause, it is a demonstration from an indication. If it yields only conjecture rather than certain knowledge, then the major premise in that syllogism likewise remains merely conjectural. This is clear. Hence many jurists have come to employ syllogistic reasoning in jurisprudence, just as analogical reasoning is employed in rational inquiries. The essential nature of the one is the same as that of the other.

Some later proponents of speculative theology and reasoned opinion, such as Abū al-Maʿālī, Abū Hāmid, al-Rāzī, Abū Muhammad al-Maqdisī, and others, maintain that analogy has no place in rational matters, but applies only to matters of religious law; in rational matters, however, reliance is on evidence that establishes the conclusion, without restriction. Their position contradicts that of Muslim rational thinkers—indeed, that of all other people of reason. For analogy is used as evidence in rational matters just as it is in matters of religious law. Once it is established that the shared attribute entails the ruling, this is

---

## Page 4383

evidence in every field of knowledge. Likewise, if it is established that there is no relevant difference between the case under consideration and the original case, this constitutes evidence in every field of knowledge. Wherever analogical reasoning cannot serve as evidence, neither can syllogistic reasoning. Abū al-Ma'ālī and the speculative thinkers before him neither follow nor approve of the logicians’ method. Rather, they reason from evidence which, in their view, entails its conclusions.

Nevertheless, the logicians and most speculative thinkers draw analogies between the unobserved and the observed whenever the shared attribute entails the ruling. Their examples include linking the two through a common definition, cause, condition, or evidence. Their opponents say:

“The ruling is not established for the unobserved because it is established for the observed. Rather, the universal proposition itself suffices to establish the point, without any need for analogy.”

The answer to them is:

“The same applies in matters of religious law. Whenever evidence establishes that the ruling is tied to the shared attribute, there is no need for the original case. The evidence establishing that the ruling is tied to that attribute is itself sufficient. Yet because this is universal, and a universal exists only in particular instances, identifying the original case provides a means of knowing that this universal is actually instantiated. This is useful in both legal and rational matters.”

Thus you know that whenever evidence establishes that the shared attribute is the basis of the ruling, or that the difference between the original case and the case under consideration is irrelevant, the analogy is sound and constitutes valid evidence, whatever its subject.

People have differed over what the term “qiyās” denotes. One group of scholars of legal theory, including Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī and Abū Hāmid al-Maqdisī, maintains that it applies literally to analogical reasoning and figuratively to syllogistic reasoning. Another group maintains the reverse: it applies literally to syllogistic reasoning and figuratively to analogical reasoning—

---

## Page 4384

as maintained by Ibn Hazm and others. The majority of scholars, however, hold that the term applies literally to both, and that rational inference encompasses them both. This is the position of most who have written on the foundations of religion, legal theory, and the various rational sciences. It is the correct view, for the essential nature of the two is the same; only the form of inference differs.

In its linguistic sense, “qiyās” means measuring one thing against another. This includes measuring a particular thing against another particular like it, and measuring it against a universal that encompasses both it and others like it. For a universal is a mental representation of its particulars; hence it corresponds to and agrees with the particular.

“Syllogistic reasoning” is the mind’s passage from a particular to the general, shared universal concept encompassing both it and other particulars, and its judging that particular according to what necessarily follows from the shared universal. It does so by passing from that necessarily entailed universal back to the original particular that entails it. Thus, it is a passage from the specific to the general, then from that general back to the specific: from a particular to a universal, then from that universal back to the original particular, of which that universal is then predicated.

For this reason, the evidence is more specific than what it establishes, namely the judgment. The existence of the evidence necessarily entails the existence of the judgment, and what is entailed cannot be more specific than what entails it. Rather, it must be either more general or coextensive with it. This is what is meant by its being more general.

---

## Page 4385

As for what is indicated in the sense of the subject of the judgment—the thing judged, spoken of, described, or taken as the subject—it is either narrower in scope than the evidence or coextensive with it. It may thus be described as narrower than the evidence; it cannot be broader. For if it were broader, and the evidence did not necessarily follow from it, the judgment could not be known to apply to it, and the evidence would not constitute evidence. It constitutes evidence only when it necessarily follows from the thing judged, described, and spoken of—called the subject or grammatical subject—and itself entails the judgment, which is an attribute, a statement about the subject, and a judgment, and is called the predicate or grammatical predicate.

An example is intoxication, which is broader in scope than the disputed drink, nabīdh, but narrower than prohibition. The evidence may also be coextensive with the judgment while necessarily following from the subject of that judgment. This, then, is what gives it its evidentiary force, whether it is formulated as syllogistic or analogical reasoning or is not formulated in either way. This is something the mind understands even when the tongue does not express it. Hence human minds infer conclusions from evidence even when people do not express this in words that explain what is in their minds. They may also express their meanings clearly without adopting the terminology of any particular group of speculative theologians, logicians, or others. Knowledge of that which entails the conclusion must therefore be self-evident or established by further evidence.

As for “analogical reasoning,” it is the mind’s passage from one particular judgment to another because they share a common universal attribute, for that judgment necessarily follows from the shared universal. If this necessary connection is not self-evident, knowledge of it must have some basis, as explained earlier. The mind first conceives of the two particulars—the original case and the new case—and then proceeds to what both entail

---

## Page 4386

, namely the shared attribute, and then to what that attribute in turn entails, namely the judgment. It must be known that the judgment necessarily follows from the shared attribute; this is what is called the major premise in syllogistic reasoning. The mind then proceeds to establish this consequence for the original particular that entails it. The two forms of reasoning are thus essentially identical. They differ only in how the evidence is presented and arranged. Otherwise, the reality that makes it evidence—its necessarily entailing the conclusion—is one and the same.

Among the injustices and signs of ignorance of these people is that they illustrate analogical reasoning with the statement:

“The heavens are composite and must therefore have come into being, by analogy with a human being.”

They then raise objections specific to this particular argument. Yet if one were to say:

“The heavens are composite, and everything composite has come into being,”

the same objections, and others besides, would arise. But when syllogistic reasoning deals with evident subject matter, there is no difference between it and analogical reasoning. A universal is a mental exemplar of its particulars, and therefore corresponds and conforms to them. Indeed, analogical reasoning may be clearer. This is why rational people employ it in their reasoning.

Likewise, their assertion that a definition cannot be obtained through an example concerns only an example by which the thing defined is distinguished from everything else, so that one recognizes what invariably accompanies it in both directions—being present wherever it is present and absent wherever it is absent. For a definition that distinguishes the thing defined is one by which a concomitant exactly coextensive with it is known. Whenever this is achieved, the thing defined has been distinguished from everything else. This is what constitutes a definition according to the great majority of theoretical thinkers, who do not permit the inclusion of the general genus in a definition.

Thus, if the aim is a nominal definition, and a non-Arab asks what the word “bread” denotes, then is shown a loaf and told, “This,” he may understand that this is a term that includes

---

## Page 4387

everything that is bread, whether it has the shape of a loaf or some other shape.

What they and the logicians have said has been discussed at length in the treatment of al-Muhassal and elsewhere. This can be seen in abstract examples. If the aim is to establish C of A, and the middle term is B, then the statements:

“All A is B, and all B is C”

yield the conclusion:

“All A is C.”

Likewise, suppose one says:

“All A is C, by analogy with D, because D is C, and it is C only because it is B. A is also B, so A is C, since both share what entails C, namely B.”

This is sound and equivalent in meaning to the first argument, but it adds an example to which A is compared, even though the middle term, B, is already present in A.

If it is objected:

“What you have stated—that a demonstration must contain a universal proposition—is correct. This is why they use it to establish only a universal conclusion. They say that demonstration yields knowledge only of universals, and that the noblest universals are purely intellectual ones that admit neither change nor alteration. Through these the soul attains perfection, becoming an intelligible world corresponding to the world that exists, unlike propositions that change and vary. And if what is sought through demonstration is intellectual universals that admit neither alteration nor change,

---

## Page 4388

then these can only be attained through rational propositions that must be accepted; indeed, they occur only in propositions whose modality is necessity, such as “Every human being is an animal” and “Every existent is either necessary or contingent,” and other universal propositions that admit no change.

For this reason, the sciences are ‘three’: one concerns what cannot be abstracted from matter, either in the mind or in external reality. This is ‘physics’, whose subject is body. Another concerns what is abstracted from matter in the mind but not in external reality. This is ‘mathematics’, which discusses magnitude and number. The remaining science concerns what is abstracted from matter. This is ‘metaphysics’, whose subject is absolute existence and the attributes that belong to it insofar as it is existence, such as its division into necessary and contingent, and into substance and accident.

Substance is divided into that which inheres in something; that which serves as a substrate; that which neither inheres in something nor serves as a substrate, but is related to these through governance; and that which neither inheres in something nor serves as a substrate, nor has such a relation to them. (The first) is form. (The second) is matter—that is, hylē, which in their language means ‘substrate’. (The third) is the soul. (The fourth) is the intellect.

(The First) is classified by most of them under the category of substance. However, some of their later thinkers, such as Ibn Sīnā, refused to call it a substance. They said that substance is that which, if it exists, exists not in a subject—that is, not in a substrate independent of what inheres in it. This applies only to that whose existence differs from its essence. The First is not like this, and therefore is not a substance. This is one of the matters in which

---

## Page 4389

they departed from their predecessors and disputed with them in a merely verbal disagreement, without offering any valid, intelligible distinction. Restricting the term “substance” to what they have described is a matter of convention. Their predecessors say, rather, that it is anything that does not exist in a subject, just as the speculative theologians define it as anything that subsists independently, occupies space, possesses attributes, or bears accidents, and so forth.

As for a substantive distinction, their claim that the existence of contingent beings is additional to their essences in external reality is false. Their claim that the First is existence qualified by negations is likewise false, as explained at length elsewhere. Our purpose here, however, is to discuss demonstration.

The response is this: although various groups have been led astray by this discourse, it is merely embellished rhetoric, containing more falsehood than could be briefly described. Here, however, we shall draw attention to some of its flaws from several perspectives:

(First: If demonstration yields only knowledge of universals, and universals are realized only in minds, not in external particulars, while nothing exists externally except particular beings, then demonstration yields no knowledge of any particular. Thus, it yields no knowledge of any existent whatsoever, but only of things posited in the mind. It is well known that, even supposing the soul’s perfection lay in knowledge alone—although this proposition is false, as explained at length elsewhere—this is not knowledge through which the soul attains perfection, since it has learned nothing of

---

## Page 4390

existent things, nor has it become an intelligible world corresponding to the existing world. Rather, it has become a world of posited universals through which nothing of the existing world is known. What good is there in this, let alone perfection?

Second: The noblest of existent beings is the “Necessary Existent,” whose existence is particular, not universal. Conceiving a universal does not preclude its being shared by many, whereas conceiving the Necessary Existent precludes such sharing. If, however, one does not know of Him that which, when conceived, precludes such sharing, but knows only a universal feature common to Him and others, then one has not known the Necessary Existent.

The same applies to what they call “intellectual substances”: the ten intellects—or more, according to those among them who hold that there are more, such as al-Suhrawardī, who was put to death, Abū al-Barakāt, and others. These are all particular substances, not universals. Thus, if we know only universals, we know none of them.

Likewise, the celestial spheres, which they maintain have neither beginning nor end, would remain unknown if we knew only universals. We would therefore know neither the Necessary Existent, nor the intellects, nor any of the souls, nor the celestial spheres, nor the elements, nor generated beings. Yet these comprise all existent things according to them. What knowledge is there here through which the soul attains perfection?

Third: Their division of the sciences into natural science, mathematics, and metaphysics, and their ranking of mathematics above natural science and metaphysics above mathematics, are among the ways in which they have turned realities upside down. For natural science—which is knowledge of bodies existing in external reality and of the principle of (1)

> **Footnotes:**
> (1) So it reads; there is a lacuna in the passage.

---

## Page 4391

their motions and their transitions from one state to another, and of the natures inherent in them—is nobler than merely conceiving abstract magnitudes and abstract numbers. For conceiving nothing but a circle, triangle, or square—even if one were to conceive everything in Euclid—or nothing but abstract numbers yields no knowledge of anything existing in external reality. This is not the perfection of the soul. Were it not intended to provide knowledge of external things that are counted and measured, namely bodies and accidents, it would not have been regarded as a science. They made geometry a preliminary to the study of astronomy only to assist them in astronomical demonstrations or to benefit from it in developing the material world. Yet their syllogistic demonstrations establish nothing with consistent certainty, free of error, except in these mathematical subjects.

For “arithmetic,” which is the science of discrete quantity, and “geometry,” which is the science of continuous quantity, constitute certain knowledge that admits no contrary whatsoever. Examples include adding, dividing, and multiplying numbers, and determining their ratios to one another. If you add one hundred to one hundred, you know that together they make two hundred. If you divide it by ten, each receives ten; and if you multiply it by ten, the product is one hundred.

Multiplication is the inverse of division. Multiplying whole numbers means repeating the units of one number as many times as there are units in the other. If the product is divided by either number, the other factor is obtained. If the quotient is multiplied by the divisor, the dividend is obtained. Thus the dividend corresponds to the product, and each of the two factors corresponds to the dividend and the divisor, and the ratio

---

## Page 4392

links all these together: the ratio of either factor to the product is the same as the ratio of one to the other factor; and the ratio of the product to either factor is the same as the ratio of the other factor to one.

These matters, and others like them discussed in arithmetic, are intelligible matters shared by all rational people. Everyone knows something of them, for such knowledge is self-evident. Hence the example they give: “One is half of two.” There is no doubt that the propositions of arithmetic are universal, must be accepted, and admit no exceptions whatsoever.

This was the starting point of their philosophy, founded by Pythagoras, whose followers were called the adherents of number. They supposed that abstract numbers existed outside the mind. Then Plato and his followers recognized the error of this view. They supposed instead that abstract essences, such as man and horse considered without qualification, existed outside the mind and had neither beginning nor end. Then Aristotle and his followers recognized the error of that view and said: “Rather, these unqualified essences exist in external reality together with individual things.” Some of Aristotle’s later followers adopted this position, but it too is mistaken. For nothing in external reality is universal at all; everything that exists externally is a particular, determinate thing.

When it is said that the natural universal exists in external reality, this means that what is universal in the mind exists externally. But when it exists externally, it can only be a determinate particular, not a universal. Its being universal is therefore conditional upon its being in the mind. And whoever posits an essence that exists neither in the mind

---

## Page 4393

nor in external reality—his claim need only be fully understood for its falsity to become evident. These matters are discussed at length elsewhere. The point here is that this science admits of valid demonstrations, yet no soul attains perfection through it, escapes punishment by it, or achieves happiness through it. Hence Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī and others said of these men’s sciences:

“They consist either of true sciences that yield no benefit—and we seek refuge in Allah from knowledge that brings no benefit—or of false conjectures that cannot be trusted—and indeed, some suspicion is sinful.”

By the former, they mean the mathematical sciences; by the latter, what these men say about metaphysics, astrology, and the like. Nevertheless, the soul may take pleasure in this, just as it takes pleasure in other things. A person enjoys learning what he did not know and hearing what he had not heard, provided he is not preoccupied with something he considers more important. Likewise, he may enjoy various activities that fall under amusement and play.

Moreover, through sustained study of these matters, the soul becomes accustomed to sound knowledge, correct and true propositions, and sound inference. This serves to refine the mind and its understanding. The soul also becomes accustomed to knowing and speaking the truth, thereby gaining assistance in attaining knowledge of a higher order. Hence it is said that the early philosophers would begin their children’s education with mathematics. Many of their masters eventually devote themselves exclusively to this, because, when they examined their methods and those of their opponents among the proponents of false speculative theology, they did not

---

## Page 4394

find any truth in them, they began to occupy themselves with mathematics, as leading philosophers such as Ibn Wāsil and others were also keen to do. Likewise, many later scholars of our school spend their leisure studying inheritance shares, arithmetic, algebra, geometry, and similar subjects, because these refresh the soul and constitute sound knowledge free of error.

It is reported that ʿUmar ibn al-Khattāb said:

“When you seek recreation, practise archery; and when you converse, discuss inheritance shares.”

The calculation of inheritance shares is a rational discipline founded on a basis prescribed by the revealed law. It thus combines intellectual exercise with preservation of the revealed law. Yet it is not a science to be pursued for its own sake, nor does the soul attain perfection through it.

Those polytheists used to worship the celestial bodies, build temples for them, and invoke them with various supplications. This is well known from accounts of them and from books composed according to their methods on polytheism, magic, the invocation of celestial bodies, and the incantations and oaths through which Iblīs and his hosts are glorified. Through their polytheism and magic, Satan would entice them with things that themselves drew them into that polytheism and magic. They would observe the celestial bodies to learn their magnitudes, the measures of their motions, and the alignments between them, using this knowledge to assist them in what they considered appropriate to those bodies.

---

## Page 4395

Since the celestial spheres are round, and their motions could not be calculated without knowledge of geometry and the properties of straight and curved lines, they studied “geometry” for this purpose and for worldly development. This is why they pursued it so extensively. Otherwise, if its only purpose had been the mere conception of numbers and magnitudes, that aim would not have warranted the effort described. Yet this may be an end in itself for some who take pleasure in it, for the pleasures of the soul are diverse. Some people take pleasure in chess, backgammon, and gambling until these distract them from what would benefit them more.

“Logic” originated in geometry. They called it “definitions,” drawing on the boundaries of those figures, so that they could pass from perceptible figures to intelligible ones. This was due to the weakness of their intellects and their inability to attain knowledge except by a circuitous route. Allah Most High has made knowledge, clear exposition, righteous deeds, and faith accessible to the Muslims, enabling them to surpass every other group of humankind. All praise belongs to Allah, Lord of all worlds.

As for “metaphysics,” which they regard as independent of matter both in the mind and in external reality, it has already become clear to you that it has no object of knowledge in external reality. Rather, it is general in scope, concerning unrestricted universals that exist as universals only in the mind. None of this contributes to the perfection of the soul.

If they know the Necessary Existent specifically, this is knowledge of a particular whose very conception precludes its applying to more than one individual. This is something that the syllogism they call “demonstration” does not establish. Their demonstration does not establish any specific particular, whether the Necessary Existent or anything else

---

## Page 4396

but establishes only a universal. The very conception of a universal does not preclude its applying to more than one individual, whereas knowledge of the Necessary Existent precludes this. Whoever has not conceived of that which cannot apply to more than one individual has not come to know Allah.

Anyone who attributes to the Lord knowledge of universals alone—as Ibn Sīnā and others like him claim—and regards this as a perfection of the Lord will, all the more, regard it as a perfection of the soul, especially if he says: “The soul perceives only universals; it is the body that perceives particulars.” This is ignorance in the extreme. These universals, through which existing particulars cannot be known, contain no perfection whatsoever. The soul desires knowledge of universals only to attain, through them, comprehensive knowledge of particulars. If this is not achieved, the soul finds no joy in that knowledge.

The fourth point:
