It may be said: Suppose that the soul is perfected through abstract universals, as they claim. What they discuss in what they consider the highest science, which examines existence and its concomitants, does not meet this description.

Merely conceiving the meaning of existence is self-evident—so much so that, in their view, its obviousness dispenses with the need for a definition. This, then, is not what is sought; rather, what is sought is its divisions. Its very divisions into necessary and contingent, substance and accident, cause and effect, beginningless and temporally originated are more specific than what the term “existence” denotes. Merely dividing a general concept in the mind into categories, without knowing those categories, does not yield any great, lofty universal knowledge beyond the conception of existence. Once you know these categories, that knowledge is not knowledge of an object that admits of neither change nor transformation. They have no evidence whatsoever establishing that the world has always been, and will always remain, as it is. And all

---

## Page 4397

the arguments they adduce for the perpetuity of the agent, agency, time, motion, and their concomitants establish only their beginninglessness and perpetuity in kind, not the beginninglessness or perpetuity of any particular thing. To assert categorically that those proofs establish this world or anything within it is sheer ignorance, with no basis beyond the lack of knowledge of anything existing other than this world. Yet the absence of knowledge is not knowledge of absence. Thus, these people have no faith in the unseen of which the prophets spoke: they believe neither in Allah, nor His angels, His Books, His messengers, nor resurrection after death.

If they say:

“We affirm the intellectual realm, or intelligible reality beyond what is perceptible to the senses, and that is the unseen,”

then this—even though some speculative theologians and philosophers have stated it—is error and misguidance. For, upon examination, the intelligible realities they affirm amount only to things postulated in the mind, not things existing in external reality. The messengers, by contrast, spoke of things that exist in external reality, whose existence is more perfect and greater than that of what we witness in this world.

What comparison can there be between the two? Since they denied what the messengers had conveyed, they said:

“The messengers intended to present the masses with imaginary representations, so that they might thereby benefit from the just order the messengers established for them.”

Some of them then say:

“The messengers knew, as we do, that these things do not exist.”

Others say:

“Rather, they did not know this; their perfection lay only in the practical faculty, not the theoretical.”

---

## Page 4398

Even the least of the messengers’ followers, once he understands the true nature of their teachings, finds them unacceptable even to the least of those followers. When he knows through rational proofs that nothing in this world can be beginningless, and knows through the prophets’ reports, supported by reason, that another world preceded it, from which it was created, and that it will undergo transformation and the Resurrection will take place, and other such matters, he realizes that even their most far-reaching universal judgments do not correspond to reality: they are ignorance, not knowledge.

Even supposing that they do not know what the messengers conveyed, reason does not establish their claim that these universal kinds in this world are beginningless and everlasting, having always existed and continuing forever. Thus, purported knowledge of this is not knowledge of unchanging universals. Most of their “first philosophy” and “higher wisdom” is of this sort.

The same applies to those who wrote according to their method, such as the author of “Eastern Investigations,” the author of “The Wisdom of Illumination,” the author of “The Subtleties of Realities” and “Symbols of Treasures,” the author of “Unveiling the Realities,” and the author of “Hidden Secrets in the Rational Sciences,” and others like them. These writers neither devoted their discourse wholly to defending the philosophers’ doctrine nor escaped altogether from the entanglements of their error. Rather, they shared much of their error and many of their absurdities, while escaping some of their harmful consequences. Yet they also failed to do them justice in some matters in which they were correct, and erred either because they did not understand what they meant or because they did not recognize that what they said was correct.

These writers, moreover, merely follow the teachings of Ibn Sīnā. Ibn Sīnā discussed matters concerning theology, prophethood, the Hereafter, and revealed laws that his predecessors had not discussed, that their intellects had not reached, and that their knowledge had not encompassed, for he learned them from

---

## Page 4399

the Muslims, although he acquired them only from heretics who claimed affiliation with the Muslims, such as the Ismāʿīliyyah. He, his household, and their followers were known among the Muslims for their heresy. The best they professed outwardly was the creed of the Rāfidah, while inwardly they concealed outright disbelief. Muslims wrote books, both large and small, exposing their secrets and tearing away their veils, and strove against them by word and deed, for they were more deserving of this than the Jews and Christians. One need only mention “Uncovering Secrets and Tearing Away Veils” by Judge Abū Bakr Muhammad ibn al-Tayyib, the book of ʿAbd al-Jabbār ibn Ahmad, the book of Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī, and the writings of Abū Ishāq, Ibn Fūrak, Judge Abū Yaʿlā, and al-Shahrastānī, besides much else that would take too long to describe.

The point here is that Ibn Sīnā himself reported that his household, his father, and his brother belonged to these heretics, and that this was what led him to take up philosophy, for he used to hear them discussing the intellect and the soul.

These Muslims with whom he claimed affiliation, despite their outward heresy and inward disbelief, knew more about Allah than his philosophical predecessors, such as Aristotle and his followers. For whatever knowledge of Allah those philosophers possessed was surpassed by that of devout Arab polytheists.

I have cited Aristotle’s own statements in his “Metaphysics,” in “Book Lambda” and elsewhere, which represent the ultimate achievement of his philosophy, and have explained some of the ignorance they contain. For among the well-known groups who discuss divine knowledge, despite their error and misguidance—such as Jewish and Christian scholars, the innovators among the Muslims, and others—none are more ignorant

---

## Page 4400

than these men, nor further removed from knowledge of Allah Most High. Admittedly, much of what they say about natural philosophy is sound. Their discussions of it are extensive, and they possess the intellectual faculties through which they acquired that knowledge. They may indeed seek the truth, without displaying obstinate resistance to it. Yet they are profoundly ignorant of divine matters, possessing only a little knowledge of them, riddled with errors.

When Ibn Sīnā acquired some knowledge of the Muslims’ religion—having received what he did from the heretics and from those better than them among the Muʿtazilah and the Rāfidah—he sought to reconcile what he had understood through reason from these men with what he had inherited from his philosophical predecessors. Among his innovations were his discussions of prophethood, the mysteries of signs and dreams, and even certain questions of natural philosophy, as well as his discussions of the Necessary Existent and similar matters. Aristotle and his followers, by contrast, make no mention in their writings of the Necessary Existent or of any properties belonging to the Necessary Existent. They speak only of “the First Cause,” establishing its existence as the final cause of celestial motion: the celestial sphere moves in order to resemble it.

Ibn Sīnā thus made some improvements to that corrupt philosophy, until it gained acceptance among students of rational inquiry who knew the religion of Islam. Some of its contradictions began to become apparent to them, and each addressed them according to the knowledge he possessed. Yet they conceded to the philosophers certain false premises in logic, natural philosophy, and metaphysics, without recognizing the falsehood embedded in them. This led them astray concerning lofty questions of faith and sublime Quranic aims, and thereby they departed

---

## Page 4401

from true knowledge and faith. As a result, in many of these matters they neither listen nor reason; rather, they resort to sophistry in rational matters and Qarmatian esotericism in matters of revelation.

The point here is to emphasize that, even if we suppose that the soul is perfected through knowledge alone, as they claim—although this claim is false—the soul has two faculties: a theoretical faculty of knowledge and a practical faculty of will. Both faculties must therefore be perfected through knowing Allah and worshipping Him. Worshipping Him encompasses loving Him and humbling oneself before Him. No soul can ever attain perfection except by worshipping Allah alone, without partner. Worship encompasses knowing Him, loving Him and being His servant. For this Allah sent the messengers and revealed the divine Books, all of which call to the worship of Allah alone, without partner.

These men, however, hold that the acts of worship commanded by the messengers are intended to refine the soul’s character, preparing it for the knowledge which they claim constitutes its perfection, or to put the household and the city in order, which is practical wisdom. They thus make acts of worship mere means to what they claim to be knowledge. Consequently, they consider these obligations to lapse for anyone who has attained the goal. Such is the practice of the Ismā‘īlī heretics and of those who have embraced heresy, wholly or in part, while affiliating themselves with the Sufis, the speculative theologians, the Shī‘ah or others.

The Jahmiyyah said: “Faith is nothing more than knowledge of Allah.” Although this statement is better than theirs—for it defines faith as knowledge of Allah, together with the knowledge of His angels, His Books and His messengers that this entails—these men identify perfection with knowledge of absolute existence and its concomitants. Yet this is something that

---

## Page 4402

even if it had any reality outside the mind, would not constitute perfection for the soul except through knowledge of its Creator, glorified and exalted is He. These Jahmiyyah are among the most extreme religious innovators. Indeed, several scholars have placed them outside the seventy-two sects, as is reported from ‘Abdullāh ibn al-Mubārak and Yūsuf ibn Asbāt. This is also the view of a group of later followers of Ahmad and others. Several leading scholars, including Wakī‘ ibn al-Jarrāh, Ahmad ibn Hanbal and others, have declared anyone who holds this view an unbeliever.

They said: “This would entail that Iblīs, Pharaoh and the Jews who recognize him as they recognize their own sons are believers.”

The Jahmiyyah’s position is therefore better than that of these men, for what the Jahmiyyah mention is the foundation upon which souls attain perfection. Yet they failed to combine the soul’s knowledge with its will, which is the source of its practical faculty. They located perfection in knowledge alone, even if neither speech nor action bears it out, and even if it is unaccompanied by reverent fear, love, veneration or any of the other foundations and necessary accompaniments of faith. These men, however, have strayed as far as possible from perfection. Our purpose here is solely to discuss their demonstration; we have mentioned only some of the consequences of their unsound principles.

Know that exposing the falsehood and logical flaws in their statements does not entail that they will be among the wretched in the Hereafter, unless Allah sent them a messenger whom they did not follow. Rather, it makes clear that anyone to whom the messengers brought the truth, but who turned away from their path to follow the path of these men, will be among the wretched in the Hereafter.

---

## Page 4403

Were it not for the prophets, these men would have been more rational than others. But the prophets brought the truth, remnants of which remain among the nations, even though they disbelieve in some of it. Even the Arab polytheists retained remnants of Abraham’s religion and were therefore better than the polytheistic philosophers who agree with Aristotle and his like on their fundamental principles.

The fifth point:

If the aim of their demonstrative syllogism is knowledge of contingent beings, none of these necessarily remains in the same state from eternity past to eternity future. Rather, they are susceptible to change and transformation. Even when an attribute is assumed to be inseparable from its subject, the subject itself does not necessarily endure. Knowledge of it is therefore not knowledge of a necessarily existent being.

They have no sound proof that anything in the world has existed without a beginning, as has been explained at length elsewhere. At most, their arguments entail the perpetual existence of agency, matter and duration as kinds. This is possible through an endless succession of individual instances of those kinds, while affirming that every effect comes into being and is preceded by nonexistence, as sound reason and authentic revelation require. For sound reason judges it impossible for a particular effect to coexist with its agent from eternity past to eternity future, whatever its agent is assumed to be—especially if the agent acts by choice, as established by conclusive proofs, not those cited by men deficient in their knowledge of the foundations of knowledge and religion, such as al-Rāzī and his like. This has been explained at length elsewhere.

As for their claim that an effect coexists with its cause, if by “cause” they mean that which brings the effect into existence, this is false according to sound reason. Hence, this is acknowledged by all sound natural dispositions that

---

## Page 4404

have not been corrupted by false imitation. Since this is firmly rooted in people’s natural dispositions, the very acknowledgment that He is the Creator of everything entails that everything other than Him came into being and was preceded by nonexistence. Even if His creating one creature after another is assumed to have continued without beginning, this does not conflict with His being the Creator of everything, or with everything other than Him having come into being after nonexistence, with nothing else coeternal with Him. Rather, this is greater in perfection, generosity and bounty.

If, however, “cause” means something other than that which brings an effect into existence—as in their examples of a ring moving with the movement of the hand, or rays proceeding from the sun—this does not concern agency at all. Rather, it concerns something dependent on a condition, and a condition may coexist with what depends on it. As for an agent, its particular effect cannot coexist with it from eternity, although it is not impossible for it to produce one thing after another. Thus, the beginninglessness of action as a kind is like the beginninglessness of motion as a kind. This does not conflict with every individual instance of motion having a beginning; rather, it entails it, since no particular instance can be beginningless.

This is the position of the vast majority of rational people among all nations, including Aristotle and his followers. Although they held that the world is beginningless, they did not affirm that it has an originator or an efficient cause, but only a final cause that the celestial sphere moves to imitate, since the sphere’s movement is voluntary. This view—that the First does not bring the world into existence but is merely a final cause to be imitated—is one of the utmost ignorance and disbelief. Nevertheless, the point is that they agreed with all other rational people that a contingent being which is an effect cannot be coeternal with its cause, as Ibn Sīnā and those who agree with him claim. Hence Ibn Rushd and other philosophers like him rejected this view, having followed the approach of

---

## Page 4405

Aristotle and all other rational people on this matter. They explained that Ibn Sīnā’s position departed from that of his predecessors and the vast majority of rational people. His intention was to construct a doctrine combining the doctrines of the speculative theologians with that of his predecessors, making the contingent being an effect of the Necessary Being while nevertheless being beginningless and coeternal with Him. His followers, including al-Suhrawardī al-Halabī, al-Rāzī, al-Āmidī, al-Tūsī and others, followed him in accepting this possibility. Al-Rāzī claimed in his Muhassal that the view that an effect brought about by a cause that acts by the necessity of its essence can be beginningless was agreed upon by the earlier philosophers whose statements have reached us, such as Aristotle and others like him. Yet this was only the view of Ibn Sīnā and those like him.

When the speculative theologians affirm the beginninglessness of the attributes and the like that subsist in the Beginningless One, they do not say that these are produced or are effects of an efficient cause. Rather, in their view, the beginningless essence possesses those attributes, which are inseparable from it. The Necessary Being cannot truly be necessary and beginningless without His inseparable attributes, as has been explained fully elsewhere. They also hold it impossible for a contingent being—which, considered apart from its agent, admits both existence and nonexistence—to be beginningless. Likewise, the leading philosophers hold it impossible for a beginningless being to admit nonexistence, and impossible for a contingent being to have always been necessary, whether it is said to be necessary in itself or through another.

But Ibn Sīnā’s and others’ claim that a contingent being may be beginningless, necessary through another, and eternal without beginning or end—as they maintain regarding the celestial sphere—is precisely what has exposed their account of “contingency” to objections that undermine their position and that they cannot answer, as has been explained fully elsewhere. For this is not the place for

---

## Page 4406

a detailed exposition of this matter; we have mentioned it only to point out that their syllogistic demonstration yields no universal truths concerning contingent beings that must remain true forever. As for the Necessary Being—blessed and exalted is He—syllogistic reasoning does not establish what is unique to Him, but only a universal notion shared by Him and others. For, in their view, categorical syllogisms yield only shared universals, which are not exclusive to the Necessary Being, the Lord of all worlds—glorified and exalted is He. Thus, through their demonstration, they have gained no knowledge of anything that must endure, whether concerning the Necessary Being or contingent beings. If the soul attains perfection only through knowledge that endures as long as its object endures, then their demonstration has provided them with no knowledge through which the soul attains perfection—let alone justify the claim that such knowledge can be attained only through their demonstration.

For this reason, the prophets—may Allah’s blessings and peace be upon them—followed the method of establishing knowledge of the Lord Most High by mentioning His signs. When they employed “inferential reasoning” in doing so, they used a fortiori reasoning, not a categorical syllogism whose instances stand on an equal footing, nor mere analogical reasoning. For the Lord Most High has no equal, nor can He and anything else fall under a universal whose instances stand on an equal footing. Rather, any perfection free of deficiency that is established for another belongs to Him all the more; and any deficiency from which another is free, He is all the more free from it. For this reason, the demonstrative rational arguments mentioned

---

## Page 4407

in the Quran are of this kind, as in the proofs He presents of His lordship, divinity, oneness, knowledge and power, the possibility of resurrection, and other sublime and lofty matters and truths concerning the Divine. These constitute the noblest knowledge and the greatest means by which souls attain perfection through understanding. Yet their perfection necessarily requires perfection in both knowledge and intention. Worshipping Allah alone is therefore indispensable; it encompasses knowing Him, loving Him, and humbling oneself before Him. As for His use of signs as evidence, this is frequent in the Quran.

The distinction between signs and inferential reasoning is that a “sign” is a mark: evidence that entails the very thing it indicates. What it indicates is not a universal notion shared by the object sought and something else. Rather, knowledge of the sign itself entails knowledge of the very thing indicated, just as the sun is a sign of daytime.

Allah Most High says:

“We made the night and the day two signs. We effaced the sign of the night and made the sign of the day illuminating.”

Thus, simply knowing that the sun has risen entails knowing that daytime has begun. Likewise, with the prophethood of Muhammad—may Allah bless him and grant him peace—knowledge of his prophethood specifically does not entail a notion shared by him and others. The same applies to the signs of the Lord Most High: knowledge of them itself entails knowledge of His own holy Self—exalted is He—not knowledge of a universal shared by Him and others.

Knowing that one thing entails another is what gives evidence its force. Every piece of evidence that exists must therefore entail what it indicates. Knowing that a particular thing entails the particular object sought is closer to our innate disposition than knowing that every particular instance

---

## Page 4408

covered by a universal proposition entails the conclusion. Such is the nature of universal propositions.

For unless the particular instances of universal propositions are known by means other than analogical reasoning, they can be known only through analogical reasoning. One must therefore know that what is indicated follows necessarily from the evidence, which is the middle term. If this is universal, one must know that every instance of the universal conclusion sought follows necessarily from every instance of the evidence. For example, when it is said:

“Every A is B, and every B is C; therefore every C is A.”

One must know that every instance of C follows necessarily from every instance of B, and that every instance of B follows necessarily from every instance of A. Clearly, knowing that a particular C follows necessarily from a particular B, and that a particular B follows necessarily from a particular A, is closer to our innate disposition than this.

If it is said, “That universal proposition arises in the mind as necessary or self-evident knowledge from the giver of reason,” the reply is: “It is even more natural for that particular proposition to arise in the mind from the giver of reason.”

It is known that every contingent thing other than Allah necessarily entails the very being of the Lord Most High. It cannot exist without the existence of the Lord’s very being—exalted and holy is He. If it also entails universal notions shared between it and other things, that is because its existence entails the existence of its necessary concomitants.

Those shared universals are among the necessary concomitants of the particular. I mean that what belongs specifically to it within that general universal necessarily accompanies it. The shared universal necessarily accompanies it on condition that it exists and that there exists a knower who conceives the common element. He—glory be to Him—knows things as they truly are: He knows His own holy Self in its distinctive reality, and He knows universals as universals. Thus, the existence of the particular entails the existence of the unrestricted universal, just as the existence of this human being entails the existence of humanity and animality. Thus, everything

---

## Page 4409

other than the Lord necessarily entails His own holy Self. Nothing other than Him can exist without His holy Self existing. Unrestricted universal existence has no reality among existing entities, much less can it be their creator and originator.

The existence of a particular also entails the existence of the corresponding unrestricted universal. Thus, when the Necessary Existent exists, so does the corresponding unrestricted existence; when the agent of all things exists, so does the corresponding unrestricted agent; when the One without beginning exists, so does the corresponding unrestricted being without beginning; when independence from all things is realized, so is the corresponding unrestricted independence; and when the Lord of all things exists, so does the corresponding Lord. As we have mentioned, when this human being and this animal exist, the corresponding unrestricted human being and unrestricted animal exist. Yet the unrestricted is unrestricted only in the mind, not in external reality.

If someone knows that an unrestricted human being and an unrestricted animal exist, he does not thereby know the particular individual itself. Likewise, if he knows an unrestricted necessary existent, an unrestricted agent, and an unrestricted self-sufficient being, he does not thereby know the Lord of the worlds Himself or what distinguishes Him from all others. That is what His signs—exalted is He—indicate. His signs entail His very being, the conception of which precludes its being shared by more than one.

Everything other than Him is evidence of His very being and a sign pointing to Him. For it is inseparably bound to His very being, and whatever is so bound is evidence of that to which it is bound. No contingent thing can exist unless His very being exists. Thus, all such things are necessarily bound to His own Self, evidence of Him, and signs pointing to Him. Through their method of analogical reasoning, however, these things indicate an unrestricted universal notion that exists only in the mind. Their demonstration therefore has not given them knowledge of what is distinctive to the Lord Most High.

---

## Page 4410

As for “a fortiori reasoning,” which the early generations employed in following the Quran, it indicates that the attributes of perfection, free of all deficiency, belong to Him in a more perfect manner than anything they know to belong to others. The difference is beyond the intellect’s capacity to measure, just as it cannot measure the difference between the Creator and the created. Indeed, if the intellect recognizes differences in excellence between one creature and another whose extent cannot be encompassed, and knows that Allah’s superiority over every creature is greater than any creature’s superiority over another, this makes clear to it that what belongs to the Lord surpasses everything belonging to all others by an immeasurable degree.

Thus, it is as though “a fortiori reasoning” imparts knowledge of something distinctive to the Lord, while the general nature of that thing is known. For this reason, discerning scholars preferred the view that terms predicated of Him and of others are predicated in a graded manner: neither as mere homonyms nor as terms sharing a meaning whose instances are equal, but as terms sharing a meaning whose instances differ in degree. Thus, the terms whiteness and blackness apply both to an intense degree—such as the whiteness of snow—and to a lesser degree—such as the whiteness of ivory. Likewise, the term existence applies to both the Necessary Existent and contingent beings, but its greater perfection and excellence in the Necessary Existent exceed the difference between these two degrees of whiteness.

Yet this difference of degree in graded terms does not prevent their underlying meaning from being common and universal. Graded terms must therefore have a shared universal meaning, even though that universal exists only in the mind. This is the subject of “division”: the division of a universal into its particulars, as when one says that what exists is divided into the necessary and the contingent. For the subject being divided is common to all the resulting categories. Further, the fact that

---

## Page 4411

the existence of this Necessary Existent is more perfect than that of a contingent being does not prevent “existence” from signifying a universal meaning shared by both. The same applies to all other names and attributes predicated of the Creator and the created, such as “living,” “knowing,” “powerful,” “hearing,” and “seeing.” Likewise, this applies to His attributes, such as His knowledge, power, mercy, pleasure, anger, joy, and all the other names and attributes of His proclaimed by the messengers.

People have disagreed on this matter. One group—including Abū al-ʿAbbās al-Nāshiʾ, one of the Muʿtazilite masters who preceded Abū ʿAlī—held that these terms apply literally to the Creator and figuratively to the created. A group among the Jahmiyyah, Bātiniyyah, and philosophers held the reverse: they apply figuratively to the Creator and literally to the created.

The great majority across the various schools held that they apply literally to both. This is the position of groups of speculative thinkers among the Muʿtazilites, Ashʿaris, Karrāmiyyah, jurists, people of hadith, and Sufis. It is also the philosophers’ position. Many of these, however, contradict themselves: they acknowledge that some terms apply literally, such as “existent,” “self,” “essence,” “reality,” and the like, yet dispute this concerning others because of the specious arguments advanced by those who deny them all. Yet what applies to the terms someone denies is analogous to what applies to those he affirms. It is only his limited understanding that leads him to distinguish between equivalent cases. Denying them all rules out His existence.

It is known that what exists is divided into the necessary and the contingent, the eternal and the temporally originated, the self-sufficient and the dependent, the produced and the unproduced. The existence of the contingent entails the existence of the necessary; the existence of what is brought into being entails the existence of the eternal; the existence of the dependent entails the existence of the self-sufficient; and the existence of the produced entails the existence of

---

## Page 4412

what is unproduced. Thus, the two kinds of existence share a common element, while the Necessary Existent possesses what distinguishes Him uniquely. The same applies to all the other terms.

Terms predicated in varying degrees are univocal with respect to their shared meaning. For this reason, earlier philosophical thinkers and others did not give such terms a separate designation; rather, “univocal” encompassed them all. Terms predicated in varying degrees therefore fall within univocal terms in the broad sense, while forming a separate category from univocal terms in the narrow sense.

Accordingly, a universal shared meaning must be affirmed: this is what univocal terms in the broad sense denote. It exists in an unrestricted form only in the mind, and this is what their demonstrative syllogism establishes. Differences in degree must also be affirmed: these are what terms predicated in varying degrees signify, as distinct from univocal terms in the narrow sense. This is what the Quran’s demonstrative arguments establish through a fortiori reasoning. One must also affirm the distinctive property of the Lord by which He is distinguished from everything else. This is what His signs—glorified be He—indicate, for their existence necessarily entails His own existence. No syllogism, whether demonstrative or otherwise, indicates this distinctive property.

It thus becomes clear that their demonstrative syllogism does not attain the objective through which the soul achieves perfection in its knowledge of existing things and their Creator, let alone justify saying that no objects of inquiry can be known except through it. This is an extensive subject, but the purpose here is to draw attention to the invalidity of their “negative proposition”: their claim that inferential knowledge can be attained only through their form of demonstration.

---

## Page 4413

Yet they were not content with this sweeping negation, by which they restricted what was broad and confined knowledge to a narrow path yielding only a result of no value. They went so far as to claim that the knowledge of Allah Most High, and that of His prophets and close servants, is attained only through a syllogism containing a middle term, as Ibn Sīnā and his followers maintain. Still, in affirming such knowledge, they are better than those predecessors of theirs who denied His knowledge and that of His prophets—men among the most ignorant of the Lord of all worlds, His prophets, and His scriptures. Ibn Sīnā, distinguished from those men by greater knowledge and intelligence, followed their logical method in establishing this position.

Although those who follow this path are more knowledgeable and accomplished than their predecessors, they are nevertheless further astray and more ignorant than the Jews and Christians. For the latter attained a measure of faith in the Necessary Existent and His attributes that these misguided men have not attained, owing to the pride and vain imaginings within their hearts. They are followers of Pharaoh and his like. Hence you find them disparaging or hostile toward Moses and those who stand with him among the followers of revealed religions and laws.

Allah Most High says:

“Those who dispute Allah’s signs without any authority having come to them—there is nothing in their hearts but pride, aspiring to a greatness they will never attain.”

He Most High also says:

“Greatly hateful is it in the sight of Allah and those who believe. Thus does Allah seal the heart of every arrogant tyrant.”

And He says:

“When their messengers came to them with clear proofs, they rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed, but the very thing they used to ridicule engulfed them.”

“When they saw Our punishment, they said, ‘We believe in Allah alone, and we reject whatever we used to associate with Him.’”

“But their faith did not benefit them once they had seen Our punishment. Such is Allah’s established way with His servants. And there the disbelievers lost everything.”

---

## Page 4414

Pharaoh’s statements, these men’s following of him and Nimrod son of Canaan and other leaders of disbelief and misguidance like them, and their opposition to Moses, Abraham, and Allah’s other messengers—may Allah’s blessings be upon them—have been discussed at length elsewhere.

Allah made the house of Abraham leaders for the believers, the people of Paradise, and the house of Pharaoh leaders for the people of Hell. He Most High says:

“He and his soldiers behaved arrogantly in the land without right, thinking they would not be returned to Us.”

“So We seized him and his soldiers and cast them into the sea. See, then, what was the end of the wrongdoers!”

“We made them leaders inviting to the Fire, and on the Day of Resurrection they will not be helped.”

“We caused a curse to follow them in this world, and on the Day of Resurrection they will be among the despised.”

“After destroying the earlier generations, We gave Moses the Scripture as a source of insight for mankind.”

And so on until His words:

“Say, ‘Then bring a scripture from Allah that offers better guidance than these two, and I will follow it, if you are truthful.’”

Concerning the house of Abraham, He says:

“We made leaders from among them, guiding by Our command, when they were patient and had certainty in Our signs.”

The point is that their later thinkers, who were more knowledgeable than their predecessors, held that the Lord’s knowledge is acquired through demonstrative syllogism, as is the knowledge of His prophets. We have refuted them at length elsewhere.

Our purpose here is to draw attention to the falsity of their claim that knowledge can be attained only through the demonstration they have described. If this negation is false with respect to the knowledge of individual human beings, then its falsity is

---

## Page 4415

all the more evident with respect to the knowledge of the Lord of all worlds—glorified and exalted is He—and then that of His angels and prophets, may Allah’s blessings be upon them all.

Section

Furthermore, they divide arguments into syllogism, induction, and analogy. They explain that inference proceeds either from the universal to the particular, from the particular to the universal, or from one particular to another. Sometimes they express this in terms of the specific and the general, saying that one infers the specific from the general, the general from the specific, or one specific case from another.

They call the first “qiyās,” meaning subsumptive syllogism, for they reserve the term qiyās for this form of reasoning. Many scholars of legal theory and dialectical theology, however, reserve the term qiyās for analogy. For most rational thinkers, the term encompasses both.

They say that inferring the universal from particulars is induction. If it is complete, it is complete induction and yields certainty; if it is incomplete, it does not yield certainty.

The former consists in examining all the particulars and judging the universal according to what is found in its particulars. The latter consists in examining most of them. An example is someone saying: “An animal moves its lower jaw when it eats, for we have surveyed animals and found this to be so.” One replies to him: “The crocodile moves its upper jaw.”

---

## Page 4416

They then say that syllogisms are divided into “conjunctive” and “exceptive.” An exceptive syllogism explicitly contains either its conclusion or the contradictory of its conclusion, whereas a conjunctive syllogism contains it implicitly. An example of the latter is a syllogism composed of categorical propositions, such as: “All nabīdh (infused drink) is intoxicating, and everything intoxicating is forbidden.”

An exceptive syllogism is composed of conditional propositions and is of two types:

The first employs an “if–then” proposition, such as: “If the prayer is valid, then the person praying is ritually purified.” Affirming the antecedent yields the consequent, while denying the consequent yields the denial of the antecedent.

The second employs a disjunctive proposition. This may preclude both joint truth and joint falsity, as when we say: “A number is either even or odd.” These two cannot both apply, nor can a number be neither.

Alternatively, it may preclude joint truth alone, as when we say: “This is either black or white,” meaning that blackness and whiteness cannot coexist, although the subject may possess neither.

As for a proposition that precludes joint falsity, it is one in which both alternatives cannot be absent together, although they may coexist.

They sometimes call the proposition that precludes both joint truth and joint falsity the genuine conditional proposition. It is coextensive with a pair of contradictories. A proposition that precludes joint truth has a narrower scope than a pair of contradictories: two contraries cannot coexist, but both may be absent, and they are narrower in scope than a pair of contradictories. A proposition that precludes joint falsity, by contrast, has a broader scope than a pair of contradictories. They may find it difficult to give an example of this, unlike the first two types, for which examples abound.

They illustrate it by saying: “This man has gone to sea, or he does not drown in it”; that is, he cannot be without

---

## Page 4417

one or the other, for he cannot drown unless he is at sea. Either he does not drown in it—in which case he has not gone to sea—or he has gone to sea. Going to sea and drowning may occur together.

Examples abound, such as our saying: “This is alive, or it lacks knowledge, power, hearing, sight, or speech.” If life is present, one of the two alternatives holds; if life is absent, these attributes are absent. Yet something may be alive without possessing these attributes. The same applies when one says: “This person is ritually purified, or he is not praying.” If prayer is absent, ritual purity is absent; if ritual purity is present, the other alternative holds. Thus, one or the other must hold.

The same applies to every instance involving the absence of a condition and the presence of that which depends upon it. When the alternatives are the presence of that which depends upon a condition and the absence of the condition, the proposition precludes joint falsity. For the condition must either be present or absent; when it is absent, the condition is absent. Thus, either that which depends upon the condition must be present, or the condition must be absent.

They then divide conjunctive syllogisms into the four figures. The middle term may be the predicate in the major premise and the subject in the minor premise—this is the natural figure, which yields all four types of conclusion: particular and universal, affirmative and negative. Alternatively, the middle term may be the predicate in both premises: this is the second figure, which yields only negative conclusions. Or it may be the subject in both, yielding only particular conclusions. The fourth figure yields particular conclusions and universal negative conclusions, but it is far removed from natural reasoning.

Then, when they wish to demonstrate how the second and third figures yield conclusions, or to establish other conclusions, they need to reason by means of contradictories, converses, and contrapositives. For the truth of

---

## Page 4418

a proposition entails the falsity of its contradictory and the truth of its simple converse and contrapositive. Thus, if our statement, “No pilgrim is an unbeliever,” is true, then our statement, “No unbeliever is a pilgrim,” is also true.

We say: What they have proposed is either false or an unnecessarily lengthy procedure that makes the reasoner’s path longer. It therefore involves either an error that obstructs the truth or a long path that exhausts the person following it before he reaches the truth, although he could reach it by a shorter route. Some of our predecessors illustrated this by comparing it to someone who, when asked, “Where is your ear?” raised [his hand] (*) very high and then swung it around to his left ear, although he could have pointed directly to either his right or his left ear.

How beautifully Allah describes His Book when He says:

“Indeed, this Quran guides to that which is most upright.”

The most upright path to the noblest goals is therefore what Allah sent His Messenger with. As for these people’s method, besides their going astray in some matters and taking a crooked, lengthy path in others, it leads them only to something that cannot save them from Allah’s punishment, let alone secure felicity for them, much less enable human souls to attain perfection through their method.

To explain: Their restriction of proof to syllogism, induction, and analogy is unsupported by any proof; indeed, it is false. Likewise, their assertion that the knowledge sought can be attained only through two premises—neither more nor fewer—is unsupported by any proof; indeed, it is false.

Their argument for this restriction is their statement: “One reasons either from the universal to the particular, or from the particular to the universal

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd (p. 259) said: “Perhaps it should read: ‘[raised] his right hand.’”

---

## Page 4419

or from one of the two parts to the other. The first is syllogism, the second induction, and the third analogy.”

The reply is:

You have offered no proof that inference is confined to these three forms. If, by inference from one particular to another, you mean analogical reasoning, then your classification is not exhaustive. It leaves out inference from one universal to another necessarily connected with it and matching it in generality and particularity. Likewise, it leaves out inference from one particular to another necessarily connected with it, such that the existence of either entails the existence of the other, and its absence entails the other’s absence. This is none of what you have called syllogism, induction, or analogy. These are signs.

An example is inferring daytime from sunrise, or sunrise from daytime. This is not inference from a universal to a particular. Rather, inferring a particular daytime from a particular sunrise is inference from one particular to another, while inferring sunrise in general from daytime in general is inference from one universal to another.

Likewise, using the stars to determine the direction of the Kaaba is inference from one particular to another—for example, using al-Jady, Banāt Na‘sh, and the small star near the pole which some people call “the Pole.” The same applies to inferring the appearance of a star from the appearance of its counterpart in latitude, or inferring from one star’s rising that another is setting or that another is culminating, and other such indications upon which people agree.

The Most High says:

“And by the stars they are guided.”

---

## Page 4420

Using places to determine times and other places is accepted by Arabs and non-Arabs, adherents of religions and philosophers alike. If the appearance of the Pleiades is used to infer the appearance of nearby stars to their east and west, right and left, this is inference from one particular to another because they are necessarily connected. It is not analogical reasoning. If this is expressed as a universal judgment, it is inference from one universal to another, not from a universal to a particular. Rather, one of two necessarily connected universals is used to infer the other.

Someone who knows the distances between the stars and knows which of them accompany the break of dawn can infer, from the stars he sees, how much of the night has passed and how much remains. This is inference from one of two necessarily connected things to the other. Likewise, someone familiar with mountains, rivers, and winds can use them to infer the places associated with them.

If this connection is permanent, with no known beginning, but has existed since Allah created the earth—as with mountains, the great rivers (the Nile, the Euphrates, Sayhān, and Jayhān), and the sea—then the inference holds consistently.

If the connection has endured for a shorter period, as in the case of the Kaaba—may Allah Most High honour it—then inferences drawn from it apply accordingly. Abraham, Allah’s close friend, built it, and it has remained venerated; no tyrant has ever prevailed over it. Thus, it can be used to infer other things, and other things can be used to infer its location. The corners of the Kaaba face the earth’s four cardinal directions: the Black Stone faces east; the western corner opposite it, also called the Syrian corner, faces west; the Yemeni corner faces south; and the corner opposite it is called the Iraqi corner—if one calls

---

## Page 4421

the corner next to it on the Black Stone side the Syrian corner; and if that one is called the Syrian corner, this one is called the Iraqi corner. This Syrian–Iraqi corner faces north, towards the celestial pole. Thus, the Kaaba can be used to determine directions, and directions can be used to locate it. Things whose duration is shorter than that of the Kaaba, such as buildings in towns and trees, can serve as indications for a correspondingly shorter period. One might say, for example, that a particular house can be identified by a tree at its entrance with such-and-such characteristics. The two remain connected for a certain period. This and similar cases involve inferring one of two necessarily connected things from the other, both being specific particulars. This is not analogical reasoning.

For this reason, Muslim scholars of rational inquiry departed from their method and said:

Evidence is that which guides one to what is sought and leads one to the intended conclusion. It is something knowledge of which entails knowledge of what is sought, or something whose sound examination leads to knowledge or to a belief judged more likely to be true. They have a terminological disagreement over whether this second kind should be called evidence or given the distinct designation of a sign. The majority call both kinds evidence, while some speculative theologians apply the term only to the first.

The criterion for evidence is that it entails what it indicates. Anything that entails something else can therefore be used as evidence for it. If the entailment runs in both directions, either can be used as evidence for the other: one reasons from whichever of the two one knows to the other, which one does not know. If the entailment is conclusive, the evidence is conclusive; but if it is apparent

---

## Page 4422

—and may fail to hold—the evidence is probabilistic. An example of the first kind is the way created things indicate their Creator—glorified and exalted is He—and His knowledge, power, will, mercy, and wisdom. Their existence necessarily entails His existence and these attributes; their existence without these is impossible. Thus, they cannot exist without indicating them. Another example is the way the Messenger’s report establishes the truth of what he reports from Allah. He speaks only the truth concerning Him, for he is divinely safeguarded in what he reports from Allah: no error whatsoever is allowed to stand in his reports from Him. Such evidence necessarily entails what it indicates, with an entailment that cannot be severed under any circumstances.

This holds whether the evidence that entails the conclusion consists in existence or nonexistence. Evidence may consist in either existence or nonexistence, and either may be used to infer existence or nonexistence. The presence of something is used to infer the absence of its contradictory and its contrary; the absence of its contradictory is used to infer its presence. The presence of that which entails something is used to infer the presence of what it entails, while the absence of what is entailed is used to infer the absence of that which entails it. Indeed, anything used as evidence entails what it indicates. This encompasses everything they have mentioned, as well as what they have not mentioned.

What they call a connected conditional amounts to inferring the presence of the consequent from the presence of the antecedent, and the absence of the antecedent from the absence of the consequent, whether this is expressed in conditional or categorical form. Differences in the wording of evidence do not change its essential nature when its meaning remains the same. The discussion concerns intelligible meanings, not words.

Thus, someone might say:

“If the prayer is valid, then the person praying is in a state of ritual purity”; “If the sun is up, then it is daytime”; “If an agent possesses knowledge and power, then he is alive”; and similar statements.

---

## Page 4423

This has the same meaning as saying: “The validity of the prayer entails the validity of the ritual purification”; or: “The validity of the prayer entails the presence of ritual purity”; or: “A person cannot be praying unless he is ritually pure”; or: “Ritual purity is a condition for the validity of prayer, and when the condition is absent, that which depends upon it is absent”; or: “Everyone who prays is ritually pure; therefore, whoever is not ritually pure is not praying.” There are similar ways of combining words and meanings that convey this inference, rather than confining people to a single formulation.

As minds and their conceptions expand, so do their forms of expression. When minds, expressions, and conceptions become narrow, a person’s intellect and tongue remain as though imprisoned. This is what happens to the adherents of Greek logic: you find them among the narrowest of people in knowledge and exposition, and among the least capable of conceiving and expressing ideas.

Thus, even an intelligent person, when engaging with the various disciplines and following the logicians’ approach, becomes long-winded and restrictive, laboured and strained. At best, he explains the evident and clarifies the obvious—an instance of inarticulacy. This may also lead him into various forms of sophistry from which Allah has spared those who have not followed their path.

The same applies to their laboured definitions, such as their definition of man, and their definition of the sun as “a celestial body that rises by day.” Is anyone who recognizes the sun through such a definition or the like anything but among the most ignorant of people? Is anything more apparent to people than the sun?

Someone who does not know the sun is either unfamiliar with the word, in which case it can be translated for him—but this is not the kind of definition they describe—or he has never seen it because he is blind, in which case he cannot see daylight, let alone the stars

---

## Page 4424

although he is bound to hear from people enough to make this known to him without recourse to their method. They themselves acknowledge that the first figure of categorical syllogisms suffices in place of all other syllogistic forms. Its form is grasped naturally and need not be learned from them. Moreover, inference does not require conceiving it in the manner they claim.

Section

As for their statement, “Inference must consist of two premises, neither more nor less,” this claim is false in both directions. The premises needed by someone drawing an inference vary from person to person. Some people need only one premise because they already know the rest, just as some need no inference at all to know the matter, but may know it directly. Others need two premises; others three; and others four or more.

Suppose someone wishes to know whether this particular intoxicant is prohibited. If he knows that every intoxicant is prohibited, but does not know whether this particular intoxicant causes intoxication, he needs only one premise: to know that it is intoxicating. If he is told, “This is prohibited,” and asks, “What is the evidence for that?” and the person presenting the argument replies, “The evidence is that it is intoxicating,” the desired conclusion has been established.

The same applies if two people disagree about a particular kind of drink: is it intoxicating or not?

---

## Page 4425

as people frequently ask about particular drinks without knowing whether or not they intoxicate, although they already know that every intoxicant is prohibited. Once it is established for the questioner, through the report of someone he trusts or through other evidence, that the drink is intoxicating, he knows that it is prohibited. The same applies to any category or particular instance whose inclusion under a universal proposition is in doubt, while the ruling expressed by that proposition is already known. Examples include people’s disagreement over backgammon and chess: do they constitute gambling or not? Likewise, their disagreement over the disputed nabīdh: does it fall under the category of wine or not?

Another example is their disagreement over oaths involving vows, divorce, and manumission: do these fall within His words,

“Allah has already ordained for you the dissolution of your oaths,”

or not? Likewise, their disagreement concerning His words,

“or the one in whose hand is the marriage contract waives it”:

is this the husband or the guardian with independent authority? There are many similar examples.

An inference may require two premises for someone who does not know that the disputed intoxicating nabīdh is prohibited and does not know that this particular drink is intoxicating. He cannot know that it is prohibited until he knows both that it is intoxicating and that every intoxicant is prohibited.

He may know that this drink is intoxicating and that every intoxicant is wine, yet not know that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited wine—because he has only recently embraced Islam, or because he grew up among ignorant people or heretics who question this.

Or he may know that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said,

“Every intoxicant is prohibited.”

Or he may know that this is wine and that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited wine, yet not know that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah. Alternatively, he may not know that he prohibited it for all believers, but suppose instead that he permitted it for some people and consider himself one of them—as someone might suppose that he permitted drinking it for medical treatment or some other purpose. For such a person, nothing suffices for knowing

---

## Page 4426

that this intoxicating nabīdh is prohibited without exception unless he knows that it is intoxicating and that it is wine; that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, prohibited every intoxicant; that he truly is the Messenger of Allah, so whatever he prohibited, Allah prohibited; and that his prohibition was universal, permitting it neither for medical treatment nor for pleasure.

Another indication that restricting inference to two premises is invalid is their definition of the syllogism, encompassing demonstrative, rhetorical, dialectical, poetic, and sophistical syllogisms:

“It is a statement composed of statements, or an expression of a composition of statements, such that, if these are conceded, another statement necessarily follows from them by virtue of those statements alone.”

They say:

“By saying ‘of statements,’ we exclude a single proposition which, by itself, entails the truth of its converse and its contrapositive, and the falsity of its contradictory, yet is not a syllogism.”

They say:

“We did not say ‘composed of premises,’ because we cannot define a premise, insofar as it is a premise, except as a component of a syllogism. Were we to include it in the definition of the syllogism, the definition would be circular.”

When a declarative proposition forms part of a syllogism, they call it a premise. When it is derived through a syllogism, they call it a conclusion. When considered apart from either role, they call it a proposition. It is also called a proposition even when designated a conclusion or a premise. It is a declarative statement, not a subject–predicate construction in the grammarians’ terminology; rather, it is broader than that. A subject–predicate construction can only be a nominal sentence, whereas a proposition may be either a nominal or a verbal sentence, as when one says, “Zayd has lied, and anyone who lies deserves discretionary punishment.”

The point here is that by ‘statement’ in their definition, ‘A syllogism is a statement composed of statements,’ they mean a proposition constituting a complete declarative sentence. They do not mean a simple expression, which is

---

## Page 4427

a term. A syllogism comprises three terms: the minor, the middle, and the major. For example, one might say, “The disputed nabīdh is intoxicating, and every intoxicant is prohibited.” Here, “nabīdh,” “intoxicating,” and “prohibited” are each simple expressions, constituting the terms of the syllogism. This, then, is not what they mean by “statement”; rather, they mean that each proposition is a statement, as they themselves have explained.

Hence they say:

“A syllogism is a statement composed of statements such that, if these are conceded, another statement necessarily follows from them by virtue of those statements alone.”

What necessarily follows is the conclusion, which is a proposition, a declarative statement, and a complete sentence—not a simple expression.

Likewise, they say, “A syllogism is a composite statement,” thus calling the two propositions taken together a statement. Since they describe a syllogism as composed of statements, meaning propositions, this need not mean precisely two statements. For a plural expression either encompasses two or more, as in His words:

“If he has siblings, his mother receives a sixth.”

Or it means three or more, which, according to the majority, is its primary meaning. It may, however, denote numerical plurality in general, thus encompassing two or more; the plural is not restricted to two.

Thus, when they say, “It is composed of statements,” if they mean numerical plurality in general, this means two or more. A syllogism may therefore be composed of three or four premises and is not restricted to two. If, however, they intend the plural in its strict sense, it could only be composed of three or more. They certainly do not intend this, so only the first interpretation remains.

If it is said that they accept this and say:

“We say that a syllogism consists, at a minimum,

---

## Page 4428

of two premises, though it may consist of several premises.”

The reply is:

“This contradicts what is in your books, for you allow only two premises.”

They have expressly stated that a syllogism yielding the desired conclusion, whether conjunctive or exceptive, consists of neither fewer nor more than two premises. They justify this by saying that a single conclusion has no more than two parts: a subject and a predicate.

If the syllogism is conjunctive, each of the two parts of the conclusion must correspond to one of its premises—that is, it must occur within that premise as either its subject or its predicate, without itself constituting the entire premise.

They say:

“The conclusion has no more than two parts and therefore requires no more than two premises. If the syllogism is exceptive, it must contain a conditional premise, either implicative or disjunctive, corresponding to the whole conclusion or its contradictory. It must also contain an exceptive premise, so there is no need for a third.”

They say:

“However, a statement additional to the syllogism’s two premises may sometimes be incorporated into it. This is either unrelated to the syllogism or related to it. If related, it serves either to make the discourse more persuasive and polished or to explain both premises or one of them.”

They call this a compound syllogism.

They say:

“In essence, it amounts to several syllogisms presented to establish a single conclusion. Of these, however, only one directly establishes the desired conclusion, and the rest

---

## Page 4429

serve to establish the syllogism’s premises.”

They say:

“Sometimes they omit some of the syllogism’s premises, either relying on the mind to grasp them or seeking to pass off a fallacy, lest stating those premises explicitly expose their falsity.”

They say:

“If the syllogisms used to establish the premises have their conclusions expressly stated, the syllogism is called ‘separated’; otherwise, it is called ‘connected’.”

They illustrate the connected form with the statement:

“Every human being is an animal, every animal is a body, and every body is a substance. Therefore, every human being is a substance.”

They illustrate the separated form with the statement:

“Every human being is an animal, and every animal is a body. Therefore, every human being is a body.” Then one says: “Every animal is a body, and every body is a substance. Therefore, every human being is an animal.” From these two it follows that every human being is a substance.

The reply to them is:

As for the desired conclusion having no more than two parts, that concerns its verbal formulation. In the mind, however, what is sought is only one thing, not two: whether the relation expressed in the judgment holds or does not hold.

Alternatively, one may describe it as the affirmation or denial of an attribute of its subject. Or one may speak of the relation of predicate to subject, or of grammatical predicate to grammatical subject, by way of denial or affirmation. These and similar expressions indicate the single meaning intended by the proposition.

Thus, if the conclusion is that nabīdh is forbidden or is not forbidden, or that a human being is capable of sensation or is not capable of sensation, and so forth, what is sought is to establish or deny that prohibition applies to nabīdh, and likewise to establish or deny that human beings possess sensation. If a single premise is relevant to that

---

## Page 4430

desired conclusion, it achieves the intended purpose. Our statement that nabīdh is wine is relevant to the desired conclusion, as is our statement that a human being is an animal. Suppose someone knows that all wine is forbidden but is uncertain whether the disputed nabīdh is called wine in the Lawgiver’s usage. If he is told that nabīdh is forbidden because it is established in the Sahīh that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Every intoxicant is wine,”

then the proposition—namely, our statement that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said, “Every intoxicant is wine”—establishes that nabīdh is forbidden, even if the statement itself contains another proposition.

Drawing this inference depends on premises already known to the listener: that whatever hadith scholars have authenticated must be believed to have been said by the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace; that whatever the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has forbidden is forbidden; and so forth. If we were required to mention everything on which knowledge depends, even when it is already known, the premises would number more than two; indeed, they might exceed ten.

According to their position, everyone who argues from the words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ought to say: “The Prophet forbade this, and whatever he forbade is forbidden. Therefore, this is forbidden.” Likewise, he would say: “The Prophet made this obligatory, and whatever the Prophet made obligatory is obligatory.”

Thus, when arguing that marrying mothers, daughters, and the like is forbidden, one would need to say: “Allah has forbidden this in the Quran, and whatever Allah has forbidden is forbidden.” And when arguing for the obligation of prayer, zakat, and Hajj on the basis of such words of Allah as:

“Pilgrimage to the House is a duty owed to Allah by mankind,”

he would say: “Allah has made Hajj obligatory in

---

## Page 4431

His Book, and whatever Allah has made obligatory is obligatory.” Such statements are regarded by reasonable people as clumsy, inarticulate speech, explanations of the obvious, and needless additions.

This pointless verbosity in their syllogisms resembles their verbosity in their definitions, as when they define the sun as “a celestial body that rises by day.” Such discourse serves only to waste time, weary minds, and multiply nonsense. Moreover, those who follow them in their definitions and demonstrations continually disagree over how to define things already known without their definitions, and dispute demonstrations of matters that have no need of their demonstrations.

Their claim, “The desired conclusion has no more than two parts and therefore requires no more than two premises,” may be answered as follows:

If you mean that it contains only two simple terms, that is not so. Rather, it may be expressed using several terms. Consider someone who is uncertain whether nabīdh is forbidden by a scriptural text or is not forbidden by either a text or analogy. If the respondent says, “Nabīdh is forbidden by a scriptural text,” the desired conclusion has three parts. Likewise, if someone asks, “Is consensus conclusive evidence?” and the respondent says, “Consensus is conclusive evidence,” the desired conclusion has three parts.

If someone asks, “Is a human being a body endowed with sensation, growth, voluntary movement, and reason, or not?” the desired conclusion here has six parts. In short, a subject and predicate—the grammatical subject and predicate—constitute a declarative sentence, which may consist of two expressions or of several expressions, if

---

## Page 4432

its content is subject to numerous qualifications. For example, Allah Most High says:

“The first forerunners among the Emigrants and the Helpers, and those who followed them in righteousness—Allah is pleased with them, and they are pleased with Him.”

And He says:

“Indeed, those who believed and those who emigrated and strove in the cause of Allah—it is they who hope for Allah’s mercy.”

And He says:

“Those who believed afterwards, emigrated, and strove alongside you—they are of you.”

There are similar qualifications that grammarians call adjectives, coordination, circumstantial expressions, adverbs of place, adverbs of time, and the like.

When a proposition contains numerous qualifications, it consists not of two expressions but of multiple expressions and multiple meanings. If what is meant is that the desired conclusion comprises only two meanings, whether expressed in two expressions or several, the reply is:

That is not so. What is sought may be a single meaning, two meanings, or several meanings. What is sought depends on what the seeker is seeking, whether he is a thinker reasoning from evidence or a learner questioning and debating. Either may seek one meaning, two meanings, or several; and what he seeks may be expressed in one expression, two expressions, or more.

Thus, if he says, “Nabīdh is forbidden,” and is told, “Yes,” this single expression suffices as an answer, just as saying, “It is forbidden,” would suffice.

If they say:

“A single proposition may be equivalent to several propositions, as in the example you gave concerning the human being. This single proposition is equivalent to five propositions, corresponding to five questions to be resolved, namely: Is he a body or not? Does he possess sensation or not? And is

---

## Page 4433

he capable of growth or not? Is he capable of movement or not? And is he rational or not? Likewise, in the earlier example: Is nabīdh forbidden or not? And if it is forbidden, is its prohibition established by an explicit text or by analogy?”

The reply is:

If you accept this approach—treating one thing as equivalent to several—then a single expression may convey an entire proposition. If someone says, “Intoxicating nabīdh is forbidden,” and the respondent says, “Yes,” the word “yes” is equivalent to saying, “It is forbidden.”

If he asks, “What is the proof for this?” and the reply is, “The prohibition of every intoxicant,” or “That every intoxicant is forbidden,” or “The words of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, ‘Every intoxicant is forbidden,’” or some similar expression, then the proof has been expressed as a single nominal expression, constituting one element. It has not been formulated as a proposition composed of two nominal elements—a subject and a predicate. For “the prohibition of every intoxicant” is a noun in a possessive construction.

Likewise, “that every intoxicant is forbidden,” with anna pronounced with an a-vowel, is a single nominal expression. This is because anna and its complement are equivalent to a single verbal noun, whereas inna, pronounced with an i-vowel, together with its complement, forms a complete sentence.

The same applies when you say, “The proof for this is the statement of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace,” or “The proof for this is the explicit text,” or “the consensus of the Companions,” or “The proof for this is such-and-such verse,” or “such-and-such hadith”; or “The proof for this is the presence of a ground for prohibition unopposed by any countervailing consideration of equal force”; or “The proof for this is that it shares with grape wine a characteristic that entails prohibition.” These and similar formulations express the proof through a single nominal expression, rather than through a proposition constituting a complete sentence. Yet when this proof, expressed through a single nominal expression, is set out in detail, it is expressed in multiple terms.

---

## Page 4434

The point is this: you say that a proof—that is, a syllogism—consists of exactly two parts. If you mean only two expressions, and that anything beyond two expressions constitutes several proofs rather than one, on the grounds that each attribute qualifying an expression requires its own proof, then the reply is:

The same could be said of the two expressions: they constitute two proofs, not one, since each premise requires a proof. Restricting the number to two, rather than allowing more, is therefore an arbitrary stipulation without any meaningful basis. For the intended point may be conveyed by a single expression, or it may require two, three, four, or more. To insist that two expressions are the required norm, to the exclusion of both more and fewer, and to treat anything additional as multiple propositions if it concerns what is to be established, or as equivalent to multiple syllogisms if it consists of premises stated in the proof, is sheer arbitrariness. It is no more justified than saying:

“Rather, the norm is that there should be one proposition to establish, with a proof consisting of a single part. If what is to be established exceeds this, it is treated as two, three, or four propositions, according to its import.”

If this were said, it would be better than their position, because the term “proof” is singular, so what it denotes should likewise be single; and a syllogism is a proof.

The word “qiyās” (syllogism) implies measurement, as when one says, “I measured this against that.” Measurement can be made against one thing. If something is measured against two or three things, however, there are two or three measurements, not one. Those measurements would therefore constitute several syllogisms, not a single syllogism. Thus, their treating more than two premises as equivalent to several syllogisms, and fewer than two as half of a

---

## Page 4435

syllogism rather than a complete syllogism, is purely conventional and has no rational basis—just as they distinguished between essential attributes and accidental attributes inseparable from essence and existence through similarly arbitrary stipulations.

It thus becomes clear that, in what they call “definition” and “demonstration,” these people have relied neither on an existing reality nor on anything rationally intelligible, but merely on convention. This resembles the dispute over the term “cause”: does it designate that which necessarily entails its effect, such that the effect never fails to follow under any circumstances, admitting neither a contrary instance nor exceptions? Or does it designate that which tends to produce the effect, although the effect may fail to follow because a condition is absent or an impediment is present?

Likewise, some practitioners of rational inquiry and disputation use the term “proof” for that which unconditionally entails its conclusion, so that the absence of countervailing evidence is included within it. Others use “proof” for that which is such as to entail its conclusion, its failure to do so arising only because a condition is absent or an impediment is present.

Practitioners of disputation have also disagreed over whether someone presenting an argument must, when stating the proof, clarify countervailing evidence in general or in detail wherever detail is possible; whether he need address it neither in general nor in detail; or whether he must clarify it in general but not in detail. These are stipulated conventions, comparable to the words people agree upon to express what is in their minds. They are not independently established truths concerning intelligible matters on which nations agree, as these people claim for their logic.

Indeed, those who use “cause” and “proof” in one or the other of these senses are closer to reason than those who maintain that a proof can consist of only two premises. For this singles out one number rather than another without any justification, whereas the former

---

## Page 4436

took account of actual properties of causes and proofs: complete sufficiency or the mere capacity to produce a result. Their considerations were therefore closer to truth and reason than those of these others, who relied on nothing but arbitrary stipulation. For this reason, knowledgeable people of sound judgment described their logic as a conventional system devised by a Greek man, which rational people do not need.

Nor does rational people’s pursuit of knowledge depend on it, any more than it depends on using their vocabulary, such as fīlāsūfiyā (philosophy), sūfistīqā (sophistics), anūlūtīqā (analytics), athūlūjiyā (theology), qātīghūriyās (categories), and other terms with which they express their meanings. No one claims that all other rational people need this language—especially those whom Allah has honoured with the noblest of languages, one that combines the highest degrees of eloquence and conveys what minds conceive in the fewest words and with the fullest clarity.

This was among the arguments Abū Saʿīd al-Sīrāfī advanced in his celebrated debate with the philosopher Mattā, when Mattā began praising logic and claiming that rational people needed it. Abū Saʿīd replied that there was no need for it, and that what was needed was to learn Arabic. Meanings are naturally accessible to reason and require no special convention, unlike the language just mentioned, which is needed to understand the meanings one is required to know and must therefore be learned. Hence, learning the Arabic upon which understanding the Quran and hadith depends is a collective obligation, unlike logic.

---

## Page 4437

Any later scholar who claims that learning logic is a collective obligation, or that it is a prerequisite for independent juristic reasoning, thereby demonstrates his ignorance of both the revealed law and the usefulness of logic. The falsity of this claim is necessarily known in the religion of Islam. The best of this community—the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, and the leading scholars of the Muslims—knew what was required of them and what would perfect their knowledge and faith before Greek logic became known.

How, then, can it be said that knowledge cannot be trusted unless weighed by logic, or that human beings’ natural faculties generally become sound only through it? If they say, “We do not maintain that people need the logicians’ terminology, but rather the concepts by which knowledge is weighed,” the answer is: There is no doubt that the unknown can be understood only through what is known. People need to weigh what they do not know against what they know. This is among the balances Allah has sent down, as He says:

“It is Allah who has sent down the Book in truth and the balance.”

And He says:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs and sent down with them the Book and the balance.”
