This is found both in our community and in other communities among people who have never even heard of Greek logic. It is therefore clear that peoples have no need of the logical concepts that the logicians expressed in their own language, namely, their discourse on secondary intelligibles.

For the “subject matter of logic” is intelligibles insofar as they serve to attain knowledge of what is not yet known. It examines the properties of secondary intelligibles: the secondary relations that attach to essences considered without qualification, if these lead to the acquisition of what has not yet been acquired or assist in doing so—not in a particular instance, but according to a universal rule. They claim that the logician examines the genus of proof, just as

---

## Page 4438

the scholar of legal theory examines evidence in Islamic law and its relative authority, distinguishing what constitutes legal evidence from what does not. He also examines the relative ranks of proofs, so that when they conflict, he gives precedence to the stronger over the weaker. They claim that the logician examines evidence as such, which is broader than legal evidence, and distinguishes what constitutes evidence from what does not. They claim that their logic stands in relation to meanings as prosody does to poetry, scales to goods, measures of time to time, and the cubit to the objects measured by it.

This is precisely what the majority of Muslim scholars and other rational people have declared false. Their logic does not distinguish evidence from what is not evidence, whether in its form or its content. Nor is there any need to weigh meanings by it; indeed, meanings cannot validly be weighed by it. Rather, this claim is among the most false of all claims.

Our dispute with them concerns the concepts they incorporated into logic, claiming that the conceptions and judgments sought cannot be attained except through them. They thus advanced “four claims” for their logic: two negative and two affirmative. They claimed that conceptions cannot be attained by any method other than the one they set out in it, and that judgments cannot be attained by any method other than the one they set out in it. These two claims are among the most manifestly false of all claims. They also claimed that their stated method yields a conception of realities not previously conceived. This too is false. Attention has already been drawn to these three claims, and there will follow

---

## Page 4439

a discussion of their fourth claim, which is more plausible than the others: that their demonstrative proof yields knowledge through judgment.

And if they say, “Knowledge through judgment—or through conception—cannot be attained without it,” then they are claiming that all avenues to knowledge are closed to rational human beings except the two methods they have described: definition and syllogism. They also claim that these two methods lead to the knowledge that human beings attain through reason, meaning that any method which leads to knowledge must follow the procedure they have described, and no other. Thus, what they have set out is a rule-based instrument by which methods of acquiring knowledge are weighed, and sound methods distinguished from unsound ones. Observing this set of rules therefore safeguards the mind against error in the reasoning through which a conception or judgment is attained. This summarizes what they have said.

All these claims are false, both in what they deny and in what they affirm. Not all the methods of others that they reject are invalid, nor are all the methods of their own that they endorse sound in the manner they claim. Some of their methods are indeed sound, just as some methods used by others are invalid. No one, whether among them or others, composes a work without its necessarily containing some truth.

The Jews and Christians possess more truth, in proportion to the totality of what they hold, than these men do. Indeed, the Arab polytheists who worship idols, and others like them, possess more truth than is possessed by

---

## Page 4440

these men, measured against the entirety of their theoretical philosophy and their practical philosophy concerning ethics, household management, and government. Thus, the Greeks were unbelieving polytheists who worshipped the heavenly bodies and idols, far worse than the Jews and Christians even after their religions had been abrogated and altered. Had Allah not favored them by bringing the religion of Christ to them, through which they acquired guidance and monotheism for as long as they adhered to his revealed law before its abrogation and alteration, they would have been like their fellow polytheists.

Then, when the religion of Christ was altered, they came to follow a religion combining pure monotheism and polytheism: some of it true and some false, yet better than the religion their forebears had followed.

Our concern here is with “exposing the misguidance of these philosophers,” who build their misguidance upon that of others, relying on falsehood in transmitted reports and ignorance in matters of reason. One example is their claim that Aristotle was the minister of Dhū al-Qarnayn, who is mentioned in the Quran. They say this because they have heard that he was Alexander’s minister, and Dhū al-Qarnayn is called Alexander. This reflects their ignorance: the Alexander whom Aristotle served as minister was the son of Philip the Macedonian. The Roman era familiar to Jews and Christians is reckoned from this Alexander. He only went as far as the Holy Land and did not reach the barrier, according to those acquainted with his history. He was a polytheist who worshipped idols.

Likewise, Aristotle and his people were polytheists who worshipped idols. Dhū al-Qarnayn, by contrast, was a monotheist who believed in Allah and lived before this Alexander. Those who call him Alexander say that he was Alexander son of Dārā.

---

## Page 4441

That is why these philosophers found acceptance only among those furthest removed from reason and religion, “such as the Qarmatians and the Bātiniyyah,” who combined Greek philosophy with the religion of the Magians to construct their doctrine, while outwardly professing Rāfidī Shiism; and among ignorant Sufis and speculative theologians. They find acceptance only under a regime steeped in ignorance and far removed from knowledge and faith—whether among unbelievers or hypocrites. Thus, those of them who gained acceptance did so among godless hypocrites, and then among the polytheist Turks. Likewise, they invariably find acceptance only among the enemies of Allah and His Messenger, whether unbelievers or hypocrites.

Our discussion now concerns the argument they advanced to establish that a proof must contain two premises, neither more nor fewer. Its weakness has already become clear.

Then, when they recognized that a proof may require several premises, while a single premise may sometimes suffice, they said that an additional proposition—that is, a third premise beyond the two—may sometimes be inserted into a syllogism for an unsound or sound purpose, such as establishing the two premises. They call this a compound syllogism.

They said that it comprises several syllogisms presented to establish more than one proposition, although only one of these is sought in its own right.

They also said that one of the premises may sometimes be omitted, either because it is already known or for an unsound purpose. They divided compound syllogisms into disconnected and connected forms.

The response is: This is an admission on your part that some propositions require several premises to establish them, while for others a single premise suffices. Yet you then said that what requires several premises is equivalent to

---

## Page 4442

several syllogisms.

You may then be told:

If you claim that what is indispensable is just one syllogism comprising two premises, and that anything beyond this amounts to further syllogisms, each establishing one of the premises, then say instead that what is indispensable is one premise, and that any additional premises serve only to establish that premise. This is more reasonable. For if an attribute’s belonging to its bearer—that is, a judgment’s applying to its subject, a grammatical predicate’s applying to its subject, or a logical predicate’s applying to its subject—can be known only through an intermediary between the two, namely the evidence, then what is indispensable is one premise. Anything beyond that may or may not be needed.

As for claiming that a syllogism consisting of two premises is needed because some questions require it, this is like claiming that three, four, or five premises are needed in some questions because some questions require them. There is no more reason to prescribe one number than another. What they say about omitting one of the two premises because it is obvious, or in order to mislead, applies equally to omitting a third or fourth premise. Anyone who argues for a conclusion using one premise that is insufficient on its own to establish it—or two or three premises that are insufficient—is asked to supply whatever further premises are needed to make the argument sufficient.

Once the premises have been stated, those open to denial are denied, and those open to counterargument are countered, until the reasoning is complete. Thus, someone asked for evidence that a particular drink is forbidden says, “This is forbidden.” When asked, “Why?” he replies, “Because it is an intoxicating fermented drink.” Thus, this

---

## Page 4443

premise is sufficient if the listener knows that every intoxicant is forbidden, provided he accepts that premise. If he challenges it, saying, “We do not accept that this is intoxicating,” it must be established through the testimony of someone trustworthy or through experience with something similar. This is reasoning by analogy, and it yields certainty. For when someone tests a portion of a large quantity of drink and knows that it is intoxicating, he knows that the remainder is intoxicating, since the same judgment applies to each portion.

Likewise, all other empirical propositions—such as knowing that bread satisfies hunger and water quenches thirst—are based on reasoning by analogy. Indeed, the same applies to all sensory judgments known to hold universally: their universality is known only through reasoning by analogy.

If, however, his interlocutor disputes that intoxicating fermented drink is forbidden, he needs two premises: to establish that this drink is intoxicating and that every intoxicant is forbidden. He establishes the second through various proofs, such as the Prophet’s saying, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Every intoxicant is forbidden.”

And:

“Every drink that intoxicates is forbidden.”

And the report that:

“He was asked about a drink made from honey called bitʿ and a drink made from sorghum called mizr. He had been granted the gift of expressing much in few words, and he said: ‘Every intoxicant is forbidden.’”

These hadiths are recorded in the Sahīh. They, and many times as many other reports, are well known from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, and show that he prohibited every intoxicating drink.

If the interlocutor says, “I know that it is intoxicating liquor, but I do not accept that intoxicating liquor is forbidden,” or, “I do not accept that it is forbidden without qualification,” then this third premise must be established, and so on. What shows you that a single premise may suffice to establish the desired conclusion is that

---

## Page 4444

a proof is that which entails the judgment it establishes, as explained earlier. Since the first term entails the middle term, and the middle term entails the third, it follows that the first entails the third.

For an antecedent of an antecedent is itself an antecedent, and a consequence of a consequence is itself a consequence. The judgment is thus a necessary consequence of the proof, but this necessary connection is known only through a middle term between them. The middle term is what follows the words “because.” This is something the logicians themselves have mentioned. Ibn Sīnā and others discussed the inseparable attributes of a subject, explaining that some are self-evidently inseparable. On this basis, they refuted those among their own school who distinguished an essential attribute from a necessary concomitant of the essence by saying that the latter requires a middle term, whereas the former does not. They replied that many inseparable attributes require no middle term: namely, those whose necessary connection is self-evident. For these logicians, the middle term is the proof.

Some people, however, have supposed that the middle term is something that actually stands between an immediate consequence and a remote consequence. This is mistaken. Nevertheless, it becomes clear that the intended conclusion follows on either understanding. We say: if some necessary consequences are so self-evidently connected to their antecedents that no intervening proof is needed, then merely conceiving such a consequence and its antecedent suffices to know that it holds of that antecedent. If there is a middle term between them, and both its necessary connection to the first antecedent and the second term’s necessary connection to it are self-evident, no further middle term is required. If either of the two antecedents is not self-evident, a middle term is needed; if neither is self-evident, two middle terms are needed. This middle term is a term for which a single premise suffices. Thus, if someone asks for proof that intoxicating fermented drink is forbidden, he is told that it is because the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is authentically reported to have said:

---

## Page 4445

“Every intoxicant is wine,” or “Every intoxicant is forbidden.”

For the believer, no further middle term is needed to establish that the prohibition of intoxicants follows from this middle term—namely, the statement of the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace. Nor is a middle term needed to establish that the prohibition of the disputed fermented drink follows from the prohibition of intoxicants. Everyone knows that if he forbids every intoxicant, he thereby forbids the intoxicating fermented drink under dispute; and every believer knows that whatever the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, forbids is forbidden.

If someone says, “The proof that it is forbidden is that it is intoxicating,” then, if his interlocutor knows that it is intoxicating and that intoxicants are forbidden, he will concede its prohibition. But he may be overlooking the fact that it is intoxicating, or he may not know that it is intoxicating. The same applies if someone says, “Because it is wine.” If the interlocutor acknowledges that it is wine, its prohibition is established. If he acknowledges this after denying it, he may previously have been ignorant and then learned, or heedless and then remembered. Not everyone who knows something has it in mind.

For this reason, these logicians disagreed over whether knowledge of the two premises suffices for knowledge of the conclusion, or whether one must also become aware of a third matter. The latter is the position of Ibn Sīnā and others. They argued that a person may know that a female mule does not give birth, then lose sight of that fact. He sees a female mule with a swollen belly and asks, “Is this one pregnant or not?”

He is asked, “Do you not know that it is a female mule?”

He replies, “Yes.”

He is asked, “Do you not know that a female mule does not give birth?”

He replies, “Yes.”

It is then, he said, that the person becomes aware that it does not give birth.

Al-Rāzī and others disputed his position, saying: “This is weak. If the inclusion of one premise under the other is something distinct from the two premises, it would constitute a further premise that must itself enter into the inference. The question of how it combines with the first two would then be no different from the question of

---

## Page 4446

how the first two combine, leading to the consideration of infinitely many premises. If, however, this is not an item of knowledge distinct from the two premises, it cannot be a condition for drawing the conclusion, since a condition is distinct from what it conditions. Here there is no such distinction, so it cannot be a condition. As for the example of the female mule, that is possible only when just one of the two premises is present in the mind—either the minor or the major premise. When both are present together in the mind, we do not concede that any doubt about the conclusion is possible at all.”

I say:

“The truth of the matter” is that this dispute arose from their assumption that two premises are necessary, not from the inferential process itself, since a condition is distinct from what it conditions. But that is not how matters stand. Rather, what is needed is whatever establishes the conclusion sought, whether one premise, two, or three. What one has lost sight of is not known while one remains heedless of it; once one recalls it, it becomes actual knowledge.

Here, the proof is the knowledge that a female mule does not give birth. He had lost sight of this premise, so he did not know it in the manner required for it to serve as proof. Something overlooked does not serve as proof while it remains overlooked; it does so only when recalled, for only then is it knowledge present to the mind.

The Lord, Most High, is free of heedlessness and forgetfulness, because these contradict the very reality of knowledge. Likewise, He is free of drowsiness and sleep, because these contradict the perfection of His life and His being the Self-Subsisting Sustainer of all. Sleep is the brother of death; hence the people of Paradise do not sleep, just as they do not die. They are inspired to glorify Allah just as we are inspired to breathe.

---

## Page 4447

The point here is that the inferential force of a proof lies in knowing that what it establishes follows necessarily from it, whether this is called bringing it to mind, becoming aware of it, or something else. Whenever a person brings this necessary connection to mind, he knows that the proof establishes its conclusion. If this connection is evident to him, that suffices; otherwise, explaining it may require one, two, three, or more premises.

Middle terms vary from person to person. The middle term that entails a judgment for one person need not be the middle term for another; the latter may arrive at a different middle term. The middle term is the proof: it mediates, in knowledge, between what is entailed and what entails it, namely the predicate and the subject. For the predicate necessarily belongs to the subject so long as it is predicated of it. These middle terms—which are proofs—vary and multiply according to the guidance Allah opens up to people. When the middle term is a truthful report, for example, the report received by one person may differ from that received by another.

When the crescent moon is sighted and its sighting confirmed at the ruler’s court, and people disperse and spread the news throughout the town, each group acquires knowledge through reports from informants other than those who informed the other groups. Likewise, the Quran and Sunnah transmitted to people from the Messenger reached each group through intermediaries different from those through whom they reached others, especially in the second and third centuries. One group had its Quran instructors and teachers, and another had its own Quran instructors and teachers. All these were intermediaries: they were the middle terms between those people and knowledge of what the Messenger said and did. They were the ones who directed them to that knowledge through their reports and teaching.

The same applies to things known through reason or sensory perception when someone draws attention to them or guides others to them. As for those who take the middle term concerning necessary attributes to be the intermediary in their actual inherence

---

## Page 4448

in the subject possessing them, their view is false on several grounds, as has been explained at length elsewhere. Even supposing it to be correct, the middle term in thought has a wider scope than its counterpart in external reality, just as a proof has a wider scope than a cause. Every cause can be used to infer its effect, but not every proof is actually a cause. Likewise, whatever serves as an intermediary in reality can be made an intermediary in thought, thus serving as a proof, but the converse does not hold. For a proof is that which entails what it establishes. A cause that entails its effect can therefore be used to infer it. Similarly, a middle term that necessarily follows from what entails it, and itself entails a more remote consequence, entails that consequence and can therefore be used to infer it.

Thus, on any account, the desired conclusion can be established through a single premise if no other premise is needed. Sometimes, however, it can be established only through several premises, which must therefore be known. Insisting that exactly two premises are needed—neither more nor fewer—is entirely arbitrary. Hence, among all the various groups of rational people and authors in the sciences, you find no one who insists on demonstrating his arguments through two premises, no more and no fewer, striving to reduce any excess to two and to make up any shortfall so as to produce two premises, except the adherents of Greek logic and those who follow their path. This is not the practice of those who have retained their sound natural disposition or followed a path other than theirs, such as the Emigrants, the Helpers, those who followed them in righteousness, the other leading Muslim authorities, scholars and thinkers, and the various religious communities.

Likewise, among grammarians, physicians and geometers, none engages in this practice except those who follow these logicians in this respect, just as they followed them uncritically in adopting definitions composed of genus and differentia. They gained no knowledge from what they received from them except concerning matters for which their false discourse is unnecessary, or concerning what

---

## Page 4449

is harmful rather than beneficial because of the ignorance or excessive prolixity it contains. Since reasoning sometimes depends on one premise, sometimes on two, and sometimes on several, Muslim thinkers have followed the practice of providing whatever evidence they need to establish the premises. They do not undertake to state two premises in every argument, as those who follow the logicians’ path do. Rather, the writings of Muslim thinkers and preachers, and their practice both in reasoning for themselves and in debating with others—whether to teach, guide, or dispute—follow the approach I have described. The same applies to all other kinds of rational people, whether adherents of religious communities or otherwise, except those who follow these logicians’ path.

Muslim thinkers have continually criticized the logicians’ method, exposing its inarticulacy, awkwardness of expression, deficient reasoning, and inability to articulate thought. They explain that it is more likely to corrupt reasoning and speech than to rectify them. They refuse to follow it in their own reasoning or in debate, whether with allies or adversaries.

Its use became widespread only from the time of Abū Hāmid. He placed an introduction to Greek logic at the beginning of his book al-Mustasfā and claimed that only someone acquainted with this logic could have confidence in his own knowledge. He also wrote Mi‘yār al-‘Ilm (The Criterion of Knowledge) and Mihakk al-Nazar (The Touchstone of Reasoning) on the subject. He wrote another book, which he called al-Qistās al-Mustaqīm (The Just Balance), in which he presented five balances: the three categorical forms, the conjunctive conditional form, and the disjunctive conditional form. He recast their formulations as examples drawn from the discourse of Muslims.

---

## Page 4450

and stated that he had addressed some adherents of the doctrine of authoritative teaching in this manner. He also wrote a book on their incoherence and explained their unbelief on account of their position on the eternity of the world, their denial of knowledge of particulars, and their denial of the resurrection. In his final writings, he explained that their method was unsound and did not lead to certainty, condemning it more severely than the method of the speculative theologians.

Earlier, he had included much of their discourse in his books, either in their own words or in different wording. Toward the end of his life, however, he condemned them vehemently. He explained that their method contained ignorance and unbelief warranting its condemnation, and that it was more corrupt than the method of the speculative theologians. He died while engaged in studying the works of al-Bukhārī and Muslim. As for the logic about which he had said so much, it neither enabled him to attain his goal nor dispelled his doubt and bewilderment. Logic availed him nothing.

Yet because of what he had done during his lifetime, and for other reasons, many thinkers began incorporating Greek logic into their disciplines. Thus, later thinkers who follow their approach come to believe that there is no other way, and that their claims concerning definition and demonstration are sound and accepted by rational people. Such a person does not know that rational and learned people, Muslims and non-Muslims alike, have continually criticized and challenged these claims.

Muslim thinkers have written numerous works on this subject. The majority of Muslims criticize it in general terms because of the effects and consequences they observe: these reveal in its practitioners qualities contrary to knowledge and faith, and their condition leads them into various forms of ignorance, unbelief, and misguidance.

The point here is that their claim that every conclusion sought depends on two premises, and no more,

---

## Page 4451

is not true. They call a syllogism in which one premise has been omitted an enthymeme, and say that the premise may be omitted because it is already known, by mistake, or to mislead. The response is this: if it is known, it is like any other known premise. In that case, there is no greater justification for leaving one premise implicit than for leaving two, three, or four implicit. If, in an argument requiring only one premise, one may claim that another premise has been left implicit and omitted, then, in an argument requiring two, one may claim that a third has been omitted; the same applies to an argument requiring three. There is no limit to this. Anyone who reflects on this will find it to be so.

This is why eloquent masters of expression, who establish demonstrations and conclusive arguments in the clearest terms, do not employ two premises in their discourse as frequently as logicians do. Rather, anyone who follows the logicians’ method becomes one of those who constrict the path to knowledge in both thought and expression. Their meanings are of a piece with their wording: one finds in them a feebleness and clumsiness that no rational person would accept.

Ya‘qūb ibn Ishāq al-Kindī was the philosopher of Islam in his day. I mean a philosopher living within the Islamic world; otherwise, the philosophers are not Muslims. Thus, people once asked one of the eminent judges of our time, “Is Ibn Sīnā one of the philosophers of Islam?” He replied, “Islam has no philosophers.”

In the course of his discourse, Ya‘qūb would say, “Nonexistence is the absence of the existence of such-and-such,” and use similar constructions. If anyone finds eloquence or rhetorical excellence in some of his discourse, as one finds in some of the discourse of Ibn Sīnā and others, this is due to what he acquired from Muslims, from their thought and expression. Otherwise, were he to follow the method of his predecessors and turn away from what he had learned from Muslims,

---

## Page 4452

his thought and expression would resemble theirs. They find their readiest acceptance among those who do not understand what they say and esteem them out of ignorance and delusion, or who understand some, most, or all of what they say, yet at that point have no conception of the true nature of what the Messenger, peace and blessings be upon him, brought, of what sound reason recognizes, or of what other rational thinkers have said in contradiction to their claims. Such a person has reached the culmination of their teachings only through toil and hardship, coupled with a favorable opinion of them, and has consequently become embroiled in their errors to an extent known only to Allah. Thereafter, unless Allah comes to his rescue—as happened to many learned men who initially thought well of them, but then discovered enough of their error to turn away from them, disavow them, and even refute them—he remains among the misguided.

Their error in matters of divinity is apparent to most people. Hence Muslim scholars versed in rational inquiry have, without exception, declared them unbelievers on that account. It is only in logic that some people have become confused, having failed to grasp its realities and implications or to learn what other rational thinkers have said about their contradictions in it. Moreover, it happens to contain some self-evident matters, such as the first figure of the syllogism. These people do not realize that whatever truth it contains can be known without recourse to the logicians. Rather, the logicians have lengthened the path, taken a rugged and narrow route, and failed to find their way to what yields a sound understanding.

The purpose here is not to explain their errors in what they affirm, but their errors in what they deny: they claim that inferential knowledge can be attained only through their demonstration, which is a form of syllogism.

They divided arguments into “three” kinds: syllogism, induction, and analogy. They claimed that analogy does not yield certainty, and that certainty is yielded only by a syllogism whose premises consist

---

## Page 4453

of the propositions they have specified.

We have explained elsewhere that analogical reasoning and syllogistic reasoning necessarily entail one another: whatever either yields, whether knowledge or probable judgment, the other yields likewise, provided the content is the same. What matters is the content of the knowledge, not the form of the proposition. Indeed, when the content is certain, it remains the same whether expressed through analogy or through syllogism, and whether the syllogism is conjunctive or exceptive—in their terminology or in whatever wording you choose, especially wording that is better than theirs, clearer to the mind, and more concise. The meaning remains the same.

This can be seen in the most obvious examples. You may say: “This is a human being, and every human being is a created being, or an animal, or sentient, or capable of voluntary motion, or rational,” or mention whatever other necessary attribute of a human being you choose. You may formulate the argument in this way, or you may say: “He is a human being; therefore, he is a created being, or sentient, or an animal, or capable of motion, like other human beings, since they share the human nature that entails these attributes.” Alternatively, you may say: “This is a human being, and human nature entails these properties; therefore, they necessarily belong to him.” Or you may say: “If he is a human being, then he possesses these attributes that necessarily belong to human beings.”

You may also say: “Either he possesses these attributes or he does not. The second alternative is false, so the first must hold, because these attributes necessarily belong to a human being, whose existence without them is inconceivable.”

As for induction, it yields certainty only when it is complete. In that case, you have attributed to the common element what you have found in every individual, and this

---

## Page 4454

is not an inference from a particular to a universal, or from the specific to the general. Rather, it is an inference from one of two mutually entailing things to the other. For if that judgment holds for every individual instance of a general universal, it must necessarily hold for that universal.

Their claim that this is an inference from what is specific and particular to what is general and universal is therefore untrue. How could that be so, when the evidence must entail what it proves? For if the evidence could exist without what it proves, and what it proves did not necessarily follow from it, then knowing that the evidence was present would not enable us to know that what it proves was also present—if we knew that the latter sometimes accompanied it and sometimes did not. If we knew this and then said that it always accompanied it, we would be affirming two contradictories simultaneously.

The necessary connection we are discussing here makes inference possible, whatever form that connection takes. The stronger, more complete and more evident the connection, the stronger, more complete and more evident its indication. This is the case with created things, which point to the Creator, glorified and exalted is He. Every created thing necessarily entails its Creator: it cannot exist without its Creator’s existence, nor indeed without His knowledge, power, will, wisdom and mercy. Every created thing points to all of these.

If what is proved necessarily follows from the evidence, then it is evident that a necessary consequent must be either coextensive with that which entails it or broader in scope. Thus, the evidence can only be broader in scope than what it proves.

When they say that in syllogistic reasoning one infers the particular from the universal, the particular is not the judgment being proved. Rather, it is the subject being described and spoken of as the locus of the judgment. This may be narrower in scope than the evidence or coextensive with it. The judgment, by contrast—which is the formulation concerning that subject and the ruling asserted of it—can only be broader in scope than the evidence or coextensive with it. For this is what is proved and necessarily follows from the evidence, while the evidence itself necessarily follows from the subject being spoken of and described.

---

## Page 4455

Thus, if one says, “Nabīdh (a drink prepared by steeping ingredients in water) is forbidden because it is khamr (intoxicating liquor),” its being khamr is the evidence, and this is a necessary attribute of the nabīdh, while prohibition necessarily applies to khamr. In a syllogism consisting of the two premises, “All the nabīdh under dispute is intoxicating or is khamr,” and “All khamr is forbidden,” you have not used the universal—“intoxicant” or “khamr”—to establish the very subject under dispute, which is narrower in scope than either khamr or nabīdh. This is therefore not an inference from that universal to the particular. Rather, you have used it to establish that this nabīdh is forbidden. Since the prohibition of this nabīdh falls within the prohibition of every intoxicant, some have described this as an inference from the universal to the particular.

The precise account, however, is that whatever holds for a universal holds for each of its particular instances. Prohibition is broader in scope than khamr, and since it applies to khamr, it applies to every particular instance of it. This is therefore an inference from one universal establishing that another universal applies to the particular instances of the former. The evidence is like a particular in relation to that other universal, while itself being a universal in relation to those particular instances. This is something they do not dispute: the evidence is the middle term, which is broader in scope than the minor term or coextensive with it, while the major term is broader in scope than the middle term or coextensive with it. The major term is the judgment, the attribute, and the predicate; it is the predicate of the conclusion. The minor term is that about which the judgment is made, the thing described, and the grammatical subject; it is the subject of the conclusion.

As for their statement that analogy is an inference from one particular to another: if this is stated without qualification and taken to mean an inference from one particular to another merely on the basis of their being particulars, it is mistaken. Analogical reasoning establishes its conclusion only through a middle term: the two cases share the cause of the ruling, or an indication of the ruling together with the cause. It is thus either an analogy based on a cause or an analogy based on an indication.

---

## Page 4456

As for “analogy based on resemblance,” if it is accepted, it falls within one of these two categories. The shared feature connecting the original case and the new case must be either the cause itself or something that entails the cause. Yet sharing something that entails the cause does not entail sharing the ruling; rather, the shared feature may or may not be accompanied by the cause. We therefore cannot know that the analogy is valid; indeed, it is valid only if both cases share the cause. We cannot know that they share it unless we know that they share either the cause itself or something that entails it, since the presence of what entails something necessitates the presence of what it entails. If we suppose that they share neither something that entails the cause nor the cause itself, the analogy is certainly invalid, since the cause would then be confined to the original case. If that is not known, the validity of the analogy is not known.

The validity of an analogy may also be known through the absence of any relevant difference between the original case and the new case, even when neither the specific cause nor its indication is known. The absence of any relevant difference necessarily means that they share the ruling. Since analogical reasoning is complete only through establishing the absence of a relevant difference or identifying a shared feature—a universal encompassing both cases that entails the ruling—and since either procedure can be cast in syllogistic form, this is not an inference that a ruling applies to one particular merely because it applies to another. Rather, it involves the ruling necessarily applying to the universal, and the universal necessarily applying to its particular instances. This is the very essence of syllogistic reasoning.

If it is asked, “How is it known that the shared feature entails the ruling?” the answer is: “By the same means through which the major premise of a syllogism is known.” Identifying the middle term amounts to identifying the shared connecting feature, while the major term necessarily applying to it amounts to the ruling necessarily applying to that shared connecting feature, as previously pointed out. One particular may also serve as evidence for another when they mutually entail one another, or when one entails

---

## Page 4457

the other, but not conversely. If the entailment arises from the essence, the inference concerns the essence; if it concerns an attribute or a ruling, the inference concerns that attribute or ruling. The defects in their restrictive classification have thus become clear.

As for their division into three types, all of them reduce to what has been stated about evidence necessarily entailing what it establishes. What they describe as a “conjunctive syllogism” can be cast in the form of an “exceptive syllogism”; likewise, an “exceptive syllogism” can be cast in the form of a “conjunctive syllogism.” The matter therefore comes down to a single consideration: the material content of the argument. This content is not learned from the syllogistic form they describe. Rather, whoever understands the content sufficiently to know that this necessarily entails that understands the inference, whether or not it is cast in syllogistic form, and whether it is expressed in their terminology or otherwise. Indeed, the expressions refined by Muslim minds and tongues are far, far better than theirs.

The “conjunctive syllogism” reduces entirely to one necessary entailment followed by another, as explained. This is precisely what the “exceptive syllogism,” constructed from implicative and disjunctive conditionals, amounts to. An implicative conditional is an inference through necessary entailment: from affirming the entailing proposition—the antecedent, or condition—to affirming the entailed proposition—the consequent, or result; or from negating the entailed proposition—the consequent, or result—to negating the entailing proposition—the antecedent, or condition.

As for the “disjunctive conditional,” which scholars of legal theory call “testing

---

## Page 4458

and division,” and which dialecticians may also call “division and the posing of alternatives,” it amounts to inferring the negation of one of two contradictories from the affirmation of the other, and the affirmation of one from the negation of the other. “It has four types.” Thus, in a disjunction that precludes both joint truth and joint falsity, all four forms of exceptive inference apply: if this is affirmed, its contradictory is negated, and likewise for the other; if this is negated, its contradictory is affirmed, and likewise for the other.

A disjunction that precludes joint truth involves inferring the negation of one of two contraries from the affirmation of the other, since the two are mutually incompatible. A disjunction that precludes joint falsity involves both contradiction and necessary entailment. Two contradictories cannot both be false, so it precludes their both being absent. However, it concerns the existence of one thing and the nonexistence of another, not the existence and nonexistence of the same thing. The existence of one thing and the nonexistence of another may stand in a relation whereby one necessarily follows from the other, even though they cannot both be absent: their simultaneous absence would entail the simultaneous absence of both the existence and the nonexistence of a thing.

In short, everything has something it necessarily entails, without which it cannot exist, and something incompatible with and opposed to its existence. Its existence can therefore be inferred from the existence of something that necessarily entails it, and its nonexistence from the absence of something it necessarily entails. Its nonexistence can also be inferred from the existence of something incompatible with it, and its existence from the absence of that incompatible thing, provided the possibilities are confined to these two, so that they can neither both be absent nor both exist.

Such inference yields knowledge of a thing’s states, what necessarily entails them, and what they necessarily entail. Once the natural disposition conceives of this, it expresses it in various ways and casts it into various forms of argument. None of this is confined to the form they describe for syllogism, let alone what they call demonstration. For demonstration, they stipulate particular material content: the propositions they have specified. They exclude from self-evident first principles what they call “estimative propositions” and what they call “commonly accepted propositions,” although the natural disposition assents to both—especially what they call estimative propositions—more strongly than it assents to many of the certainties they have made the material content of demonstration.

---

## Page 4459

I have discussed this at length, explaining their statements on the matter and their contradictions, and showing that their exclusions also exclude the means of attaining the noblest branches of theoretical and practical knowledge. They are left with nothing but things conceived in the mind that have no reality outside it. Were there room here to quote their own words on this subject and the objections I have raised against them, I would do so. I have already set all this out in its appropriate places in the universal and theological sciences, for these are the sciences ultimately sought.

The discussion of “logic” arose only because they claimed that it is a rule-governed instrument whose observance protects the mind from error in thought. We therefore needed to examine this instrument: is it as they claim, or is it not?

Some of their leading authorities, when shown flaws in their statements that expose their misguidance, and unable to rebut this, say: “These are sciences that minds have refined for more than a thousand years and that learned men have accepted.”

There are several answers to this.

First: this is not the case. Men of reason superior to these have always criticized them and exposed their errors and misguidance. As for the ancients, their numerous disagreements are well known. The books recording their histories and doctrines contain accounts of these that this is not the place to recount. As for the Islamic era, Muslim speculative thinkers have written extensively to expose the unsound elements in their logical and theological principles—indeed, even their physical and mathematical principles. Every school of Muslim speculative thinkers has composed works on this, even the Rāfidah.

---

## Page 4460

And as for the testimony of all the other communities of believers and scholars that they are misguided and guilty of disbelief, this is a widespread judgment that only an obstinate opponent would deny. The believers are Allah’s witnesses on earth. Since the foremost intelligent and distinguished figures of the various schools, together with the rest of the people of knowledge and faith, openly declare them mistaken and misguided, whether in general or on particular points, it cannot be true that all people of reason have accepted their teachings.

(Second response)

This is not a valid argument. Aristotle criticized much of the philosophy that preceded him and had been accepted by his predecessors, exposing their errors. Ibn Sīnā and his followers disagreed with the ancients on a number of their doctrines and demonstrated their errors. Philosophers refute one another more than the members of any other school refute one another. Abū al-Barakāt and others like him have refuted Aristotle extensively, as Allah has willed, for they say: “We seek only the truth; our aim is not partisan allegiance to any particular thinker or doctrine.”

(Third)

The religion of the idol-worshippers is older than their philosophy, and the communities that have embraced it are more numerous than those that have embraced their philosophy. Likewise, the altered religion of the Jews is older than Aristotle’s philosophy, while the altered religion of the Christians arose close to Aristotle’s time. Aristotle lived approximately three hundred years before the Messiah, during the time of Alexander son of Philip, from whom the Roman era used by the Jews and Christians is reckoned.

---

## Page 4461

(Fourth)

It may be said: Grant that this is so. These disciplines are purely rational: they do not rest on following anyone’s authority, but are known through reason alone. They therefore cannot be validated by transmitted reports; indeed, they must be discussed solely on rational grounds. When clear reason demonstrates the falsity of their erroneous elements, its judgment cannot be rejected. Their adherents have not claimed that these disciplines derive from any source that must be believed, but rather from pure reason. They must therefore be judged according to what clear reason requires.

Section

They have argued, as mentioned, that induction is inferior to syllogistic reasoning—specifically, subsumptive syllogism—and that reasoning by analogy is inferior to induction. They say:

“Reasoning by analogy yields only probability, and the subject of its judgment may be a particular. Induction, by contrast, may yield certainty, and the subject of its judgment can only be a universal.”

They say:

“This is because induction consists in judging a universal on the basis of what has been established in its particular instances. If this has been established in all its instances, the induction is complete. An example is judging that everything that moves is corporeal, since corporeality is predicated of all the particular instances of moving things: inanimate objects, animals, and plants. Incomplete induction is exemplified by judging that an animal, when eating, moves its lower jaw as it chews, because this is found in most instances. Yet the opposite may be true of those instances that have not been examined

---

## Page 4462

—as in the case of the crocodile. The former is useful in matters of certainty, unlike the latter, although the latter is useful in dialectical reasoning. As for ‘reasoning by analogy,’ it consists in assigning to one thing the judgment assigned to another on the basis of a shared feature. For example, they say: ‘The world exists, so it is eternal, like the Creator’; or, ‘It is corporeal, so it was brought into existence, like a human being.’ Such reasoning comprises a secondary case, a primary case, a cause, and a judgment. In these examples, the secondary case is the world; the primary case is the Creator or a human being; the cause is existence or corporeality; and the judgment is being eternal or having been brought into existence.”

They said:

“It differs from induction in that the subject of its judgment may be a particular, whereas the subject of a judgment reached through induction can only be a universal.”

They said:

“It does not yield certainty. Two things sharing a common feature need not also share the judgment assigned to one of them, unless that common feature is shown to be the cause of that judgment. Yet everything offered to establish this yields only probability. For, according to those who accept this method, the supporting arguments in rational matters are confined to co-presence and co-absence, and division and elimination.

“As for co-presence and co-absence, this means nothing more than the necessary concomitance of the judgment and its cause, both in their presence and in their absence. Establishing this necessarily requires induction, but there is no way to claim it for the secondary case, since it is not

---

## Page 4463

what is sought; the induction is therefore incomplete. This is especially so because the cause of the judgment in the primary case may consist of shared attributes together with other attributes, all actually present in that case. When the shared feature is present in the primary case, the judgment holds because its cause is complete; when it is absent, the judgment ceases to hold because the cause is incomplete. It therefore does not follow that the presence of the shared feature in the secondary case establishes the judgment, since the remaining attributes, or some of them, may be absent.

“As for ‘division and elimination,’ it amounts to claiming that the attributes of the primary case are confined to a specified set, then ruling out all but the one retained. This, too, does not yield certainty. The judgment may hold in the primary case because of the very nature of that case, rather than because of something extrinsic; otherwise, an infinite regress would follow. Even if it holds because of something extrinsic, it always remains possible that this is something other than the enumerated attributes, even though inquiry has failed to uncover it. Matters of ordinary experience are different: when our sight is sound and nothing obstructs it, we have no doubt that there is no sea of mercury or mountain of gold before us if we do not see one.

“Even if the possible causes are confined to the enumerated attributes, the judgment may be explained by all of them together, or by some that are not present in the secondary case. The presence of the judgment alongside the shared feature in a particular case, despite the absence of other attributes accompanying it in the primary case, does not establish that this feature alone accounts for the judgment. In that particular case, the judgment may have another cause, and there is nothing impossible about this. Even if it has no cause other than the shared feature, that feature may be a cause by virtue of its particular character rather than its general nature. If it is demonstrated that the attribute entails the judgment by virtue of its general nature, then—quite apart from the difficulty of establishing this—reasoning by analogy becomes unnecessary.”

---

## Page 4464

They say:

“Physiognomy based on bodily features is itself reasoning by analogy, except that the common feature linking the primary and secondary cases is an indication of the cause, not the cause itself. This is what jurists call ‘analogy based on an indication’: one infers the existence of a cause from one of its effects, then infers its other effect from the existence of that cause. This rests on the premise that bodily temperament causes both an inward disposition and an outward feature. The outward feature thus indicates the temperament, which in turn indicates the inward disposition. An example is inferring courage from breadth of the upper body, on the grounds that both result from the same temperament, as is found in the lion.

“Establishing the cause in the primary case necessarily requires covariation or division, as explained above. Even if we assume that the two judgments in the primary case have a single cause, nothing prevents one of them from holding in the secondary case through a cause other than that operative in the primary case. In that event, the other judgment does not necessarily follow.”

Such is their argument.

The response is:

Their distinction between syllogistic reasoning and reasoning by analogy—that the former may yield certainty, whereas the latter yields only probability—is invalid. Rather, whenever either yields certainty, the other does too; and whenever either yields only probability, the other likewise yields only probability. Whether an argument yields certainty or probability does not depend on its taking one of these two forms rather than the other. It depends instead on whether it contains something that yields certainty. If either contains something that entails the judgment with certainty, certainty is attained through it. If it contains only something that establishes the judgment as probable, it yields only probability.

What is called the middle term in one is the shared attribute in the other. And the major premise, which states that the major term necessarily follows from the middle term, explains the efficacy of the attribute

---

## Page 4465

shared by the primary and secondary cases. Whatever establishes the truth of the major premise also establishes that the shared attribute necessarily entails the judgment. Thus, the major term’s following necessarily from the middle term is the same as the judgment’s following necessarily from the shared attribute.

If you say:

“Nabīdh is forbidden by analogy with wine, because wine was forbidden only because it is intoxicating, and this attribute is present in nabīdh,”

this is equivalent to saying: “All nabīdh is intoxicating, and whatever is intoxicating is forbidden.”

The conclusion is your statement: “Nabīdh is forbidden.” “Nabīdh” is its subject and the minor term; “forbidden” is its predicate and the major term. “Intoxicating” mediates between the subject and predicate and is the middle term: the predicate of the minor premise and the subject of the major premise.

If you say:

“Nabīdh is forbidden by analogy with grape wine, because the cause in the primary case is its intoxicating effect, which is also present in the secondary case; the prohibition therefore applies because its cause is present,”

you have inferred the prohibition of nabīdh from its intoxicating effect, which is the middle term. In reasoning by analogy, however, you have additionally mentioned the primary case through which the ruling on the secondary case is established. This is because the mind’s awareness of a case analogous to the secondary case yields firmer knowledge than merely subsuming it under the universal shared attribute. Once evidence establishes the efficacy of the shared attribute, mentioning the primary case is unnecessary.

Analogy necessarily proceeds either by identifying the shared attribute or by eliminating any relevant difference. The “shared attribute” is either the cause or an indication of it. In analogy based on eliminating any relevant difference, that elimination is itself the middle term.

---

## Page 4466

Thus, if it is said:

“This is equal to that, and things equal to the same thing are equal to one another,”

equality is the middle term. Eliminating any relevant difference is simply an expression of equality.

If it is said:

“There is no difference between the secondary and primary cases except such-and-such, which is impossible,”

this is equivalent to saying: “This is equal to that, and the judgment applying to one of two equals applies to the other.”

As for their statement:

“Everything that indicates that the shared attribute is the cause of the judgment is conjectural,”

we reply:

“We do not concede this. It is a universal claim for which you have supplied no evidence.”

We further say: What establishes that the shared attribute is the cause also establishes the truth of the major premise. Whatever establishes the truth of the major premise in syllogistic reasoning also establishes that the shared attribute is the cause in analogical reasoning, whether that evidence is conclusive or conjectural. For the shared attribute in analogy is the middle term. The judgment’s following necessarily from that attribute is the major term’s following necessarily from the middle term. The middle term’s following necessarily from the minor term is the shared attribute’s following necessarily from the minor term—namely, the presence of the cause in the secondary case.

Thus, if the shared attribute—called the common attribute, the cause, the indication of the cause, the basis of the judgment, or whatever other name it may bear—is present in the secondary case and necessarily belongs to it, this establishes the truth of the minor premise. If the judgment applies to the attribute and follows necessarily from it, this establishes the truth of the major premise. Mentioning the primary case serves to establish

---

## Page 4467

one of the two premises. If the analogy proceeds by eliminating any relevant difference, a specific primary case is indispensable, for the shared attribute is the equality and equivalence of the two cases. This elimination of any relevant difference is the middle term. If the analogy proceeds by identifying the cause, mentioning the primary case may be unnecessary when establishing the attribute’s causal character does not depend on it. But if establishing its causal character requires the primary case, that case is mentioned because it completes the evidence establishing that the shared attribute is the cause; this is the major term.

Those who distinguish between analogical and syllogistic reasoning have sought to show that one of them is conjectural in particular subject matters. Yet those subject matters that yield only conjecture in analogical reasoning likewise yield only conjecture in syllogistic reasoning. Conversely, when they employ it in matters in which syllogistic reasoning yields certainty, analogical reasoning also yields certainty. Indeed, the certainty it yields is even more evident there.

Thus, if the following syllogism is stated:

“Every human is an animal, and every animal is a body; therefore, every human is a body,”

“animal” is the middle term. It is also the shared attribute in analogical reasoning, expressed by saying: “A human is a body by analogy with a horse and other animals,” for those animals’ being animals necessarily entails their being bodies.

If the causal basis of the judgment in the primary case is disputed, and someone objects, “We do not concede that being an animal necessarily entails being a body,” this is a dispute over the statement, “Every animal is a body.” For when the attribute shared by the primary and secondary cases is called a cause, what is meant is simply that which necessarily entails the judgment, whether it is the cause that necessitates the judgment’s existence in external reality or something that necessarily entails it.

---

## Page 4468

Some people call all of these causes, especially those who maintain that a cause means only an indicator—a sign, mark, or proof—not a motive or incentive. Those who say that it may mean an incentive, that is, a motive, say this concerning the causes of actions. As for things other than actions, a cause may be understood as an attribute that necessarily entails another, as being human necessarily entails being an animal, and being an animal necessarily entails being a body, even if neither attribute produces the other.

However, we have explained elsewhere that the same grounds on which one knows that an animal is a body also establish that a human is a body. There we explained that the syllogistic reasoning they describe is of little or no benefit, and that, in rational matters, the grounds establishing the truth of the major premise also establish the truth of its individual instances, among which is the minor premise. Indeed, those same grounds establish the truth of the conclusion.

He then said:

Their contradictions and the unsoundness of their assertions are too numerous to recount. Our purpose here is to discuss “logic” and what they have said about demonstration: they exalt syllogistic reasoning and disparage analogical reasoning, claiming that the latter yields only conjecture and that certainty can be attained only through the former. This is not so. In reality, the two belong to the same category. Sound analogical reasoning is better suited than syllogistic reasoning alone to establishing the desired conclusion, whether with certainty or conjecture.

For this reason, rational people generally employ analogical reasoning more often than syllogistic reasoning. Indeed, syllogistic reasoning concerning a universal matter is valid only through the mediation of analogical reasoning. Every argument used to establish the validity of syllogistic reasoning in certain cases can also be used to establish the validity of analogical reasoning in those

---

## Page 4469

cases. We have illustrated this with their assertion, “Only one proceeds from one,” which is among their best-known false theological assertions.

The same is also evident in the case of sound assertions. Syllogistic reasoning must include a universal affirmative proposition, for they agree that no conclusion follows from two negative premises or from two particular premises. A universal is universal only in the mind. Knowing that some of its instances exist in external reality helps establish knowledge of the universal affirmative proposition. When a person perceives particular external instances, he abstracts a universal attribute from them, especially when those instances are numerous. Knowledge that the shared attribute is present in an original case in external reality is the foundation of knowledge of the universal proposition. Thus, analogical reasoning is foundational to syllogistic reasoning: either it causes such reasoning to arise, or it must be said that syllogistic reasoning cannot exist without it. How, then, can syllogistic reasoning alone be stronger than analogical reasoning?

These thinkers illustrate universals with statements such as: “The whole is greater than the part”; “Two contradictories cannot both be true or both be false”; and “Things equal to the same thing are equal to one another.” Many external instances of every one of these universals are already known. When one wishes to establish such a universal proposition firmly in the mind, one illustrates it with a particular instance and demonstrates either that there is no relevant difference between it and the other instances, or that a common attribute is present. The intellect then judges that the ruling applies to that shared universal attribute. This is the very essence of analogical reasoning.

---

## Page 4470

Even if we suppose that syllogistic reasoning does not depend on analogical reasoning, and that knowledge of universal propositions does not depend at all on knowledge of any particular instance, one still cannot say that knowing a universal, together with knowing that some of its instances exist in external reality, is less complete than knowing it without knowledge of that particular instance. Knowledge of the particular only makes that knowledge more complete. Thus, the form of inference they reject is more complete than the one they affirm.

Know that in “metaphysical logic”—indeed, even in “natural logic”—they altered some of what Aristotle had stated. Yet their additions in metaphysics are better than Aristotle’s own statements, for I have examined both accounts. In metaphysics, Aristotle and his followers are far, far more ignorant than the Jews and Christians. In natural philosophy, however, most of what he says is sound. As for logic, his statements on it are better than his statements on metaphysics.

Their disparagement of “analogical reasoning” comes only from their later thinkers, who observed that jurists commonly employed it. Jurists frequently use it with content that yields only probable knowledge. Here, however, the probability arises from the content, not from the form of the inference. Had they cast that content in syllogistic form, it would likewise have yielded only probable knowledge. These thinkers, however, supposed that the weakness lay in the form. They therefore deemed their own form of inference productive of certainty, and the jurists’ form productive only of probability. They illustrated this with examples drawn from speculative theology, seeking to establish their contention: “The speculative theologians argue against us using merely probabilistic inferences.” One example they gave of an argument used against them was that the celestial sphere is a composite body and therefore originated in time, by analogy with a human being and other generated beings. They then proceeded to discredit this inference. Yet it was only

---

## Page 4471

because of the weakness of its content that they discredited it. For the arguments advanced by the Jahmiyyah, the Qadariyyah, and those among the Ashʿariyyah and others who agree with them to establish that bodies originated in time are weak because of their content, not because their form yields only probability. Hence, it makes no difference whether they cast them in analogical or syllogistic form.

Section

As for (the fourth point)—their claim that syllogism or demonstration yields propositional knowledge—it is the most subtle of these points.

[For the logicians’ error concerning the three points—namely, denying that conception is possible except through definition, attaining conception through definition, denying that judgment is attained through definition, and denying that judgment is attained through syllogism—is clear upon the slightest reflection. The grounds for recognizing it are readily accessible, and knowledge of it is evident] (*).

They merely confuse people through intimidating displays and prolixity. Their most obviously erroneous claim is that the conceptions sought can be attained only through the definitions they prescribe. Next comes their assertion that none of the judgments sought can be attained except through the syllogisms they prescribe. There is no way to know that such a sweeping denial is true, nor is there any evidence whatsoever to support it. Indeed, its falsity is known from the judgments sought that are attained without their prescribed syllogisms, just as conceptions sought are attained without their prescribed definitions—unlike the present point

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd states (p. 79):
> “For the error in the three premises—namely, denying that conception (tasawwur) is possible except through definition (hadd), asserting that definition yields conception, and denying that judgment (tasdīq) can be attained except through syllogism (qiyās)—is clear upon the slightest reflection. The grounds for recognizing it are readily accessible, and knowledge of it is evident.”
> 
> The three preceding positions mentioned by the Shaykh, may Allah have mercy on him, are:
> The first: Conception can be attained only through definition (8/83).
> The second: Definition yields a conception of things (8/88).
> The third: No judgment can be known except through syllogism (8/103).

---

## Page 4472

—the fourth—for it is indeed true that a syllogism consisting of two premises yields a conclusion.

However, Muslim thinkers, in their discussions of this Greek logic attributed to Aristotle, have shown that the syllogistic forms and content these logicians set out offer no benefit in attaining knowledge, despite the immense effort involved. Everything that can be known through their syllogism can be known without it. Their syllogism therefore imparts no knowledge of anything unknown that could not be known without it, and there is no need for something that can be dispensed with in acquiring knowledge. Thus, its presence or absence makes no difference to the attainment of knowledge. Moreover, it involves much exhausting prolixity. Besides contributing nothing to knowledge, it tires the mind, wastes time, and entails much nonsensical rambling. Yet the purpose of arguments and demonstrations is to elucidate knowledge and explain the paths leading to it.

They said: This does not yield the knowledge sought; rather, it may actually impede its attainment because of the considerable mental fatigue it entails. It is like someone wishing to travel to Mecca or another city. If he follows the familiar, direct road, he arrives in a short time with moderate effort. But if someone happens to lead him along roundabout routes—‘asf in Arabic means proceeding off the path, so that the guide leads him round in circles and takes him along paths that veer off course—he will suffer great fatigue before reaching the direct road, if he ever reaches it. Otherwise, he may arrive somewhere other than his intended destination, and thus come to hold false beliefs. He may also be unable to continue because of

---

## Page 4473

the fatigue and exhaustion he suffers, so that he neither attains his goal nor finds rest. This is if he remains in simple ignorance. The same is true of these men.

Thus, someone present at the death of al-Khūnajī, the leading logician of his time, reported that he said as he lay dying:

“I am dying knowing nothing except that the contingent depends on the Necessary.”

Then he said:

“Dependence is a negative attribute. I am dying without having learned anything.”

Such is their condition when all they ultimately reach is simple ignorance. As for those who end up in compound ignorance, they are many.

Those among them who do attain knowledge are likened to someone asked, “Where is your ear?” He circles his hand over his head and reaches his ear with difficulty, although he could have brought his hand to his ear from below—a shorter and easier route.

When matters grasped innately are approached through methods contrary to that innate disposition, this only torments people without benefiting them. It is as though a man were told, “Divide these dirhams equally among these people.” This can be done without difficulty. But suppose someone said to him:

“Wait! You cannot perform the division until you know its definition and distinguish it from multiplication. Division is the reverse of multiplication: multiplication consists in repeating the units of one number as many times as there are units in the other, while division consists in distributing the units of one number among the units of the other. Thus, multiplying the quotient by the divisor gives back the dividend, and dividing the product by either factor yields the other factor.”

Then it is said:

“What you have stated as the definition of multiplication is incorrect, for it only

---

## Page 4474

covers multiplication of whole numbers, not fractions. Rather, a definition encompassing both would be: multiplication is finding a quantity whose ratio to one factor is the same as the ratio of one to the other factor. Thus, if one is told, ‘Multiply one-half by one-quarter,’ the result is one-eighth, whose ratio to one-quarter is the same as the ratio of one-half to one.”

Although this is correct, it is clear that someone who has money and wishes to divide it equally among a known number of people would be tormenting himself to no benefit if he insisted on understanding all this before making the division. He might not understand this explanation, and it might present him with difficulties.

Likewise, evidence and demonstration guide one to what one seeks and lead one to the intended conclusion. Whenever something entails something else, it can be used as evidence for it. Hence the definition:

“Evidence is that which, when soundly considered, leads to knowledge or probable belief.”

The point is that whatever entails something else, such that the latter necessarily follows from it, serves as evidence and demonstration for it—whether both involve existence, both involve nonexistence, or one involves existence and the other nonexistence. Thus, invariably, the evidence entails what it establishes, and what it establishes necessarily follows from the evidence.

The evidence may consist of a single premise which, once known, makes the desired conclusion known. The person reasoning may need two premises, or may need three, four, five, or more. There is no fixed number that applies equally to everyone in every inquiry; rather, this

---

## Page 4475

depends on the inquirer’s knowledge of the nature of the conclusion sought and the evidence, of what they entail, and of what entails them. Suppose that a single premise is all he needs to know to reach the desired conclusion: the evidence that needs to be explained to him is that premise. For example, someone may know that khamr (wine) is forbidden and that the nabīdh whose status is disputed is intoxicating, yet not know that every intoxicant is khamr. He needs only this premise.

If he is told that it is authentically reported that the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Every intoxicant is khamr,”

he reaches his desired conclusion. There is no need to tell him, “Every nabīdh is intoxicating, and every intoxicant is khamr,” or, “Every intoxicant is khamr, and all khamr is forbidden.” All this is already known to him. His only uncertainty was whether the term khamr applies exclusively to certain intoxicants, as some Muslim scholars thought, or encompasses every intoxicant. Once it is established for him that the bearer of the revealed law used it generally rather than restrictively, he reaches his desired conclusion.

This hadith appears in Sahīh Muslim and is transmitted in two wordings:

“Every intoxicant is khamr.”

“And every intoxicant is forbidden.”

He did not say, “Every intoxicant is khamr, and all khamr is forbidden,” following the Greek arrangement. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is far too exalted in his knowledge and exposition to speak in the manner of their ravings. If his purpose is merely to make the ruling known, his statement needs no accompanying proof. If his purpose is to explain the evidence—as Allah has explained in the Quran the principal theological conclusions that establish faith in Allah, His messengers, and the Last Day—then he, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, is the most knowledgeable of all creation concerning the truth and the best at explaining it. Thus, it is clear that not every conclusion requires two premises, nor are all conclusions sufficiently established by

---

## Page 4476

two premises. Rather, one states whatever provides clarification and proof, whether it consists of one premise, two, or more. Where general guidance is intended—as in the Quran, which Allah sent down as an explanation for mankind—the proofs presented are those that benefit people generally. Here, only their general categories can be explained. What is specific to each individual, however, cannot be encompassed by a general rule and set out in words. Rather, what troubles him is dispelled through means particular to him, such as his supplication for himself, someone addressing him in a manner suited to his circumstances, his reflection on matters specific to his situation, and the like.

Furthermore, the syllogism they describe does not yield knowledge of any particular existing thing. Moreover, each of those universal propositions can be known by means easier than their syllogism. No universal proposition is known through their syllogism without its particulars being knowable independently of it—and perhaps more easily. For knowledge of particular things may be clearer than knowledge of universals. This is discussed at length elsewhere.

The point here is that the aim is knowledge, and the way to it is proof. Whoever knows the proof for the conclusion he seeks knows that conclusion, whether or not he arranges it in their syllogistic form. Whoever does not know its proof gains nothing from their syllogism. Nor can it be said that their syllogism distinguishes sound proofs from unsound ones. Only an ignorant person who does not understand the true nature of their syllogism would say this, for their syllogism concerns nothing beyond the structure and form of a proof. As for whether a particular proof entails its conclusion, their syllogism contains nothing that addresses

---

## Page 4477

this, either affirmatively or negatively. Rather, this depends on one’s knowledge of the premises contained in the proof. Their syllogism does not establish the truth or falsity of any premise. They address this only when discussing the matter of the syllogism—that is, the premises considered in terms of the grounds for assenting to them. Their discussion of this contains many errors, as noted elsewhere.

The point here is that the essential consideration in every demonstration and proof in the world is entailment. Whoever knows that one thing necessarily follows from another can infer the former from the latter, even without mentioning the word “entailment” or conceiving the meaning of that word. Indeed, whoever knows that one thing cannot be without another, or that if one thing is the case, another must also be the case, and so forth, has understood entailment.

Likewise, one knows that everything in existence is a sign pointing to Allah, for it depends on Him, needs Him, and must have one who brought it into being. As the Most High says:

“Or were they created by nothing, or are they themselves the creators?”

Jubayr ibn Mut‘im said: “When I heard this verse, I felt my heart split open.”

This sets out an exhaustive division of the possibilities: were they created without a creator who created them? This is impossible according to the self-evident principles of reason. Or did they create themselves? This is even more impossible. It is thus known that they have a Creator who created them.
