He—glory be to Him—presented the proof as a rhetorical question rejecting these possibilities, to show that the proposition on which His argument rests is innate, self-evident, and firmly established in human souls, such that no one can deny it.

No one whose natural disposition is sound can claim that something came into being without someone bringing it into being. Nor can he say: “This brought itself into being.”

---

## Page 4478

Many speculative thinkers follow a particular method of proving a conclusion and say: “The desired conclusion can be reached only by this method.” Their exclusion of other methods is incorrect, even if they are right about the validity of their own method. For the greater people’s need to know something, the easier Allah makes it for their minds to recognize the evidence for it. Thus, the proofs of the Creator’s existence and oneness, and the signs and proofs of prophethood, are exceedingly numerous, as are the ways people come to recognize them.

Most people have no need for many of these methods. They are needed only by those who know no others or who turn away from other methods. For some people, the subtler, more obscure, and lengthier a method is, and the more premises it contains, the more beneficial it is. This is because their minds have become accustomed to examining subtle matters. If a proof has few premises, or its premises are obvious, they take no pleasure in it. With such people, methods of speculative theology and logic, among others, may be used because they suit their habitual way of thinking—not because knowledge of the desired conclusion invariably depends on them.

Some people feel that knowing what most people know, or what even those of no particular intelligence can understand, does not distinguish them from others in knowledge. They therefore love to learn about obscure, subtle matters involving many premises. For this reason, many scholars of the Sunnah are drawn to studying sound and exact sciences, such as algebra and intricate problems concerning inheritance, bequests, and circular calculations. Such knowledge is sound in itself.

“The science of inheritance is of two kinds”: legal rulings and calculation.

Knowledge of the legal rulings is of three kinds. The first is knowledge of the rulings according to the school of a particular jurist. Next comes knowledge of the Companions’ opinions concerning matters

---

## Page 4479

concerning which there was disagreement. This is followed by knowledge of the evidence for those rulings from the Quran and Sunnah.

As for “inheritance calculation,” it consists of determining the base numbers of inheritance calculations and adjusting them to yield whole-number shares, calculating successive inheritances before an estate has been divided, and distributing estates. This second branch consists entirely of rational knowledge accessible through reason, like other calculations concerning transactions and the other kinds of calculation people need.

They have also discussed, in this connection, the calculation of unknowns, known as algebra. This is an ancient science, but the first person known to have applied it to bequests, circular calculations, and similar matters was Muhammad ibn Mūsā al-Khwārizmī. Some people relate that ʿAlī ibn Abī Tālib discussed it and learned it from a Jew. This is a fabrication falsely attributed to ʿAlī.

The term “circular dependence” is used for three kinds. The first is “circular dependence in existence,” discussed in rational arguments: this cannot exist until that exists, and that cannot exist until this exists. Some speculative thinkers used to hold that it is impossible. The correct view, as al-Āmidī and others explain, is that it comprises two kinds: “circular dependence involving priority” and “simultaneous interdependence.”

The former is impossible. It is the kind discussed in connection with causes, agents, producers of effects, and similar matters. For example, one may say that each of two things cannot bring the other into existence, because this would entail circular dependence: this would have to precede that, and that would have to precede this.

“Simultaneous interdependence,” however, is possible. It is the interdependence of a condition and what is conditional upon it, or of two mutually correlative things. For example, fatherhood exists only together with sonship, and sonship exists only together with fatherhood.

---

## Page 4480

The second type:
Circular dependence in legal rulings, as discussed in the Surayjiyya problem and elsewhere. We have devoted a separate work to this, explaining that it is invalid both rationally and under Islamic law. We have also addressed whether anything of this kind of circular dependence exists in the revealed Law or not.

(The third type:
Computational circular dependence: one says that this cannot be known until that is known. This is the kind that one seeks to resolve through arithmetic and algebra. We have explained that every question concerning the revealed Law brought by the Messenger, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, can be answered without algebraic calculation. Even if algebraic calculation is sound, we have explained that Islamic law and knowledge of it do not depend at all upon anything learned from non-Muslims, even if it is a valid method. Indeed, algebraic methods involve lengthy procedures for which Allah provides alternatives that make them unnecessary, as we have explained regarding logic.

The same applies to everything with which the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, was sent, such as knowing the direction of the qiblah, the prayer times, the onset of dawn, and the appearance of the new crescent. All this can be known through the methods followed by the Companions and those who followed them in righteousness, without their needing anything else. Many people have nevertheless devised other methods, and many of them think that knowledge of the revealed Law is impossible without them. This stems from their ignorance.

Similarly, some people think that the direction of the qiblah cannot be known without knowing the longitudes and latitudes of places. Even if this is a sound mathematical science accessible to reason, Muslims’ knowledge of their qiblah does not depend upon it. Rather, it is authentically reported from the bearer of the revealed Law

---

## Page 4481

may Allah bless him and grant him peace, that he said:

“Whatever lies between east and west is qiblah.”

Al-Tirmidhī said: “An authentic hadith.” Thus, according to the great majority of scholars, a person praying need not determine the direction by the celestial pole, the Pole Star, or anything else. Rather, if someone in Syria or a similar region places west to his right and east to his left, his prayer is valid.

Likewise, the time when the new crescent appears cannot be precisely determined by calculation. Although they know that the moon derives its light from the sun, that no light from it is visible when the two discs come into conjunction during the moon’s dark phase, and that light appears on it as it moves away from the sun, the most they can determine by calculation is its distance from the sun at sunset. This assumes that their calculation of celestial positions and their corrections are accurate. They call this the science of calculating and correcting celestial positions because they take the highest and lowest values of the celestial bodies’ motion, determine their mean, and calculate on that basis.

Even supposing that they have estimated its altitude at sunset, this provides no indication that it will or will not be visible. Visibility is a matter of sensory perception affected by numerous factors: the clarity or haziness of the air, the observer’s higher or lower vantage point, and the sharpness or weakness of eyesight. One person may not see it, while another with sharper eyesight does, and so forth.

For this reason, the early scholars of astronomy, such as Ptolemy, the author of the Almagest, and others, did not say a single word about this. Only some later scholars, such as Kūshyār al-Daylamī and others like him, addressed it. When they saw that the revealed Law made sighting the criterion, they wished to determine it by calculation

---

## Page 4482

and so they went astray and led others astray. Whoever says that it cannot be seen at twelve degrees, ten degrees, or the like is mistaken. Some people see it at less than that, while others do not see it even at that point. Thus, they neither took reason into account nor understood the revealed Law. For this reason, the accomplished experts in their own discipline rejected their claims.

He then said:

The validity of the syllogistic form is not denied. Rather, we explain that it yields no knowledge of existing things. Likewise, their stipulation of exactly two premises, neither more nor fewer, is invalid. Even if it produces certainty, it does not, as such, yield any sought-after certainty about anything that exists.

We say, then:

There is no doubt that the syllogistic form yields certainty when its content is known. If it is said, “Every A is B, and every B is C,” and both premises are known to be true, then this arrangement undoubtedly yields knowledge that every A is C. Yet we say that their elaborate account of the many figures and the conditions under which they yield conclusions is a lengthy treatment of little benefit and much trouble. Whenever the content is sound, it can be expressed in the first figure, which is naturally grasped. The remaining figures are therefore unnecessary. They yield conclusions only by being reduced to the first figure, whether by refuting the contradictory proposition, as in a proof by contradiction, or through simple conversion or contraposition. For establishing one of two contradictories entails negating the other, provided they are contradictory in every respect. Thus, from the truth of a proposition they infer the falsity of its negation and the truth of its simple converse and its contrapositive. Indeed, the mind’s conception

---

## Page 4483

of the form of the proof resembles a person’s reckoning of his holdings in slaves and real estate. The innate disposition grasps a sound syllogism without instruction. People naturally employ the three forms—inclusion, implication, and division—just as they employ arithmetic and the like. The logicians may concede this.

In short, we do not deny that a syllogism yields knowledge when its premises are known with certainty. But we say:

The knowledge obtained through it does not require the logical syllogism; rather, it can be obtained without it. Thus, no knowledge depends upon this syllogism. Moreover, the certain premises they enumerate yield no knowledge of existing things. They therefore achieve no purpose through which souls are purified, nor even knowledge of realities in the external world as they actually are, except of the same kind as that obtained through analogical reasoning. No knowledge can ever be obtained through the inclusive logical syllogism, which they call demonstrative, without that same knowledge also being obtainable through analogical reasoning, which they regard as weak. For that syllogism must contain a universal proposition.

Yet certainty that a universal proposition is indeed universal is impossible without certainty that its individual instances are alike in what they share—and this is established through analogical reasoning.

We shall explain this in several ways.

First:

They have restricted premises known with certainty to the categories familiar to them.

---

## Page 4484

The first category consists of sense perceptions. It is well known that the senses do not perceive anything universal or general at all. Mere sense perceptions therefore contain no universal proposition suitable to serve as a premise in a demonstration yielding certainty.

When they illustrate this by saying that fire burns, or something similar, they have no knowledge that this proposition holds universally. All they have is experience and customary regularity, which are of the same kind as analogical reasoning.

As for knowing this through fire’s possession of a power to burn, the knowledge that every fire must possess this power is itself a universal judgment. If it is said, “The form of fire must contain this power, and whatever lacks this power is not fire,” this claim, even if sound, does not establish with certainty that everything possessing this power burns whatever it encounters. Even if this is what usually happens, analogical reasoning, the inclusive syllogism, customary regularity, and incomplete induction all share in this—assuming this is conceded to them.

How, then, can their claim stand when it is known that fire does not burn the salamander, the ruby, or objects coated with specially prepared substances? I know of no universal proposition based on sense perception that admits of no counterexample. Indeed, a universal proposition is not itself sensory. The sensory proposition is simply that this particular fire burns, for the senses perceive only particular things.

As for intellectual judgment, they say that when the soul sees these particular instances, it is prepared to receive, by emanation, a universally applicable proposition. It is well known that this is of the same kind as analogical reasoning. Its universal applicability cannot be trusted unless it is known that the general judgment necessarily follows from the feature shared by those instances. If this is known, it is known in all the particular instances; knowledge of those instances therefore does not depend upon that universal proposition. Moreover, all rational people agree that no proposition based on customary regularity is universal in such a way that it admits of no counterexample.

---

## Page 4485

The second category consists of “inner perceptions,” such as each person’s awareness of his own hunger, pain, and pleasure. These are all particulars. Indeed, people do not share in perceiving each particular instance of these, as they may share in perceiving certain external objects, such as the sun and moon. Inner perceptions thus involve a particularity in both the perceiver and the perceived that external sense perceptions do not. Even if people share the same kinds of inner perception, these resemble judgments based on customary regularity. They have offered no proof that souls must be alike in these states, nor even that individual rational souls are alike.

The third category consists of “empirical judgments,” all of which are particular, since experience concerns only specific instances. The same applies to “judgments based on mass transmission,” for what is transmitted in this way is something known through sense perception, whether heard or seen. What is heard is a particular utterance, while what is seen is a particular body, color, action, or occurrence.

As for “intuitive judgments,” if they are regarded as yielding certainty, they are analogous to empirical judgments. The difference between them does not concern universality and particularity; rather, empirical judgments concern the actions of those conducting experiments, while intuitive judgments arise from their actions. Some people call both kinds experimental judgments.

They are therefore left with nothing but first principles, which are self-evident rational truths. Universal first principles are merely unqualified propositions concerning numbers, magnitudes, and the like—for example, “One is half of two,” and “Things equal to the same thing are equal to one another.” These are things posited in the mind; they do not exist as universals in external reality.

---

## Page 4486

It has thus become clear that the universal demonstrative propositions they employ in their syllogisms, whose universality must be known with certainty, are not applied to anything that actually exists, but only to things posited in the mind. They therefore cannot acquire knowledge of existing things through demonstrative syllogism. This is precisely the point to be established. For this reason, they have no knowledge of an exhaustive classification of everything that exists. Indeed, when Aristotle confined the genera of existing things to the “ten categories”—substance, quantity, quality, place, time, position, acting, being acted upon, possession, and relation—they agreed that there is no way to establish that this classification is exhaustive.

The second argument:

One may say: If every syllogism must contain a universal proposition, then that universal proposition must ultimately be known without a syllogism; otherwise, circularity and infinite regress would necessarily follow. Since they must therefore have universal propositions known without syllogism, we say:

Whenever our natural understanding knows a universal proposition about existing things without a syllogism, its knowledge of the particular instances falling under that proposition is stronger than its knowledge of the universal proposition itself. Examples include our statements: “One is half of two,” “A body cannot be in two places,” and “Two contraries cannot coexist.” Our natural understanding knows more firmly that this particular one is half of two than that every one is half of every two. The same applies to whatever individual instances may be supposed.

Thus, we say: the purpose of these universal propositions is either to acquire knowledge of what exists in external reality

---

## Page 4487

or to acquire knowledge of things posited in the mind.

The latter offers little benefit. As for the former, the judgment concerning any particular existing thing is clearer and more firmly known through knowing it as a particular than through a universal syllogism that encompasses it. Thus, syllogism yields little benefit here; rather, it merely prolongs the argument. Syllogism is used in such cases only for someone who is mistaken or obstinate: an example is given and the universal proposition stated to counter his error and obstinacy. Someone whose natural understanding is sound has no such need.

The same applies to their statement:

“Two contraries cannot coexist.”

Whenever two things are known to be contraries, it is known that they cannot coexist before one calls to mind the universal proposition that no two contraries can coexist. Likewise, any particular body is known to be incapable of occupying two places before one knows that no body can occupy two places. Examples of this are numerous. Thus, any particular whose knowledge is sought through these universal propositions is known before the universal proposition itself is known, and knowledge of it does not require that proposition. What is known through such propositions is only similar cases posited in the mind that have not existed in external reality. Things that exist in external reality, however, are known without this syllogism. If it is said that some people come to know certain particular things in external reality through this syllogism, then its basis is analogical inference, which they deny can yield certainty.

They therefore face two alternatives:

If they acknowledge that analogical inference belongs to the same genus as syllogistic inference and is likewise divided into certain and conjectural forms, their distinction collapses. If, however, they assert a distinction between the two, claiming that syllogistic inference can yield certainty whereas analogical inference cannot, this claim is rejected, and it is shown to them that certainty cannot be attained

---

## Page 4488

in these matters except through analogical inference. Knowledge of previously unknown particulars existing in external reality is thus attained by analogy with those already known. This is a truth that no rational person disputes. Indeed, it is among the most distinctive features of the intellect that set it apart from sense perception: sense perception apprehends only particulars, whereas the intellect apprehends things in a universal, unrestricted form, but through analogy. The intellect then apprehends all these universals even when the particular examples are absent from its awareness. Originally, however, they became general universals in the mind only after it had conceived particular examples of their individual instances.

When a long time has passed since the mind considered those particular instances, it may frequently err by making its judgment either too broad or too narrow. This often happens to people: a person supposes that the universal propositions he holds are sound, whereas scrutiny reveals otherwise. They conceive something intellectually, and what they conceive is an object of intellectual apprehension. They then discuss it, imagining that they have discussed an essence abstracted in itself, considered simply as such, without its existing either in external reality or in the mind. Thus they speak of:

“The human being as such,” “existence as such,” “blackness as such,” and the like.

They imagine that this essence, which they have stripped of all negative and affirmative qualifications, is realized in external reality in this abstracted form. This is an error like that of their predecessors concerning the numbers, Platonic Forms, and other things they abstracted. Rather, these abstractions are only posited in the mind. Not everything the mind supposes can exist in external reality. This is what is called mental possibility. For possibility is of two kinds.

---

## Page 4489

“Mental”: this is when something is presented to the mind and its impossibility is not known. One then says, “This is possible,” not because one knows it to be possible, but because one does not know it to be impossible—even though it may be impossible in external reality.

“External”: this is when something is known to be possible in external reality. Such knowledge comes from knowing that the thing itself exists, that something comparable to it exists, or that something further removed from existence than it exists. If that which is further removed from existence actually exists and is therefore possible, then that which is nearer to existence is all the more possible. This is the Quran’s method of demonstrating “the possibility of resurrection.”

It demonstrates this in several ways. Sometimes Allah tells of those whom He caused to die and then brought back to life, as He tells of the people of Moses who said:

“Show us Allah openly.”

He says:

“So the thunderbolt seized you while you looked on.”

“Then We raised you up after your death.”

He also tells of:

“Those who left their homes in their thousands, fearing death. Allah said to them, ‘Die’; then He brought them back to life.”

And of:

“Or like the one who passed by a town that had fallen in upon its roofs. He said, ‘How will Allah bring this back to life after its death?’ So Allah caused him to die for a hundred years, then raised him up.”

And of Abraham, when he said:

“My Lord, show me how You give life to the dead”—and the rest of the account.

Likewise, He tells of the Messiah, who used to bring the dead to life by Allah’s permission, and of the Companions of the Cave, who were awakened after three hundred and nine years.

At other times, He demonstrates this through the first creation, for bringing something back is easier than creating it in the first place, as in His words:

“If you are in doubt about the resurrection, then indeed We created you from dust”—and the rest of the verse.

---

## Page 4490

and His words:

“Say, ‘He who created them the first time will bring them to life.’”

“Say, ‘He who created you the first time.’”

“It is He who originates creation, then brings it back, and that is easier for Him.”

At other times, He demonstrates this through the creation of the heavens and the earth, for creating them is greater than bringing the human being back to life, as in His words:

“Do they not see that Allah, who created the heavens and the earth without growing weary in creating them, is able to give life to the dead?”

At other times, He demonstrates its possibility through the creation of vegetation, as in His words:

“It is He who sends the winds bearing good tidings,”

through to His words:

“Thus do We bring forth the dead.”

It has thus become clear that whatever truth is contained in the rational proofs advanced by the leading speculative theologians and philosophers concerning divine matters is presented in the Noble Quran, together with proofs more compelling and complete, in the finest manner. Moreover, the Quran is free of the many fallacies found in their arguments. Their errors in these matters are exceedingly numerous; indeed, their misguidance may well exceed their guidance, and their ignorance exceed their knowledge. Hence Abū ‘Abd Allāh al-Rāzī said toward the end of his life in his book *Aqsām al-Dhāt*:

“I have reflected on the methods of speculative theology and the approaches of philosophy, but I have not found them to heal the ailing or quench the thirsty. I have found the most direct path to be the way of the Quran. Read, concerning affirmation:

‘The Most Merciful rose over the Throne.’

‘To Him ascends good speech.’

And read, concerning negation:

‘There is nothing like Him.’

‘They cannot encompass Him in knowledge.’

Whoever has experienced what I have experienced will know what I have come to know.”

---

## Page 4491

The point is that possibility in external reality is known through actual existence, not merely through the absence of knowledge that something is impossible, as maintained by a group that includes al-Āmidī. Even more far-fetched than his establishing possibility in external reality through possibility in the mind is the approach taken by philosophers such as Ibn Sīnā: establishing possibility in external reality merely because something can be conceived in the mind. Thus, when they sought to establish an entity existing outside the mind that is apprehended by the intellect but cannot in any way be perceived by the senses, they argued from the conception of the unrestricted universal concept of the human being, encompassing individuals existing outside the mind. Yet this establishes only the possibility of such concepts existing in the mind, for a universal exists as a universal only in the mind. How can their methods of establishing possibility in external reality compare with the Quran’s method?

They then draw analogies through these unsound methods, seeking to make people abandon the certain knowledge and rational proofs innate to them, as well as the divine reports about Allah and the Last Day brought by the Messengers. They seek to turn such false propositions and corrupt imaginings into rational principles with which to challenge the signs that Allah sent His Messengers with and revealed in His Books, the truths He made innate to His servants, and what is established by rational proofs admitting of no doubt.

Through their principles, they have corrupted knowledge derived from both reason and revelation. Reason rests on the soundness and integrity of the innate disposition, while knowledge derived from revelation rests on affirming the truthfulness of the Prophets, may Allah bless them. The Prophets, may Allah bless them, perfected both for humanity: they directed people to the rational proofs through which they could attain knowledge of matters accessible to reflection and inference. Alongside this, they informed them of details of the unseen that they could not discover through reflection and inference alone.

---

## Page 4492

The teaching of the Prophets, peace and blessings be upon them, is not confined to merely conveying reports, as many suppose. Rather, they expounded rational proofs through which knowledge of divine matters is attained—proofs that these men do not possess at all.

Their teaching, may Allah bless them, thus encompasses both rational and revealed proofs. By contrast, the teaching of those who oppose them provides neither rational nor revealed proofs, while their souls harbour a pride whose ambitions they will never fulfil. As Allah Most High says:

“Those who dispute Allah’s signs without any authority having come to them have nothing in their hearts but an ambition for greatness which they will never attain. So seek refuge in Allah. Indeed, He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.”

He also says:

“Those who dispute Allah’s signs without any authority having come to them—this is greatly detested by Allah and by those who believe. Thus does Allah seal the heart of every arrogant tyrant.”

And He says:

“When their Messengers came to them with clear proofs, they rejoiced in the knowledge they possessed, and what they used to ridicule engulfed them.”

There are many similar passages in the Quran. I have written a book entitled “Repelling the Conflict between Revelation and Reason.”

Thus, because they conceived things in their minds which they thought existed in external reality, most of their knowledge in “divine science” and “mathematics” was built upon this. When someone well versed in the realities of things examines their discussions across their various sciences, he finds that they possess no knowledge of things existing in external reality except in the branch they call “natural science” and the mathematics connected with it. As for “mathematics” abstracted in the mind, it consists of judgements concerning mental quantities that have no existence in external reality. As for what they call “metaphysics,” if it is carefully examined:

---

## Page 4493

it is found to contain no knowledge of anything existing in external reality. Rather, they have conceived things posited in their minds that have no reality outside them. Thus, the ultimate outcome of their inquiry, and the culmination of their philosophy and wisdom, is absolute, universal existence, and existence conditioned upon the negation of all positive realities. The point is that, in demonstrative inquiries and rational matters, they often advance claims concerning things they have merely posited in their minds.

They say, “We are discussing universals and purely rational matters.” When something is mentioned to them, they say, “We are discussing something more general than that, and reality considered in itself,” and similar expressions. They are then challenged to establish the external reality of what they have described and are asked, “Explain what exactly this is.” At that point, their ignorance becomes apparent: what they speak of is something posited in the mind, with no reality among actually existing things.

For example, they may be told, “Give an example of that.” An example is a particular instance. If they cannot provide an example and say, “We are discussing universals,” then know that they are speaking without knowledge. They are speaking about matters which they do not know to have any corresponding object in external reality—indeed, about matters which have no corresponding object in external reality, and matters which cannot possibly have one. For when knowledge of existing things is universal, the objects of that knowledge do exist in external reality.

Al-Khusrawshāhī was one of their leading figures and one of al-Rāzī’s foremost associates. He used to say, “All we have found are these universals.” He had fallen into such perplexity and doubt that he would say, “By Allah, I do not know what to believe! By Allah, I do not know what to believe!”

---

## Page 4494

The point is that whatever knowledge of universals they claim to possess—if it is indeed knowledge—can be known through analogical reasoning; it does not depend at all on the categorical syllogism of logic. Indeed, the particular instances they seek to establish through this syllogism can be known without it, more readily and easily. To establish them through what they call a demonstrative syllogism is to argue for what is more evident on the basis of what is less evident. In the art of definition, they themselves criticize defining what is clear by means of what is obscure. Yet this is an even greater fault in the art of demonstration, since the sole purpose of demonstration is to explain what is being demonstrated, make it known, disclose it, and clarify it. If that object is already clearer and more evident, this amounts to explaining what is clear by means of what is obscure.

He said:

Moreover, the philosophers who adhere to this demonstrative logic devised by Aristotle, together with the physics and metaphysics that follow it, are not a single community but disparate groups. Their divisions and disagreements, which only Allah can enumerate, are many times greater than those within any one religious community, such as the Jews or the Christians.

The further people stray from following the messengers and the revealed Books, the greater their divisions and disagreements become, for they become more deeply misguided. Thus, in the hadith narrated by al-Tirmidhī from Abū Umāmah, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“No people have gone astray after following guidance without becoming given to disputation.” He then recited the words of the Most High: “They cite this to you only for the sake of argument. Rather, they are a contentious people.”

For nothing can judge between people concerning their disputes except a revealed Book and a divinely sent prophet. As the Most High said: “Mankind was one community; then Allah sent the prophets as bearers of glad tidings and warners, and sent down with them the Scripture in truth

---

## Page 4495

to judge between people concerning their disputes,” and so on in the verse. He also said:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs, and sent down with them the Scripture and the balance, so that people might uphold justice.”

And He said:

“If you disagree over anything, refer it to Allah and the Messenger,” and so on in the verse.

In His Book, Allah has set forth illustrative examples and rational arguments through which truth can be distinguished from falsehood. Allah has commanded unity and harmony, forbidden division and disagreement, and declared that those granted His mercy do not disagree. He said:

“They will continue to disagree, except those upon whom your Lord has mercy.”

Thus, those who follow the Messenger most closely have fewer disagreements than any of the groups professing adherence to the sunnah. The closer people are to the sunnah, the fewer their disagreements compared with those further removed from it, such as the Muʿtazilah and the Rāfidah, whom we find to be the groups with the most disagreements.

As for the disagreements among the philosophers, no one can enumerate them. In his Kitāb al-Maqālāt (Book of Doctrines), Imam Abū al-Hasan al-Ashʿarī discussed “the doctrines of non-Muslims,” presenting a vast number beyond those mentioned by al-Fārābī and Ibn Sīnā. Likewise, Qādī Abū Bakr ibn al-Tayyib did so in his book al-Daqāʾiq (Subtle Matters), in which he refuted the philosophers and astrologers and argued for the superiority of the logic of the Arab speculative theologians over Greek logic. The speculative theologians of the Muʿtazilah, the Shīʿah, and others likewise did so in their refutations of the philosophers. Al-Ghazālī also wrote al-Tahāfut (The Incoherence) to refute them. Muslim scholars engaged in rational inquiry have continually written works refuting their logic and exposing their errors

---

## Page 4496

in what they said concerning both definition and syllogism, just as they expose their errors in metaphysics and other subjects. None of the Muslim scholars engaged in rational inquiry paid heed to their method. Rather, the Ashʿarīs, Muʿtazilah, Karrāmiyyah, Shīʿah, and all the other groups engaged in rational inquiry criticized it and demonstrated its unsoundness. The first to mix their logic with the foundational principles of the Muslims was Abū Hāmid al-Ghazālī. Muslim scholars criticized him in terms that would take too long to recount.

Such refutations of them appear in many works of speculative theology. The book al-Ārā wa-l-Diyānāt (Opinions and Religions), by Abū Muhammad al-Hasan ibn Mūsā al-Nawbakhtī, contains a good section on this subject. After describing Aristotle’s method in logic, he said:

“Some Muslim speculative theologians objected to these formulations of logic, saying that the logician’s claim that a syllogism cannot be constructed from a single premise is mistaken. For example, someone wishing to establish that a human being is a substance can argue directly for the very point at issue without first setting out two premises. He can say: ‘The proof that a human being is a substance is that he admits contrary attributes at different times.’ He has no need of a second premise, namely: ‘Everything that admits contrary attributes at different times is a substance.’ For the particular is included in the general, so whichever of the two he establishes, he has no need of the other. Likewise, when a person observes an effect, he may infer that it has a cause, and when he observes writing, that it has a writer, without needing two premises to establish the validity of that inference.

---

## Page 4497

They said: ‘Our response is that two premises are indispensable. When one is stated, the other need not be mentioned because the person addressed already knows it. The speaker therefore omits it, having no need to state it.’ We replied: ‘We do not find two universal premises by which the truth of a conclusion is established. For when someone says, “Every living being is a substance, and every human being is alive,” the conclusion is: “Every human being is a substance.” Yet, to the intellect, saying “Every living being is a substance” is on the same footing as saying “Every human being is a substance.” In practical inquiries, they cannot find a conclusion that depends upon two self-evident premises. If that is the case, one of them suffices. We say to them: Show us two primary premises requiring no prior demonstration, by which a disputed point is established, with both premises more readily acceptable to reason than the conclusion. If you cannot find this, your claim is invalid.’”

Al-Nawbakhtī said: “I have asked more than one of their leading figures to produce such an example, but none has produced one. What Aristotle described is therefore neither found nor known.”

He said: “As for the two remaining figures he subsequently mentioned, neither is used in the form in which he constructed it. If they become valid by converting their premises until they revert to the first figure, then what has been said about the first figure applies to them as well.” End of quotation.

Ibn Taymiyyah said:

His point is that the other figures yield conclusions only by being reduced to the first figure, as explained earlier. Thus, the other figures and the derivation of their conclusions involve needless effort and difficulty, since the first figure can accommodate all affirmative and negative propositions

---

## Page 4498

whether universal or particular. Since its lack of benefit has been established, its derivative forms—which yield results only by being reduced to it—are all the more clearly without benefit. The point is that this community—praise be to Allah—has always included people who discern and refute the falsehood in the statements of those who uphold it. Through Allah’s guidance, they agree in accepting truth and rejecting falsehood, whether in reasoned judgment or transmitted reports, without being aware of one another’s views or acting in concert. What these thinkers have drawn attention to agrees with what we have pointed out. It shows that logical syllogisms can be dispensed with; indeed, using them merely prolongs and multiplies thought, inquiry, and discourse to no benefit.

The third point:

General, universal propositions do not exist as universals in external reality. They are universal only in the mind, not in concrete existence. Things that exist externally, however, are particular entities. Every existent has a reality peculiar to it, distinguishing it from everything else, which no other thing shares. Consequently, a syllogism cannot establish the existence of a specific, individual entity. They themselves acknowledge this, stating that a syllogism does not indicate a particular entity. They sometimes express this by saying that it indicates only a universal, not a particular.

Thus, syllogistic reasoning does not yield knowledge of any individually existing thing. Yet everything that exists exists as an individual. It therefore yields no knowledge of the realities of existing things, but only unrestricted universals conceived in the mind, not realized in concrete existence. Accordingly, none of the syllogistic arguments that these thinkers call demonstrations establishing the Creator—glorified be He—identifies Him specifically. They indicate only something conceived without qualification, whose conception does not preclude its applying to more than one.

---

## Page 4499

Thus, when someone says, “This has been brought into existence, and everything brought into existence must have an originator,” this points only to an unrestricted, universal originator whose conception does not preclude its applying to more than one. His specific identity is known only through another kind of knowledge that Allah places in people’s hearts. They themselves acknowledge this, because the conclusion cannot go beyond the premises, and the premises must necessarily include a universal proposition.

A universal does not identify a particular entity. This differs from the signs Allah mentions in His Book, as in His words, Exalted is He:

“Indeed, in the creation of the heavens and the earth”

—and the rest of the verse, along with other such passages. These signs point to a particular entity, just as the sun is the sign of daytime. Evidence is broader than syllogistic reasoning: one particular thing may serve as evidence for another, just as a star and other heavenly bodies are used to determine the direction of the Kaaba. Thus, the signs point to the Creator Himself—glorified be He—not to something shared between Him and another. Everything other than Him depends upon Him Himself; hence, its existence necessarily entails the existence of the Creator Himself.

The fourth point:

The middle term repeated in a universal syllogism—namely, “wine” in the statement, “Every intoxicant is wine, and all wine is forbidden”—is the basis of the ruling in analogical reasoning: the common feature linking the original case and the new case. The two forms of reasoning therefore entail one another. Whatever can be known through either can also be known through the other. Moreover, if the evidence is conclusive, it is conclusive in both; if it is probable, it is probable in both.

As for the claim made by some logicians and their followers that certainty can be attained only through universal syllogistic reasoning, not through analogical reasoning, it is utterly unsound. It is the statement of

---

## Page 4500

someone who has not grasped the true nature of these two forms of reasoning.

It may be known from a revealed text that every intoxicant is forbidden, as established in the authentic hadith. In that case, universal syllogistic reasoning is not the only means of establishing the ruling; indeed, no form of inferential reasoning is required, since the ruling may be known without it. Thus, their claim that no propositional knowledge is possible except through logical syllogism is invalid, as explained earlier.

The purpose here is to show how little benefit it offers, if any. For if a proposition conveying a universal rule is known from the Messenger—namely, that every intoxicant is forbidden—then the desired conclusion has been established. Universal propositions received from the Messenger yield knowledge concerning divine matters.

As for what is derived from their sciences, its universal propositions are either refuted by counterexamples, equivalent to analogical reasoning, or incapable of yielding knowledge of particular existing things, yielding instead knowledge of mentally posited quantities, as in arithmetic and geometry. Although such knowledge applies to whatever exists with those quantities, whether a particular thing falls within its scope is known through sensory perception, not syllogistic reasoning. The syllogism therefore does not establish what is sought. Alternatively, these propositions concern matters in which they have no exclusive expertise, but which all other nations share without their logic ever crossing their minds. Moreover, analogical reasoning and universal syllogistic reasoning remain on an equal footing.

Establishing knowledge of the Creator and of prophethood does not depend on these forms of reasoning. Rather, such knowledge is attained through signs pointing to a particular entity, not something shared by several; it arises as immediate knowledge requiring no discursive reasoning. Whatever is attained concerning these matters through universal syllogistic reasoning is equivalent to what is attained through analogical reasoning: a universal notion that does not yield knowledge of what is unique to the Lord or what is unique to the Messenger unless supplemented by other knowledge.

---

## Page 4501

The fifth argument:

Concerning universal syllogistic reasoning—which consists in knowing that a predicate holds of every individual—we say: it is known, and they concede, that knowledge that certain predicates hold of certain individuals must be immediate. For if a conclusion requires two premises, the process must ultimately reach two premises known without recourse to two further premises; otherwise, it would entail circular reasoning or an infinite regress, both of which are invalid.

If two premises are known by the same means, there is no need for a syllogism. Consider, for example, knowing that every human being is an animal and that every animal is capable of sensation and voluntary movement. Knowing that every human being is capable of voluntary movement is clearer and more evident. Thus, if both premises are known by the same means and have already been established, neither needs to be demonstrated. If they are known by different means, someone who does not know one of them needs that premise demonstrated, but does not need a demonstration of the other, which he already knows. This is evident in every case posited. Their logic thus wastes time, multiplies senseless talk, and exhausts minds.

The sixth argument:

There is no doubt that the major premise is more general than the minor premise or equal to it in scope; it cannot be narrower. The conclusion is narrower than the major premise or equal to it in scope, and more general than the minor premise or equal to it; it cannot be narrower than the minor premise.

Sense perception first apprehends particulars, then proceeds from them to general propositions. It observes this human being, then that human being, and each one observed is capable of sensation and voluntary movement. We say, then: either knowledge of a general proposition is attained through a syllogism—in which case the syllogism must itself contain a general proposition. It would follow that a general proposition can be known only through another general proposition, which entails circular reasoning or an infinite regress. The process must therefore ultimately reach a universal proposition

---

## Page 4502

known immediately. This they concede. If a general proposition can be known without the mediation of a syllogism, another can likewise be known in this way. For whether a proposition is self-evident or requires reasoning is not an inherent property of it that must be the same for everyone. Rather, it is relative and depends on people’s circumstances. For someone who knows it without proof, it is self-evident; for someone who needs reflection and inference, it requires reasoning. The same applies to all other matters. Thus, if some universal propositions are known without proof or syllogism, and the dividing line is not inherent in the propositions themselves but depends on people’s circumstances, one cannot say of something Zayd knows through a syllogism that no one else can know it without a syllogism. Such a denial is false.

The seventh argument:

It has already been shown that universal syllogistic reasoning can be recast as reasoning by analogy, and vice versa.

If it is asked, “How do you know that the shared feature entails the predicate?” the answer is: “By the same means through which we know the major premise in universal syllogistic reasoning.”

Thus, if someone says, “This is an agent who performs his work with precision, and everyone who performs his work with precision is knowledgeable,” whatever is cited as the ground for this universal proposition is also present in reasoning by analogy. The latter has the additional feature that there is a case serving as the basis of comparison, in which the predicate is found together with the shared feature. In universal syllogistic reasoning, however, none of the individual instances in which the predicate is established is mentioned.

---

## Page 4503

And it is well known, as all rational people agree, that mentioning a shared universal together with some of its instances establishes it more firmly in the mind than mentioning it in isolation from all its instances.

For this reason, they say that the intellect follows sensory perception: when the senses perceive particulars, the intellect apprehends a universal feature common to them. Universals thus arise in the mind after particular instances have been known. Knowledge of particular instances is therefore among the most important means of knowing universals. How, then, could mentioning them weaken the reasoning, while omitting them would strengthen it? This is the distinctive function of the intellect: to know universals through knowledge of particulars. Whoever denies this denies the distinctive function of the human intellect. Anyone who maintains that mentioning universals without any of their particular instances is more effective than mentioning them alongside examples of those instances is obstinately denying the obvious.

Rational people agree that giving examples helps one understand universals, and that presenting a universal with an example is not the same as presenting it without one. Anyone who reflects on all the rationally known universals that people discuss in medicine, arithmetic, crafts, commerce, and other fields will find this to be the case.

A person may deny something until he sees a single instance of its kind; he then acknowledges the kind and thereby acquires a universal judgment. For this reason, Allah, glorified be He, says:

“The people of Noah denied the messengers.”

“Ād denied the messengers.”

And there are other similar statements. Only one messenger came to each of these peoples. Yet they rejected messengers as a class; their rejection was not directed specifically at that one messenger as an individual.

---

## Page 4504

Among the greatest faculties of the intellect is its ability to recognize similarity and difference. When it observes two similar things, it knows that one is like the other and applies the same judgment to both—as when it compares water with water, earth with earth, and air with air, then makes a universal judgment concerning their shared feature. When it applies corresponding judgments to particular instances and their like, while stating what they share, the explanation is clearer. This is direct analogy. When it observes two different things, such as water and earth, it distinguishes between them. This is inverse analogy.

The reflection Allah commands in His Book encompasses both direct and inverse analogy. Since He destroyed those who rejected the messengers because of their rejection, one lesson to draw is that whoever acts as they did will suffer as they suffered. One therefore avoids rejecting the messengers for fear of punishment. This is direct analogy. One also understands that whoever does not reject the messengers will not suffer that fate. This is inverse analogy, and it is the intended lesson to be drawn from those who were punished. For the intended conclusion in the new case is the opposite of the ruling in the original case, not a corresponding ruling. Lessons may be drawn through either form of analogy.

Allah Most High says:

“There was certainly in their stories a lesson for those of understanding.”

And He says:

“There has already been a sign for you in the two armies,”

up to His words:

“Indeed, in that is a lesson for those with insight.”

Allah Most High also says:

“It is Allah who has sent down the Book in truth and the balance.”

And He says:

“We certainly sent Our messengers with clear proofs and sent down with them the Book and the balance so that people might uphold justice.”

The early generations explained “and the balance” as justice, while some explained it as that with which things are weighed. The two meanings are inseparable. Allah Most High has stated that He sent this down, just as He sent down the Book, so that people might uphold justice. Thus, that by which one recognizes

---

## Page 4505

the likeness of corresponding attributes and quantities is part of the balance. The same applies to that by which one recognizes differences between dissimilar things. Thus, when we know that Allah Most High prohibited wine for the reasons He mentioned—that it turns people away from remembering Allah and from prayer, and breeds enmity and hatred among believers—and then see that nabīdh (a drink made from steeped fruit) resembles it in these respects, the shared feature constituting the underlying reason for the ruling is the balance Allah has sent down into our hearts. By it we weigh the one and treat it like the other, making no distinction between two like things. Sound analogy is therefore part of the justice Allah has commanded.

Whoever knows universals without knowing the particular possesses only the balance. Yet their purpose is to weigh things that exist outside the mind. Without their particular instances, universals would have no significance, just as there would be no need for a balance without things to weigh. Undoubtedly, when one of the two things being weighed is present and is compared with the other using the balance, the weighing is more complete than when the balance—the shared universal attribute—is in the mind, and whatever individual entity is present is weighed by it while the other is absent.

No rational person should suppose that the intellectual balance Allah sent down is Greek logic, for several reasons.

The first:

Allah sent down the balances with His scriptures in the days of Noah, Abraham, Moses, and others, before He created the Greeks. Aristotle devised this Greek logic three hundred years before Christ. How, then, could earlier nations have weighed things by it?!

---

## Page 4506

The second:

Our community, the people of Islam, has always weighed matters using rational balances. Yet no mention of this Greek logic was heard among the early generations. It appeared among Muslims only when Greek books were translated into Arabic during the reign of al-Maʾmūn, or around that time.

(The third)

Even after it was translated into Arabic and became known to them, Muslim thinkers continued to criticize and condemn it, paying no heed to it or its proponents when applying their rational and religious balances. Let no one say, “The only things peculiar to them in it are verbal conventions; otherwise, rational meanings are shared by all nations.” That is not the case; rather, it contains many unsound meanings.

Moreover, they made it the balance by which all rational balances—that is, rational inferences—are weighed. They claimed that it is a rule-governed instrument whose observance protects the mind from errors in thought. But this is not so, for if a balance required another balance, an infinite regress would follow.

Furthermore, if the natural disposition is sound, it is weighed by the rational balance; but if it is dull or corrupt, logic only increases its dullness and corruption. Thus, most of those who weigh their knowledge by it inevitably become confused and fail to present rational proofs properly. Whenever they do present them properly, they turn away from testing them by logic because of its inadequacy, prolixity, circuitousness, its rendering of clear matters obscure, and its many errors and ways of leading others into error. For when they turn away from natural, rational knowledge of particular things to universal syllogisms, they devise

---

## Page 4507

their terminology, which becomes ambiguous, encompassing both truth and falsehood. This gives rise to misguidance contrary to the very purpose of balances. These balances thus become unjust rather than just. In using them, they become defrauders:

“Who, when they take a measure from people, take in full,”

“But when they give by measure or weight to them, they give less.”

Yet how can defrauding people of wealth compare with defrauding them in matters of reason and religion? Most of them, however, do not intend to defraud. Rather, they are like someone who inherited scales from his father and uses them to weigh, sometimes to his advantage and sometimes to his disadvantage, without knowing whether they are just or unjust. The balance that Allah sent down with the Book is a just balance. It involves comparing a thing with what resembles it and what differs from it, treating like things alike and distinguishing between unlike things, through the recognition of similarities and differences that Allah has instilled in His servants’ natural dispositions and minds.

If it is asked, “If this can be known through reason, why did Allah include it among the things with which He sent the Messengers?”

The answer is: Because the Messengers set forth rational comparisons for people through which they could recognize similarities and differences. The Messengers directed and guided people to the means of recognizing justice and sound rational inferences by which religious truths are established. Prophetic knowledge is therefore not confined to reports. Rather, the Messengers—may Allah’s blessings be upon them—explained the rational knowledge through which Allah’s religion is perfected in knowledge and practice. They set forth parables, thereby perfecting the natural disposition through what they brought to its attention and guided it toward when it had been heedless of it

---

## Page 4508

or had been corrupted by the false opinions and desires that affected it, a corruption they then removed. The Quran and hadith are full of examples of this: Allah explains truths through rational comparisons and illustrative parables. He explains how to treat like things alike and distinguish between unlike things. He condemns those who depart from this, as when He says:

“Or do those who commit evil deeds think that We will make them like those who believe and do righteous deeds”—to the end of the verse.

And He says:

“Shall We then treat the Muslims like the criminals?”

“What is the matter with you? How do you judge?”

That is, this is an unjust judgment, not a just one, because it treats unlike things alike.

An example of treating like things alike is His saying:

“Are your disbelievers better than those?”

And His saying:

“Or do you think you will enter Paradise without facing trials like those who passed away before you?”—to the end of the verse.

The point is to draw attention to the fact that the rational balance is sound, as Allah has stated in His Book. It is not confined to Greek logic; rather, it consists of sound inferences that treat like things alike and distinguish between unlike things, whether formulated as syllogistic deduction or analogical inference. Analogical formulations are primary; they involve assimilating one case to another. The balance is the common element—the attribute linking the two.

(The eighth argument): Just as they restricted certainty to the syllogistic form, they also restricted it to the material they specified: propositions based on sense perception, self-evident first principles, mass-transmitted reports, experience, and intuition. Yet it is well known that there is no proof for excluding propositions other than these. Moreover, even in matters of sensory and rational knowledge and the like, they took into account only what is ordinarily shared by

---

## Page 4509

human beings. Yet they contradicted themselves in this, for all human beings share only certain sights and sounds. They all see the same sun, moon, and stars, and all see such phenomena as clouds and lightning, even though what one group sees may not be the very same thing another sees. Likewise, they share in hearing the sound of thunder. As for the speech and voices they hear from one another, humanity does not share in hearing the very same sounds; rather, each group hears what others have not heard. The same applies to most things seen.

As for smell, taste, and touch, there is no particular object that all people share in perceiving through these senses. What one group smells, tastes, and touches is not the very same thing another group smells, tastes, and touches. The objects may, however, be alike in kind without being individually identical. The same applies to what is known through mass transmission, experience, and intuition: reports may reach one group through mass transmission without reaching others in that way, and one group may experience what others have not experienced. Nevertheless, their knowledge may concern things of the same kind. For example, one group may try certain medicines, while others try medicines of the same kind. They thus share knowledge of the kind, not knowledge of the particular thing tested.

Yet, despite this, they say in logic:

“Propositions based on mass-transmitted reports, experience, and intuition are confined to those who know them; thus no demonstration based on them can establish a proof for anyone else.”

They may be answered: The same applies to things perceived through smell, taste, and touch. Indeed, shared access

---

## Page 4510

to mass-transmitted reports is more widespread among people, for a mass-transmitted report is conveyed by a large number of people, so it has many listeners who share in hearing it from that large number. By contrast, sensory perception is confined to those who have experienced it. If someone says, “I saw,” “I heard,” “I tasted,” “I touched,” or “I smelled,” how can he use this to establish a demonstration binding on someone else? Even supposing that a number of people shared those sensory perceptions with him, it does not follow that others perceived them. Those who have not perceived them can know of them only through reports.

Most of their universal knowledge concerning the properties of existing things consists of knowledge of the regular behavior of the thing concerned. This is what they call “propositions based on intuition.” Most of their rational knowledge of nature and their celestial sciences, such as astronomy, fall into the category of knowledge based on experience. No demonstration can be established in these matters, for most people know only through transmitted reports that these natural bodies and movements have been investigated experimentally. Mass transmission concerning such matters is rare. At most, an account of the relevant experience is transmitted from certain physicians or mathematicians.

The most one finds is Ptolemy saying, “This is something that so-and-so observed,” or Galen saying, “This is something I tested,” or, “So-and-so told me that he tested it.” None of this constitutes mass transmission. Even supposing that someone else also tested it, that is still a single report. Most people have not tested everything those men tested, nor have they learned through astronomical observations what those men claim to have

---

## Page 4511

learned. Even if they mention a group who made astronomical observations, this amounts, at most, to mass transmission confined to a particular group. How can someone who claims that reports mass-transmitted from the prophets cannot establish a demonstration binding upon him then establish a demonstration binding upon others through mass transmission of this kind, extol astronomy and philosophy, and claim that they constitute rational knowledge established by demonstration? Such is the state of what they regard as the foremost subject of rational demonstration. What, then, should one think of their metaphysics? When a discerning, rational person examines and reflects upon what their First Teacher, Aristotle, said about it, he learns only that they were among the most ignorant of all people concerning the Lord of the worlds, and that the unbelievers among the Jews and Christians knew more about these matters than they did.

The ninth point:

The prophets and the close servants of Allah possess knowledge through revelation and inspiration that lies beyond the syllogistic reasoning these men have described. Indeed, discernment and similar faculties also lie beyond it.

If they include such knowledge within the sensory and rational categories they have mentioned, they cannot exclude what they have not mentioned, and they are left with no defining criterion. Ibn Sīnā and his followers have stated that the propositions which must be accepted, and which constitute the material of demonstration, are self-evident first principles, sensory propositions, propositions based on experience, propositions based on intuition, and mass-transmitted propositions. Sometimes they add propositions accompanied by their terms. Yet they have offered no proof that these categories are exhaustive. Their defenders have therefore acknowledged that this classification is open-ended rather than exhaustive, and that no proof can be established for it.

Since this is so, it does not follow that everything outside their syllogistic reasoning cannot be known. Logic, then, cannot be an instrument providing rules whose observance safeguards against error. For

---

## Page 4512

if propositions that can be known by some other method are presented, they cannot be assessed by these forms of proof. Most of these logicians deny whatever has not been established through their syllogistic reasoning. This is the height of ignorance, especially when what they deny consists of reports conveyed by the prophets.

If, then, the noblest knowledge cannot be attained by their method, two consequences follow:

First, they have no grounds for denying anything for which their syllogistic reasoning provides no proof.

Second, what they know is paltry compared with what they do not know. How much more so when it is known that their knowledge brings neither salvation nor happiness!

The tenth point:

They treat genuine knowledge, which must be accepted as true, as though it were not knowledge, while treating what is false and is not knowledge as knowledge. They claim that what the prophets taught concerning knowledge of Allah, His attributes, and the Hereafter has no corresponding reality. They claim that the prophets merely presented the masses with imagined representations of these matters, so that these would help them secure their worldly welfare, not so that they might thereby know the truth; and that this amounts to lying for people’s benefit.

They say that a prophet is well versed in the practical teachings of religion, but not its doctrinal teachings. Some of them regard the philosopher as superior to every prophet, including our Prophet—may the finest blessings and peace be upon him. They do not consider it obligatory to follow any particular prophet, neither Muhammad nor

---

## Page 4513

anyone else.

Thus, when the Tatars emerged and some of them wished to embrace Islam, it is reported that one of the philosophers accompanying Hulagu advised him not to do so, saying: “His language is Arabic, and you have no need of his religious law.” Those among them who follow the Prophet in practical religious laws do not follow him in the foundations of religion and belief. Rather, they regard the Prophet as the speculative theologians regard one of the four imams. When the leading speculative theologians adhere to one of the four schools, they confine their adherence to matters of jurisprudence and do not consider themselves bound to agree with its imam on doctrinal foundations and questions of divine oneness. Indeed, they may regard their own teachers in speculative theology as superior to those imams in these matters.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, has told us about Allah, His specific names and attributes, and about the angels, the Throne, the Footstool, Paradise, and Hell. None of this can be known through their syllogistic reasoning. Likewise, he has told us about particular matters, past and future, none of which can be known through their syllogistic reasoning, whether demonstrative or otherwise. Their syllogisms yield only universals, whereas these are particular matters.

He, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, foretold specific events, even telling of the Tatars who appeared six hundred years after his prediction. Likewise, he foretold the fire that broke out before the Tatars arrived, in 655 AH. Could their syllogistic reasoning and demonstration conceivably point to a particular individual or a particular nation, let alone one possessing the characteristics he described?

---

## Page 4514

Another of their grievous errors and statements of unbelief is their claim that the Creator—exalted is He—does not know particulars, does not know Moses, Jesus, or anyone else as individuals, and knows nothing of the details of events. This claim and its refutation are discussed at length elsewhere. The point here is to recognize that they make statements of ignorance and unbelief that represent the utmost misguidance, seeking to evade an implication for whose denial they have never had any evidence.

The eleventh point:

They acknowledge things perceived through the external and internal senses, such as hunger, pain, and pleasure. Yet they deny the existence of things that some people alone may see, such as angels, jinn, and what the soul sees at death. The Quran and Sunnah explicitly affirm these things. A detailed discussion of these matters belongs elsewhere. The point here is that the unsound logical principles they have adopted—denying what has not been established to be nonexistent—have led them into ignorance and unbelief that have become a barrier to them, leaving them worse off than the disbelievers among the Jews and Christians.

The twelfth point:

It may be said that, according to them, a proposition’s being “demonstrative” means that it is known to the person reasoning from it; its being “dialectical” means that it is conceded; and its being “rhetorical” means that it is widely held, accepted, or considered probable. All these distinctions are relations and attributions incidental to the proposition. None is an inseparable attribute of it, let alone

---

## Page 4515

an essential attribute of it according to their own principles. Indeed, none of these is an attribute of the proposition in itself; rather, they are relative attributes depending on someone’s awareness of it. It is well known that a proposition may be true without a person even being aware of it, let alone considering it probable or knowing it to be true. Likewise, a proposition may be rhetorical or dialectical while being true in itself; indeed, it may also be demonstrative, as they themselves have acknowledged.

Since this is so, the Messengers—may Allah’s blessings be upon them—conveyed propositions that are true in themselves and can never be false or erroneous. They also explained generally accessible methods of knowing by which the truth of propositions can be established, thus benefiting humankind. This is knowledge that benefits people.

These would-be philosophers, however, did not follow this path. Instead, they approached propositions in relative terms: they classified as demonstrative whatever the person reasoning knew, and did not classify anything else as demonstrative, even if another person reasoning knew it. Accordingly, propositions considered demonstrative by one person or group may not be considered demonstrative by others. Propositions known to be true therefore cannot be encompassed by a comprehensive definition; rather, they vary according to the circumstances of those who know them and those who do not. Indeed, practitioners of each discipline know propositions that others do not.

Consequently, their method cannot distinguish truth from error or true statements from false ones on the basis of how matters actually stand—what is true or erroneous, truthful or false—within each discipline. Nor can its benefit be shared by all human beings. This is unlike the method of the Prophets, who conveyed true propositions that distinguish truth from error

---

## Page 4516

and true statements from false ones. Whatever contradicts a true statement is false, and whatever contradicts the truth is erroneous. For this reason, Allah made the Scripture He revealed a judge between people concerning their disagreements. He also sent down the balance and that by which things are weighed and truth is distinguished from error. Every truth has a balance by which it is weighed. This is unlike what the philosopher-logicians devised: it cannot guide to the truth or distinguish truth from error, nor is it a balance by which truth can be distinguished from error. As for the speculative theologians, whatever in their discourse agrees with what the Prophets brought derives from it; whatever contradicts it belongs to the innovations that are invalid according to both revelation and reason.

If it is said:
“We regard demonstrative propositions as relative. Whatever a person knows through its premises is demonstrative for him, even if it is not demonstrative for someone else.”

The reply is:
“They have not done so. Anyone who followed this path would not find the material of demonstration confined to particular things, when many people may know other matters without those particular materials they have specified.”

If they say, “We do not specify the materials,” then one of the constituent parts of logic has been invalidated—which is precisely the point.

The thirteenth point:
When they supposed that their method was universal and encompassed all the ways by which human beings acquire knowledge—even though this is not the case, and people have acquired knowledge either through sense perception or

---

## Page 4517

through reason or truthful reports, about many matters that cannot be known by the methods they have described, including the knowledge possessed by the Prophets, may Allah bless them—they sought to subject all this to their flawed framework.

They said:
“A prophet possesses a faculty stronger than that of others, enabling him to arrive at the middle term without a teacher’s instruction. Once he forms a conception, he apprehends through that faculty the term which others may find difficult or impossible to apprehend without instruction, for the souls’ capacities for apprehension have no fixed limit.”

Thus, they maintained that the Prophets’ reports of the unseen came only through logical syllogism. This is utterly unsound. Logical syllogism yields knowledge only of universals, as explained earlier, and they themselves concede this. Yet the Messengers reported specific, individual, particular matters—past, present, and future. This establishes that the Messengers’ knowledge did not come through logical syllogism. Indeed, Ibn Sīnā even placed the Lord’s knowledge of what He brings into existence in this category. Exalted is Allah far above what he says!

What has been established demonstrates the falsity of their attempt, in logic, to restrict the means of acquiring knowledge in respect of both matter and form. It also shows that they excluded bodies of true knowledge far greater in dignity, importance, and extent than those they acknowledged. The method they described yields only a little knowledge of a lowly kind, neither abundant nor noble. Such is the standing of these people: they are among the basest of people in knowledge and deeds. The unbelievers among the Jews and Christians are superior to them in knowledge and deeds in many respects. All of philosophy cannot bring its adherent to the level of the Jews and Christians after the abrogation and alteration of their religions

---

## Page 4518

let alone their level before that. Ibn al-Qushayrī recited these verses in refutation of Ibn Sīnā’s al-Shifā’ (The Healing):

We severed the bonds of brotherhood with a people
Whom The Healing had afflicted with disease.
How often I said, “My people, you stand
On a precipice’s edge because of The Healing!”
When they dismissed our warning,
We turned to Allah until He sufficed us.
They died following the religion of Aristotle,
While we lived following the faith of the Chosen One.

If it is said:

“Something similar to the logicians’ restriction of the means of acquiring knowledge appears in the writings of Muslim speculative theologians. Indeed, some state precisely the same thing, either in the logicians’ own terms or in different wording.”

The answer is:

Not everything the speculative theologians say is true. Rather, everything the Messengers brought is true. Whatever the speculative theologians and others say that agrees with it is true, and whatever they say that contradicts it is false. The condemnation of newly introduced speculative theology by the early generations and the leading scholars is well known.

He said:

What is astonishing is that some people sought, as they claimed, to defend the revealed Law through their deficient reasoning and unsound syllogisms. Yet what they did emboldened the heretics, the enemies of religion

---

## Page 4519

against it. Thus, they neither defended Islam nor defeated its enemies. Another astonishing thing is that they forsake following the infallible Messengers, who speak nothing but the truth, and turn away from following their example. Instead, they imitate and keep company with someone who opposes what the Messengers brought, although they know that he is not infallible and that he is sometimes wrong and sometimes right. Allah alone grants success in reaching the truth.

Al-Suyūtī said:

This concludes my abridgment of Ibn Taymiyyah’s book. For the most part, I have reproduced his wording verbatim, without alteration. I have omitted much from his book, which comprises twenty quires, but I have omitted nothing of importance. To Allah belong all praise and gratitude.

---

## Page 4520

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah sanctify his soul—said:

Section: On setting out the general principles of “logic” and its flaws

They constructed it on the premise that the means of acquiring knowledge are confined to “definition” and related forms, such as descriptive definition and the like, and to syllogism and related forms, such as induction and analogy. For knowledge is either “conception,” which is knowledge of individual concepts, or “assent,” which is knowledge of the relations between them through negation or affirmation. Each of these two kinds of knowledge is either “self-evident,” requiring no method of acquisition, or “inferential,” requiring such a method.
