The method for acquiring conceptions is “definition,” while the method for attaining assent is “syllogism,” which they call demonstration if its premises are certain.

They then said: “Definition” is an expression indicating the essence of a thing, although it may also refer to the thing defined itself, just as a name is an expression indicating the thing named and may also refer to the thing named itself,

---

## Page 4521

since what is understood from a definition and a name is the thing defined and the thing named, just as “essence” is what is stated in answer to “What is it?” It is also described as the answer to a question formulated as “What is it?” Thus, essence is the definition, and it is also the thing itself. These verbal nouns derived from interrogative expressions are later coinages, such as māhiyya and mā’iyya (whatness), kayfiyya (howness), haythiyya (respect or aspect), ayniyya (whereness), and limmiyya (whyness). They are comparable to verbal nouns derived from declarative expressions, such as hawqala (saying “There is no power nor strength except through Allah”), qalhada, basmala (saying “In the name of Allah”), hamdala (saying “Praise be to Allah”), and the like.

They then said that “essence” is composed of essential attributes. They explained the distinction between essential and accidental attributes by saying that “essential” attributes are those without conceiving which it is impossible to conceive the thing possessing them. The thing therefore depends on them both in existence and in conception, as an animal depends on corporeality. Whatever is not of this kind is “accidental.”

An accidental attribute is then divided into an inseparable attribute and a separable accident. An inseparable attribute is either inseparable from the essence, as evenness is from four and oddness from three, or inseparable from its existence rather than its essence, as blackness is from pitch and coming into existence is from an animal. A separable accident either passes away slowly, like youth and greying hair, or quickly, like the redness of embarrassment and the pallor of fear.

The difficulty lies in distinguishing an essential attribute from an attribute inseparable from the essence, since neither is separable from the thing itself, whether in external existence or in the mind.

---

## Page 4522

They therefore distinguished between them by saying that an essential attribute is conceived before the essence is conceived, so that the thing itself cannot be understood without it, unlike an attribute inseparable from the essence.

Both essential and accidental attributes may be shared throughout a genus, yielding the general genus and the common accident; or exclusive to a species, yielding the differentia and the distinctive property; or combine what is shared with what distinguishes, yielding the species. These, then, are the “five universals”: genus, differentia, species, distinctive property, and common accident. Discussing these attributes and their divisions is chiefly useful for definitions and the essential realities of things.

As for the syllogism, it is a statement composed of statements which, if conceded, necessarily entail another statement by virtue of themselves. It must contain two premises. A “premise” is a proposition—that is, a declarative sentence—and must contain two simple terms. The discussion thus proceeds on three levels:

(First) The discussion of simple terms, their expressions and meanings: signification by correspondence, inclusion, and necessary implication; synonymous, distinct, equivocal, and univocal names; simple and compound expressions; and the universal and the particular.

The “second level” concerns propositions and their divisions: particular, universal, and unqualified propositions; affirmation and negation; and the modalities of propositions. It also concerns the rules governing propositions, such as the falsity of the contradictory and the truth of the converse and the contrapositive.

---

## Page 4523

And the “third level” concerns the syllogism, its moods, and the conditions under which it yields a conclusion: it must contain a universal affirmative proposition; no conclusion follows from two negative premises, two particular premises, or a negative minor premise and a particular major premise. Rather, there must be either two affirmative premises, one of which is universal, or a negative minor premise and a particular major premise. It also concerns other rules governing the forms and types of syllogism, established through proof by contradiction—which is reduced to the rule governing the contradictory of a proposition—or through reduction to the proposition’s converse or contrapositive.

They then explained the material content of syllogisms, dividing them into:

“Demonstrative”: those whose premises are certain. They restricted certainties to the categories they mentioned: inward and outward sense perceptions, self-evident truths, mass-transmitted reports, and propositions established by experience. Some also added propositions established by intuition.

“Rhetorical”: those whose premises are widely accepted, whether certain or uncertain.

“Dialectical”: those whose premises are conceded by the opponent, whether certain, widely accepted, or otherwise.

“Poetic”: those whose premises are felt rather than believed, such as those that evoke joy, sorrow, or laughter.

“Fallacious, sophistical”: those whose premises are disguised with semblances of truth.

---

## Page 4524

Section

There are three views concerning what the term qiyās denotes:

The first is that it literally denotes analogical reasoning and figuratively denotes syllogistic reasoning. This is the view of al-Ghazālī and Abū Muhammad.

The second is the reverse. This is the view of Ibn Hazm.

The third is that it literally denotes both. This is the more correct view, held by the majority.

According to our scholars and the majority, qiyās is divided into rational reasoning, for which reason alone suffices, and reasoning based on revelation, which necessarily requires a foundation known through revelation.

Both rational reasoning and reasoning based on revelation—and everything termed qiyās—are divided into analogical reasoning and syllogistic reasoning. The former consists in treating something like its counterpart; the latter consists in bringing something under the rule of a general concept that encompasses it.

Each is connected to the other: two similar things must share a common feature that encompasses them both, while a concept encompassing two or more things necessarily entails treating any two of them alike with respect to that concept. Thus, qiyās is present in both: it consists in measuring, comparing, and reckoning.

---

## Page 4525

Section

The flaws in logic lie in demonstration and definition.

As for “demonstration,” its logical form is valid, and when its premises are sound, it undoubtedly yields sound knowledge. The error, however, lies in two things:

First: There is absolutely no evidence for restricting its premises to the categories they have mentioned. They are correct in what they affirm, but not in what they deny. On what grounds can one judge that certainty can arise only from these particular sources? If someone bases this on what he finds within himself, how can he know that the rest of humanity—even the prophets and the friends of Allah—cannot attain certainty by other means? Indeed, reality shows otherwise.

Second: This demonstration does yield knowledge. But how is it known that no human heart can acquire knowledge except through demonstration of the kind described? We have, in fact, seen many things that some people know immediately or through sense perception, while others know them through reflection and syllogistic reasoning. Thus, they denied what they did not fully comprehend: knowledge acquired through premises outside these restricted categories, or without any analogical or syllogistic reasoning at all. Indeed, the most accomplished of their later thinkers claimed that the knowledge of the prophets and the friends of Allah can be acquired only

---

## Page 4526

through syllogistic reasoning. Their statements imply that the same holds for the Lord’s knowledge. Yet he has no evidence whatsoever for this, beyond simply drawing an analogy between the prophets and friends of Allah and himself, and between the Lord and the angels and human beings.

This is a point of which the believer should be certain. He should know that these people and others have generally gone astray because of what they denied and rejected, not because of what they affirmed and knew. This is why logic can lead to heresy in someone whose heart lacks firm faith: he believes that knowledge can be attained only through these particular kinds of premises and this logical form, yet he finds these lacking in most of what the prophets have conveyed. He therefore doubts it, rejects it, or turns away from believing and accepting it as true. His lack of faith and knowledge thus stems from his mistaken belief that knowledge can arise only from these particular kinds of premises—a belief for which there is absolutely no evidence. Although these premises do yield knowledge, there is an obvious difference between saying that they yield it and saying that they yield it and that it cannot be attained otherwise.

A further clarification is that syllogistic reasoning establishes only universal knowledge, as they themselves acknowledge, since it must contain a universal affirmative premise. A universal indicates only what is shared in common, which is itself universal. Thus, syllogistic reasoning does not establish any particular realities in their individuality. All that can be known through it—if anything is known—is an attribute that they share with other things. Through it, therefore, nothing whatsoever can be known of the distinctive attributes of Lordship, nor of what is distinctive to any angel, prophet, or friend of Allah, nor even to any king or any being in the higher or lower realms. Thus, knowledge of these things must either be absent or be acquired

---

## Page 4527

by means other than syllogistic reasoning. Both cases occur: such knowledge is absent among them, since they have no method other than syllogistic reasoning, whereas the prophets and their followers possess it. Indeed, such knowledge is possessed, in general, by all those endowed with knowledge among the angels, the prophets, and other human beings.

Furthermore, since syllogistic reasoning must contain a universal premise, if that premise requires reasoning, it will require another premise. If it is self-evident, then, if all the individual cases it covers can be known self-evidently, what prevents some of those cases from being known in this way—an easier task?

As for definition, it calls for discussion on several points.

First: their claim that concepts acquired through reasoning can be known only through the kind of definition they describe. The same applies here as to their claim that judgments acquired through reasoning can be attained only through the demonstrative proof whose premises they have restricted to certain kinds. There is no evidence for this.

The weakness of this claim is shown by the following: if the person giving a definition knows the thing being defined through someone else’s definition, circular reasoning or an infinite regress necessarily follows. If he knows it without a definition, the claim is disproved.

If it is said, “Rather, he knows it through the definition formed in his own mind, just as he attains propositional knowledge through the demonstrative proof formed in his mind before expressing it in words,” the reply is that a demonstrative proof is distinct from its conclusion: knowledge of the two premises is not itself knowledge of the conclusion. The definition formed in the mind, however, is itself knowledge of the thing being defined—and that is precisely what is sought. Where, then, is the definition that imparts knowledge of the thing being defined? This is one of the points that makes the matter clear.

---

## Page 4528

Second point:

It may be argued that a definition cannot, under any circumstances, make known the essence of what it defines, unlike a demonstrative proof, which provides evidence for the conclusion sought. As regards the person giving the definition, this is because he knew the thing before defining it; otherwise, his definition could not be valid. A definition must correspond exactly to what it defines, being neither broader nor narrower in scope. Without knowing the thing beforehand, he could not know that this correspondence holds.

As regards the listener, if this is not self-evident to him and the person giving the definition offers no proof of it, he cannot acquire knowledge merely from that person’s assertion. Hence, you find the listener challenging a definition and producing counterexamples showing that it includes what it should exclude or excludes what it should include. Had he not conceived the thing independently of the definition, such challenges and counterexamples would have been impossible.

The benefit of a definition is only to distinguish what is defined from other things, not to furnish a conception of it. This accords with the linguistic meaning of hadd—a boundary separating one thing from another. A person may conceive the essence of something without qualification. For example, someone may conceive a command, a statement, or knowledge without qualification, rather than as a universal. A definition then distinguishes the universal concept encompassing every statement, every instance of knowledge, or every command.

From this, it becomes clear that what is conceived self-evidently of the things denoted by these names—the unqualified reality—is different from what is sought through definition—the universal reality. Furthermore, the distinction sometimes concerns names and sometimes attributes. A definition may therefore concern the name: this is a verbal definition, needed when reasoning from the Quran, the Sunnah, or the words of any scholar. Alternatively, it may concern the description: this conveys an understanding of a reality whose description is known, and can be achieved through a descriptive definition, distinctive properties, and other means.

Third point:

The distinction between an essential attribute and an accidental attribute inseparable from the essence, such that one claims

---

## Page 4529

that the essence cannot be understood without the former, unlike the latter. Yet when a rational person examines his own mind, he does not find one preceding the other. Moreover, if identifying an essential attribute depends on conceiving the essence, while conceiving the essence depends on identifying its essential attributes, the reasoning is circular. For we know the essence only through its essential attributes, yet we cannot identify those attributes until we conceive the essence. Essential attributes are those upon which knowledge of the essence depends. An attribute can therefore be recognized as essential only when we know that understanding the essence depends on it. Thus, you do not seek to understand the essence until you know which attributes are essential. Much more could be said in elaboration of this.

(Fourth point):

Their claim that essences are composite—yet there is no composition in the mind.

---

## Page 4530

Shaykh al-Islām—may Allah Most High have mercy on him—said:

Section

I have already written a summary of “logic” as translated into Arabic, which the Arabs transmitted from the Greeks and rendered into Arabic in both wording and meaning. They refined its wording and made its meanings precise. It is attributed to Aristotle the Greek, whom his followers among the Sabaean philosophers who introduced religious innovations call “the First Teacher,” because he established the disciplines they study: logic, natural philosophy, and metaphysics.

When these disciplines reached the Muslims and their books were translated into Arabic, alongside works on medicine, arithmetic, astronomy, and other subjects—with these translations becoming widespread during the reign of the Caliph Abū al-ʿAbbās, known as al-Maʾmūn—the Muslims took them up and refined both their wording and meaning. Yet they contain much falsehood and misguidance.

---

## Page 4531

Some followed these disciplines while professing Islam. These are the Sabaeans among the Muslims, known as philosophers. They thus came to believe in some of the Scripture but not the rest, like the religious innovators among the Jews and Christians prior to abrogation, when they altered some of the scriptures in their possession.

Others did not intend to follow these disciplines, but adopted ideas from their adherents, supposing that all of them agreed with Islam and supported it, whereas many contradicted and undermined it. Such is the condition of many Muʿtazilī practitioners of speculative theology. Hence they have been called “the effeminates of the philosophers.”

Others turned away from these disciplines in a general, undifferentiated manner, yet did not follow those teachings of the Quran and Islam that would suffice in place of all the truth these disciplines contain and repel their falsehood. Nor did they strive against their adherents in the prescribed manner. Such is the condition of many scholars of hadith and jurisprudence, and others. I have previously written about aspects of this in various discussions of foundational principles. In my summary of the main principles of logic, I also mentioned the ignorance and misguidance it has caused, as well as some of its defects.

Here I summarize that discussion, saying:

The discussion concerns the methods of acquiring knowledge in the form of conceptions and assents. (The first) includes definition and description; (the second) includes syllogism in its various forms, demonstrative and otherwise, as well as analogy and induction. They may claim that the conceptions sought can be attained only through definition in the broad sense

---

## Page 4532

and that the assents sought can be attained only through syllogism in the broad sense. The general category of syllogism may be called “relation,” just as the general category of explanatory discourse is called “definition.” What is self-evident in either category requires neither definition nor demonstration. This account thus maintains that definitions enable us to conceive realities, and that this cannot be achieved otherwise; and that syllogism yields assent concerning realities, and that this cannot be achieved otherwise. Error has occurred in both matters. As regards definition, the error lies in both propositions, negative and affirmative—in what they affirm and what they deny.

As for the first:

A definition does not convey a conception of realities; it merely distinguishes what is defined from other things. A conception of realities cannot be acquired merely through a definition, which consists of the definer’s words. Rather, those realities must be apprehended through inward and outward perception. If they are not apprehended, an illustrative example is given of them. Through that example—an analogy serving to form a conception, not to establish assent—a kind of apprehension is attained, such as our apprehension of the reward and punishment Allah has promised in the Hereafter. The parables set forth in the Quran sometimes serve to form conceptions and sometimes to establish assent. This point has been established in several ways; the purpose here is merely to summarize the matter.

As for the second, namely the negative claim, apprehending realities conceived in the abstract does not depend on definition, even if it were granted that they could be known through definition. Rather, such conceptions are attained through the recognized means of apprehension.

---

## Page 4533

and they may also be attained through the use of single terms, just as through definition. Indeed, a term may sometimes be more useful in this respect than a definition. This too has been established.

As for syllogism, there is no doubt that it yields assent when its premises and their arrangement are sound. Here, however, the error lies in the negative claims, again in two respects.

(First)
The acquisition of knowledge involving assent in the mind does not depend on syllogism; rather, it can be attained by other means.

(Second)
The premises of demonstrative syllogism are not confined to the categories they have listed: propositions based on sensory perception, inward experience, self-evidence, reasoning, mass transmission, experimentation, and intuition. I have explained this elsewhere. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 4534

He was asked about “books on logic.”

He replied:

As for “books on logic,” they contain no knowledge whose study is required by Islamic law, although some people, through their independent judgment, have concluded that studying it is a collective obligation. Others have said that the sciences cannot be established without it, as Abū Hāmid stated. This is a grave error, both rationally and according to Islamic law.

As for “reason,” rational people from every group engaged in scholarly discourse have developed their disciplines with precision without Greek logic. As for “Islamic law,” it is necessarily known in the religion of Islam that Allah has not obliged people of knowledge and faith to learn this Greek logic.

As for logic itself, some of it is true and some false. Much—or most—of the truth it contains is unnecessary. As for whatever is needed, most people of sound natural disposition can grasp it unaided. The dull-witted derive no benefit from it, while the intelligent have no need of it, and its harm to

---

## Page 4535

those who are not well versed in the knowledge of the prophets outweighs its benefit. For it contains unsound negative principles that have gained acceptance among many distinguished scholars and have caused their hypocrisy and corrupted their knowledge.

The claim that it is all true is false. Rather, their discussions of definition, essential and accidental attributes, the categories of syllogism, and demonstration and its premises contain errors that we have explained elsewhere. Muslim scholars have also explained this. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 4536

Shaykh al-Islām, the imam and eminent scholar Taqī al-Dīn Ahmad ibn Abd al-Halīm ibn Abd al-Salām Ibn Taymiyyah—may Allah be pleased with him—was asked:

Is the human “intellect” an accident? What is the “spirit” that governs the body? Is it the soul? Does it have a mode of being that can be known? Is it an accident or a substance? Is it known where it resides within the body, and where the intellect resides?

He replied:

All praise is due to Allah, Lord of all worlds. In the Book of Allah, the Sunnah of His Messenger, and the words of the Companions, the Successors, and all the other leading Muslim scholars, the “intellect” is something that inheres in the rational person, whether it is called an accident or an attribute. It is not a self-subsisting entity, whether called a substance, a body, or anything else.

The term “intellect” is used to denote a rational entity that is a self-subsisting substance only in the discourse of certain philosophical thinkers who discuss the intellect and the soul and claim that ten intellects exist. This is mentioned by some followers of Aristotle and other Peripatetic philosophers, and by those affiliated with religious communities who adopted it from them. These thinkers have been discussed at length elsewhere, and it has been shown that what they speak of

---

## Page 4537

as intellects, souls, immaterial entities, separate entities, and intellectual substances can be substantiated only in the case of the human soul and the knowledge and its concomitants that inhere in it. Their designation of these things as “separate entities” derives originally from the soul’s separation from the body at death. This is correct: the deceased person’s soul does indeed separate from the body at death. This rests upon the fact that the soul subsists in its own right and remains after leaving the body at death, experiencing either bliss or punishment. This is the position of the religious communities, Muslim and otherwise, and the view of the Companions, those who followed them in righteousness, and all the other leading Muslim scholars, although many speculative theologians claim that the soul is the life that inheres in the body.

Some of them say that it is a constituent part of the body, like the air circulating within it or the vapour issuing from the heart. In short, according to the early generations of the Muslim community and its leading scholars, the soul that separates from the body at death is neither a constituent part of the body nor one of its attributes. These two views are held only by certain adherents of newly invented speculative theology among the followers of the Jahmiyyah, the Muʿtazilah, and others like them. The Peripatetic philosophers acknowledge that the soul remains after separating from the body, but they ascribe false attributes to it, claiming that once it separates from the body, it becomes an intellect.

For them, the intellect is that which is free of matter and material attachments, and by matter they mean body. They may also say: “It is that which is free of attachment to hayūlā,” with hayūlā in their terminology meaning a substratum. They also speak of “matter and form.”

---

## Page 4538

For them, the intellect is a substance that subsists in its own right, is described as neither moving nor at rest, and undergoes no new states whatsoever. What their position actually entails is that, once the soul separates from the body, no new state arises in it: no knowledge or conceptions, no hearing or sight, no volitions, no joy or delight, nor anything else that might newly arise or occur. Rather, according to them, it remains in a single state from eternity to eternity, as they claim concerning the intellect and the soul.

Some of them say that souls are numerically one, while others say that they are multiple. Their discourse contains falsehoods that this is not the place to discuss at length. The purpose here is only to draw attention to what is relevant to the present discussion.

They call whatever is associated with matter—that is, hayūlā, which here means body—a soul, such as the human soul that governs the body. They claim that the celestial sphere has a soul that moves it, just as human beings have souls. Their earlier thinkers, however, used to say that the soul of the celestial sphere is an accident inhering in the sphere, like the souls of beasts, and just as desire and anger inhere in a human being. But some of them, such as Ibn Sīnā and others, claimed that the soul of the celestial sphere is a substance subsisting in its own right, like the human soul.

As long as the human soul governs the body, they call it a soul; once it separates from the body, they call it an intellect. This is because, for them, the intellect is that which is free of matter and material attachments, whereas the soul is attached to the body by governing and directing it.

---

## Page 4539

Their designation of these as immaterial entities originally derives from the fact that a person abstracts universal intelligible notions from particular objects of sense perception. When he sees individual human beings, such as Zayd and ʿAmr, he apprehends an element common to human beings and to the universal, shared humanity conceived in his heart. When he sees horses, mules, donkeys, livestock, and other individual animals, he apprehends a universal element common to these individuals: universal animality as conceived by the intellect. When, alongside these, he considers animals, trees, and plants, he apprehends a common universal element: a body that grows and takes nourishment. They sometimes call this the vegetative soul.

When, alongside these, he considers all the other bodies—the celestial bodies above and the elemental bodies below—he apprehends a common universal element: body in its general, unrestricted sense. When he considers the other things that exist, he apprehends a common universal element: general, universal existence, which is divided into substance and accident. According to them, this existence is the subject matter of the “highest science,” which examines existence and its concomitants. This is what they call “first philosophy” and “supreme wisdom.”

They divide existence into substance and accident, and classify accidents into “nine kinds.” This is what Aristotle set forth. His followers include this within logic because it deals with the simple terms to which composite definitions ultimately reduce. The same is true of those who followed their approach in writing on this subject, such as Ibn Hazm and others.

Ibn Sīnā and his followers, however, said: “Discussion of this subject is not confined to logic.”

---

## Page 4540

so they excluded these categories from logic, as did those who followed Ibn Sīnā’s approach, such as Abū Hāmid, al-Suhrawardī who was executed, al-Rāzī, al-Āmidī, and others. These are the “ten categories,” which they designate as substance, quantity, quality, place, time, relation, position, possession, acting, and being acted upon. They have been brought together in two lines of verse:

Zayd, tall and black, the son of Mālik, … was reclining in his house yesterday;
In his hand a sword—he drew it, and it was drawn: … these are ten categories in full.

Most people, both his followers and others, rejected the restriction of accidents to nine genera, saying: “There is no proof for this.” They maintain that these can be reduced to three genera or to other numbers.

They classified substances into “five kinds”: body, intellect, soul, matter, and form. Body is a substance perceived by the senses, while the remaining kinds are intelligible substances. Yet the evidence they offer to establish intelligible substances establishes their existence only in minds, not in external reality. These are what they call “immaterial intelligibles.”

They say that substances are divided into material and immaterial substances. Material substances are those that subsist in matter—that is, prime matter, which is body—while immaterial substances are those abstracted from matter. What they call immaterial entities have their origin in these notions

---

## Page 4541

—universals apprehended in the human soul—just as the notion of separate entities originates in the soul’s separation from the body. Both of these are undeniable.

However, they make false claims about the soul’s attributes and states. They also assert the existence of intelligible substances subsisting in themselves, concerning which they say: “The one who understands, what is understood, and understanding itself are one and the same.” They say the same concerning the Lord of the worlds:

“He is the one who understands, what is understood, and understanding itself; the lover, the beloved, and love itself; that which gives delight, the one who experiences delight, and delight itself.”

They identify the attribute with the subject possessing it, and each attribute with every other attribute. Thus, they identify understanding itself—which is knowledge—with the one who understands and knows, and passionate love itself—which is love—with the passionate lover, the one who loves. They identify delight itself with knowledge itself and love itself. They also identify power and will with knowledge itself, making knowledge identical with power, will, love, and delight. They identify the one who knows, wills, loves, and experiences delight with knowledge itself, which they identify with will itself, love itself, and delight itself. Thus, they make distinct realities one and the same, and identify the various attributes themselves with the very essence that possesses them.

Then they contradict themselves by affirming that He has knowledge that is not identical with His essence, as Ibn Sīnā contradicted himself in his Ishārāt, as did other leading thinkers among them. A detailed refutation of them is given elsewhere.

The point is that they use the term “intellect” to designate a substance subsisting in itself, and affirm intelligible substances that they call “immaterial entities” and “entities separate from matter.” Yet when their position is rigorously examined, they have nothing beyond the human soul, which they call “rational,” and the quality subsisting in it that is called intellect.

---

## Page 4542

Aristotle and his followers called “the Lord” an intellect and a substance. According to them, He knows nothing other than Himself, wills nothing, and does nothing. They call Him “the Principle” and “the First Cause,” because, in their view, the celestial sphere moves in order to resemble Him, or to resemble the intellect. Thus, according to them, the celestial sphere needs the First Cause only insofar as it seeks to resemble it, just as someone following an imam in prayer emulates him, or a pupil emulates his teacher.

He may also say that He moves the sphere as the beloved moves his lover. They do not hold that He has brought anything into existence or done anything. Nor did they call Him the Necessary Existent, or divide existence into necessary and contingent while regarding the contingent as something eternal and beginningless, like the celestial sphere in their view. Rather, this was the work of Ibn Sīnā and his followers. In this, they opposed their predecessors and all rational people; they also contradicted themselves. For they explicitly affirmed what their predecessors and all other rational people had affirmed: that what is contingent—capable of existing or not existing—must have come into being after not existing. As for that which is beginningless, which has always existed and will always exist, they and all other rational people consider it impossible for it to be contingent, admitting either existence or nonexistence. Rather, whatever admits existence and nonexistence must have come into being. This is among the arguments used to establish that everything other than Allah has come into being, preceded by nonexistence, existing after it did not exist, as has been explained at length in its proper place.

Yet Ibn Sīnā and his followers contradicted themselves, stating elsewhere that existence is divided into necessary and contingent, and that a contingent being may be eternal and beginningless, having always existed and continuing forever, such that it is impossible

---

## Page 4543

for it not to exist. They say that it is necessary through another, and they place the celestial sphere in this category. Thus, by affirming a contingent being that can either exist or not exist, yet is nevertheless eternal, beginningless and everlasting, incapable of nonexistence and necessarily existent through another, they departed from the consensus of rational people with whom they had previously agreed. All rational people regard this as impossible. This is evident to sound reason for anyone who understands the true nature of a contingent being that admits existence and nonexistence, as has been explained at length in its proper place.

These philosophers gained the upper hand over the speculative theologians—the Jahmiyyah, the Muʿtazilah and those who followed their path—only because the latter did not truly understand what Allah had sent His Messenger with, nor did they support the positions they championed with sound rational arguments. These speculative theologians were therefore deficient in their understanding of both revelation and reason.

(They went so far as to say that Allah had never done anything or spoken by His will. [Then what came into being did so without any new cause arising, and they claimed that it was perpetually impossible for the Lord to speak by His will.] Then what came into being did so without any new cause arising, and they claimed that it was perpetually impossible for the Lord to speak by His will and do whatever He wills, because they held that events could not continue perpetually.) (*)

Their leading figures, such as al-Jahm ibn Safwān and Abū al-Hudhayl al-ʿAllāf, then adopted the view that events could not continue perpetually into either the future or the past. Al-Jahm maintained that Paradise and Hell would cease to exist, while Abū al-Hudhayl maintained that all movement within them would cease and their inhabitants would remain forever motionless. Some who followed this path claim that this is what reason dictates, and that everything with a beginning must have an end.

When they saw that revelation affirmed the everlasting bliss of the people of Paradise, as the Most High says:

“Its fruit

> **Footnotes:**
> (*) Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 79):
