> “They went so far as to say: Allah had never done anything or spoken by His will. Then whatever came into being did so without any new cause arising. They claimed that it was perpetually impossible for the Lord to speak by His will and do whatever He wills, because they maintained that events could not continue perpetually.”

---

## Page 4544

is everlasting, and so is its shade”—and He says:

“Indeed, this is Our provision; it will never run out”—

they thought that revelation had to be believed where it differed from the conclusions of rational thinkers. They did not realize that clear rational proof does not contradict authentic scriptural proof. Indeed, valid proofs cannot conflict, whether they are rational, derived from revelation, or a combination of the two. If two proofs conflict, this indicates that one or both are unsound.

Many of them came to accept that events could continue perpetually into the future, but not into the past. They advanced subsidiary arguments whose weakness their most discerning scholars recognized, as has been explained at length elsewhere. Their position commits them to holding that the Lord was once without power and then became possessed of power, without any new cause arising that would account for His becoming capable; and that He could not act or speak by His will, but then action became possible for Him without any cause arising to account for this new possibility.

When this is pointed out to them, they say, “He possessed power in pre-eternity, as He always had.” They are answered: “One cannot possess power over something while that very thing is impossible. Rather, having power over the impossible is itself impossible. One can have power only to do what is possible. Thus, if He has always possessed power, it has always been possible for Him to act.”

Since this was their foundational premise, they divided into three positions concerning Allah’s speech.

One group said: “Speech does not subsist in the Lord Himself; rather, His speech can only be created. For it must be either beginningless or originated, and it cannot be beginningless, because He speaks by His will and power, whereas what is beginningless does not come about through power and will. And if speech comes about

---

## Page 4545

through power and will, it must be created and cannot subsist in Him. For if it subsisted in Him, originated events would subsist in Him. Yet originated events cannot subsist in Him, because if they did, He would never be free of them, and whatever is never free of originated events is itself originated.”

They said: “For it is through this principle that we established that bodies have a beginning, and through it that the world was established to have a beginning.”

They said: “It is known that whatever does not precede an originated event cannot exist before it: it must exist either alongside it or after it. Whatever exists alongside an originated event or after it is itself originated.”

Many of them failed to recognize the distinction between events as a kind and a particular event. A particular event, or a finite set of events, cannot have existed perpetually without beginning. Whatever did not exist before them must exist either alongside them or after them, and whatever is of this description is certainly originated. This is plain to everyone. The question requiring examination and giving rise to dispute, however, concerns “events as a kind”: can the kind be perpetual, such that the Lord continually speaks or acts by His will and power, or is that impossible?

When a group recognized this distinction, they said: “This too is impossible, because a beginningless succession of events is impossible.” They advanced arguments for this, such as the argument from correspondence, the argument that what is infinite cannot come to an end, and similar arguments. Most of what has been stated on this subject and related matters has already been discussed elsewhere; every occasion calls for its own discourse.

When those philosophers saw that this position was demonstrably false according to clear reason, that events cannot originate without a newly arising cause, and that it is impossible for the Lord to become an agent after

---

## Page 4546

not having been one, and that the effect of a complete cause cannot lag behind it—they supposed that, by refuting this position, they had secured their claim concerning “the beginninglessness of the world,” including the celestial spheres, generated things as a kind, and the matter of the elements. They fell into profound error, contradicting clear reason and rejecting the testimony of every messenger. For the messengers unanimously affirm that everything other than Allah is originated and created, coming into existence after not having existed; that nothing alongside Allah is beginningless as He is; and that He created the heavens, the earth, and everything between them in six days.

Sound reason recognizes that originated events must have an originator. If there were nothing but an eternal, beginningless cause necessarily entailing its effect, no originated events would exist in the world. For it is impossible for an originated event to arise from an eternal, beginningless cause necessarily entailing its effect. If its effect were inseparable from it, that effect would be beginningless along with it, never coming after it. Thus, none of these events would have a cause necessitating its occurrence, and all originated events would have arisen without an originator. These men fled from the notion that an agent endowed with power should originate them without a newly arising cause, only to hold that they arise without any originator whatsoever, whether endowed with power or not. What they fled to was therefore worse than what they fled from, and they were worse than someone seeking refuge from scorching ground in fire.

These men believed that a particular thing brought about, fashioned, and originated—such as a celestial sphere—coexists with its agent without beginning or end: the agent does not precede it in time. The others said: “Rather, the effect of a complete cause is delayed, then comes into existence without any cause necessitating its occurrence.” The former established clear rational proofs refuting this, just as the latter established clear rational proofs

---

## Page 4547

refuting the position of the other group. Undoubtedly, the position of these proponents of simultaneity is far more unsound, and more contrary to clear reason and authentic revelation, than that of the proponents of delay.

Neither group arrived at the third position, which is supported by clear reason, acknowledged by rational people generally, and attested by the Book, the Sunnah, and the statements of the early generations and the imams.

This position is that a fully operative cause necessarily entails its effect occurring immediately after it becomes fully operative, neither simultaneously with it nor after a delay. This is as when you divorce a woman and she becomes divorced, free a slave and he becomes free, break a vessel and it breaks, or cut a rope and it parts. The resulting emancipation and divorce are not simultaneous with the actual acts of divorcing and emancipating, such that they coexist with them; nor are they delayed after them. Rather, they follow immediately upon them, without a gap.

The effect may be said to be with its cause yet distinct from it, insofar as it follows immediately upon it without a gap. Likewise, it may be said to be after it and subsequent to it, insofar as it occurs only after the full causal act. Hence Allah, Most High, says:

“His command, when He wills something, is only to say to it, ‘Be,’ and it is.”

He, glorified be He, brings into being whatever He wills to bring into being. When He brings it into being, it exists immediately after His act of bringing it into being, without a gap. It does not exist simultaneously in time with that act, nor is it delayed after it with a temporal interval between them. Rather, it follows His act without a gap, just as the parts of motion and time adjoin one another. This is among the arguments establishing that everything other than Allah is originated, coming into existence after not having existed, even while affirming that He has always been acting and speaking. These matters are discussed at length elsewhere.

---

## Page 4548

The point here is that this is the underlying premise of those who say that the Quran is originated, and of those who say that no speech or will subsists in the Lord—indeed, neither knowledge, life, power, nor any attribute whatsoever.

When the unsoundness of this position became evident in light of revelation and reason, a group who shared their fundamental premise said: “He does not speak by His will and power. Rather, His speech is inseparable from His essence, just as life is inseparable from His essence.” Some of them then said that His speech is a single meaning, because an infinite number of meanings cannot coexist at a single instant, and because it cannot be restricted to one number rather than another. They said: “That meaning is the command enjoining everything commanded and the information concerning everything spoken of. If expressed in Arabic, it is the Quran; if expressed in Hebrew, it is the Torah; and if expressed in Syriac, it is the Gospel.”

They also said: “Commands and prohibitions are attributes of speech, not types of it. Thus, the meaning of the ‘Verse of the Throne,’ the ‘Verse of Debt,’ ‘Say: He is Allah, One,’ and ‘May the hands of Abu Lahab perish’ is one and the same.”

The vast majority of rational people replied: “Merely conceiving this position is enough to recognize its unsoundness.” They also asked them: “Did Moses hear all of Allah’s speech, or only some of it? If you say all of it, it follows that he knew all that Allah knows. If you say some of it, then His speech admits of division.”

They further said to them: “If you allow that the reality of a declaration is the reality of a command, and that the reality of prohibiting everything prohibited and commanding everything commanded is the reality of information concerning everything spoken of, then allow that the reality of knowledge is the reality of power, and that the reality of power is the reality of will.”

Their most accomplished thinkers acknowledged that this consequence was inescapable for them. Their position also entailed the possibility that the reality of the essence is the reality of the attributes, and that the reality of necessary existence is the reality of contingent necessity. A group of them accepted this consequence and said:

---

## Page 4549

“Existence is one, and necessary, eternal existence—the Creator—is identical with contingent, created, originated existence.” This is the foundation of the position of those who profess the unity of existence, such as Ibn ‘Arabī al-Tā’ī, Ibn Sab‘īn, and their followers, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Among those who maintain that He does not speak by His will and power, while affirming that speech subsists in Him, some said:

“His individual utterances consist of particular letters and sounds that are eternal and beginningless, have always existed, and will always exist.”

They claimed that the Quran, the Torah, and the Gospel each consist of eternal, beginningless letters and sounds that have always existed and will always exist. The vast majority of rational people replied:

“It is self-evident that the letter bā’ precedes sīn, and that sīn precedes mīm. How, then, can they coexist from eternity and forever? It is also known that a particular sound does not endure for two successive moments. How, then, can it be beginningless, having always existed and continuing forever?”

Then the third group—among those who followed the approach of those speculative theologians—said:

“Rather, we say that He speaks by His will and power, with speech that subsists in His essence, as indicated by the Quran, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations and the leading scholars. Even if this entails that temporal occurrences subsist in Him, there is nothing objectionable in that, whether according to revelation or reason. Indeed, this consequence is inescapable for all groups of rational people, and it is indicated by numerous scriptural texts and statements of the early generations and the leading scholars.

“We also say that He speaks the Arabic Quran by His will and power, and that He called to Moses with a voice that Moses heard, as indicated by the scriptural texts and the statements of the early generations. But we say that He was not speaking in the beginningless past, and that it is impossible for Him to

---

## Page 4550

have always spoken by His will and power, because that would entail temporal occurrences without a beginning.” This is their foundational premise.

They were answered:

“It is well known that speech is an attribute of perfection, not deficiency, and that one who speaks by his will and power is more perfect than one who is unable to do so. Accordingly, one who has always spoken by his will is more perfect than one who became able to speak after having been unable to do so.”

They were also told:

“If you say that He spoke after speech had been impossible for Him, without any cause bringing about His ability to speak or making speech possible for Him, then you are saying that He had always been unable to speak, and speech had always been impossible for Him, but then He became able to speak by His will without anything having occurred. This contradicts plain reason, denies the Creator the attributes of perfection, and makes Him like a created being who became able to speak after having been unable to do so.”

The early generations and the leading scholars explicitly stated that the Lord, Most High, has always spoken whenever He wills and however He wills. This was expressly stated by ‘Abd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak, Ahmad ibn Hanbal, and other leading religious scholars and early Muslims. These are the very people who affirmed that the Quran is the speech of Allah, sent down and uncreated. None of them said that He does not speak by His will and power. Nor did any of them say that it is created and separate

---

## Page 4551

from Him. Nor did any of them say that He became a speaker, or became able to speak, after not having been so. These matters have been discussed at length elsewhere.

The point is that these doctrines—advanced by the speculative theologians among the Jahmiyyah, Mu‘tazilah, Kullābiyyah, Karrāmiyyah, and Sālimiyyah, and by those later scholars who agreed with them while affiliating themselves with some of the four imams—contradicted the consensus of the early generations and the leading scholars, what the Quran and Sunnah brought, and the clear rational understanding that Allah has made innate in His servants. It was these doctrines that enabled those eternalist philosophers to gain the upper hand over them. Nevertheless, the philosophers’ position is even more unsound in terms of both rational and transmitted evidence.

Section

The point here is that, among Muslims and most rational people, the term “reason” denotes an attribute, what is called an accident inhering in the rational being. This is what the Quran indicates in the words of the Most High:

“So that you may understand.”

And His words:

“Have they not travelled through the land so that they may have hearts with which to reason?”

And His words:

“We have made the signs clear to you so that you may understand.”

These and similar passages indicate that ‘aql (reason) is the verbal noun of ‘aqala–ya‘qilu (to reason). Accordingly, the term “reason” does not apply to mere knowledge upon which its possessor does not act, nor to action without knowledge. Rather, it applies only to knowledge

---

## Page 4552

that is put into practice, and to action based on knowledge. Hence the inhabitants of the Fire will say:

“If only we had listened or reasoned, we would not have been among the companions of the Blaze.”

And the Most High says:

“Have they not travelled through the land so that they may have hearts with which to reason?”

Reason, as a prerequisite for religious accountability, must consist in knowledge by which a person distinguishes between what benefits him and what harms him. A mentally unsound person who cannot distinguish between dirhams and copper coins, distinguish the days of the week, or understand what is said to him does not possess reason. Someone who understands speech and distinguishes between what benefits him and what harms him, however, possesses reason.

Some people say that reason consists in necessary knowledge, while others say that it consists in acting in accordance with what that knowledge requires. The correct view is that the term “reason” encompasses both. Reason may also refer to the innate faculty within a person by which he knows, distinguishes, and seeks what is beneficial rather than harmful. As Ahmad ibn Hanbal, al-Hārith al-Muhāsibī, and others have said, reason is an innate faculty.

The majority of rational people affirm the existence of this innate faculty, just as the majority of rational people affirm that the eye possesses a faculty by which one sees, the tongue a faculty by which one tastes, and the skin a faculty by which one feels through touch. Some people deny faculties and natural properties, as is the position of Abū al-Hasan and those who followed him among the adherents of Mālik, al-Shāfi‘ī, Ahmad, and others. And those who deny faculties and natural properties

---

## Page 4553

also deny causes and say that Allah acts when these causes are present, not through them. Thus, they say that Allah does not satisfy hunger through bread, quench thirst through water, or cause crops to grow through water; rather, He acts when these things are present, not through them. These people contradict the Book, the Sunnah, and the consensus of the early generations, as well as clear reason and sensory experience. For Allah says in His Book:

“And it is He who sends the winds as good tidings before His mercy, until, when they carry heavy clouds, We drive them to a dead land and send down water through them, then bring forth through it every kind of fruit. Thus will We bring forth the dead, so that you may take heed.”

He thus states that He sends down water through the clouds and brings forth fruit through water. The Most High also says:

“And the water Allah sends down from the sky, giving life thereby to the earth after its death.”

And He says:

“And We sent down blessed water from the sky, causing gardens and grain for harvest to grow thereby.”

And He says:

“Fight them; Allah will punish them through your hands.”

And He says:

“There has come to you from Allah a light and a clear Book.”

“Through it Allah guides those who pursue His pleasure to the ways of peace.”

And He says:

“So they say, ‘What did Allah intend by this example?’ He misleads many thereby and guides many thereby.”

There are many such passages in the Quran.

People know through their senses and reason that some things are causes of others. They know, for example, that hunger is satisfied by eating, not by counting, and by eating food, not pebbles; and that water is a cause of life for plants and animals, as He says:

“And We made from water every living thing.”

They also know that an animal’s thirst is quenched by drinking water, not by walking. There are many such examples. A fuller discussion of these matters belongs elsewhere.

---

## Page 4554

Section

The spirit that governs the body and departs from it at death is the spirit breathed into it. It is also the soul that departs from it at death.

When the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, slept through the prayer, he said:

“Allah took our souls wherever He willed and returned them wherever He willed.”

Bilāl said to him:

“O Messenger of Allah, the One who took your soul took mine.”

Allah Most High says:

“Allah takes the souls at the time of their death, and those that do not die He takes during their sleep. He then keeps those for which He has decreed death and releases the others for a specified term.”

Ibn ‘Abbās and most Quran commentators said: He takes them in two ways: at death and during sleep. Then, during sleep, He takes those that die and releases the others for a specified term, until their appointed time of death arrives.

It is established in the two Sahīh collections that the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, would say when going to sleep:

“In Your name, my Lord, I lay down my side, and through You I raise it. If You retain my soul, forgive it and have mercy upon it; and if You release it, protect it as You protect Your righteous servants.”

It is also established in the Sahīh:

“Allah has placed the souls of the martyrs in the crops of green birds that roam

---

## Page 4555

in Paradise, then return to lamps suspended from the Throne.” It is also established through authentic chains of transmission:

“When a person’s spirit is taken, the angels say: ‘Come out, O good soul that dwelt in a good body. Come out content and well-pleasing.’ And it is said: ‘Come out, O wicked soul that dwelt in a wicked body. Come out discontented and under wrath.’”

Another hadith states:

“The believer’s soul is a bird that feeds on the fruit of Paradise, then returns to lamps suspended from the Throne.”

Thus he called it a nasama (soul).

Likewise, the authentic hadith concerning the Ascension states:

“Adam, peace be upon him, had dark figures to his right and dark figures to his left. When he looked to his right, he laughed; when he looked to his left, he wept. Gabriel told the Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him: ‘These figures are the souls of his descendants: the blessed are to his right, and the wretched to his left.’”

In the hadith of ‘Alī:

“By Him who split the seed and created the living soul!”

And in an authentic hadith:

“When the spirit is taken, the gaze follows it.”

Thus, what is taken at death and during sleep is called both a spirit and a soul. What is taken up to heaven is likewise called both a spirit and a soul. It is called a soul in view of its governance of the body, and a spirit in view of its subtle nature, for the word rūh (spirit) conveys subtlety. Hence the wind is also called a rūh. The Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him, said:

“The wind is from the spirit of Allah.”

That is, from the spirit Allah created. Attributing the spirit to Allah therefore denotes ownership, not an attribute of His. For anything attributed to Allah, if it is an entity that subsists independently, belongs to Him; whereas if it is an attribute subsisting in something else, with no locus in which it subsists, then it is an attribute of Allah.

---

## Page 4556

The first category is illustrated by His words:

“Allah’s she-camel and her share of water.”

And His words:

“Then We sent to her Our spirit”—namely Gabriel—“and he appeared before her as a man in perfect form.”

“She said, ‘I seek refuge in the Most Merciful from you, if you fear Allah.’”

“He said, ‘I am only a messenger from your Lord, to grant you a pure son.’”

He also said:

“And Mary, daughter of ‘Imrān, who guarded her chastity, and We breathed into her of Our spirit.”

And He said concerning Adam:

“When I have fashioned him and breathed into him of My spirit, fall down before him in prostration.”

The second category is illustrated by our expressions: Allah’s knowledge, Allah’s speech, Allah’s power, Allah’s life, and Allah’s command. However, a verbal noun may be used to denote the object of the action: what is known may be called “knowledge”; what is brought about by power, “power”; what is commanded, a “command”; and what is created by the word, a “word.” In such cases, what is denoted is created.

Examples include His words:

“The command of Allah has come, so do not seek to hasten it.”

And His words:

“Allah gives you glad tidings of a word from Him, whose name is the Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary, honoured in this world and the Hereafter.”

And His words:

“The Messiah, Jesus, son of Mary, was only Allah’s Messenger, and His word which He conveyed to Mary, and a spirit from Him.”

Also in this category is the saying:

“When Allah created mercy, He created it in one hundred portions. He sent down one portion and kept ninety-nine with Himself. When the Day of Resurrection comes, He will join this one to those and show mercy to His servants through them.”

Another example is His statement to Paradise in the authentic hadith:

“You are My mercy; through you I show mercy to whomever I will among My servants.”

Likewise, He said to the Fire:

“You are My punishment; through you I punish whomever I will. Each of you will have its fill.”

---

## Page 4557

Section

However, the terms rūh (spirit) and nafs (soul or self) are used to convey several meanings. Rūh may mean the air exhaled from the body and the air inhaled into it. It may also mean the vapour that emerges from the heart’s cavity, from its innermost part, and flows through the blood vessels. This is what physicians call the spirit, also known as the vital spirit. Both these meanings are distinct from the spirit that departs at death, which is the soul.

The nafs of a thing may also mean the thing itself, its very identity, as when one says, “I saw Zayd himself, in person.” Allah Most High says:

“You know what is within myself, and I do not know what is within Yourself.”

He also says:

“Your Lord has prescribed mercy for Himself.”

And He Most High says:

“Allah warns you of Himself.”

An authentic hadith relates that he said to the Mother of the Believers:

“Since leaving you, I have said four phrases which, if weighed against what you have said, would counterbalance it: Glory be to Allah, as numerous as His creation; glory be to Allah, as weighty as His Throne; glory be to Allah, to the extent of His own pleasure; glory be to Allah, as abundant as the ink of His words.”

In an authentic divine hadith reported from the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Allah Most High says: ‘I am as My servant thinks of Me, and I am with him when he remembers Me. If he remembers Me within himself, I remember him within Myself; and if he remembers Me in an assembly, I remember him in an assembly better than that one.’”

In these passages, according to the majority of scholars, what is meant by the term nafs is Allah Himself

---

## Page 4558

, that is, His Essence, endowed with His attributes. It does not mean an essence separate from the attributes, nor an attribute of the Essence. Some people class it among the attributes, just as others suppose it means the Essence stripped of attributes. Both views are mistaken.

The word nafs may also mean the blood within an animal, as when jurists speak of “creatures with flowing blood and creatures without flowing blood.” Hence nafisat is said of a woman when she menstruates, and nufisat (*) when she gives birth; from this comes nufasā’ (a woman experiencing postnatal bleeding). The poet likewise says:

“Our blood flows upon the edges of swords;
Upon nothing but sword edges does it flow.”

Neither of these two meanings of nafs is a meaning of rūh.

Many later authors use nafs to refer to its blameworthy qualities. Thus they say, “So-and-so has a nafs,” or, “Abandon your nafs.” An example is the statement of Abū Marthad:

“I saw the Lord of Might in a dream and said, ‘My Lord, what is the way to You?’ He said, ‘Abandon your nafs.’”

Clearly, he does not abandon his very self; rather, he abandons its desires and blameworthy deeds. Such expressions are common in speech. People say, “So-and-so has a tongue,” “So-and-so has a far-reaching hand,” or, “So-and-so has a heart.” What is meant is an articulate tongue, an active, productive hand, and a living heart that recognizes the truth and desires it. Allah Most High says:

“Indeed, in that is a reminder for whoever has a heart or listens while fully attentive.”

Likewise, because the soul so often follows its desires while attached to the body, it came about that

> **Footnotes:**
> Shaykh Nāsir ibn Hamad al-Fahd stated (p. 80):
> It appears thus in al-Majmū‘ without vowel marks. The vowelled forms are nafisat—with a fathah (a) on the n—meaning that a woman menstruated; and nufisat—with a dammah (u) on the n—meaning that she gave birth.

---

## Page 4559

the word nafs (soul) is used to refer to the soul that follows its desires, or to its following those desires. By contrast, the word rūh (spirit) is not used in this sense, since it does not designate the soul in its capacity as the governor of the body.

Souls are said to be of three types:

The “soul that commands evil,” which is predominantly driven to follow its desires by committing sins and acts of disobedience.

The “self-reproaching soul,” which sins and repents. Both good and evil proceed from it, but when it does evil, it repents and turns back to Allah. It is called self-reproaching because it reproaches its possessor for sins, and because it wavers—that is, fluctuates between good and evil.

The “tranquil soul,” which loves and desires goodness and good deeds, and hates and abhors evil and bad deeds. This has become its character, its habit, and its settled disposition.

These are attributes and states of one and the same entity; each human being has only one soul. This is something a person recognizes within himself.

Some physicians who espouse philosophy have said that there are three souls: a vegetative soul located in the liver, an animal soul located in the heart, and a rational soul located in the brain. If they mean by this three faculties associated with these organs, that is conceded. But if they mean three distinct, self-subsisting entities, this is a manifest error.

---

## Page 4560

Section

As for the questioner’s words, “Does it have a knowable mode of being?” this question is ambiguous. If he means knowing what can be known of its attributes and states, these are indeed knowable. But if he means whether it has a counterpart of the same kind as the bodies he observes, or whether it belongs to the same kind as any of them, then this is not the case. It is not of the same kind as the elements—water, air, fire, and earth—nor as animal, plant, or mineral bodies, nor as the celestial spheres and stars. It has neither an observable counterpart nor a familiar kind to which it belongs. This is why it is said that its mode of being is unknown.

It is also said, “Whoever knows himself knows his Lord,” by way of inference, contrast, and impossibility.

As for “inference,” a person knows that he is living, knowing, capable, hearing, seeing, and speaking. Through this, he comes to understand what Allah has revealed about Himself: that He is Living, All-Knowing, All-Powerful, All-Hearing, and All-Seeing. Had he not conceived of these meanings through his own experience and reflection upon himself, he could not understand what lies beyond his perception—just as, were it not for his conception of what exists in this world, such as honey and milk

---

## Page 4561

and water, wine, silk, gold, and silver, he could not conceive of what he has been told of such things in the unseen.

Yet this does not mean that the unseen must be like the visible world. Ibn ‘Abbās, may Allah be pleased with him, said, “Nothing of what is in Paradise exists in this world except its names.” The realities we have been told exist in Paradise are not identical to the things in this world, such that the same things would be possible, necessary, or impossible for both, their substance would be the same, and they would undergo the same transformations. We know that the water of Paradise neither spoils nor becomes stagnant, its milk does not change in taste, and its wine neither gives its drinker a headache nor deprives him of his reason. Its water does not spring from the earth or descend from clouds like water in this world, nor is its milk produced from livestock as it is in this world; and likewise with other such things.

Thus, one created thing shares a name with another, and they have a common measure of meaning and resemblance through which we understand what we are being told, even though their actual realities are not alike. The Creator—exalted is His Majesty—is far further removed from resembling His creatures than the things in Paradise are from resembling those in this world. When He describes Himself as Living, All-Knowing, All-Hearing, All-Seeing, and All-Powerful, this does not entail that He resembles His creation, for the disparity between Him and His creation is greater than the disparity between any created beings. Every small animal has life, strength, and activity, yet these animals do not resemble the created angels. How, then, could the Lord of all worlds resemble any created being?

Allah, glorified and exalted is He, has given Himself and His attributes names that He has also applied to some created beings. Thus, He has called Himself Living, All-Knowing, All-Hearing, All-Seeing, Almighty, the Compeller, Supreme in Majesty,

---

## Page 4562

the Sovereign, Most Kind, Most Merciful; and He has described some of His servants as learned, some as forbearing, some as kind and merciful, some as hearing and seeing, some as kings, some as mighty, and some as tyrannical and arrogant. It is well known that the All-Knowing is not like one who is learned, nor the Forbearing like one who is forbearing, nor the All-Hearing like one who hears. The same applies to all the other names.

He, glorified and exalted is He, says:

“Indeed, Allah is All-Knowing, All-Wise.”

And He says:

“They gave him glad tidings of a learned boy.”

And He says:

“Indeed, He is ever Forbearing, All-Forgiving.”

And He says:

“So We gave him glad tidings of a forbearing boy.”

And He says:

“Indeed, Allah is Most Kind and Most Merciful to mankind.”

And He says:

“Kind and merciful to the believers.”

And He says:

“Indeed, Allah is All-Hearing, All-Seeing.”

And He, exalted is He, says:

“Mingled fluids, that We may test him; and We made him hearing and seeing.”

The same applies to everything else mentioned. Yet a person uses what he knows to understand what he does not know. Were it not for this, the paths to knowledge of unseen things would be closed to him.

As for “contrast,” it is said: Whoever recognizes his own servitude recognizes his Lord’s lordship; whoever recognizes his own need recognizes his Lord’s self-sufficiency; whoever recognizes his own incapacity recognizes his Lord’s power; whoever recognizes his own ignorance recognizes his Lord’s knowledge; and whoever recognizes his own lowliness recognizes his Lord’s might. The same applies to similar examples. For the servant has, of himself, nothing but nonexistence, and all attributes of imperfection ultimately amount to nonexistence.

The Lord, exalted is He, however, possesses the attributes of perfection, which are inseparable from His essence. It is impossible for Him ever to be without the attributes of perfection, whether in eternity past or eternity future. Their nonexistence is impossible, for His existence is necessary, without beginning or end. His attributes of perfection are inseparable from His essence, and what is inseparable from something cannot cease to exist unless that thing itself ceases to exist. Thus, none of His attributes of perfection can be considered nonexistent unless His essence is nonexistent.

---

## Page 4563

and nonexistence is impossible for His essence, so it is likewise impossible for any of His attributes of perfection.

As for “inability and impossibility,” it is said: If a person does not know the modality of his own soul or fully comprehend its reality, although it is the nearest thing to him—indeed, his very identity—then a servant is all the less able to know the modality of the Creator, majestic is His glory, or fully comprehend His reality. Hence the best of creation and the most knowledgeable of his Lord, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“O Allah, I seek refuge in Your pleasure from Your wrath, in Your pardon from Your punishment, and in You from You. I cannot enumerate Your praises; You are as You have praised Yourself.”

It is established in Sahīh Muslim and elsewhere that he would say this in prostration. Al-Tirmidhī and others also narrated that he would say it during the supplication in the witr prayer. Even if this hadith is open to question, the former is authentic and firmly established.

Section

As for the questioner’s inquiry, “Is it a substance or an accident?” the term “substance” (jawhar) is ambiguous. Clearly, the questioner did not intend the ordinary linguistic meaning of jawhar—although it has been said that the word jawhar is not native Arabic but an Arabicized borrowing. Rather, he intended its technical meaning in the division of existing things into substances and accidents.

---

## Page 4564

Some of these thinkers use “substance” to mean that which occupies space; thus, for them, a body occupying space is a substance. They may also mean by it the indivisible atom: the part that cannot be divided. Thinkers disagree over its existence. The question is whether bodies are composed of indivisible atoms, of matter and form, or of neither. There are three views, the soundest being the third: bodies are composed neither of indivisible atoms nor of matter and form. This is the view of many groups among the speculative theologians, such as the Hishāmiyya, Dirāriyya, Najjāriyya, Kullābiyya, and many of the Karrāmiyya. It is also the view of the majority of jurists, hadith scholars, Sufis, and others. Indeed, it is the view of most rational thinkers, as has been explained at length elsewhere.

Those who apply the term “substance” to what occupies space disagree as to whether a substance that does not occupy space can exist. Some of them say that everything that exists is either a substance or an accident, and include the Necessary Existent within the designation “substance.” Others say that everything that exists is either a body or an accident, and include the Necessary Existent within the designation “body.” Both views have been held by some Muslim and non-Muslim speculative thinkers.

Among the philosophers and Christians are those who call Him a substance but not a body. It is also reported of one Muslim speculative thinker that he calls Him a body but not a substance, though, in his terminology, a body

---

## Page 4565

is that which can be pointed to or that which subsists in itself; in his terminology, “substance” means the indivisible atom. In ordinary Arabic, the term rendered “accident” means something that appears and passes away, as Allah Most High says:

“They take the fleeting gains of this lowly world.”

In the technical usage of speculative theology, however, “accident” may mean anything that subsists in something else, without qualification, or it may mean the attributes that subsist in a body. Outside this terminology, it has other meanings.

It is well known that the position of the early generations, the leading imams, and Ahl al-Sunnah wa-l-Jamā‘ah generally is to affirm Allah’s attributes: that He has knowledge, power, life, and speech. They call these attributes. Some of them say: “They are attributes, not accidents, because an accident does not persist for two successive moments, whereas these endure.” Others say: “Rather, they may be called accidents, because an accident may endure. The claim that no accident persists for two successive moments is weak. If enduring attributes are called accidents, then these may also be called accidents.” Still others say: “I do not use that designation,” holding that such usage must be grounded in revelation.

There are two well-known views on whether Allah may be called by a term whose meaning is sound according to language, reason, and revelation, even if neither a scriptural text nor consensus sanctions its use, or whether only terms sanctioned by a scriptural text or consensus may be used. Most speculative thinkers use terms for which there is neither a scriptural text nor consensus sanctioning their use, such as “eternal” and “essence”

---

## Page 4566

and the like. Some distinguish between the names by which He is invoked and the terms used to speak about Him when needed. He, glorified be He, is invoked only by the most beautiful names, as He says:

“To Allah belong the most beautiful names, so call upon Him by them.”

When, however, there is a need to speak about Him—for example, if someone says, “He is neither eternal nor existent nor a self-subsisting essence,” or the like—one may reply, affirming the truth: “Rather, He, glorified be He, is eternal and existent, and He is a self-subsisting essence.” Likewise, if someone says, “He is not a thing,” one may reply, “Rather, He is a thing.” This is permissible, even though He is not invoked by such designations, which contain nothing indicative of praise, as when someone says, “O thing!” For this term encompasses everything that exists. The same applies to the terms “essence,” “existent,” and the like—unless “existent” is used as a designation meaning the One whom whoever seeks Him finds, as in His words:

“And finds Allah there.”

This meaning is more specific than “existent” in the sense that encompasses both the Creator and created beings.

With this established, the soul—which is the spirit that governs the human body—belongs to the category of that which subsists in itself, termed a substance and a self-subsisting entity. It does not belong to the category of accidents, which are attributes subsisting in something else. As for describing it by the terms “substance” and “body,” there is disagreement, partly terminological and partly substantive. Whoever understands “substance” to mean that which subsists in itself considers it a substance. Whoever understands “body” to mean that which can be pointed to, and holds that the soul can be pointed to, considers it a body. Whoever understands “body” to mean that which is composed

---

## Page 4567

of indivisible atoms or of matter and form may likewise regard the soul as a body; some who hold this view do so. Among those who understand “substance” to mean that which occupies space and is divisible, some consider the soul a substance. The correct view is that it is composed neither of indivisible atoms nor of matter and form, and that it does not belong to the category of familiar, observable bodies that occupy space. As for pointing to it, it can indeed be pointed to. It ascends and descends, leaves the body and is drawn out of it, as the scriptural texts attest and rational evidence indicates.

Section

As for the question, “Where in the body does it reside?” the spirit is not confined to any particular part of the body. Rather, it permeates the body, just as life—which is an accident—permeates the entire body. Life depends upon the spirit: when the spirit is in the body, the body is alive; when the spirit leaves it, life leaves it too.

---

## Page 4568

Section

As for his question, “Where in the body does the intellect reside?” the intellect subsists in the human soul, which reasons. As regards the body, it is connected to the heart, as Allah Most High says:

“Have they not travelled through the land, so that they may have hearts with which to reason?”

Ibn ‘Abbās was asked, “How did you acquire knowledge?” He replied, “With an inquisitive tongue and an understanding heart.”

However, the word “heart” may refer to the pine-cone-shaped piece of flesh on the left side of the body, which contains a black clot. As reported in the two Sahīh collections, the Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Indeed, in the body there is a piece of flesh: when it is sound, the rest of the body is sound, and when it is corrupt, the rest of the body is corrupt.”

“Heart” may also refer to a person’s entire inner being, for the heart of something is its interior, like the kernel of a grain of wheat, an almond, a walnut, and the like. Hence a well is called a qalīb, because its heart—its interior—has been removed. Accordingly, when “heart” is used in this sense, the intellect is also connected to the brain. This is why it has been said that the intellect is in the brain, as many physicians maintain. This view has also been reported from Imam Ahmad. A group of his followers say, “The intellect originates in the heart; when it reaches maturity, it extends to the brain.”

The correct conclusion is that the spirit, which is the soul, is connected to both, and whatever it possesses

---

## Page 4569

of intellect is connected to both. However, thought and reflection originate in the brain, while the will originates in the heart.

“Intellect” may refer to knowledge or to action. Knowledge and voluntary action are rooted in the will, and the will originates in the heart. A person cannot will something without first conceiving what is willed, so the heart must form a conception of it. Both therefore proceed from the heart. This begins in the brain, and its effects ascend to the brain: it is both the starting point and the endpoint. Each of the two views has a valid aspect.

This is as much as these pages can accommodate. Allah knows best.

---

## Page 4570

The shaykh—may Allah have mercy on him—was asked:
“Which is superior: knowledge or intellect?”

He replied:
If “knowledge” means the knowledge of Allah Most High that He has sent down, namely the Book—as He, Most High, says:

“Whoever disputes with you concerning him after knowledge has come to you”

—then this is superior to human intellect, because it is an attribute of the Creator, whereas intellect is an attribute of the created being. An attribute of the Creator is superior to an attribute of the created being.

If “intellect” means that a servant understands Allah’s commands and prohibitions, doing what he is commanded and refraining from what he is forbidden, then this intellect brings its possessor into Paradise. It is superior to knowledge that does not bring its possessor into Paradise, as in the case of someone who knows but does not act.

If “intellect” means the innate faculty Allah has placed in the servant, through which he attains knowledge and acts, then what is attained through it is superior. Knowledge is the intended end, while the innate faculty of intellect is a means to it, and ends are superior to their means.

If “intellect” means the knowledge acquired through this innate faculty, then this is itself a form of knowledge, so one should not ask: “Which

---

## Page 4571

is superior: knowledge or intellect?” Rather, one should ask: “Which is superior: this knowledge or that knowledge?” Some kinds of knowledge are superior to others. Knowledge of Allah is superior to knowledge of His creation. This is why the Verse of the Throne is the greatest verse in the Quran: it describes Allah Most High.

And “Say, ‘He is Allah, One’” is equivalent to one-third of the Quran, because the Quran comprises “three thirds”: one-third concerns Allah’s oneness, one-third consists of narratives, and one-third consists of commands and prohibitions. The third concerning Allah’s oneness is superior to the others.

The answer to this question—the question of knowledge and intellect—must distinguish between their different meanings, because each of these two terms can have many meanings. It is therefore impermissible to give an unqualified answer without making these distinctions. This is why there has been so much disagreement over this question among those who fail to distinguish between these meanings. Whoever makes these distinctions in his answer is correct. And Allah knows best.

---

## Page 4572

And Shaykh al-Islām, the learned and erudite scholar, Shaykh al-Islām Taqī al-Dīn Abū al-Abbās Ahmad ibn Taymiyyah al-Harrānī—may Allah sanctify his soul and illuminate his grave—said:

Section

Furthermore, Allah, glorified and exalted is He, created the human heart so that a person might know things through it, just as He created the eye to see things and the ear to hear things. Likewise, He—glorified is He—created each bodily organ for a particular purpose and function: the hand for grasping, the foot for walking, the tongue for speaking, the mouth for tasting, the nose for smelling, and the skin for touching. The same applies to all the other organs, internal and external.

When a person uses an organ for the purpose for which it was created and equipped, this is the rightful order and the justice by which the heavens and the earth stand. This is good and beneficial for that organ, its owner, and whatever it is used upon. And that righteous person

---

## Page 4573

is one whose condition is sound. Such people are described thus:

“Those are upon guidance from their Lord, and it is they who are successful.”

If an organ is not used for its rightful purpose but is left idle, that is a loss, and its owner is the loser. If it is used contrary to the purpose for which it was created, that is misguidance and ruin, and its owner is among those who have exchanged Allah’s blessing for disbelief.

The master and chief of all the organs is the heart, as its very name suggests. The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said:

“Indeed, in the body there is a piece of flesh: if it is sound, the whole body is sound, and if it is corrupt, the whole body is corrupt. Indeed, it is the heart.”

He also said, may Allah bless him and grant him peace:

“Islam is outward, while faith is in the heart.” Then he pointed with his hand to his chest and said, “Indeed, mindfulness of Allah is here. Indeed, mindfulness of Allah is here.”

Since the heart was created as a means of acquiring knowledge, its turning toward things in pursuit of knowledge of them is thought and reflection. Similarly, directing the ear toward speech in order to hear it is attentive listening, and turning one’s gaze toward things in order to see them is looking. Thought is thus to the heart what attentive listening is to the ear, and likewise what looking is to the eyes, as mentioned above. When the heart comes to know what it has reflected upon, it has attained its goal, just as the ear does when it hears what it has listened for, or the eye when it sees what it has looked toward. Yet how many people reflect and think without acquiring or attaining knowledge, just as many look for the crescent moon without seeing it, or listen for a sound without hearing it!

---

## Page 4574

Conversely, someone may be granted knowledge of something he has neither examined nor previously thought about, just as someone may unexpectedly see the crescent moon without looking for it, or hear words without listening attentively. None of this is because the heart receives knowledge on its own; rather, it depends upon certain conditions and a readiness that may result from a person’s own effort, in which case it is something sought, or may come through Allah’s bounty, in which case it is a gift.

The heart’s soundness, its proper function, and the purpose for which it was created thus lie in comprehending things. I do not say merely knowing them, for someone may know something without comprehending it, remaining heedless of it and disregarding it. Someone who comprehends a thing grasps it, masters it, retains it, and fixes it firmly in his heart, so that he is well equipped when he needs it. His deeds then accord with his words, and his inward state with his outward conduct. Such a person has been granted wisdom:

“Whoever is granted wisdom has certainly been granted much good.”

Abū al-Dardā’ said:

“Some people are granted knowledge but are not granted sound judgment. Shaddād ibn Aws was among those granted both knowledge and sound judgment.”

Moreover, people differ in their very comprehension of things, ranging from complete to deficient, and in what they comprehend, whether little or much, great matters or subtle ones, and so forth.

These three organs are the principal means by which knowledge is acquired and perceived. I mean the knowledge that distinguishes human beings from other animals, rather than that which they share with them through smell and taste

---

## Page 4575

and touch. In this sphere, such knowledge enables one to recognize what one likes and dislikes and to distinguish between those who treat one well and those who mistreat one, and so forth.

Allah Most High says:

“Allah brought you out of your mothers’ wombs knowing nothing, and gave you hearing, sight, and hearts, so that you might be grateful.”

He also says:

“Then He fashioned him and breathed into him of His spirit, and gave you hearing, sight, and hearts. How little thanks you give!”

And He says:

“Do not pursue that of which you have no knowledge. Indeed, hearing, sight, and the heart—one will be questioned about all of these.”

And He says:

“We gave them hearing, sight, and hearts.”

And He says:

“Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and over their eyes is a covering.”

Regarding the function and capacity of each of these organs, He says:

“We have certainly created for Hell many of the jinn and humankind. They have hearts with which they do not understand, eyes with which they do not see, and ears with which they do not hear.”

The eye falls short of the heart and the ear, differing from them in one respect: through it, a person sees only things that are present and corporeal, such as forms and bodies. Through the heart and the ear, however, a person can know what is absent from him, as well as spiritual matters and abstract concepts that lie beyond the scope of sight. The latter two then differ from one another:

The heart itself comprehends things, since knowledge is its nourishment and distinctive attribute. The ear, by contrast, conveys speech containing knowledge to the heart; in itself, it merely carries words and speech. When these reach the heart, it draws from them the knowledge they contain. Thus, the true possessor of knowledge is the heart. All the other organs are merely its gatekeepers, conveying to it information that it would not

---

## Page 4576

acquire on its own. Indeed, whoever loses one of these organs thereby loses the knowledge for which it served as the means. A deaf person does not know the knowledge conveyed in speech, and a blind person does not perceive the profound wisdom embodied in visible forms. Likewise, whoever looks at things without engaging his heart, or listens to the words of people of knowledge without engaging his heart, understands nothing. Everything therefore hinges on the heart.

This makes clear the wisdom in the words of Allah Most High:

“Have they not travelled through the land, so that they might have hearts with which to reason or ears with which to hear?”

Here He does not even mention the eye, as He does in the preceding verses, because the discussion concerns unseen matters and wisdom discerned from the outcomes of events—matters beyond the scope of eyesight. Similar are His words:

“Or do you think that most of them hear or reason?”

The reality of the matter becomes clear in His words:

“Indeed, in that is a reminder for whoever has a heart or lends an ear while fully attentive.”

Those who are granted wisdom and benefit from knowledge fall into two categories. One person recognizes the truth for himself, accepts it, and follows it, without needing anyone to call him to it. This is the person who has a heart.

Another does not understand it for himself, but needs someone to teach him, explain it to him, admonish him, and instil proper conduct in him. Such a person listens attentively, and thus:

“lends an ear while fully attentive.”

That is, his heart is present, not absent. As Mujāhid said:

“He was given knowledge, and it became a reminder for him.”

His following words also become clear:

“And among them are those who listen to you. Can you then make the deaf hear, even if they do not

---

## Page 4577

reason?”

“And among them are those who look at you. Can you then guide the blind, even if they do not see?”

And His words:

“And among them are those who listen to you, but We have placed coverings over their hearts so that they cannot understand it, and deafness in their ears.”

Now, if the heart’s proper task is to know the truth, Allah is indeed the Manifest Truth:

“That is Allah, your true Lord. What is there beyond the truth except error?”

For everything that meets a passing glance or crosses the mind in a fleeting thought has Allah as its Lord, its Maker, its Creator and its Originator. One can encompass in knowledge only what belongs to His manifest signs in His earth and His heavens.

The truest words ever spoken by a poet were those of Labīd:

“Indeed, everything apart from Allah is vain.”

That is, whenever you consider anything in itself, you find it tending towards nonexistence, and it is not dependent on the Ever-Living, the Sustainer of all. But when you consider it as something embraced by providential care, through the decree of Him who gave everything its form and then guided it, you then see it existing, clothed in robes of bounty and beneficence.

It has thus become clear that the heart was created only for the remembrance of Allah, glorified be He. Hence one of the early sages of Syria—I think it was Sulaymān al-Khawwās, may Allah have mercy on him—said:

“Remembrance is to the heart what food is to the body. Just as the body finds no pleasure in food when it is ill, so the heart finds no sweetness in remembrance when it harbours love of this world.”

Or words to that effect. Thus, when the heart is occupied with Allah, comprehending the truth and reflecting on knowledge, then it has

---

## Page 4578

been put in its proper place, just as the eye is put in its proper place when directed towards looking at things. But if the heart is not directed towards knowledge and the truth is not retained within it, then it has forgotten its Lord and has not been placed anywhere; rather, it is lost. We need not say that it has been put in the wrong place: it has not been placed anywhere at all. Its place is the truth, and whatever lies beyond the truth is falsehood. If it is not placed within the truth, nothing remains but falsehood. Falsehood is nothing at all, and what is nothing is even less fit to serve as a place. The heart itself accepts only the truth. If it is not placed there, it will not accept anything other than that for which it was created.

“Allah’s established way.”

“And you will never find any change in Allah’s established way.”

Yet the heart is not simply left empty and unattended. It continues wandering through the valleys of thought and the realms of wishful thinking; it cannot remain idle and unoccupied as the eye and ear can. Thus it has been placed where there is no place—neither free nor suspended, placed yet without a place. This is indeed astonishing! Glory be to our Lord, the Almighty, the All-Wise.

This condition becomes apparent to a person only when he returns to the truth, whether in this world when he turns back to Allah, or when he passes into the Hereafter. He then sees how wretched his former condition was and how far his heart had strayed from the truth. This is the case when the heart is directed towards falsehood.

But if it were left in the natural state in which it was created, unoccupied by any remembrance and free of every thought, it would accept knowledge untainted by ignorance and perceive the truth in which there is no doubt. It would then believe in its Lord and turn to Him. For every child is born upon the natural disposition, and it is his parents who make him a Jew, a Christian, or a Magian, just as an animal gives birth to an offspring whole and intact, with no sign of mutilation.

---

## Page 4579

“The natural disposition with which Allah created mankind—there is no altering Allah’s creation. That is the upright religion.”

What generally stands between the heart and the truth is its preoccupation with other things: worldly temptations, bodily demands, and the desires of the soul. In this state, it resembles an eye looking down at the ground, unable at the same time to see the crescent moon. Or it may incline towards those distractions, which then prevent it from following the truth; it is then like an eye with a speck in it, unable to see things.

Desire may intervene before a person knows the truth, preventing him from examining it, so that the truth never becomes clear to him. As it has been said, “Your love of a thing makes you blind and deaf.” He thus remains in the darkness of his thoughts. This often arises from pride that prevents him from seeking the truth:

“Those who do not believe in the Hereafter—their hearts deny, and they are arrogant.”

Desire may also intervene after he has recognized the truth, causing him to deny it and turn away from it, as our Lord, glorified be He, says of such people:

“I will turn away from My signs those who behave arrogantly on earth without right. Even if they see every sign, they will not believe in it. If they see the path of sound guidance, they will not take it as their way; but if they see the path of error, they will take it as their way.”

The heart, moreover, holds knowledge as a vessel holds water, a container holds honey, and a valley holds floodwater. As Allah Most High says:

“He sends down water from the sky, and valleys flow according to their capacity,” and the remainder of the verse.

The Prophet, may Allah bless him and grant him peace, said: “The guidance and knowledge with which Allah has sent me are like rain that falls upon a land: one part of it absorbs the water and brings forth herbage and abundant grass

---

## Page 4580

, while other parts were barren ground that held the water, which people used for watering and cultivation. The rain also fell upon another part consisting only of level plains that neither held water nor produced herbage. This is the likeness of one who gains understanding of Allah’s religion and benefits from what I have been sent with, and of one who pays it no heed and does not accept Allah’s guidance with which I have been sent.”

In the report narrated by Kumayl ibn Ziyād from ʿAlī, may Allah be pleased with him, he said:

“Hearts are vessels, and the best of them are those that retain the most.”

It has also reached us that one of the early generations said:

“Hearts are Allah’s vessels on His earth, and those dearest to Allah Most High are the tenderest and purest.”

This is a fine analogy. When the heart is tender and soft, it receives knowledge readily and easily; knowledge takes firm root within it, remains there, and has its effect. But when the heart is hard and coarse, receiving knowledge becomes difficult and arduous. Alongside this, the heart must be pure, clear, and sound, so that knowledge may flourish within it and bear good fruit. Otherwise, even if it receives knowledge, any impurity and corruption within it will spoil that knowledge. It is like weeds among crops: even if they do not prevent the seed from sprouting, they prevent it from flourishing and yielding good produce. This is clear to those with insight.

In summary, when the heart is employed in the service of truth, it has two aspects:

One aspect turns towards the truth. In this respect, the heart is called a vessel or a receptacle, for this necessarily implies something held and placed within it. This is a positive, affirmative attribute.

The other aspect turns away from falsehood. In this respect, the heart is described as pure and sound

---

## Page 4581

and clean; for these terms denote the absence of evil, impurity, and corruption. This is an attribute of absence and negation. It thus becomes clear that when the heart is turned towards falsehood, it likewise has two aspects.

Its affirmative aspect is that it turns towards falsehood and is occupied with it. Its negative aspect is that it turns away from truth and does not receive it. This reveals the clarity, beauty, and truth contained in the words:

“If you place the heart where it does not belong,
Without a vessel, it is a wasteful heart.”

When he wished to describe someone who wastes his heart and thereby wrongs himself—by occupying himself with falsehood and filling his heart with it until no room remains for truth and no way remains for truth to enter—he described both aspects of that heart and portrayed both its orientations. He first mentioned its affirmative attribute, saying:

“If you place the heart where it does not belong.”

He means: if you occupy it with something for which it was not created, turning it towards falsehood until it becomes lodged therein.

Falsehood, in turn, falls into two categories:

---

## Page 4582

The first distracts from the truth without opposing it, such as thoughts and worries concerning worldly attachments and the desires of the soul.

The second opposes the truth and turns people away from it, such as false opinions and ruinous desires involving disbelief, hypocrisy, religious innovations, and the like. Indeed, the heart was created only for the remembrance of Allah; anything else is not its proper place.

He then mentions, secondly, its negative attribute, saying, “Without a vessel.” He means: when you place it without a vessel, you waste it, having no vessel with you—just as you might say, “I attended the gathering without an inkwell.” Thus, the phrase describes the state of the one placing it, not of the thing being placed. And Allah knows best.

The explanation of this statement—and Allah knows best—is that he is saying: when you put your heart where it does not belong, it becomes occupied with falsehood, and you have no vessel in which the truth may be placed and into which remembrance and knowledge, which are the heart’s due, may descend. Your heart is therefore wasted: you have wasted it in two ways, although these amount to the same thing. You have placed it where it does not belong, and you have no vessel to contain the truth that it ought to receive.

It is like saying of a king who has turned to amusement: “If he occupies himself with something other than his kingdom, while there is no one in the kingdom to govern it, he is a king who has lost his way.” Here, however, the vessel is the heart itself. This is because nothing else can take the heart’s place in receiving what must be placed within it:

“No bearer of burdens will bear the burden of another.”

---

## Page 4583

The wording presents them as two only because it mentions two attributes of a single thing. A similar example occurs in the words of Allah Most High:

“He has sent down to you the Book in truth, confirming what came before it, and He sent down the Torah and the Gospel previously, as guidance for mankind, and He sent down the Criterion.”

Qatāda and al-Rabī' said: “It is the Quran, in which He distinguished between the lawful and the unlawful, and between truth and falsehood.” This is because, when a single thing has two major attributes, considering one attribute presents it as one thing, while considering both gives it the standing of two. Indeed, if its attributes are numerous, it may have the standing of several persons. Do you not see that a man skilled in arithmetic and medicine is equivalent to both a calculator and a physician, while a man skilled in carpentry and building is equivalent to both a carpenter and a builder?

Since the heart receives remembrance and knowledge, it is like a vessel into which water is placed. In this line of verse, he specifically calls it a vessel, rather than using any of the heart’s other names, because a vessel can be delicate and clear, and because it is what someone seeking food and charity brings, in the position of a destitute pauper. Since the heart also turns away from falsehood, it is pure and sound. It is thus as though it were two things.

To make the vessel appear distinct from the heart in this illustration, he says:

When you place your heart where it does not belong—although it is where remembrance and knowledge are to be placed—and you have no vessel with you in which to receive what you seek, you are like a man who heard that a wealthy person was distributing food to people, and who had a bowl

---

## Page 4584

or a small dish, but left it behind and then came asking for food. He was told: “Bring a vessel, and we will give you food.” But if you come having left your bowl at home, for example, with no vessel in which to receive the food, you receive nothing and return empty-handed.

Anyone with insight into the forms of eloquence and the varied expressions of language who reflects on these words will find them apt and eloquent, both in their Arabic expression and in their wisdom. The opposite of the condition described is for the heart to turn toward truth, knowledge, and remembrance, turning away from everything else. That is hanīfiyya (pure monotheism), the religion of Abraham, peace be upon him. For hanaf means the turning and inclination of one foot toward the other. Thus, hanaf is turning away from one thing by turning toward another. The purely monotheistic religion therefore consists in turning to Allah alone and turning away from all besides Him.

This is the sincere devotion expressed by the word of truth, the good word:

“There is no deity worthy of worship except Allah.”

O Allah, keep us steadfast upon it in this world and the Hereafter. There is no power nor strength except through Allah. This is the last of what has come to mind at this time. Allah knows best, and may Allah bless Muhammad.
